ii 
 
PREFACE 
I, the Chairman of the Department -related Parliamentary Standing Committee on 
Health and Family Welfare, having been authorized by the Committee to present the Report 
on its behalf, hereby present this 172 nd Report of the Committee on the  Demands for Grants 
(Demand No. 46) for the year 2026 -27 of the Department of Health and Family Welfare, 
Ministry of Health and Family Welfare. 
 
2.  The Department -related Parliamentary Standing Committees are entrusted with the 
constitutional role of ensu ring accountability of executive to the Parliament.  In this regard, 
Rule 270 (a) of Rules & Procedure and Conduct & Business in the Council of States (Rajya 
Sabha) empowered the Department-related Standing Committees to consider the Demands for 
Grants of the related Departments/Ministries with the objective of ensuring financial 
accountability of the executive and report thereon. In exercise of its constitutional mandate, 
the Department -related Parliamentary Standing Committee on Health & Family Welfare 
scrutinised the Demands for Grants for the year 2026 -27 (Demand No. 46) of the Department 
of Health and Family Welfare, Ministry of Health and Family Welfare. The Committee, at its 
meeting held on 24th February, 2026 for examination of Demands for Grants (20 26-27) of the 
Department of Health and Family Welfare heard the Secretary and other Officers of the 
Department. 
 
3.  The Committee, in its meeting held on 18th March, 2026, considered the Draft Report 
and adopted the same.  
4.  The Committee while making i ts observations/recommendations has mainly relied 
upon the following documents:– 
i. Detailed Demands for Grants of the Department of Health and Family Welfare for 
the year 2026-27; 
ii. Annual Report of the Department for the year 2025-26; 
iii. Detailed Explanatory Not e on Demands for Grants of the Department of Health 
and Family Welfare for the year 2026-27; 
iv. Written replies furnished by the Department to the Questionnaires sent to them by 
the Secretariat;  
v. Presentation made by the Secretary (Ministry of Health and Fami ly Welfare) and 
other concerned officers; 
vi. Economic Survey 2025-26; 
vii. National Health Accounts 2021-22;  
viii. Global Multidimensional Poverty Index 2024; 
ix. Budget Speech of the Finance Minister, President Speech; and 
x. WHO's World Health Statistics 2022 Report etc. 
xi. Expenditure Budget 
xii. Output - outcome Budget 
 
5.  For facility of reference and convenience, observations and recommendations of the 
Committee have been printed in bold letters in the body of the Report.

1 
 
CHAPTER I 
INTRODUCTION 
1.1.1 India‘s healthcare system has undergone a profound transformation, evolving from its 
early foundations in community -oriented care to a modern, mul ti-tiered framework that 
integrates primary, secondary, and tertiary services. Rooted in the ancient ethos of Arogya 
(holistic well-being) and reinforced by successive national health policies, the system today 
reflects a balance between public responsibil ity and private sector innovation. Guided by the 
principles of Sabka Saath, Sabka Vikas, Sabka Prayas , India envisions health as the 
cornerstone of national development and human capital growth. 
1.1.2 The government‘s vision, articulated through initiative s like the National Health 
Policy 2017 and Ayushman Bharat, is to achieve Universal Health Coverage (UHC) by 
ensuring equitable access, financial protection, and quality care across all socio -economic 
segments. Commitment for UHC is embedded in the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), 
specifically target 3.8, which measures progress through two key indicators: service coverage 
and financial protection against out -of-pocket expenditure. India‘s policies and programmes 
are designed to accelerate progress toward these goals before the 2030 deadline.  
1.1.3 At the heart of this vision , India has launched ambitious national initiatives under 
Ayushman Bharat, which integrates primary care delivery through Ayushman Arogya 
Mandirs, financial protection via Pradhan Mantri Jan Arogya Yojana (PMJAY), infrastructure 
strengthening under Ayushman Bharat Health Infrastructure Mission ( PM-ABHIM), and 
digital integration through Ayushman Bharat Digital Mission ( ABDM). These pillars 
collectively advance UHC by expanding servi ce coverage and reducing financial hardship. 
India‘s healthcare objectives also emphasize mission -mode disease elimination and 
immunisation coverage, aligning with the UHC service coverage index. India is advancing 
both dimensions of UHC: expanding essenti al service coverage and reducing financial 
hardship. 
1.2 MINISTRY OF HEALTH AND FAMILY WELFARE 
1.2.1 The Ministry of Health and Family Welfare is the nodal Ministry that is responsible 
for formulation and implementation of various schemes, programmes and n ational initiatives 
to provide universal access to quality healthcare. The Ministry of Health and Family Welfare 
comprises of the following two Departments: 
(a) Department of Health and Family Welfare; and 
(b) Department of Health Research 
1.2.2 The Minist ry of Health and Family Welfare has also the following two attached 
Offices: 
(a) Directorate General of Health Services (DGHS):  It is an attached office of the 
Department of Health and Family Welfare and has subordinate offices spread all over 
the country. The  DGHS renders technical advice on all medical and public health 
matters and is involved in the implementation of various health services. 

2 
 
(b) National Health Authority  (NHA):  It has been set up as an attached Office to 
Ministry of Health & Family welfare vide extraordinary Gazette Notification no. 
3(4)12018- H&FW (Part.III) Vol.2 dated 01.02.2019 of NITI Aayog. NHA is the 
apex body responsible for implementing India's flagship public health 
insurance/assurance scheme called "Ayushman Bharat - Pradhan Mantri Jan  Arogya 
Yojana" & has been entrusted with the role of designing strategy, building 
technological infrastructure and implementation of "National Digital Health Mission" 
to create a National Digital Health Eco-system. 
1.3 VISION, MISSION AND OBJECTIVES 
1.3.1  The Vision, Mission and Objectives of the Ministry of Health and Family 
Welfare are indicated as under: 
Vision 
Attainment of highest possible level of health and well -being for all, 
through preventive and promotive health care and universal access to goo d 
quality health services without anyone having to face financial hardship as a 
consequence. 
Mission 
(i) To ensure availability of quality healthcare on equitable, accessible and 
affordable basis across regions and communities with special focus on under -
served population and marginalized groups. 
(ii) To establish comprehensive primary healthcare delivery system and well -
functioning linkages with secondary and tertiary care health delivery system. 
(iii)To develop the training capacity for providing human resources for he alth 
(medical, paramedical and managerial) with adequate skill mix at all levels. 
(iv) To regulate health service delivery and promote rational use of 
pharmaceuticals in the country. 
Objectives 
(i) To improve health status of the people through concerted action. 
(ii) To expand preventive, promotive, curative, palliative and rehabilitative 
services provided through public health sector with focus on quality. 
(iii)To progressively achieve Universal Health Coverage. 
1.3.2 The Committee takes note of the Vision, Mission, and Obje ctives of the Ministry 
of Health and Family Welfare, which collectively outline the strategic roadmap for the 
Ministry‘s activities. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department of 
Health and Family Welfare should undertake all projects  and schemes in strict 
consonance with the vision of providing universal access to quality healthcare without 
causing fina ncial hardship to the citizens and execute these initiatives with the clear 
intention of achieving its cherished objectives, particularly the progressive realization of 
Universal Health Coverage and the establishment of robust linkages between primary, 

3 
 
secondary, and tertiary care. The Committee believes that the Department must move 
forward with 'Management by Objective' (MBO) rather than 'Mana gement by 
Activities' (MBA). This approach is essential to ensure equitable healthcare for 
marginalized communities, optimize human resources, and achieve the broader targets 
set out in the National Health Policy (NHP) 2017. 
1.4 DEPARTMENT OF HEALTH AND FAMILY WELFARE 
1.4.1 In this report, the Committee will undertake a comprehensive examination of the 
objectives, functioning, budgetary allocations, and utilization patterns of the Department of 
Health and Family Welfare, along with the institutions operatin g under its administrative 
control. The analysis will extend beyond financial oversight to assess the efficiency, 
transparency, and equity of resource deployment, ensuring that allocations translate into 
measurable improvements in healthcare delivery. 
1.4.2 The Committee will also evaluate the implementation of flagship healthcare policies 
and schemes with particular attention to their design, coverage, and impact. This will include 
a review of institutional performance, inter -sectoral coordination, critica l appraisal of 
achievements, and identifying the gaps and challenges, including disparities in access, quality 
of care, and sustainability of health financing. The overarching objective is to support the 
creation of a resilient, inclusive, and future -ready healthcare system that advances both 
national development and the well-being of all citizens. 
1.4.3 The Department of Health and Family Welfare , as stated in its Annual Report 2025 -
26, is committed to ensure the highest possible level of health and well -being of all through a 
preventive and promotive healthcare orientation in all developmental policies and universal 
access to good quality health care services while mitigating financial hardship.  The approach 
of the Department is to increase access to decen tralised public health systems by establishing 
new infrastructure in deficient areas and by upgrading the infrastructure in existing 
institutions with efforts being made for adequate staffing of these facilities by qualified health 
personnel. There is also  a need to strengthen the role of the public sector in social protection 
against the rising costs of healthcare services and the need to provide a comprehensive 
package of services with due prioritization to the health of women and children. The 
Department also acknowledges the importance of improved availability of quality drugs with 
robust logistical arrangement for procurement and storage of these drugs. 
1.4.4 In pursuance to this commitment  and approach  of the Department , several 
statutory/regulatory bodies and autonomous bodies/institutions work under the administrative 
control of the Department.

1.4.5 The Committee is of  the view that the statutory/ regulatory bodies  and 
autonomous institutions act as the vital organs and play a pivotal role in the successfu l 
functioning of the Department. T heir efficient and effective working is , therefore,  
indispensable for the result -oriented implementation of the core commitments  
undertaken by the Department , such as expanding decentralized public health 
infrastructure, ensuring adequate staffing, and providi ng a comprehensive package of 
services to achieve the objectives of universal access to healthcare , prioritizing women 
and children.  
1.4.6 The Committee , moreover,  feels that a streamlined monitoring mechanism 
utilizing Always Better Control (ABC) would f urther ensure the desirable Rate of 
Returns (RoR) on the heavy investments made in upgrading health infrastructure and 
mitigating financial hardship for ci tizens. The Committee, moreover , recommends that 
the Department should keep a constant Strength, Weak nesses, Opportunities and 
Threats (SWOT) Analysis of its organs/institutions for better results and efficient social 
protection. 
 
1.5 ESTABLISHMENT EXPENDITURE 
1.5.1 As indicated in the Notes on Demands for Grants 2026 –27, the establishment -related 
expenditure of the Department‘s Secretariat is met under the head ―Secretariat.‖ In addition, 
budgetary support is provided under the head ―Direction and Administration‖ to strengthen 
technical expertise in medical and public health and family welfare matters. Th is head also 
supports the implementation and monitoring of various health and family welfare 
programmes, and enables the Department to function as a focal point for the collection, 
processing and supply of biomedical and health -related information within t he country and 
abroad. 
1.5.2 The strengthening, capacity building and operational efficiency of the Secretariat and 
its administrative machinery are critical for achieving the vision, mission and objectives of the 
Department. The Secretariat plays a pivota l role in policy formulation, appraisal, supervision 
and performance monitoring of regulatory/autonomous bodies, research and teaching 
institutions, and hospitals under the Department‘s administrative control. Adequate 
augmentation of human resources, inst itutional capacity and infrastructure is therefore 
essential for effective implementation of healthcare policies and schemes across the country 
and for ensuring equitable, accessible, affordable and quality healthcare. A robust Secretariat 

6 
 
also enhances th e absorption capacity of various schemes, ensures optimal utilisation of 
allocated funds, and enables realistic assessment and projection of future budgetary 
requirements. 
 
 
 
1.5.3 It is observed that the allocation under the head ―Secretariat‖ was reduce d at the 
Revised Estimates (RE) stage during FYs 2023 –24 and 2024 –25. Further, the BE for FY 
2024–25 (Rs. 251.50 crore) was lower than the BE for FY 2023 –24 (Rs. 267.40 crore), which 
appears to be attributable to lower utilisation in FY 2023 –24, where the actual expenditure 
was Rs. 203.24 crore (about 76 per cent of BE). The BE for FY 2025–26 also remained below 
the BE level of FY 2023 –24. Although  in FY 2025 –26, the allocation at the RE stage 
enhanced to Rs. 299.25 crore, the actual expenditure up to 31 st January 2026 remained 72.73 
per cent of the revised allocation.

1.5.4 Under the head ―Direction and Administration‖, a similar trend of under -utilisation 
has been observed. The actual expenditure during FYs 2023 –24 and 2024–25 was only 78.13 
per cent a nd 68.23 per cent of BE, respectively. In FY 2025 –26, the expenditure up to 31 st 
January 2026 stands at 80.89 per cent of the RE allocation. While the BE for FY 2025 –26 is 
almost at the level of BE 2023–24, the BE for FY 2026–27 is lower than that of FY 2024–25.  
 
1.5.5 The Committee believes that given the Secretariat‘s pivotal role in driving 
national healthcare policies, the persistent trend of fund underutilisation under head 
―Direction and Administration‖ is a matter of concern. The Committee, therefor e, 
recommends that the Department must overhaul its exp enditure planning for BE 2026 -
27 to ensure optimal utilization of allocated fund as per  originally conceived plan . The 
Committee is of the view that the Department should undertake a comprehensive revi ew 
of the factors leading to persistent under -utilisation of funds under the heads 
―Secretariat‖ and ―Direction and Administration‖ and put in place a robust expenditure 
planning and monitoring mechanism, including quarterly utilisation targets and 
outcome-based review. 
 
1.5.6 The Committee emphasises that a strong and well -equipped Secretariat and 
administrative framework is essential for effective policy implementation, monitoring 
and optimal utilisation of resources in the health sector. Keeping in view inflationary 
pressure, and the need to strengthen institutional capacity and infrastructure, along 
with the expanding scope of programme implementation and monitoring 
responsibilities, the Committee believes that the allocation of fund under the heads 
―Secretariat‖ and ―Direction and Administration‖ for FY 2026–27 needs to be enhanced 
aligning with actual functional requirements. This would strengthen institutional

1.6 STAFF STRENGTH IN THE DEPARTMENT 
 
 1.6.1  As per the information submitted by the Department of Health and Family 
Welfare, the group-wise (A, B and C) sanctioned strength, in-position strength and vacancy in 
various divisions under the Department for the last three years are as below:  
 
 
 
1.6.2 The Department submitted that it has been reporting vacancies to the concerned Cadre 
Controlling Authorities from time to time, at different points of time, in the relevant vacancy 
year and has also been continuously following -up the matter at regular intervals in 
coordination with these authorities and recruiting agencies, including follow up visits 
wherever feasible. The Department stated that a s a result of sustained efforts and recruitment 
measures, the vacancies have been reduced from 428 (in 2024) to 380 (in 2025).  
1.6.3 The Department informed that the reasons behind present level of vacancies inter-alia 
include attrition mostly at Assistant Section Officer (ASO) and Multi-Tasking Staff (MTS) 
level; and non-joining or resignation later on by some of the newly recruited candidates. The 
remaining vacancies, which currently stands at 380, are being filled up t hrough a coordinated 
and time -bound process in consultation with respective Cadre Controlling Authorities and 
recruiting agencies for expeditious recruitment through UPSC/SSC and by convening pending

Departmental Promotion Committees. The recruitment pace largely depends on recruitment 
cycles of UPSC/ SSC/ recruiting agency, finalization of Recruitment Rules, feeder cadre for 
promotions and inter-cadre allocation issues.  
1.6.4 In further submission, the Department stated that it ha s not undertaken a unifie d 
workload analysis to assess alignment of sanctioned strength with present functional 
requirements. However, individual Programme Divisions in the Department assess their 
workload as per their functional needs and, thereafter, DoHFW requests to the concer ned 
Cadre Controlling Authorities. Depending upon the gap in requirement and availability of 
manpower, many Programme Divisions engage contractual technical staff, wherever required, 
to meet immediate operational requirements. 
1.6.5  The Committee observes with concern that the Department of Health and 
Family Welfare continues to face a significant level of vacancies under its different 
Divisions, with 380  posts remaining unfilled against the sanctioned strength of 14 67 for 
the year 2025. While the Committe e appreciates the efforts made by the Department in 
reducing vacancies from 428 in 2024 to 380 in 2025 through regular follow -up with 
Cadre Controlling Authorities and recruiting agencies, the persisting shortfall is likely to 
adversely affect the efficien cy of policy implementation, programme monitoring and 
administrative functioning.  The Committee, therefore, reiterates its recommendation 
made in the 143rd and 163rd Reports that the Ministry needs to take appropriate steps to 
fill up the vacant positions at various levels and implement better work management 
practices in the Department. The Committee further recommends the Department  to 
take up the matter of vacancies with the Cadre Controlling Authorities and recruiting 
agencies to expedite the recruitment of officers/Staffs at all levels in the Department.  
1.6.6 The Committee further notes with concern that the Department has not 
undertaken a comprehensive workload analysis to assess whether the existing sanctioned 
strength is adequate  to align with its expanding responsibilities. Given the increasing 
scope and complexity of health and family welfare programmes, the Committee 
recommends that the Department conduct a scientific manpower and workload 
assessment at the earliest and rationalise the sanctioned strength accordingly and 
subsequently, undertake special drive to fill up the vacant posts. 
1.6.7 The Committee is of the view that while the engagement of contractual technical 
staff may provide short -term operational support, the core and long -term func tions 
should be supported by regular staff to ensure institutional continuity, accountability 
and capacity building. The Committee, therefore, recommends the Department for 
filling up vacancies, improving manpower planning and forcasting, and strengthening its 
human resource capacity to ensure effective implementation and monitoring of health 
and family welfare programmes. The Committee also urges the Department to analyse 
the reasons for high attrition and non -joining, particularly at the ASO and MTS level s, 
and take corrective measures to improve retention.

CHAPTER II 
 
ANALYSIS OF BUDGETARY ALLOCATIONS  
 
2.1 BUDGETARY PROJECTION VIS-A-VIS ALLOCATION AND 
EXPENDITURE  
 
2.1.1 While examining the Demand for Grants (2026 –27) of the Department of Health and 
Family Welfare, the Committee posed certain questions to the Department based on the 
budgetary documents submitted. In its meeting held on 24 th February 2026, the Committee 
also heard the views of the Secretary, Department of Health and Family Welfare. From the 
replies furnished by the Department and the submission made by the Secretary, the following 
details regarding the budgetary allocation to the Department of Health and Family Welfare 
have been noted: -

2.1.2 On being asked about the reasons for the consistent pattern of reduction in allocation 
at the Revised Estimate (RE) stage from FY 2022 –23 to FY 2025 –26, and the Dep artment‘s 
inability to fully utilize even the reduced allocation in FY 2022–23, the Department submitted 
that the Ministry of Finance undertakes a mid -term appraisal of all Demands every year 
during the Pre-Budget Meeting. The assignment of Revised Estimat es is broadly governed by 
the following parameters: (i) overall scheme envelopes as approved by the 
Cabinet/Expenditure Finance Committee and Standing Finance Committee; (ii) approved 
Central and State Action Plans; (iii) trends of expenditure; (iv) specif ic plans for the 
remaining part of the financial year at the RE stage; and (v) balances available under Single 
Nodal Agency (SNA)/Central Nodal Agency (CNA) and Treasury Single Accounts (TSA) 
accounts. 
 
2.1.3 The Department further stated that although the  Budget allocation was reduced at the 
RE stage, it made efforts to ensure maximum utilization, achieving expenditure of 96.99%, 
103.44%, and 100.83% with respect to the RE allocations for FY 2022 –23, 2023 –24, and 
2024–25, respectively. 

11 
 
 
2.1.4. Regarding th e reduction of over ₹3,000 crore at the RE stage in FY 2025 –26, the 
Department submitted that the Budget Estimate of ₹95,957.87 crore was reduced to 
₹92,925.86 crore at the RE stage. Being the last year of the Fifteenth Finance Commission 
award period, the annual envelope of schemes was restricted to the residual amount available. 
Further, considering the overall demand, pace of infrastructure projects, and expenditure 
trends, the Ministry of Finance capped the expenditure ceiling at ₹92,924.86 crore. The 
reduction in allocation primarily affected NACO, PMSSY, PM -ABHIM, Tertiary Care, and 
Establishment Heads. The BE 2026 –27 marks the first year of the Sixteenth Finance 
Commission award period. The Department stated that lessons from past trends, third -party 
reviews of schemes, monitoring and stakeholder consultations by Programme Divisions, 
defined targets and goals, and online monitoring through IT platforms have enabled more 
realistic budgeting for FY 2026–27. 
 
2.1.5 From the submissions made by the Department, the Committee observed that the 
projected demand has grown steadily, reaching ₹1.22 lakh crore in FY 2026 –27. The 
Budget Estimates have consistently been lower than the proposed/projected demands, 
reflecting partial acceptance of the Department‘s requ ests by the Ministry of Finance. 
The persistent gap between proposed demand and BE may constrain the expansion of 
health programmes. In FY 2026 –27, the gap between the proposed demand and BE 
exceeds 17%, which is substantial. 
 
2.1.6 The Department observes  that the BE for 2026 –27 has increased by 
approximately 6% over BE 2025 –26, however, considering the average inflation rate of 
around 5%, population growth, and the National Health Policy target of raising 
government health expenditure to 2.5% of GDP by 20 25, the Committee is of the view 
that the effective increase is marginal. In its 163 rd Report, the Committee had 
recommended higher allocations to significantly enhance healthcare infrastructure and 
services nationwide, particularly in light of the challen ges experienced during the 
COVID-19 pandemic, to ensure preparedness for future public health emergencies. 
 
2.1.7 Moreover, mid-year adjustments have resulted in reductions of approximately ₹6,600 
crore, ₹8,500 crore, and ₹3,000 crore in FYs 2022 –23, 2023–24, and 2025 –26, respectively. 
Frequent downward revisions may affect the continuity of long -term health ini tiatives. The 
concern is further reinforced by the fact that, except for FY 2023 –24, actual expenditure has 
remained below the Revised Estimates, which themselves are lower than the Budget 
Estimates. This raises concerns regarding the effective and timely utilization of allocated 
funds. 
 
2.1.8 It has also been noted that in FY 2025 –26, the Department utilized ₹70,345.61 crore 
as of 31st January 2026, which constitutes only 75.70% of the already reduced RE allocation. 
With only two months remaining in the financial year, there is a possibil ity of expenditure 
being rushed to enhance utilization. The Ministry of Finance has repeatedly issued guidelines 
to regulate expenditure within a financial year. In its 143 rd and 163rd Reports, the Committee 
recommended that the Department cap its expendit ure in the final month of the financial year 

12 
 
in accordance with existing guidelines and closely monitor fund utilization during the last 
quarter to ensure prudent and efficient use of public funds. 
 
2.1.9 The Committee expresses concern over the consistent  gap between the 
Department‘s projected demand and the Budget Estimates (BE), which has constricted 
the expansion of health programmes. In FY 2026–27, the gap exceeds 17%, a substantial 
shortfall that risks undermining the objectives of the National Health  Policy, 
particularly the target of raising government health expenditure to 2.5% of GDP. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department should engage more 
proactively with the Ministry of Finance during the pre -budget consultations to ensure 
that allocations are better aligned with projected needs, especially for critical schemes 
such as NACO, PMSSY, PM-ABHIM, and tertiary care services. 
 
2.1.10 While the BE for FY 2026 –27 shows a nominal 6% increase, this is effectively 
neutralized by inflation averaging around 5% and population growth, and is insufficient 
to meet the rising healthcare demands of the nation. Further, the Department submitted 
that the Annual GDP growth rate during the financial year 2025 -26 is 7.3% on constant 
price. In view there of, keeping in view the factors of inflation, population growth and 
alignment with the National Health Policy‘s mandate of increasing government health 
expenditure to 2.5% of GDP, the year -on-year budget allocation growth should be 
significantly more than the rate of 7.3%, must be around 14 -15%. The Committee, 
therefore, recommends that future allocations be significantly enhanced to strengthen 
healthcare infrastructure, expand service delivery, and ensure preparedness for public 
health emergencies, in line with lessons learned from the COVID -19 pandemic. Further, 
the Committee also believes that by leveraging its IT platforms, third-party reviews, and 
stakeholder consultations, the Department should formulate realistic, evidence -based 
initial budget project ions that permit full funding and minimize the recurring cycle of 
mid-year downward revisions. 
 
2.1.11 The Committee further observes that frequent mid -year reductions at the 
Revised Estimate (RE) stage have disrupted the continuity and momentum of long -term 
health initiatives. The Department must take proactive measures to safeguard core, 
long-term health initiatives, such as NACO, PMSSY, PM -ABHIM, and Tertiary Care 
projects, from being disproportionately derailed by mid -year financial cuts. Reductions 
of ₹6,600 crore, ₹8,500 crore, and ₹3,000 crore in FYs 2022 –23, 2023–24, and 2025 –26 
respectively highlight the need for more stable and predictable funding for better result. 
Given the vulnerabilities exposed during the COVID -19 pandemic, consistent and 
uninterrupted funding is non -negotiable for building nationwide healthcare resilience. 
The Committee, in this regard, recommends that the Department should strengthen its 
expenditure planning and monitoring mechanisms to demonstrate consistent fund 
absorption capacity, thereby minimizing the likelihood of downward revisions. 
Enhanced transparency in reporting the reasons for underutilization will also improve 
accountability and strengthen the case for higher allocations in ensuing financial years. 
 

13 
 
2.1.12 The Committee expresses deep concern over the chronic issue of underutilization 
of funds, highlighted again in FY 2025 –26, where only 75.70% of the reduced RE 
allocation was spent by 31 January 2026. With limited time remaining in the financial 
year, there is a  risk of skewed expenditure in a particular quarter of a financial year, 
which may compromise efficiency and value for money. The Department must 
implement stringent mechanisms for the even distribution of funds throughout the 
financial year. The Committee reiterates its earlier recommendations made in 143rd and 
163rd Reports that the Department cap its expenditure in the final month of the 
financial year in accordance with Ministry of Finance guidelines, and closely monitor 
fund utilization during the last  quarter to ensure prudent and efficient use of public 
funds. 
 
2.2 GOVERNMENT HEALTH EXPENDITURE IN INDIA  
 
2.2.1 The Department of Health and Family Welfare furnished details on the Budget 
Estimates (BE) allocation as a percentage of the Total Union Budget  and GDP (at current 
prices), along with the actual expenditure as a percentage of GDP, as follows:

2.2.2 The deta ils furnished by the Department indicate that the Budget Estimates (BE) 
allocation to the Department of Health and Family Welfare has shown a gradual increase in 
absolute terms over the years, rising from ₹83,000 crore in 2022 –23 to ₹1,01,709.21 crore in 
2026–27. However, as a percentage of the Total Union Budget, the allocation has declined 
from 2.10% in 2022 –23 to 1.82% in 2024 –25, before marginally improving to 1.89% in 
2025–26 and 1.90% in 2026 –27. Similarly, the BE as a percentage of GDP (at current pr ices) 
has steadily decreased from 0.31% in 2022 –23 to 0.26% in 2026 –27. The actual expenditure 
has also remained lower than the BE in most years. These trends indicate that while 
allocations have increased in nominal terms, the share of health expenditure relative to both 
the Total Union Budget and GDP has either stagnated or declined over the period. 

14 
 
2.2.3 The Committee noted from a print media article that the health cess, introduced in 
2018–19 to augment health expenditure, has allegedly offset a decline  in core budgetary 
support rather than supplementing it. The Department‘s allocation as a share of the total 
Government budget has fallen from 2.4% in 2017 –18 (prior to the cess) to 1.9% in BE 2026 –
27. Excluding the cess, which constitutes over 30% of the Department‘s allocation in BE 
2026–27, the effective allocation would be only about 1.3% of the total budget, nearly half the 
2017–18 level. It was highlighted that had the earlier 2.4% share been maintained, the 
allocation would have been around ₹1.2 lakh crore (excluding cess), whereas the present BE 
stands at ₹1 lakh crore inclusive of the cess. Further, against the National Health Policy target 
of 2.5% of GDP for health spending by 2025, with the Centre‘s share at 0.9% of GDP (about 
₹3.5 lakh crore as per NSO data), the current BE 2026 –27 allocation amounts to only 0.26% 
of GDP, including the cess. The article also noted that the 4% health and education cess 
(introduced in 2018) does not specify a defined share for health; transfers to the Pradhan 
Mantri Swasthya Suraksha Nidhi are decided annually by the Centre. As cess pro ceeds lie 
outside the regular budgetary process and are not subject to parliamentary oversight or State 
sharing. The comments of the Department were sought on the information contained in the 
article. 
 
2.2.4 In its submission, the Department stated that, a ccording to National Health Accounts 
estimates, Government Health Expenditure (GHE) as a percentage of GDP was 1.84% in 
2021–22, with the Union Government‘s share at 41.8%. Union Government expenditure on 
healthcare includes spending by the Ministry of Hea lth & Family Welfare (MoHFW) as well 
as other ministries such as Defence, Labour & Employment, Mines, Railways, AYUSH, 
Science & Technology, Posts, Telecommunications, and Home Affairs. It also encompasses 
expenditure by State/UT Governments and by urban a nd rural local bodies. Government 
initiatives to increase health expenditure are evident from the National Health Accounts data, 
which show that Government spending as a share of Total Health Expenditure (THE) rose 
from 29% in 2014 –15 to 48% in 2021 –22. Co rrespondingly, GHE as a share of GDP 
increased from 1.13% in 2014 –15 to 1.84% in 2021 –22. The MoHFW has urged States to 
prioritize health and raise their allocations to at least 8% of their total budgets, in line with 
national goals. As health is primarily  a State subject, the Union Government contributes 
through Centrally Sponsored Schemes (CSS) and Central Sector Schemes. 
 
2.2.5 Expenditure by the Department of Health and Family Welfare has risen from ₹51,382 
crore in 2017 –18 to ₹1,01,709 crore in BE 2026 –27. This period spans the 14th Finance 
Commission (2015 –2020), the 15th FC (2020 –2026), and the beginning of the 16th FC 
(2026–2031). During these cycles, States‘ share in the divisible pool (vertical devolution) 
increased from 32% under the 13th FC (2010 –2015) to 42% under the 14th FC, and 41% 
under both the 15th and 16th FCs. 
 
2.2.6 The levy, collection, and distribution of the health cess among ministries is determined 
by the Ministry of Finance. For the MoHFW, allocations are made under PMSSN, for whic h 
the Ministry acts only as the implementing agency. In BE 2026 –27, PMSSN accounts for 
30.21% of the Department‘s total allocation. The 4% health and education cess introduced in 

15 
 
2018 is not statutorily divided under the Finance Act. Instead, the Governmen t follows an 
administrative allocation pattern: 
 50% to the Primary Education Fund 
 25% to the Secondary and Higher Education Fund 
 25% to the health component, transferred to PMSSN 
 
2.2.7 A statement showing year-wise allocation of funds by Ministry of Finan ce as PMSSN 
and % of PMSSN in respect of BE allocation is as follows: - 
Table-2.3 
Percentage of PMSSN allocation in respect of corresponding BE 
(Rs. in Crores) 
Year Total BE PMSSN PMSSN as % of BE 
2022-23 83000.00 23750.00 28.61 
2023-24 86175.00 14589.00 16.93 
2024-25 87656.90 14757.68 16.84 
2025-26 95957.87 17678.74 18.42 
2026-27 101709.21 30725.00 30.21 
 
2.2.8 The health cess is credited to PMSSN, a designated reserve fund in the Public 
Account, and utilized for specific Centrally Sponsored Scheme s, including: (i) Flexible Pool 
for RCH & Health System Strengthening, National Health Programme, and National Urban 
Health Mission (NHM component); (ii) Infrastructure Maintenance (NHM component); (iii) 
Human Resources for Health and Medical Education (HR H & ME); and (iv) Pradhan Mantri 
Jan Arogya Yojana (PMJAY). Scheme -wise allocations are made by the Ministry of Finance. 
Utilization of the reserve fund involves creating budget provisions as per scheme 
requirements, with amounts drawn from PMSSN under the  Public Account. Correspondingly, 
expenditure figures in the Consolidated Fund are reduced by the same amount. This process, 
followed for all reserve funds, is integral to the budgetary framework and subject to audit by 
the C&AG of India. 
 
2.2.9 It is a factual statement that States have no claim on cess funds, which are spent at the 
sole discretion of the Centre. However, in the case of health schemes, all PMSSN funds are 
routed through Centrally Sponsored Schemes, with support transferred to States/UTs di rectly 
or via implementing agencies as the Central share. PMSSN was introduced by the Ministry of 
Finance at the RE 2020 –21 stage, and the allocation of cess under PMSSN for MoHFW 
continues to be determined annually by the Ministry of Finance. 
 
2.2.10 The Committee observes that although the Budget Estimates (BE) allocation to 
the Department of Health and Family Welfare has increased in absolute terms over 
recent years, its share relative to the Total Union Budget and GDP has either stagnated 
or declined. Further, reliance on the health cess, constituting over 30% of the 
Department‘s allocation in BE 2026 –27, appears to have substituted for core budgetary 
support rather than supplementing it. While the Department has clarified that PMSSN 
forms part of the es tablished budgetary framework and is subject to audit, concerns 

16 
 
remain regarding the increasing dependence on cess proceeds and the decline in core 
budgetary support as a share of the total Government budget. The Committee 
recommends that the Department ta ke up the matter with the Ministry of Finance to 
ensure that cess proceeds are genuinely additional to core allocations and do not 
substitute regular budgetary support. 
 
2.2.11 The Committee is of the view that the Department should pursue measures to 
ensure that health expenditure aligns with the National Health Policy target of 2.5% of 
GDP, with the Centre contributing at least 0.9% of GDP. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends that the Department, in consultation with the Ministry of Finance, prepare 
a cl ear medium -term roadmap indicating year -wise targets for enhancing the Union 
Government‘s health outlay as a proportion of both GDP and the Total Union Budget. 
Such a roadmap should align with Finance Commission cycles and national health 
priorities, and b e placed before Parliament to ensure transparency and accountability. 
In parallel, the Department may strengthen coordination with States to encourage them 
to raise health allocations to at least 8% of their budgets, thereby ensuring balanced 
progress toward national health goals. 
 
2.3 CAPITAL VIS-A-VIS REVENUE ALLOCATION 
 
2.3.1 The BE and RE allocation of Department of Health & Family Welfare and actual 
expenditure (AE) under Capital head is given below: -

2.3.2 The details furnished by the Department indicate that the allocation to capital is just 3 -
6 % of t he total BE (Budget Estimates) or RE (Revised Estimates) allocations which is 
minuscule in proportion to revenue share and further declining under both the BE  and RE 
over the years. Under BE, capital allocation decreased from ₹5,632.57 crore in 2022 –23 
(6.79% of total BE) to ₹2,928.30 crore in 2026 –27 (2.88% of total BE), despite an overall 
increase in total BE during the same period. Similarly, at the RE stage , capital allocation fell 

17 
 
from ₹3,582.14 crore (4.69% of total RE) in 2022 –23 to ₹2,437.82 crore (2.62% of total RE) 
in 2025–26. The proportion of capital expenditure relative to total allocation has thus steadily 
declined at both BE and RE stages, indicat ing a reduced emphasis on capital spending within 
the Department‘s overall budgetary framework. The utilisation of reduced capital allocation is 
also not found satisfactory.  
 
2.3.3 The Department submitted that, being part of the Social Service Sector, it  has limited 
scope for capital expenditure, which is determined on a need basis under two major 
components—Buildings & Structures and Machinery & Equipment —depending on 
requirements projected by Programme Divisions. Capital expenditure in the health sector  
primarily supports long-term investments such as construction of new AIIMS, upgradation of 
central hospitals, procurement of advanced medical equipment, and modernization of 
facilities. The Department stated that infrastructure upgradation is being streng thened through 
both Capital and GIA–Capital budget heads (under revenue expenditure) for schemes such as 
PM-ABHIM, HRH&ME, PMSSY, Central Hospitals, and Autonomous Bodies. It was further 
stated that capital projects involve lengthy processes including tend ering, global procurement, 
land availability, statutory clearances, and other logistical factors, which may delay execution. 
Regular monitoring mechanisms have been put in place to track physical and financial 
progress, and inclusion of GIA –Capital to Stat es/UTs and Autonomous Bodies enhances the 
overall effective capital expenditure. 
 
2.3.4 For reduction in capital allocation at RE stage, the Department cited several reasons as 
actual pace of expenditure in first two quarters after setting off opening bala nces and pending 
UCs of Autonomous Institutes/Hospitals/Training Institutes/funds Transfer to States/UTs, 
thus re -assessment of requirement of funds by programme divisions at Revised Estimates 
stage after working out the amount utilized till September, 202 4 and requirement of funds for 
the remaining months of the financial year.  For underutilization of earmarked capital funds, 
the reasons stated were non -filling of vacant posts, less establishment expenditure, non -
finalisation of outsourcing manpower, non -settlement of items procured through GeM within 
same financial year, non -submission of UCs by State, UTs and implementing agencies, pace 
of capital expenditure due to delay in construction work, delay in procurement of machinery 
equipment , inadequate viable  projects from implementing agencies, non -utilisation of Letter 
of authorisations issued by programme divisions to implementing agency, cancellation of 
tendering process etc. 
 
2.3.5 The Committee expresses concern on the persistent decline in the propor tion of 
capital allocation within the Department of Health & Family Welfare‘s overall 
budgetary framework. Despite steady increases in total Budget Estimates (BE), the 
capital component has fallen from 6.79% in 2022 –23 to just 2.88% in 2026 –27. At the 
Revised Estimates (RE) stage, the share has similarly contracted, reaching only 2.62% 
in 2025–26. This trend reflects a diminishing emphasis on long -term infrastructure and 
equipment investments, even as the demand for expanded and modernized health 
facilities continues to grow. While the Department has explained that health, being a 
social service sector, has limited scope for capital expenditure, the Committee 
emphasizes that the sector‘s future resilience and accessibility depend on sustained 

18 
 
capital investm ent. Revenue allocations, though critical for recurring expenses and 
service delivery, cannot substitute the structural need for robust infrastructure. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends that capital allocations be progressively enhanced to 
ensure balanc ed growth between immediate service requirements and long -term 
capacity building. 
 
2.3.6 The Committee also observes recurring underutilization of earmarked capital 
funds due to delays in tendering, procurement bottlenecks, non -submission of utilization 
certificates, and inadequate viable projects from implementing agencies. To address 
these inefficiencies, the Department should strengthen project planning, streamline 
procurement and clearance processes, and enforce stricter timelines for States, UTs, and 
autonomous bodies. Capital investments must be prioritized for constructing tertiary 
centres, upgrading primary and secondary facilities, and equipping them with modern 
machinery alongside adequate human resources to ensure functionality. The Committee 
therefore recommends reorienting budgetary priorities to arrest the declining trend in 
capital allocation, improve fund utilization efficiency, and commit to sustained 
infrastructure and equipment investments, thereby strengthening healthcare capacity, 
accessibility, and long-term resilience.

2.4.2 On a specific query the Department submitted the targets related to healthcare and the 
progress, as per the National Health Policy, as follows:  
(i) Targets: 
a)   Increase health expenditure by the Government as a percentage of GDP from 
the existing 1.15% to 2.5 % by 2025.  

19 
 
b)   Increase State sector health spending to more than 8% of their budget by 2020. 
c)   Decrease in proportion of households facing catastrophic health expendi ture 
from the current levels by 25% by 2025. 
 
(ii) Progress: 
a) & b) As per the National Health Accounts (NHA) Estimates, Government Health 
Expenditure (GHE) as a share of Total Health Expenditure (THE) has increased, from 
1.15% in 2013 -14 to 1.84% in 2021 -22. The provision of health services is 
decentralised and is primarily a state subject. The main impetus for increasing the 
GHE should come from the states. As per the RBI State Finances report, the 
Expenditure on Medical and Public Health and Family Welf are as a per cent of 
Aggregate Expenditure for all States and UTs with legislature for FY 2025 -26 (BE) is 
5.7%. 
 
c)  According to NHA estimates, the share of Out -of-Pocket Expenditure (OOPE) 
in Total Health Expenditure has declined from 64.2% in 2013-14 to 39.4% in 2021-22. 
This declining share of OOPE is an indicator of a reduced financing burden on 
households when accessing healthcare services.  
 
2.4.3 On being asked the reasons for t he allocation to the Health Sector comprising M/o 
H&FW and Ayush during the last five years varying from 0.28% to 0.33% (on a declining 
trend) of GDP in the respective years despite the fact Government is making efforts to 
standardize the healthcare to the global standard, the Department submitted that according to 
the latest available National Health Accounts estimates (NHA) for FY 2021 -22, the 
Government Health Expenditure (GHE) as percentage of GDP is 1.84%. GHE in absolute 
terms is Rs 4,34,163 crores and the GDP value is Rs 2,35,97,399 crores.  The share of 
Government Healt h Expenditure (GHE) in Total Health Expenditure (THE) has increased 
from 28.6% in FY 2013 -14 to 48.0% in FY 2021 -22. GHE includes expenditure by both 
Union and State Governments. Union Government's share is 41.8% while the State 
governments' share is 58.2% . Concurrent to this increase in GHE, the share of Out of Pocket 
Expenditure (OOPE) in THE has declined from 64.2% to 39.4% during the same period. This 
declining share of OOPE is indicative of increasing access to health services.

2.4.5 The Committee has been informed that as per the Sustainable Development Report 
2025 datasheet available, the individual score for the SDG-3 (ens uring healthy lives and 
wellbeing) for 167 developing and developed countries for the year 2024 ranges from the 
lowest 12 for Central African Republic to the highest 97 for Norway/Iceland. The world 
average stood at 72 and India scored 67 for this goal. Th e average scores for low -income-
countries and lower -middle-countries and high -income-countries stood at 45, 61 and 91 
respectively.  
 
2.4.6 The Committee takes into consideration that the latest data provided by the 
Department in relation to THE, GHE, OOPE and GHE as % of GDP pertains to the FY 2021 -
22. The data for FYs 2022 -23, 2023-24 and 2024-25 are not yet available. On a query related 
to status of public health expenditure as % of GDP in FY 2024 -25, the Department replied 
that the GHE/public health exp enditure estimate of the NHA is based on actual expenditure. 
The actual expenditure data comes after a lag of two years in the budget documents. The 
NHA estimates for FY 2022 -23 and FY 2023 -24 are under preparation. These reports will 
include the update on  the indicator of GHE as a % of GDP. However, in contrary to the 
submission of the Department, the Committee finds that the FY 2025 -26 is coming to an end 
and, even after lag of two years, the data for FY 2023-24 is also not available.  
 
2.4.7 The Committe e observes with deep concern that the allocation to the health 
sector, comprising the Department of Health & Family Welfare, Department of Health 
Research, and Ministry of Ayush, has remained stagnant at 0.28–0.33% of GDP over the 
last five years, which is  not in consonance with the National Health Policy (NHP), 2017 
target of raising Government Health Expenditure (GHE) to 2.5% of GDP by 2025. 
Although GHE has increased from 1.35% of GDP in 2017 –18 to 1.84% in 2021 –22, the 
pace of growth is insufficient to achieve the NHP target within the stipulated timeframe. 
The Committee reiterates its earlier recommendations made in the 126th and 163rd 
Reports, wherein it had urged the Government to increase health expenditure to 5% by 
2025, however, the FY 2025 -26 has now come to an end. The Committee, therefore, 
urges the Department to review the target under NHP to 5% in the next five years. The 
Committee is of considered view that Health Sector must be recognized as a priority 
sector for both economic and social deve lopment, requiring sustained investment in 
public health infrastructure, preventive care, and equitable service delivery. 
 
2.4.8 The Committee is not satisfied over delays in updating National Health Accounts 
(NHA) data, with information currently availabl e only up to FY 2021 –22. Despite the 
acknowledged two -year lag, data for FY 2023 –24 should already be available, yet it 
remains pending. In its 163rd Report, the Committee had highlighted that outdated data 
hampers effective monitoring and evaluation of go vernment schemes. The Committee, 
therefore, reiterates its recommendation that the Government leverage Information 
Technology and Artificial Intelligence for real -time data collection and processing, 
ensuring that expenditure data for a given financial yea r is made available by the last 
quarter of the following year. This will enable timely assessment of progress and 
alignment of schemes with intended objectives. 
 

21 
 
2.4.9 While the decline in Out -of-Pocket Expenditure (OOPE) from 64.2% in 2013 –14 
to 39.4% in 2021–22 is encouraging, the Committee finds that OOPE still remains a 
significant barrier to healthcare access, particularly given India‘s large population 
living in poverty. As per the Global Multidimensional Poverty Index 2024, India is home 
to approxima tely 234 million poor people, the highest in the world, and is the only 
country among the five poorest nations with a medium Human Development Index 
(HDI). As highlighted in the 163rd Report, the Committee is optimistic that current 
figures may be lower, b ut underscores the urgent need to reduce OOPE further to 
ensure affordability and accessibility of healthcare. The Committee therefore 
recommends intensifying efforts to strengthen public health infrastructure, expand 
preventive and primary care services, leverage Public -Private Partnerships, and 
institute robust monitoring mechanisms to reduce OOPE and enhance accessibility. 
 
2.4.10 The Committee also emphasizes the need to strengthen preventive and primary 
healthcare services as a cornerstone of India‘s h ealth system. A strategic focus on 
preventive care and efficient resource utilization is crucial to achieving long -term 
improvements in accessibility and quality. Preventive interventions reduce the burden 
on tertiary care facilities, lower Out -of-Pocket E xpenditure (OOPE), and improve 
overall health outcomes. Given India‘s score of 67 on SDG -3 (ensuring healthy lives and 
wellbeing) in the Sustainable Development Report 2025, below the world average of 72 
and significantly lower than high -income countries a veraging 91, the Committee 
reiterates that stronger investments in preventive health, including immunization, 
maternal and child health, nutrition, and early detection of non -communicable diseases, 
are essential to bridge the gap. 
 
2.4.11 The Committee als o highlights the importance of leveraging Public -Private 
Partnerships (PPP) and innovative financing mechanisms to bridge the resource gap in 
the health sector. In its earlier reports, including the 163rd Report, the Committee 
emphasized that the current pace of increase in Government Health Expenditure (GHE) 
is inadequate to meet the National Health Policy target of 2.5% of GDP by 2025. Given 
the stagnant allocation of 0.28 –0.33% of GDP to the Ministry of Health & Family 
Welfare, Department of Health Resea rch, and Ministry of Ayush, it is imperative to 
explore supplementary avenues of investment. The Committee expects that PPPs can 
play a crucial role in expanding infrastructure, introducing advanced technologies, and 
improving service delivery, particularl y in underserved rural and tribal areas, 
therefore, the Ministry should strive in that direction.

Note: GHE- Government health Exp; GGHED - Domestic General Government Health Exp; 
GHE/GGHED PPP_PC - Government Health Exp/ Domestic General Government Health 
Exp per capita PPP (Int $); CHE - Current Health Exp; GHE/GGHED PPP_PC for India is 
estimated by dividing GHE per capita in Rs by the PPP exchange rate.  
2.5.4 In India, the per capita GHE has grown in recent years, from Rs 1,042 in 2013 -14 to 
Rs 3,169 in 2021 -22. During the same period, the OOPE % CHE has declined, from 67% to 
45.1%. Further, India ranks 69th in the list of 189 countries in Out -of-Pocket Expenditure per 
capita in PPP (Int $) for 20214. 
 
2.5.5 The Committee observes that India‘s Current Health Expenditure (CHE ) as a 
percentage of GDP stands at 3.3 percent, which is considerably lower than that of other 
BRICS countries such as Brazil (9.6 percent), South Africa (8.7 percent), and Russia (7.0 
percent), as well as G20 nations where the average often exceeds 10 per cent. 
Furthermore, Government Health Expenditure (GHE) in India is only 1.84 percent of 
GDP, far below the 4 –10 percent range observed in most BRICS and G20 countries. 
This limited public investment translates into a high reliance on household spending, 
with Out -of-Pocket Expenditure (OOPE) accounting for 45.1 percent of CHE. 
Comparative analysis shows that countries with higher government spending, such as 
Germany, Japan, and the United Kingdom, not only achieve stronger health outcomes 
but also maintain O OPE levels below 15 percent, thereby ensuring greater financial 
protection for their populations. In light of this, the Committee recommends that India 
adopt a phased strategy to raise GHE to at least 5 percent of GDP by 2030, beyond the 
targets set under the National Health Policy (2017). This increase should be directed 
                                                           
4 https://nhsrcindia.org/sites/default/files/2024-09/NHA%202021-22.pdf 

25 
 
towards strengthening primary and preventive healthcare with essential infrastructure, 
and reducing inequities in access to healthcare. 
 
2.5.6 The Committee notes that despite some progres s in reducing OOPE from 67 
percent in 2013–14 to 45.1 percent in 2021 –22, India continues to face one of the highest 
household health expenditure burdens among BRICS and G20 countries. This level of 
OOPE is comparable to Mexico (41.4 percent) but remains f ar above countries such as 
South Africa (6.6 percent) and France (9.2 percent), where robust public financing and 
insurance systems provide strong financial protection. Evidence from both high -income 
countries like Canada and Japan, and medium -HDI countries such as Namibia (6.5% 
GHE and 7.3% OOPE) and lower -income countries like Rwanda (5% OOPE), 
demonstrates that effective risk pooling and community -based insurance models can 
substantially reduce household vulnerability to catastrophic health spending. In this 
context, the Committee recommends that India expand financial protection mechanisms 
by strengthening Ayushman Bharat and state -level insurance schemes, with particular 
emphasis on outpatient care, diagnostics, and medicines, which are major drivers of  
OOPE. Risk pooling should be incentivized through employer -based and cooperative 
insurance models, especially targeting informal sector workers who remain outside 
formal coverage.

expenditure by all the states for 2023 -24 is 5.3% which was 5.4% in 2022 -23. Further, the 
allocation for 2024-25 (BE) as a share of Total State Budget is 5.6%.  The details are provided

2.5.8 The Department further stated that both the Union and State governments contribute to 
the GHE. As health is a state subject, the responsibility of ensuring the provision of healthcare 
services lies with the state. Union Government contributes through Centrally Sponsored 
Schemes (CSS), Central Sector Schemes, etc. Along with these schemes, the Government of 
India (GoI) has encouraged the states to allo cate a higher share of their budget to the health 
sector through the following initiatives:  
o Incentivising states to spend more: The GoI adopted a conditionality framework 
under the National Health Mission. The framework follows Result -Based 
Financing (RBF ), where the States get more budgetary support if they perform 
well on the agreed indicators5. 
o Public Health Management Cadre (PHMC): Under PHMC, States/UTs will be 
creating sanctioned posts by creating specific cadres of healthcare workers, thus 
increasing the spending on quality human resources also includes increased 
expenditure in public health services through strengthening management capacity 
of the states. 
o Indian Public Health Standards (IPHS) 2022: These standards enable service 
provision of quality  services through assured infrastructure, human resources, 
diagnostics, equipment, medicines, etc. 
o National Quality Assurance Standards (NQAS): With the commitment of GoI to 
achieve NQAS certification of all facilities by Dec 2026, states are encouraged to  
expedite their development and adoption of standards to provide quality care to all. 
o State-specific innovations in Healthcare in health service delivery6 
                                                           
5https://nhm.gov.in/WriteReadData/l892s/50923145171570520489.pdf 
6https://nhsrcindia.org/sites/default/files/2025-02/Best%20Practices%20CTB%20Final.pdf 

27 
 
Further, under NHM, to improve the financial utilization of funds the following steps have 
been taken by the Ministry: 
(i) Performance Audit by the Comptroller and Auditor General (CAG) of India, 
(ii) Annual Statutory Audits by CAG impanelled major Chartered Accountant Audit 
firms, 
(iii) Concurrent Audits by Chartered Accountant Audit firms,   
(iv) Public Financial Management System (PFMS) and SNA -SPARSH in NHM to 
track flow of funds,  
(v) Submission of quarterly Financial Monitoring Reports (FMRs) and Statement of 
Fund Position (SFP) by the States/UTs, 
(vi) Review and monitoring of scheme components. 
 
2.5.9 The Committee notes that while the National Health Policy (2017) recommended that 
States/UTs increase their health spending to more than 8 percent of their total budget, the actual 
expenditure remains significantly below this benchmark, averaging only 5 .3 percent in 2023 –24 
and projected at 5.6 percent in 2024 –25. Although some states such as Delhi (11.4 percent), 
Odisha (8.0 percent), and Puducherry (8.7 percent) are approaching or surpassing the target, 
most others continue to allocate between 4–6 percent, reflecting a persistent gap in prioritization 
of health within state budgets. Given that health is a state subject, the Committee emphasizes 
that the Department encourage States/UTs to adopt a phased strategy to progressively raise 
health spending clo ser to the 8 percent benchmark. This should be supported by Union 
Government initiatives such as conditional Result -Based Financing under the National Health 
Mission, strengthening of the Public Health Management Cadre, and adoption of Indian Public 
Health Standards (IPHS) 2022. To ensure accountability and efficient utilization of funds, 
mechanisms such as CAG audits, PFMS tracking, and quarterly financial monitoring must be 
rigorously implemented, thereby ensuring equitable access to quality healthcare ac ross the 
country. 
 
2.5.10 In addition to urging States/UTs to progressively raise their health allocations closer to 
the 8 percent benchmark, the Committee recommends that greater emphasis be placed on 
strengthening state -level innovation and capacity buil ding in health service delivery. While 
Union initiatives such as Result -Based Financing under the National Health Mission, the Public 
Health Management Cadre, and adoption of IPHS and NQAS standards provide a strong 
framework, their impact will depend on h ow effectively states adapt these measures to local 
needs. States should be encouraged to pilot innovative models in areas such as digital health, 
telemedicine, and community -based outreach, particularly in underserved rural and tribal 
regions, and to inte grate these with centrally sponsored schemes for maximum efficiency. 
Moreover, capacity building of state health departments through specialized training, improved 
data systems, and performance-linked incentives will ensure that increased allocations trans late 
into measurable improvements in service delivery. By combining higher budgetary 
prioritization with innovation and institutional strengthening, States/UTs can accelerate 
progress towards equitable, quality healthcare and contribute meaningfully to nat ional health 
goals. 
 
 
 
 

28 
 
2.6 15th FINANCE COMMISSION HEALTH GRANTS 
 
2.6.1 On a specific query on utilisation of the ₹70,051 crore grants provided by the 15th 
Finance Commission, the Department provided that the total Allocation under XV -FC from 
the FY 2021 -22 to FY 2025 -26 is Rs.70051 Crores. The total approvals given to the states 
under XV-FC from the FY 2021 -22 to FY 2025 -26 is Rs.68832.43 Crores. The total releases 
to the states under XV -FC from the FY 2021 -22 to FY 2025-26 is Rs.44495.12 Crores (as on 
18th Jan 2026). The total expenditure reported by States on NHM -PMS portal from the FY 
2021-22 to FY 2025-26 is Rs.25367.75 Crores (as on 26th Dec, 2025). 
2.6.2 The Department also provided the key findings from the NHSRC –NIPFP study 
regarding systemic challenges in fund utilization as under: - 
 
a) The capital -intensive nature of the 15 th FC grants and their execution 
architecture has retarded the pace of progress of the grants. The tied nature of 
the grant with rigid budget provisions placed within a centralize d institutional 
arrangement for re-appropriation across components compounded the problem. 
 
b) Though provisions for human resources were made in at least 4 of the 6 
components, support for these was withdrawn after the first two years of the 
grant. Most stat es found it challenging to draw up a comprehensive plan on 
human resource deployment in the upgraded units poses uncertainty about the 
outcomes of these investments. 
 
c) The performance of the grant suffered significantly due to execution 
challenges arising f rom a de novo implementation architecture and 
unfamiliarity with the new fund-flow mechanism at the state level. 
 
d) Several states faced a mismatch of recommended allocation vis -à-vis the need 
in certain components calling for re -appropriation of funds. The procurement 
delays and financial bottlenecks were observed in the following States: 
 
(i) In Gujarat, although funds were released to procurement and other 
implementation agencies, progress in terms of actual outputs in the diagnostic 
component was impacted due to central procurement related delays. 
 
(ii) Besides cost escalations due to delays in procurement of land; Odisha 
being a coastal state has several water -logged districts where construction 
required further expenditure on appropriate strengthening of  building 
foundations. Procurement of raw materials were also stated to be costlier in 
remote and tribal regions which further puts upward pressure on costs. 
 
(iii) In states like Madhya Pradesh and Uttar Pradesh, penal interest was 
initially booked under the wrong budget head, impacting fund flow. 
 

29 
 
(iv) Delays attributable to issue of indents for HR recruitment & equipment 
procurement only after 75% of a building structure was complete. 
 
2.6.3 The Department further stated that National, State, and Distric t-level Committees 
(DLCs) were established to oversee and monitor grant works. The study recommends 
empowering DLCs to decide if a local body has the capacity for construction; if not, they 
should be authorized to assign the work to appropriate health depa rtment agencies. The 
Ministry currently uses the NHM -PMS portal for tracking expenditure and physical progress. 
However, the study brought out that the current data reporting has significant ―missing 
values‖ and ―divergence‖ between portal data and actual ground-level expenditure. 
 
2.6.4 The utilisation of XV -Finance Commission (XV -FC) Health Grants is monitored 
through a structured framework of input, output, and outcome indicators aligned with NHM 
and XV-FC guidelines, and tracked through national portals  and dashboards. Key indicators 
include the number of building -less SHCs/PHCs/CHCs constructed, functional SHC -AAMs 
and PHC -AAMs, availability of essential and desirable diagnostics at primary facilities, 
CHCs functioning as First Referral Units (FRUs), an d the percentage of funds utilised against 
funds released. The grants support strengthening of primary healthcare through construction 
of infrastructure, establishment of Urban AAMs/HWCs and BPHUs, upgradation of 
diagnostic facilities, and conversion of SH Cs/PHCs into AAMs for Comprehensive Primary 
Health Care (CPHC) services, thereby helping bridge primary healthcare gaps. States with 
higher fund utilisation have reported measurable improvements such as enhanced facility 
readiness, expanded service coverage—particularly for NCD screening and preventive care —
increased patient footfall at primary facilities, reduced burden on higher -level hospitals, and 
improved availability of diagnostics and essential medicines, with progress regularly tracked 
through routi ne programme reports, NP -NCD and AAM portals, and periodic state and 
national reviews. 
 
2.6.5 The Committee is displeased to note that although the 15th Finance Commission 
allocated ₹70,051 crore for health grants for FY 2021 –22 to FY 2025 –26, utilisation 
remains suboptimal. Against ₹44,495.12 crore (63.52% of the allocation) released to 
States, only ₹25,367.75 crore (57.01% of funds released) has been reported as 
expenditure. As observed in its 163rd Report, as of 30 January 2025, only 39.42% of the 
total approved funds had been released, with full disbursement limited to FY 2021 –22, 
and States had utilised just 59.31% of the released funds. The Committee reiterates that 
such low utilisation defeats the very objective of the financial support to orient heal th 
sector as patient -centric. It therefore strongly recommends that the Government 
undertake a comprehensive study to identify the causes of under -utilisation and 
implement corrective measures. The Union Government should also establish a 
dedicated institutional mechanism for regular coordination with States/UTs to address 
bottlenecks and ensure timely and optimal utilisation of these critical funds for 
strengthening primary healthcare. 
 
2.6.6 The NHSRC–NIPFP study highlights systemic challenges, including the capital-
intensive nature of the grants, rigid budget provisions, withdrawal of human resource 

30 
 
support after initial years, and execution difficulties arising from unfamiliar fund -flow 
mechanisms. States have also faced mismatches between allocations an d local needs, 
procurement delays, cost escalations in difficult terrains, and financial misclassifications 
disrupting fund flow. These constraints have slowed infrastructure development and 
service readiness despite the availability of funds. In view of t hese findings, the 
Committee recommends enhancing flexibility in fund re -appropriation to better align 
allocations with State -specific needs, restoring and sustaining human resource support 
to ensure service continuity, and empowering District-Level Committees to assign works 
to suitable agencies where local bodies lack capacity. Procurement processes should be 
streamlined, and data reporting systems strengthened to improve accuracy and 
transparency, minimising discrepancies between portal data and actual e xpenditure. 
Addressing these structural and operational bottlenecks will accelerate utilisation of 
Finance Commission health grants and facilitate timely infrastructure expansion, 
improved diagnostic capacity, and strengthened primary healthcare services a cross 
States.

CHAPTER-3 
 
SCHEMES AND PROJECTS  
 
3.1 CENTRAL GOVERNMENT HEALTH SCHEME (CGHS):  
 
3.1.1 The Central Government Health Scheme (CGHS) is a health scheme of the 
Government providing comprehensive medical facilities to Central Government employees, 
pensioners and members of their families in addition to other specified categories like 
Members of Parliament (MPs), ex -MPs, ex-Governors, ex-Vice-Presidents, Retired Judges of 
Supreme Court and High Court, Freedom Fighters an d members of their family etc. The 
facilities under this scheme include outpatient care through a network of allopathic, 
ayurvedic, homeopathic, unani/siddha dispensaries/units. The scheme was started in Delhi in 
1954. Over the time, it has spread to 81 ci ties. It serves nearly 15,93,741 primary cardholders 
and 42,30,229 beneficiaries through a network of 342 Allopathic Wellness Centres and 111 
AYUSH Units.

component (approximately ₹1,100 crore) is incurred under the Materials and Supplies head 
for procurement of medicines and consumables for serving CGHS beneficiaries and their 
dependent family members. The Capital Head requirement of ₹41.50 cror e is proposed for 
acquisition of medical equipments, furniture, ICT infrastructure, and for construction and 
renovation of buildings of CGHS units, aimed at strengthening service delivery, digitisation 
and infrastructure augmentation.

3.1.3 The budget under PORB is primarily utilized for settlement of hospital bills relating to 
cashless treatment provided to pensioner beneficiaries, a s well as reimbursement of medical 
claims submitted by pensioners. A substantial portion of this allocation is also utilized for 
procurement of life -saving medicines, settlement of Government Medical Store Depot 
(GMSD) bills and payments to Jan Aushadhi outlets. 
3.1.4 The Committee notes that the expenditure on the CGHS (including PORB) during the 
last FY 2024 -25 was ₹7750.28 crore (2104.30+5645.98) against the revised allocation of 
₹7719.70 crore (5440.00+2279.70) i.e. approx. 100.40% and during the FY 202 5-26 is 
₹7169.38 crore (up to 31.01.2026) (5191.84+1977.54) against the revised allocation of 
₹8106.96 crore, translating into 88.43% utilisation up to 31.01.2026. However, under the 
Capital Head of CGHS, the Committee notes that, in the FY 2025 -26, the BE  amounting to 
₹75.31 crore has been reduced to ₹38.86 crore at RE stage (51.6% of original allocation).
