3.1.6 The Department subm itted that the revision of CGHS rates (average increase of 
approximately 58%) and expansion of empanelled hospitals to Tier II and Tier III cities are 
likely to increase expenditure under PORB, while implementation of the Drug Procurement 
Policy dated 15.01.2026 and operationalisation of 22 new Wellness Centres (WCs) will raise 
expenditure under Supplies & Materials (S&M) and establishment costs. Since the Budget 
Estimates (BE) for FY 2026 –27 were finalised in Q2 of FY 2025 –26 and these measures 
became operational in Q3, their full financial impact could not be factored in. Accordingly, an 
additional ₹3000 crore over and above the projected BE 2026 –27 has been sought under 
S&M, PORB, and establishment heads at the BE/RE stage to ensure adequate provision. Th e 
Department further stated that the BE 2026 –27 allocation for CGHS is lower than both its 
projected demand of ₹2831.15 crore and the BE 2025 –26 allocation of ₹2370 crore, and is 
constrained by ceilings under RE 2025 –26 and BE 2026 –27. No additional funds have been 
secured under the Materials and Supplies head for procurement of medicines for CGHS 
dispensaries nationwide; the proposed BE 2026 -27 was ₹2831.15 crore, however, at least 
₹2700 crore will be required, which will be pursued at the RE stage. 
3.1.7 The Committee notes that while the Central Government Health Scheme (CGHS) 
has expanded significantly, budgetary allocations remain constrained in relation to 
projected demand. In FY 2024 –25, expenditure under CGHS and PORB was fully 
utilized. In FY 2025 –26 also utilisation stood at 88.43 percent up to January 2026, 
however, with notable reductions under the Capital Head where allocations were cut by 
nearly half at the RE stage. For FY 2026 –27, the Directorate projected a requirement of 
₹9,431.65 crore, but  allocations were limited to ₹8,697.86 crore, leaving a gap of over 

33 
 
₹700 crore, and no additional funds secured under the Materials and Supplies head for 
procurement of medicines nationwide.  With the revision of CGHS rates, expansion of 
empanelled hospital s to Tier II and Tier III cities, and operationalisation of 22 new 
wellness centres, expenditure pressures are expected to rise further. The Committee, 
therefore, recommends that the Department pursue additional allocations of fund at the 
RE stage to meet genuine expenditure, ensure adequate provisioning for medicines and 
consumables, and safeguard continuity of services for pensioners and beneficiaries. 
Further, capital investments in infrastructure, ICT systems, and medical equipment 
should be protected f rom mid -year reductions to strengthen service delivery, 
digitisation, and expansion of CGHS facilities, thereby ensuring that the scheme remains 
responsive to the growing needs of its diverse beneficiary base. 
3.1.8 On a specific query pertaining to reduct ion in the capital outlay from ₹75.31 crore to 
₹38.86 crore at RE stage in FY 2025 -26, the Department informed that t he budget under 
Capital Head was majorly attributed to ICT Equipment Head and Building and Structures 
Head which amounts to ₹19.1 crore and 10.63 crore respectively. ICT budget was distributed 
to all the 24 CGHS units for purchase of CDAC compatible computers and to CGHS Delhi 
for the E -Sushrut programme (a 5 year approved project) for which ₹ 9.5 crore were 
earmarked during this year. Under Buildin gs and Structures Head, sanction order of ₹10.63 
crore had been issued to CGHS Delhi, Chennai, Nagpur and Kolkata. Remaining Budget was 
utilized to purchase furniture and Machinery for all CGHS dispensaries. At the time of 
projecting demand for BE 2025 -26 under Capital Head, the construction of state -of-the-art 
multifacility centre at Koliwada was under discussion and its detailed project report was also 
prepared. Hence an amount of ₹36 crore was submitted for this Koliwada project, however, 
the project cou ld not get administrative approval and hence the amount of ₹36 crore was 
surrendered. Moreover, ₹ 45 lakhs were demanded for purchase of vehicle by 2 CGHS Units, 
but they also could not purchase and hence 45 lakhs were also surrendered. 
3.1.9 The Committee  notes that the reduction in capital outlay from ₹75.31 crore to 
₹38.86 crore at the RE stage in FY 2025 –26 was primarily due to the non -approval of 
the proposed ₹36 crore state -of-the-art multifacility centre at Koliwada and the 
surrender of ₹45 lakh earm arked for vehicle purchases by two CGHS units. While 
allocations under ICT Equipment and Buildings and Structures Heads were partially 
utilised for procurement of CDAC -compatible computers, implementation of the E -
Sushrut programme, and sanctioned works in Delhi, Chennai, Nagpur, and Kolkata, the 
surrender of major project funds significantly lowered capital utilisation. The 
Committee recommends that the Department strengthen project appraisal and approval 
mechanisms to ensure that large-scale proposals such as the Koliwada centre are cleared 
in advance of budget finalisation, thereby avoiding mid -year surrender of funds. 
Further, capital allocations should be safeguarded with improved coordination between 
CGHS units and administrative authorities to ensure timely approvals and utilisation. 
This will enhance predictability in capital planning, prevent under -utilisation, and 
ensure that CGHS facilities are adequately modernised to meet the growing needs of 
beneficiaries. 

34 
 
3.1.10 On a specific query, the Departm ent stated the following new initiatives proposed in 
the coming financial year, (i) Implementation of CGHS Drug Procurement Policy, 2025 to 
strengthen uninterrupted availability of quality medicines, transparency, and supply -chain 
efficiency; (ii) Creation of a Project Management Unit (PMU) for planning/monitoring major 
CGHS reforms/projects; (iii) Establishment of an on -site Call Centre for beneficiary 
facilitation and grievance redressal; and (iv) Setting up of 22 new CGHS Wellness Centres, 
subject to availability of premises, staff and administrative approvals. 
3.1.11 In its Annual Report 2025 -26, the Department stated the following recent initiatives 
taken for improvement of CGHS: - 
a) A sanction order for opening of 22 new CGHS Allopathic Wellness Centr es across 
the country. 
b) Rates for nearly 2000 tests/ investigations have been revised which came into effect 
from 13.10.2025. The applicability of these revised rates shall be governed by the tier -
wise classification of cities, namely Tier -I, Tier -II and  Tier-III. Health Care 
Organizations (HCOs) situated in these cities shall now be eligible for empanelment 
under CGHS as per the revised norms. CGHS beneficiaries shall be entitled to avail 
medical services directly from such empanelled hospitals in accord ance with the 
existing CGHS guidelines.  
c) CGHS has made significant strides in adopting technology to enhance service 
delivery. A major achievement is the customization and deployment of "E -Sushrut," a 
Hospital Management Information System (HMIS) develo ped in partnership with the 
Centre for Development of Advanced Computing (CDAC). This system integrates 
critical modules such as the CGHS Card Module, Registration Module, and Medical 
Reimbursement Claim Module etc.  
d) The digital transformation extends t o beneficiary engagement through the 
"myCGHS 2.0" mobile application. This app allows beneficiaries to book 
appointments, access medical history (medications and referrals), and download 
electronic CGHS cards and geo-locate CGHS wellness centres and offices.  
e) To ensure the availability of essential medicines, CGHS has implemented the Drug 
& Vaccine Distribution Management System (DVDMS). This web-based supply chain 
solution manages the distribution and demand prediction of drugs across the network, 
featuring a central dashboard for oversight. The system ensures that common 
medicines are dispensed immediately from wellness center stocks, while non-available 
items are indented through Authorized Local Chemists (ALC) and typically made 
available by the next working day. 
3.1.12 In its submission to the Committee, the Department further provided information on 
policies proposed or under consideration for review/revamp initiatives intended to further 
modernize and review the CGHS as under: - 
a) Integration with Gov ernment Portals: The integration of CGHS with the Bhavishya 
portal (for pensioners) and e -HRMS (for serving employees) is under consideration to 
streamline data flow and service delivery. 

35 
 
b) Expansion of Coverage: There is a continuous endeavour to include mo re cities and to 
improve accessibility to quality healthcare. Currently extension to 22 new cities have 
been approved. 
c) Digital Health Records (EMR/EHR): All empanelled HCOs are being pushed to 
achieve ABDM "M3" compliance and implement standardized Electro nic Medical 
Records (EMR/EHR) to ensure continuity of care and better audit trails. 
d) Anti-Fraud Strengthening: The establishment of a dedicated Anti -Fraud Cell and the 
use of data analytics to monitor billing patterns (e.g., upcoding or unbundling) are key 
components of the ongoing revamping effort. 
e) Decentralized Card Printing: A proposed shift to each city having its own CGHS card 
printing agency to reduce the turnaround time for plastic cards. 
3.1.13 On being asked about the measures taken to improve the m anagement of patients and 
service delivery, the Department further submitted the following steps taken: - 
a) Comprehensive Rate Revision (OM Oct 3, 2025): To prevent hospitals from denying 
treatment, rates for nearly 1998 procedures, investigations etc were r evised to reflect 
current market costs. 
b) Fresh Empanelment Scheme 2025 for existing and new HCOs to apply under revised 
terms, featuring differential rates based on city tiers (X, Y, Z) and mandatory ABDM 
registration 
c) Formulation of a ‗comprehensive drug procurement policy‘ to strengthen supply chain 
resilience, prioritize generic medicines, and establish centralized managed databases 
for uniformity. 
d) Integration of CGHS with the 'Bhavishya' portal has ensured that pensioner CGHS 
cards are processed proactive ly, allowing retirees to receive their medical cards 
immediately upon retirement without the traditional procedural delays. 
e) Access to cashless treatment at AIIMS (New Delhi), PGIMER (Chandigarh), and 
NEIGRIHMS (Shillong) for both OPD and IPD without requir ing a mandatory CGHS 
referral. 
f) Implementation of TMS 2.0 software platform for all new registrations and billing 
starting midnight 13-10-2025 to enhance digital transparency. 
g) Advancement of CGHS HMIS and CGHS mobile app to provide enhanced services to 
CGHS beneficiaries. 
3.1.14 The Committee notes that the Department has proposed several new initiatives 
for the coming financial year, including the implementation of the CGHS Drug 
Procurement Policy, 2025, creation of a Project Management Unit (PMU), establishment 
of an on -site call centre, and setting up of 22 new allopathic wellness centres, alongside 
recent reforms such as deployment of the E -Sushrut HMIS, launch of the myCGHS 2.0 
mobile app, and adoption of the DVDMS supply -chain system. While these measur es 
represent milestone progress in modernising CGHS, the Committee recommends that 
the Department ensure adequate provisioning of funds and staffing for the new wellness 
centres, strengthen ICT -enabled monitoring systems, and expedite integration with 
government portals to streamline service delivery. Further, the Committee emphasizes 

36 
 
the need for regular review and inclusion of the latest and advanced medical procedures 
and investigations in the CGHS package, so that beneficiaries have access to 
contemporary standards of care without facing denial of treatment or delays. By 
combining expansion, digitisation, supply -chain resilience, and continuous updating of 
medical services, CGHS can evolve into a more responsive, transparent, and 
beneficiary-centric health system. 
3.1.15 The Committee further recommends that the Department place greater emphasis 
on beneficiary-centric monitoring and feedback mechanisms  to ensure that the 
expansion and digitisation efforts under CGHS translate into tangible improvements in  
patient experience. While systems such as E -Sushrut, DVDMS, and the myCGHS 2.0 
app have strengthened transparency and supply -chain efficiency, their effectiveness 
depends on consistent user adoption and responsiveness to beneficiary concerns. 
Beneficiaries should be made aware of these tools through structured outreach, and a 
public dashboard should display feedback and grievances received, timelines for 
resolution, pendency, and cases resolved, thereby enhancing transparency and 
accountability. The proposed on-site call centre must be expanded into a 24×7 facility to 
address emergency grievances, recognizing health as an essential service. Moreover, the 
proposed Anti-Fraud Cell and data analytics framework should not only monitor billing 
irregularities but also track service quality indicators such as waiting times, medicine 
availability, and claim settlement efficiency. Embedding robust feedback loops, 
grievance tracking, and real -time responsiveness into the CGHS framework, periodic 
beneficiary satisfaction surveys, and integration of feedback into policy adjustments  will 
ensure that reforms remain beneficiary-centric, equitable, and aligned with the evolving 
needs of its diverse beneficiary base. 
3.1.16 The Committee takes into account the submission of t he Department that the 
consolidated incumbency position of Group A, B, and C posts under CGHS reveals a 
substantial vacancy burden, with 2,159 posts lying vacant out of 6,865 sanctioned 
positions, translating into nearly one -third of the workforce.  Critic al shortages are 
evident among medical cadres such as General Duty Medical Officers (328 vacant), 
Specialists (96 vacant), Nursing Officers (151 vacant), Medical Attendants (332 vacant), 
and Pharmacists (Allopathic- 78 vacant, Ayurvedic- 38 vacant, Homeopathic- 23 vacant) 
which overburdened the available workforce and directly affect the delivery of 
healthcare services at CGHS wellness centres. Equally concerning are gaps in technical 
posts such as Medical Lab Technologists (86 vacant), ECG Technician (8 va cant), and 
OT Assistants (5 vacant), which compromise diagnostic and treatment capacity. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department urgently prioritize recruitment 
drives, streamline promotion pathways , time -bound or assured career progressio n 
scheme to keep the morale of the staff high, skill upgradation, timely convening of  
Departmental Promotion Committees (DPCs) , and rationalisation of recruitment rules 
to remove bottlenecks,  particularly for pharmacists whose ineligibility for promotion 
has created anomalies in staffing. The Committee also notes that certain cadres, 
particularly pharmacists  (including Ayurvedic and Homeopathic) , have limited or no 
promotion avenues within the existing service structure, leading to career stagnation 
and aff ecting staff motivation. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the 

37 
 
Department undertake a cadre restructuring to establish a clearer career progression 
pathway for such categories of staff, addressing long -standing anomalies which would 
improve retention and ensure a more motivated workforce within the healthcare system. 
 
3.1.17 The Committee further observes that administrative and support functions are 
severely constrained, with 29 out of 31 Accountant posts, 16 out of 25 Administrative 
Officer posts, and all 44 Assistant Depot Manager posts lying vacant, thereby weakening 
financial management, supply -chain oversight, and institutional efficiency. Vacancies in 
posts such as Office Superintendents, Drivers, Translators, and Nursing Midwives also 
hinder day-to-day operations and patient support services. To address these challenges, 
the Committee recommends that the Department institute a robust human resource 
planning framework that aligns sanctioned posts with actual service delivery needs, 
supported by digital HR management systems for real-time monitoring of vacancies and 
deployments. A phased but time -bound strategy to fill these posts, coupled with 
improved coordination between CGHS units and administrative authorities, will be 
essential to ensure un interrupted healthcare services, strengthen administrative 
efficiency, and enhance the overall responsiveness of CGHS to its growing beneficiary 
base. 
 
3.1.18 The Committee also reiterates its recommendation in 163 rd Report for periodic 
quality control of Generic Medicines at various centers of CGHS through sudden test 
and investigation. A third party assurance mechanism may be evolved in this regard. 
The Committee further reiterates its another recommendation in 155 th and 163rd Report 
to simplify referral procedures further as there is need to significantly reduce the 
burden on patients and their families by eliminating the requirement for repeated visits 
to dispensaries during illness. The Committee notes the provision made allowing CGHS 
pensioner benefici aries aged 70 and above to directly consult specialists without 
requiring a CGHS referral, however, the Committee reiterates its recommendation to 
further review the guidelines and extend the benefit to all CGHS 
pensioners/beneficiaries aged 60 and above f or continuing medical care to CGHS 
beneficiaries. Allowing direct specialist consultations at empanelled private hospitals 
without a CGHS referral would ensure consistency and continuity of care with the 
existing system, where pensioners/beneficiaries aged  60 and above already receive 
cashless treatment from these hospitals. 
 
3.1.19 The Committee is aware that the Medical Centre at the Parliament House 
Annexe caters to a significant influx of Parliamentarians and dignitaries whose 
professional responsibilit ies often involve prolonged sittings, frequent travel and 
considerable physical strain. The Committee notes that the absence of a dedicated 
physiotherapist from the CGHS cadre presently limits the availability of timely 
physiotherapy support, often necessi tating referrals to external facilities which may 
delay treatment and affect optimal recovery. The Committee, therefore, recommends 
the deployment of a full -time physiotherapist from the CGHS cadre at the Medical 
Centre, Parliament House Annexe, so as to e nsure prompt physiotherapy care and 
strengthen healthcare support for Parliamentarians and other dignitaries. 

38 
 
 
3.1.20 The Committee observes that the contact details of Additional Directors of 
various zones available on the CGHS website and related sources  appear to be outdated, 
with no visible updates since September 2025. As a result, beneficiaries, empanelled 
hospitals and other stakeholders often face difficulties in reaching the concerned offices 
due to non -functional or changed telephone numbers. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends that the Department create a dedicated and regularly maintained webpage 
containing the updated contact details of all Additional Directors, including their names, 
telephone numbers and official email IDs. The Committee furt her recommends that 
these details be reviewed and updated on a quarterly basis to improve accessibility, 
strengthen grievance redressal and facilitate better coordination with stakeholders in 
line with ongoing digital health initiatives.

3.2.3 The budget analysis for SJH and VMMC shows that revenue expenditure has 
been consistently well-utilised, however, capital expenditure continues to face significant 
challenges, with allocations fluctuating sharply due to delays in project clearances, 
phased funding releases, and slow procurement processes. The reduction of capital 
allocations from ₹238.50 crore at BE 2025 –26 to ₹88.79 crore at RE 2025 –26 highlights 
systemic inefficiencies in planning and execution. Critical projects such as the 
construction of lecture theatres, auditoriums, hostels, and advanced medical facilities 
have been dela yed, undermining institutional growth and service delivery. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department establish a dedicated 
mechanism to expedite inter -departmental clearances, adopt milestone -based funding 
for infrastructure projects, and s trengthen procurement planning through framework 
contracts and vendor management. Budgetary projections should be made more 
realistic by aligning estimates with past utilisation trends, and quarterly expenditure 
reviews should be introduced to identify bottlenecks early.

3.2.7 The Committee observes with con cern that capital expenditure continues to face 
delays and underutilisation. For instance, in FY 2024 –25, capital spending of ₹148.44 
crore was directed towards procurement of high -end medical equipment, ICT, 
furniture, and housing projects, whereas in FY 2025–26, capital utilisation dropped to 
₹67.81 crore due to delays in clearances and slow progress in infrastructure projects. 
The major area of concern is the significant delay and subsequent cost escalation in 
ongoing infrastructure works, most notably t he Dwarka Housing Project. The cost for 
this project has increased from the initially approved ₹81.03 Crore to a revised estimate 
of ₹119.20 Crore (an almost 47% increase), while the completion date has been 
prolonged from May 2024 to August 2026. The Committee is of the view that the reasons 
cited by the CPWD, such as monsoon waterlogging and the annual Graded Response 
Action Plan (GRAP) construction bans, are foreseeable, recurring events in Delhi. The 
Committee recommends that to prevent this recurring i ssue, the Department should 
strictly make the project management agencies (like CPWD and EPIL) accountable for 
poor execution and ensure it mandatory for them to factor predictable local, seasonal, 
and regulatory disruptions into their initial baseline schedules and cost estimates.  
 
3.2.8 Earlier also, the Committee in its 134 th, 143rd and 163rd Reports had flagged t he 
issues related to slow pace of construction activities and recommended the Department 
to adhere to the timeline for completion of the Dwarka Project. 
3.2.9 The Committee further observes that the lecture theatre, auditorium, and hostel 
projects remain stalled pending Stage -II forest clearance. The Committee believes that 
the Department needs to establish a dedicated, high -level steering commi ttee to 
aggressively follow up on external dependencies, such as the pending Stage -II MoEFCC 
tree transplantation clearance for the ₹243 Crore VMMC Auditorium and Hostel 
project, ensuring these administrative bottlenecks do not translate into further physi cal 

43 
 
delays and financial liabilities. This pattern highlights the need for stronger project 
execution mechanisms to ensure that capital allocations translate into timely physical 
achievements. The Committee recommends that the Department adopt a more robus t 
monitoring and accountability framework for capital projects. A dedicated inter -agency 
coordination cell should be established to expedite statutory clearances and approvals, 
particularly for projects involving environmental permissions. 
 
3.2.10 In addit ion to strengthening monitoring and procurement processes, the 
Department should place greater emphasis on project management capacity and risk 
mitigation for large-scale infrastructure works. These delays not only affect utilisation of 
capital allocations  but also compromise institutional capacity expansion. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department establish a dedicated project 
management unit within SJH and VMMC to oversee timelines, coordinate with CPWD 
and other executing agencies, and p roactively address risks such as environmental 
clearances, weather disruptions, and regulatory bans. Procurement processes for 
medical equipment and ICT should be streamlined through framework contracts and 
vendor management to avoid delays and ensure time ly availability of critical resources. 
Such approach will ensure stabilised capital expenditure, ensure optimal utilisation of 
funds, and support the timely completion of projects such as the Dwarka housing 
colony, lecture theatre complex, and Maternal & C hild Care Centre, thereby 
strengthening institutional capacity and aligning with national health sector priorities. 
 
3.2.11 The Committee further finds the clear financial mismatch between the projected 
demands and the allocated Budget Estimates (BE) for F Y 2026-27. The Department has 
projected a total demand of ₹2424.87 Crore, but the proposed appropriation is only 
₹2170.75 Crore, leaving a substantial shortfall of over ₹254 Crore. Because major 
capital projects like the Maternal & Child Care Centre (MCCC)  are merely at the 
conception stage and the Emergency Block ramp is at the tender issuance phase. In view 
thereof, the Committee recommends that the Department must implement a phased 
financial prioritization strategy. Available capital funds should be hea vily front-loaded 
toward projects with high existing physical progress or critical immediate needs, such as 
the procurement of vital hospital machinery and ICT equipment. Concurrently, the 
Department should prepare a rigorous, milestone -based justification  detailing the 
progress of the ongoing projects and equipment needs to successfully secure additional 
supplementary grants during the Revised Estimates (RE) stage later in the year, 
ensuring patient care and ongoing civil works are not stalled. 
 
3.2.12 On being asked about the k ey concern areas of SJH and VMMC for optimum 
functioning, the Department stated that i n the infrastructure projects of VMMC, Tree Cutting 
Permissions are urgently needed. To overcome the manpower constraint a consolidated 
proposal fo r creation of posts is being prepared. There are also some space constraint and 
limited scope for expansion in the Hospital premises. Safdarjung Hospital adopts a multi -
dimensional performance monitoring framework integrating financial prudence, infrastruc ture 
utilization, clinical quality, and patient satisfaction indicators. Regular internal audits, 

44 
 
departmental reviews, and Ministry -level monitoring ensures accountability, transparency, 
and efficient service delivery aligned with national healthcare objectives. 
 
3.2.13 The Committee urges the Department to address the physical and operational 
bottlenecks that are limiting the optimum functioning of SJH and VMMC. Given the 
severe space constraints and the lack of horizontal expansion scope within the exist ing 
hospital premises, the Department needs to pivot toward a comprehensive urban spatial 
master plan prioritising brownfield redevelopment with vertical expansion, and 
potentially relocating non -clinical administrative functions off -campus to allow 
premium space for patient care. Simultaneously, to resolve the cited manpower 
constraints, the Department should actively leverage "multi -dimensional performance 
monitoring framework." By explicitly linking current staffing deficits to empirical data 
on clinical  quality, infrastructure underutilization, and patient satisfaction indicators, 
the Department can build a highly robust, data -driven case to expedite the approval of 
its consolidated proposal for new posts. Such dual approach ensures that existing spaces 
are maximized and that future infrastructure is adequately staffed from the very 
beginning. 
 
3.2.14 On being asked about the status of the 1,100 proposed posts sanctioned and notified 
across different cadres, as stated by the Department in its Action Taken Notes on 163rd Report 
of the Committee, the Department stated that c reation of 1100 posts across various categories 
is under process. The Department further stated that recruitment against  933 posts across 
various categories has been done during 2024 -25 a nd 509 posts in the year 2025 -26 (as on 
31.12.2025). The on -boarding of selected personnel is currently underway. However, a 
number of posts remain vacant due to the non -availability of suitable candidates, non -joining 
of selected individuals, and frequent  resignations of newly joined candidates. In the Teaching 
Sub-Cadre, out of 352 in -position strength, 34 posts are on contractual basis. Further, total 
232 contractual nursing officers are informed to be working in SJH.  
 
3.2.15 The Department, in its submission to the queries of the Committee, also informed that 
in last three years, all the  vacant seats of Junior Residents (JRs) have been filled through 
regular recruitment. If the selected candidate(s) have not joined, vacant posts are filled by the 
wait l isted candidates. In case of Senior Residents, in the year 2023, 41 candidates did not 
join out of 305 selected candidates. In the year 2024, the recruitment was delayed and shifted 
to year 2025. In 2025, two recruitments done and 52 of 376 Candidates did not join in the first 
recruitment, whereas the second recruitment is currently ongoing. Reasons cited by the 
candidates for not joining are selection in other institution of choice, preparation for super 
specialty courses, excessive workloads, and opportunities abroad with better emoluments. It is 
estimated that a large  percentage of resignations is due to working conditions, pay scales, or 
career progression issues. 
3.2.16 The analysis of the staffing situation at SJH and VMMC highlights that despite 
the r ecruitment of 933 posts in 2024 –25 and 509 in 2025 –26, posts such as Clinical 
Research Assistant, Medical Lab Technologists, Senior OT Technician,  Radiographers, 
Clinical Psychologists, Administrative Officers, and Nursing staff show vacancy rates 
ranging from 40% to 100%. This situation directly impacts patient care, teaching 

45 
 
capacity, and administrative efficiency. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the 
Department strengthen recruitment pipelines by diversifying candidate sourcing, 
introducing brid ge training programs to expand the pool of qualified applicants, and 
offering incentives such as retention allowances, career progression pathways, and 
housing facilities to reduce attrition. A more proactive engagement with professional 
councils and unive rsities could also help ensure a steady inflow of qualified candidates 
into specialist and technical roles. 
3.2.17 The Committee expresses its concern to combat the high rates of non -joining and 
frequent resignations among medical professionals, the Depart ment must transition 
from simple recruitment to a holistic retention strategy. This should begin with a data -
driven "Exit and Non -Joiner Survey" to precisely identify why candidates prefer other 
institutions or opportunities abroad. Findings should inform a "Total Rewards" 
framework that provides doctors with amenities at par with civil servants, including 
expedited access to the Dwarka housing project, streamlined transportation, and 
dedicated administrative support to minimize non -clinical burdens. For sp ecialized 
technical cadres currently facing up to 100% vacancy, the Department should introduce 
"Targeted Lateral Entry" and clear career progression pathways. By aligning 
emoluments and working conditions with market standards and providing a structured 
growth trajectory, the Department can make niche roles at SJH and VMMC more 
competitive against the private sector. 
3.2.18 The Department should immediately launch a time -bound "Regularization and 
Administrative Reinforcement Plan" to reduce the systemic re liance on contractual 
staff, which currently includes 232 Nursing Officers and 34 Teaching Staff. Such 
reliance dilutes institutional accountability and "belongingness." Priority must be given 
to fast -tracking the on boarding of the 1,100 proposed permanen t posts and filling 
critical 100% vacancies in leadership and oversight roles, such as the Deputy Director 
Accountant and Office In -charge (Ambulance). A vital component of the strategy is 
stabilizing the "administrative backbone", specifically addressing the 96% vacancy in 
Assistant Admin Officers and the high shortages in LDC/UDC cadres. By replacing a 
"stop-gap" contractual approach with a robust permanent administrative layer, the 
Department must ensure continuity of service and alleviate the "excessive  workload" 
that currently forces clinical staff to manage logistical complexities instead of patient 
care.

3.3.1 The Committee notes that in FY 2024 -25, the Capital Outlay was reduced from ₹ 
558.17 crore to ₹ 233.43 crore at RE stage. Similarly, in FY 2025 -26, the Capital outlay has 
again been reduced from ₹ 338.10 crore to ₹ 147.72 crore at RE stage. On being asked about 
the reason behind this significant variation and drastic reduction at RE stage, the Department 
informed that the reduction in capital outlay at the RE stage was mainly  due to delay in 
completion of SSB construction and non -finalization of the Medical College tendering 
process and delay in start of construction.  
 
3.3.2 The Committee notes with concern the recurring and substantial reductions in 
Capital outlay at the Rev ised Estimates stage for Dr. RML Hospital and ABVIMS, due 
to delays in completion of the Super Speciality Block (SSB) and non -finalization of 
Medical College tenders, indicating persistent weaknesses in project planning, 
tendering, and execution. The volat ility in Capital spending undermines infrastructure 
augmentation and long -term capacity expansion. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends that the Department should institute time -bound pre -construction 
clearances, adopt milestone -based monitoring with quart erly physical and financial 
progress reviews, fix accountability for procedural delays, strengthen coordination with 
executing agencies, and create a realistic phasing mechanism for large projects to 
minimise surrender of funds at the RE stage, thereby safeguarding timely infrastructure 
development and service delivery. 
 
3.3.3 On a specific query related to implementation status of ongoing/new projects a t Dr. 
RML hospital and ABVIMS, the Department furnished the following details: - 
Project 1 – SSB Building 
i. Physical progress: 96%; Financial progress: 89.1%. 
ii. The project has been delayed by approximately three years from the original target 
completion date.  
iii. Approved EFC cost of SSB was ₹482.16 crore. The discovered tender cost was 
₹398.78. So far, MoHFW has sanctioned ₹407.0 crores which includes escalation cost 
like 10CC. GST, etc.). Multiple Reviews on the status of construction of SSB have 
been conducted a t Department and Hospital level with CPWD and contractor 
company from time to time for early completion of the project, and efforts are being 
made to make the building fully functional by April 2026.  
Project 2 – Medical College Building: The construction work of the Medical College building 
has not yet commenced. The delay is primarily due to lack of statutory clearances from 
respective agencies viz. Delhi Fire Service, Forest Clearance and NDMC.  
Proposed works for FY 2026–27: The projected capital demand includes funds for completion 
of SSB and construction work of Medical College. 
3.3.4 On being asked about the key areas of concern, the Department stated that the key 
areas of concern relate to inadequate infrastructure, patient flow, lack of space, moder nization 
of facilities, and strengthening of safety standards. To achieve global standards, the hospital is 

47 
 
focusing on upgradation of infrastructure, phased redevelopment, and modernization of 
clinical infrastructure, improved project monitoring, and enha nced coordination with 
executing agencies for timely completion of works. 
3.3.5 In view of the near -completion yet prolonged delay of the Super Speciality Block 
(SSB) at Dr. RML Hospital and ABVIMS, with 96% physical and 89.1% financial 
progress but a time  overrun of nearly three years, the Committee recommends that the 
Department should ensure time -bound commissioning roadmap integrating statutory 
clearances, utility connections, equipment installation, fire and occupancy approvals, 
and operational readine ss, with clearly defined responsibility matrices and weekly 
milestone monitoring at senior level to meet the April 2026 deadline. The Committee, 
further, recommends that the examination and timely imposition of liquidated damages, 
wherever contractually wa rranted, should be concluded expeditiously to reinforce 
financial discipline and contractual accountability besides discouraging recurrence of 
time overruns in future infrastructure projects. The Committee also advises the 
Department to conduct a post -occupancy audit in late 2026 to identify the specific 
bottlenecks that led to the initial 36 -month delay, ensuring these lessons are integrated 
into the hospital‘s broader modernization and redevelopment goals. 
 
3.3.6 With respect to the Medical College buildi ng, the Committee notes with concern 
that construction has not commenced due to pending statutory clearances and during 
tendering process, the quoted cost of 44.61% above the estimated cost necessitating 
revised approval of Project Investment Board. The Co mmittee, therefore, recommends 
that the Department should establish a pre -tender statutory clearance mechanism, and 
undertake realistic cost estimation based on prevailing market conditions before bid 
invitation to avoid such mistake in future projects. In  this regard, the Committee urges 
the Department to expedite appraisal before the Project Investment Board to avoid 
further slippages.  The Department should establish a dedicated inter -agency liaison cell 
to secure the stalled clearances from the Delhi Fi re Service, Forest Department, and 
NDMC, as these are prerequisites for any meaningful progress. 
 
3.3.7 The Committee expresses its concern on the observed pattern of uneven fund 
utilisation and recurring fund surrenders. To move away from the current stop -gap 
approach to creation of infrastructure, the Committee recommends the Department to 
develop a rolling multi -year capital planning framework, may be a five to ten year 
Capital Investment Plan, aligned with projected patient load, academic expansion and 
modernization goals, supported by quarterly milestone -based monitoring and 
strengthened coordination with executing and local authorities, so as to ensure optimal 
utilisation of capital allocations, and systematically address infrastructure gaps. This 
framework should include a Modernization Roadmap that prioritizes the upgrading of 
safety standards and clinical infrastructure in older wards while new blocks are under 
construction, thereby addressing the patient flow constraints and mitigating inadequate 
infrastructure concerns without waiting for the completion of mega-projects. 
 

48 
 
3.3.8 A query on needs-assessment survey on the land allocation in respect of a plot of 2020 
sqm area designated for a sports complex was raised with the Department considering the  
critical shortage of clinical space and beds, patient attendant shelter or an additional 
diagnostic block. In this respect the Department submitted that i n proposed Redevelopment 
Plan, diagnostic facilities like Biochemistry, Pathology, Microbiology and R adiology have 
already been planned in such a way that these facilities will be easily commuted to all the 
departments smoothly.   Institute need a sports complex as per the NMC norms. At this 
moment there is no space available for this purpose, so it has be en planned to allot the said 
area for the development of the sport complex. Also, a Patient attendant shelter has already 
been planned in the Dharamshala area in the proposed Redevelopment Plan. 
 
3.3.9 The Committee acknowledges that while compliance with National Medical 
Commission (NMC) norms necessitates the development of a sports complex for UG/PG 
students. The Committee is of the view to balance student welfare and patient -centric 
infrastructure to achieve a holistic redevelopment plan that strengthen s both academic 
standards and patient care. The Committee, thus, believes that equal priority should 
also be accorded to patient -centric infrastructure such as attendant shelters, clinical 
infrastructure, additional diagnostic block and mobility support. T he proposed 
allocation of the 2020 sqm plot for a sports complex is justified for the mental and 
physical well-being of medical students, but the Committee urges the Department that 
patient attendant facilities should also be given attention and not to be treated as 
auxiliary. Many attendants accompany patients from distant areas and require round -
the-clock accommodation near the hospital. The Committee, therefore, recommends 
that the Department should plan to design and construct a spacious, well -equipped 
patient attendant shelter, enough to cater to number of attendants in proportion to at 
least half of the in -patient beds, with basic amenities such as sanitation, drinking water, 
and resting areas, located close to the main hospital building. Alternatively , a free 
electric cart service (shuttle services) should be provided 24×7 to connect the shelter 
with hospital blocks, diagnostic facilities, and parking areas, benefitting elderly 
attendants and patients, improving inclusivity and ease of movement.

by the Department as follows: -

The Committee has been apprised o f Posts filled up on Contractual Basis that includes:  (a) 
Doctors – 19; (b) Nursing Officer – 24; (c) Lab Technician – 8; (d) Others – 16; (e) Sr 
Residents – 95; (f) Jr Residents – 4. Further, for filling up of vacant posts, vacancies of 
institution are timely reported to concerned agencies for advertisement to UPSC, SSC, AIIMS 
etc. 
 
3.3.11 The Committee notes with concern the critical vacancy rate among Senior 
Residents (47.4%) and Group A specialists (25.7%) and is of the view that s uch gaps, 
especially i n clinical and frontline categories, are likely to adversely affect service 
delivery, patient safety, academic supervision, and overall institutional efficiency. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department adopt a time -bound 
recruitment strate gy to fill all critical clinical and technical posts, particularly Senior 
and Junior Residents, nursing staff, and allied health professionals with an immediate 
transition from emergency contractual hiring to a permanent cadre -strengthening 
model. While co ntractual engagement may be used as a short -term stop -gap 
arrangement, it should not substitute regular appointments, as excessive reliance on 
contractual staff may dilute institutional accountability and continuity. The Committee, 
further, recommends that  the Department should adopt a Total Support model for 
medical professionals to attract and retain qualified doctors and specialists in the public 
health system by providing improved service conditions in terms of career progression 
avenues, academic incen tives, etc. and providing parity with civil servants in terms of 
accommodation, transport facilities  and administrative helpers, thereby allowing 
doctors to focus on clinical duties rather than non-medical tasks. 
3.3.12 The Committee further observes that persistent human resource shortages, 
coupled with unregulated and alarmingly prolonged working hours of Junior and 
Senior Residents, sometimes extending up to 24 -36 hours at a stretch, pose serious risks 
to both healthcare providers and patients. The Commi ttee, therefore, recommends that 

50 
 
the Department should implement a mandatory working -hour regulatory framework 
for Resident Doctors to minimize fatigue -induced clinical errors and to safeguard their 
mental and physical well -being, drawing an analogy from r egulated working -hour 
norms in other critical sectors such as fatigue management protocols used for 
commercial pilots in  civil aviation. The Committee further urges the Department to 
devise a transparent mechanism to ensure the presence and accountability of senior 
faculty members and professors during prescribed working hours to provide adequate 
supervision and enhanced clinical training to Resident Doctors, and to prevent 
unsupervised decision -making in critical cases.  The Committee observes that majority  
work load is handled by the Resident doctors who form the backbone of the health care 
delivery system but the requisite hand holding by the senior faculty members is absent. 
The Committee recommends for fixing of strong accountability amongst the regular and 
senior faculty members.  
3.3.13 The Committee believes, to mitigate the systemic shortage of human resources 
across Groups B and C, and to compliment administrative work, the Department should 
adopt responsible usage of AI and ICT tools to optimize hos pital administration, patient 
data management, diagnostic support, and routine monitoring. Such technological 
integration, coupled with structured training for staff, can enhance productivity, reduce 
administrative burden on clinical personnel, and partial ly mitigate human resource 
constraints.

3.4.1  The total budgetary allocation to the tune of ₹ 795.60 crore as BE 2026-27 has

outlay. The BE 2024-25 amounting to ₹ 101 crore marked for Capital outlay was reduced to ₹

3.4.4 The budgetary trends of Lady Hardinge Medical College (LHMC) and Smt. 
Sucheta Kriplani Hospital (SSKH) indicate a recurring pattern of under -utilisation 
under the Capital head. In FY 2024-25, the Capital allocation was reduced sharply from 
₹101 crore at BE stage to ₹51.50 crore at RE stage, with actual expenditure declining 
further to ₹31.02 crore, largely on account of re -tendering and single responsive bids 
leading to deferment of procurement proposals. Further, in FY 2026 -27, against a 
projected Capital demand of ₹171.60 crore, only ₹92.50 crore has been allocated. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department institute a robust pre-tendering 
and project appraisal mechanism, including advance preparation of detailed project 
reports (DPRs), realistic cost estimation, wider vendor participation, and strict 
procurement timelines to prevent repeated surrender or deferment of capital funds. A 
quarterly monitoring framework at the level of the Ministry may be introduced to track 
progress of major works and procurement proposals, ensuring that capital allocations 
translate into tangible asset creation aligned with institutional expansion plans. 
 
3.4.5 Further, the proposed combined project for construction of UG/PG hostels and 
extension of the IPD Block (96 beds) at an estimated cost of ₹498 crore remains at the 
planning stage, w hile the allocated capital budget for 2026 -27 appears insufficient to 
meaningfully advance such a major infrastructure initiative. Given the growing 
academic and clinical load of LHMC and SSKH, and the projected overall requirement 
of ₹1,028.37 crore again st an allocation of ₹795.90 crore, the Committee recommends 
that the Department prioritise phased implementation of critical infrastructure projects, 
beginning with those directly augmenting patient care capacity and residential facilities 
for students and resident doctors. Adequate front-loading of capital funds, coupled with 
time-bound administrative approvals, may be ensured to avoid cost escalation and 
project delays. Aligning capital planning with realistic budgeting and execution capacity 
is essential  to strengthen institutional infrastructure and improve service delivery 
outcomes. 

52 
 
3.4.6 From the details of post -wise and cadre-wise sanctioned strength vis -à-vis in-position 
strength of the institution, as submitted by the Department, the Committee notes  that there are 
652 vacancies out the total of 2225 sanctioned posts. Further, some vacancies have been filled 
up on contractual basis like 112 Nursing Officers, 7 Lab Technicians, 3 ECG Technicians and 
9 OT Technicians. The major vacancies are reproduced as below: -

3.4.7 On a specific query, the Department also sub mitted that at present more than 262 
vacancies (not included CHS cadre posts) of Group A, B & C of LHMC & SSKH had been 
advertised through agencies like UPSC, SSC, AIIMS etc. Out of which, document verification 
on almost 200 vacancies is going on and will be finalized shortly. Further, vacancies of 
institution are timely reported to concerned agencies for advertisement to UPSC, SSC, AIIMS 
etc. Further, 140 vacancies on hold due to court cases. Around 80 posts are promotional and 
in some of promotional posts  no one in the feeder cadre available for promotion. Around 12 
posts are vacant due to non -availability of Recruitment Rules. Around 5 -10% of advertised 
vacancies have remained unfilled due to non-availability of candidates in particular categories 
specially in Ex -servicemen & PwBD, due to non -acceptance of offer letter, medically unfit, 
terminated due to fake documents etc. These vacancies are reported in next recruitment cycle 
as backlog vacancies. 
 
3.4.8 In its further submission, the Department stated t hat with reference to this institution, 
the student-teacher ratio meets the requirements stipulated under the UGMSR and PGMSR of 
the National Medical Commission. However, faculty responsibilities in medical colleges 
extend beyond academic teaching and also  encompass the delivery and management of 
hospital services. In this institution, there is a substantial clinical workload, and multiple 
national health programs are being implemented across both hospitals. In order to effectively 
manage this workload and ensure the continuity and quality of patient care and academic 
activities, it is imperative that the existing faculty vacancies are filled at the earliest. Due to 
vacancies, existing faculties have to put in extra classroom & clinical teaching, examination , 
assessment & research supervision. Once teaching posts are filled in line, the measurable 

53 
 
outcomes are expected in academics, hospital efficiency and research productivity & more 
peer-reviewed outputs. 
 
3.4.9 The Committee expresses with concern that out  of a sanctioned strength of 2,225 
posts, 652 posts (nearly 29%) remain vacant in the institution, with particularly 
alarming shortages in critical and supervisory categories such as Tutors in the College 
of Nursing (91% vacancy), Administrative Officer (1 00%), Radiotherapy Technologist 
(100%), OT Technicians and related cadres (70 –80%), Nursing Officers (31%), and 
Doctors in the Teaching Cadre (21%). Such extensive vacancies, especially in teaching, 
nursing, and technical posts directly linked to patient c are and academic delivery, are 
likely to adversely affect hospital efficiency, quality of clinical services, research 
productivity, and faculty workload. Although the Department has initiated recruitment 
for over 262 posts and is processing document verifi cation in a substantial number of 
cases, the Committee recommends the adoption of a time -bound, mission -mode 
approach to clear all pending vacancies, including those on hold due to court cases, non -
finalisation of Recruitment Rules, or absence of feeder ca dres. Recruitment Rules 
should be urgently notified wherever pending, and a periodic review mechanism may be 
instituted to prevent recurrence of structural bottlenecks such as promotional 
stagnation or backlog vacancies. The Committee also recommends for s trengthening the 
manpower availability by instituting an independent study so that recruitment of 
specialist doctors is done on a larger scale to provide adequate health support to the 
patients and to prevent backlog and overcrowding.  
 
3.4.10 The Committee is of the view that while contractual engagement may serve as a 
temporary stop -gap arrangement, it should not substitute regular appointments, as 
long-term reliance on contractual staff may undermine accountability, institutional 
continuity, and morale. Priority should be accorded to filling up of posts directly linked 
to patient care, such as nursing, OT, laboratory, and radiotherapy staff, to ensure that 
expanding infrastructure is matched with adequate human resources. 
 
3.4.11 The Committee further obs erves that faculty members are currently burdened 
with excessive academic, clinical, examination, and research responsibilities due to 
vacant teaching posts, even though the student -teacher ratio may formally meet 
regulatory norms. Given the substantial cl inical workload and implementation of 
multiple national health programmes, the Department should ensure expeditious filling 
of teaching cadre posts and strengthen supportive administrative structures to enable 
faculty to focus on academic excellence and pa tient care. In addition, improved working 
conditions, including adequate accommodation, transport facilities, career progression 
avenues, and structured leadership opportunities for doctors with managerial 
qualifications, may be considered to enhance retention and motivation.  
 
3.4.12 The Committee also believes that duty hours of Junior and Senior Residents 
should be rationalised and strictly regulated to prevent fatigue -related clinical errors, 
with a mechanism ensuring mandatory supervision by senior fac ulty during prescribed 
working hours. To partially mitigate human resource constraints and improve 

54 
 
efficiency, the Department may promote adoption of AI and ICT -based hospital 
management systems, coupled with structured training for staff.  
 
3.4.13 The Com mittee recognises the rising burden of mental health concerns, and 
recommends for promotion of institutional wellness initiatives including yoga and 
elements of Indian Knowledge Systems among students and healthcare workers. The 
committee notes with concer n that despite availability of a large number of applicants 
for Senior Residency in LHMC, the intake is low, resulting in long working hours and 
delays for patients.

3.5.1 The Committee, upon examination of the Demands for Grants, observes that in FY 
2024–25 the Budget Estimate (BE) of ₹180 crore was scaled down to ₹155.85 crore at the 
Revised Estimate (RE) stage. Notably, the Capital outlay witnessed a sharp reduction of 50.75 
per cent, declining from ₹7.94 crore at the BE stage to ₹3.91 crore at the RE stage. Although 
the position appears to have improved in FY 2025 –26, with the Capital outlay enhanced from 
₹8.50 crore at BE to ₹ 14.40 crore at RE, the actual utilisation remains unsatisfactory, as only 
₹2.74 crore has been expended under the Capital head as on 12.02.2026.   
 
3.5.2 On a query on implementation status of ongoing/new projects at KSCH, the 
Department stated that at present, no project of the Hospital is under construction, however , a 
new project i.e. up -gradation of KSCH from 394 beds to 894 beds is in planning stage at an 
estimated cost of ₹ 496.94 Cr. Expression of Interest (EOI) for selection of Project 
Monitoring Agency has also been floated for this project.   The selection of Agency for the 
project is in final stages. For this project, Revised Estimate is ₹ 10.00 Cr for the budget head 
‗Building & Structure‘ in the current financial year for payment of mobilization advance.  For 
the next financial year 2026 -27, ₹ 20.00 Cr have been requested. For the project for 
upgradation of KSCH, additional funds will be required at a later stage as per the progress of 
the project. 
 
3.5.3 On a query related to capital fund utiliza tion and tender on GeM, the Department 
provided that In past three financial years around 30 GeM tenders (including bids for 
Ventilators/ CPAP/ different types of Scopes for Surgery/ Bronchoscopes/ Gastro Video 
Endoscope/ Vital Sign Monitors/ High Flow Nas al Oxygen Therapy Units/ Ultrasound 
Machine etc.) have been cancelled due to lack of competition or other administrative 
reasons. With a view to avoid lack of competition and to increase the number of bidders, 

55 
 
committees, having external experts from other  Hospitals, have been formed to generalize the 
technical specifications, representations have been invited by holding pre -bid meetings and 
accordingly changes have been made to the technical specifications. 
  
3.5.4 The Department further provided that CPWD had assessed the condition of KSCH old 
building and as per Final Report received, CPWD informed  that the old building is in 
distressed condition and beyond repair.  Hence, the CPWD has requested to vacate the old 
KSCH building at the earliest.  It was decided that old KSCH building will shift to the vacant 
space allotted in the old building of SSKH of LHMC. The building was lying vacant and 
renovation by CPWD was required.  Accordingly, KSCH Wards and other Departments have 
been vacating and shifting in the o ld SSKH building in a phase -wise manner after renovation 
by CPWD as per requirement of concerned Units/ Departments.  The following Key Concern 
areas have been reported: - 
 
i. The existing OPD area is small to cater to the daily patient footfall, resulting in 
overcrowding. 
ii. Due to high admission rates, indoor wards often function with double occupancy of 
beds, exceeding optimal capacity. 
iii. Human Resource (like Nursing personnel, OT technicians, other support staff etc.) 
strength needs to be increased to cater to s ignificant rise in patient numbers and 
maintain an adequate patient-nurse ratio. 
iv. Expansion of sub -specialty services over the past decade has resulted in more 
intensive care needs compared to general paediatric care. 
v. CTVS and neurosurgical services are to be provided. 
vi. Need for separate 24x7 emergency operation theatres and critical care units 
  
Strategic Measures Undertaken / Proposed 
i. Infrastructure Expansion : A proposal for hospital expansion is already under 
implementation, and availability of space and i nfrastructure is expected to improve 
progressively over the next few years. 
ii. Patient Streamlining and Referral Optimization : Development and implementation of 
standard operating procedures (SOPs)/Guidelines for referring patients from primary 
and secondary health care facilities would reduce overcrowding by diverting patients 
with minor ailments to appropriate levels of care, allowing tertiary services to focus on 
patients requiring intensive and specialized management. 
iii. Strengthening Human Resources like Nursing staff, OT technicians  
iv. Improvement in infrastructure and deputation/posting of CTVS/Neurosurgery 
specialists in the hospital. 
Performance Indicators for Monitoring 
i. Bed occupancy rate and admission-to-bed availability ratio. 
ii. OPD footfall trends (daily, weekly, monthly). 
iii. Annual and monthly service statistics. 
iv. Mortality and morbidity reviews. 
v. Hospital-acquired infection (HAI) surveillance  
vi. Periodic clinical audits and review meetings. 

56 
 
 
3.5.5 The Committee expresses with concern the recurring pattern of do wnward 
revision and under -utilisation of funds in Kalawati Saran Children‘s Hospital (KSCH), 
particularly under the Capital head. In FY 2024 –25, the Budget Estimate (BE) of ₹180 
crore was reduced to ₹155.85 crore at the Revised Estimate (RE) stage, with Capital 
outlay declining sharply by over 50  per cent from ₹7.94 crore to ₹3.91 crore. Although 
the allocation improved in FY 2025 –26, with Capital outlay increasing from ₹8.50 crore 
at BE to ₹14.40 crore at RE, the actual utilisation remained significantly low at only 
₹2.74 crore as on 12.02.2026. The Committee is anguished to find notes that nearly 30 
tenders on the Government e -Marketplace (GeM) had to be cancelled in the past three 
financial years due to lack of competition and administrative constraints, delaying 
procurement of essential medical  equipment. In view of these trends, the Committee 
recommends that the Department strengthen procurement planning and tendering 
processes through wider vendor outreach, standardisation of technical specifications, 
and improved pre -bid consultations to ensu re greater participation and timely 
finalisation of tenders. 
 
3.5.6 The Committee also emphasises the need for a robust monitoring mechanism to 
ensure that capital allocations are translated into timely procurement of essential 
equipment and infrastructure , thereby improving service delivery in the premier 
paediatric institution. 
 
3.5.7 The Committee further observes that KSCH is functioning under considerable 
infrastructural and operational stress, with the existing OPD facilities being inadequate 
to manage the daily patient load and indoor wards frequently operating beyond optimal 
bed capacity. The Committee also notes with concern the finding of the Central Public 
Works Department (CPWD) that the old KSCH building is in a dilapidated  condition 
and beyond  repair, necessitating the phased shifting of wards and departments to the 
renovated premises of the old SSKH building of LHMC, which may pose transitional 
challenges in maintaining uninterrupted patient care. The Committee takes note of the 
Department‘s p roposal for upgradation of KSCH from 394 beds to 894 beds at an 
estimated cost of ₹496.94 crore, which is currently at the planning stage. The Committee 
recommends that the project be accorded priority and implemented in a time -bound 
manner with adequate budgetary provision in subsequent years to avoid delays and cost 
escalations.  
 
3.5.8 The Committee takes note that the expansion of sub -specialty services over the 
past decade has significantly increased the demand for intensive and specialised 
paediatric care, while the existing human resource strength and critical care 
infrastructure remain inadequate. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the 
Department prioritise strengthening of human resources, particularly nursing 
personnel, OT technicians, and o ther essential support staff, to maintain appropriate 
patient–nurse ratios and ensure safe clinical practices. Simultaneously, steps must be 
taken to operationalise specialised services such as cardiothoracic and vascular surgery 
(CTVS) and neurosurgery, a long with the establishment of dedicated 24×7 emergency 

57 
 
operation theatres and critical care units. The Committee further recommends 
implementation of effective referral protocols and patient -streamlining mechanisms to 
manage overcrowding, alongside contin uous monitoring of key performance indicators 
such as bed occupancy, OPD trends, hospital -acquired infection surveillance, and 
regular clinical audits to ensure improved quality of paediatric healthcare services.  The 
Committee strongly recommends for addit ional neonatal facility so that specialised 
neonatal care is extended on a larger scale. The Committee also recommends for 
introduction of fellowship programs in collaboration with established bodies like the 
National Neonatology Forum (NNF) which is a ove r four decade old leading body with 
over ten thousand Neonatologists with global expertise. This fellowship program would 
help to impart the requisite skills on a larger scale to the post graduate doctors 
specialising in Paediatrics.  
 
3.5.9 The details su bmitted by the Department about post -wise and cadre -wise sanctioned 
strength vis -à-vis in -position strength has been examined and finds that there is 100% 
vacancy for two posts of Paediatrician (non -teaching specialist), five posts of Nursing 
Superintendent, and two posts of Prosthotist & Orthotist. Futher, 10 vacant posts out of total 
16 sanctioned post in Operation Theatre Cadre. 10 posts of Nursing Officers are on 
contractual basis. Further, for filling up of vacant posts, vacancies of institution are ti mely 
reported to concerned agencies for advertisement to UPSC, SSC, AIIMS etc.  
 
The details of posts laying  vacant due to  pending Recruitment Rule amendments are as 
under:

3.5.10 The Committee observes with concern that several critical posts in KSCH remain 
vacant, including 100 per cent vacancies in the posts of Paediatrician (Non -teaching 
Specialist), Nursing Superintendent, and Prosthetist & Orthotist, besides significant 
shortages in the Operation Theatre cadre where 10 out of 16 san ctioned posts are 
vacant. Such large number of vacancy in key clinical and supervisory positions must 

58 
 
have adversely affected the quality of patient care, operational efficiency of surgical 
services, and effective nursing administration. The Committee also  notes that certain 
nursing posts are being filled on a contractual basis, which, though useful as a stop -gap 
arrangement, however such arrangement cannot substitute for a stable and accountable 
regular workforce. While the Department has reported that vac ancies are being 
regularly referred to recruiting agencies such as UPSC, SSC, and AIIMS, the 
Committee recommends that a time -bound recruitment strategy be adopted to fill up 
vacant critical posts on priority, particularly those directly linked to patient care and 
hospital management. Simultaneously, the Committee urges the Department to consider 
improving working conditions and career progression opportunities for doctors and 
healthcare professionals, including lucrative salary, adequate accommodation, tra nsport 
facilities, and leadership pathways for professionals with managerial expertise, in order 
to attract and retain skilled personnel in public health institutions.  The Committee 
reiterates that contractual employees should either be regularized or that  regular 
recruitment be undertaken on a priority basis to provide specialized healthcare to 
children, considering the critical nature of health services and the level of accountability 
involved in such services. 
3.5.11 The Committee further notes that a nu mber of posts remain vacant due to 
pending framing or amendment of Recruitment Rules, including posts such as Nursing 
Superintendent, Assistant Nursing Superintendent, Administrative Officer, Clinical 
Psychologist, Physiotherapists, and technical superviso ry positions. Such procedural 
delays in finalising Recruitment Rules create structural bottlenecks in the recruitment 
and promotion process, thereby affecting institutional functioning. The Committee 
therefore recommends that the Department must expedite t he finalisation of these 
Recruitment Rules in a mission-mode manner and ensure that Departmental Promotion 
Committees are convened promptly thereafter.  
3.5.12 In addition, the Committee urges the Department to explore greater use of AI 
and ICT-based hospital management and administrative tools to enhance efficiency and 
reduce the administrative burden on clinical staff. Given the increasing stress and 
mental health challenges within the medical community, the Committee emphasises the 
importance of strength ening psychiatric services and promoting wellness initiatives, 
including yoga and elements of Indian Knowledge Systems, for healthcare workers and 
students, thereby fostering a more supportive and resilient institutional environment.

3.6.1 The Pradhan  Mantri Swasthya Suraksha Yojana (PMSSY) was announced in 2003 
with the objectives of correcting regional imbalances in  the availability of affordable/ reliable 
tertiary healthcare services and also to augment facilities for quality medical education in the 
country. PMSSY has two components: - 
(i) Setting up of AIIMS  wherein each New AIIMS  to add State of the art Modular O Ts and 
diagnostics facilities; 15 -20 super specialty departments; 750/960 Bedded Hospital; 100 -125 
UG (MBBS) seats; 60 B.Sc. (Nursing) seats; and Focus on PG education and Research. Total 
22 new AIIMS have been announced for establishment so far under this component.   
(ii) Upgradation of Government Medical College (GMC)/ Institutions. Each  up-gradation 
project would be adding 8 -10 Super Speciality  Departments; Around 15 new PG seats; and 
150-250 beds. 75 Projects have been approved across the country under  component under 
different phases.  Out of  75 Government Medical Colleges  (GMCs) approved for 
upgradation, 71 have been completed. The details of remaining 4 Projects are as below:  
Table – 3.6.2

(as on 31.01.2026)

3.6.2 On a query asking for the reasons of the consistent trend of reduction of allocated 
funds at RE stage, the Depa rtment submitted that d uring consideration of BE 2023 -24, the 
Ministry expected that construction of new AIIMS  at Darbhanga, Bihar, Madurai, Tamil 
Nadu and Rewari, Haryana  to start in 2023 -24. However, due to non -provision of 
encumbrance free site, by the State Government, the work of  AIIMS Darbhanga could not 
commence. The work of AIIMS Rewari was awarded in January, 2024 and AIIMS Madurai 
awarded in March 2024 i.e towards the end of the financial year. Since for large infrastructure 
projects mobilisation of men and machine takes time only nominal expenditure could not be 
booked in 2023-24 for the two projects.  
3.6.3 In 2024 -25 the civil works of AIIMS Madurai and AIIMS Rewari took off in full 
swing only after mid of 2024 -25 due to the time taken in obtain ing requisite statutory 
clearances including environmental clearance from the State Govt. The State Govt. of Bihar 
has handed over 187.44 acre land in September 2024 and after necessary consultation with 
IIT Deli (in view of the site conditions) pre investment works started. The reduction under the 
Capital head of PMSSY in 2025 -26 has been made at the RE stage as the revised project cost 
of AIIMS Darbhnaga and AIIMS Awantipora are under process for the approval of the  

60 
 
Cabinet and enhanced expenditure under  the projects is expected to be made  in the next 
financial year ( 2026-27) after obtaining approval of the Cabinet.

as follows: - 
a) Award of the main civil works of AIIMS Darbhanga project and start of on 
ground. 
b) Completion of AIIMS Awantipora. 
c) Completion of AIIMS Bibinagar. 
d) Completion of Satellite Centre of AIIMS Rishikesh at Udham Si ngh Nagar, 
Uttarakhand. 
e) Starting of OPD facilities and teaching/learning in AIIMS Rewari.  
f) Starting upgradation work of GTB Hospital, Delhi and Super Specialty Block in 
GMC Keonjhar in Odisha.  
g) Completion of upgradation of PMCH, Patna and GMC, Surat.  
h) Procurement and timely supply of medical equipment to the new AIIMS to be 
ensured. 
i) Completion of balance works in AIIMS Kalyani, AIIMS Jammu, AIIMS Rajkot,

courses in new AIIMS. 
k) Increasing healthcare services, OPD/IPD, teaching learning and research & 
innovation in new AIIMS.

26. 
 
3.6.7 On a query about the Key Concern Areas of functioning of these AIIMS like Institutes 
under PMSSY, the Department made submission on challenges being faced that  AIIMS are 
very lar ge and complex social infrastructure projects. The challenges faced in project 

62 
 
implementation include: provision of encumbrance free land by the State Govt, time taken in 
obtaining Statutory Clearances including Environmental Clearance, site related hindra nces 
 both natural and manmade including removal of HT Lines , IOC Pipe line passing through 
the land in Madurai,  natural formations like nallah, rock outcrops etc., timely provision of 
supply potable water, electricity, road connectivity including  road overbridge, construction of 
subway to join two land patches across the road etc, land acquisition related issues, 
encroachment/land disputes, limited availability of labour and harsh weather conditions in 
remote locations like Awantipora, creation of posts  for new AIIMS, recruitment of Faculty 
and Technicians –issues in retaining faculty and staff in remote locations , issues relating to 
procurement of medical equipment  particularly single tenders in case of propriety items and 
time taken  in import of high end equipment, delay in signing of MoU by the State Govt etc. 
3.6.8 The Department further stated the measures/strategy  in place / taken to combat these 
challenges as under: 
i. A Project Review Committees under the chairpersonship of Joint Secretary (PMSSY)  
has been set for each ongoing project of setting up of AIIMS. Further, progress of 
projects is reviewed regularly in the Ministry at various levels with the Executing 
Agencies and Procurement Support Agency to ensure timely completion of the 
project. 
ii. The Project Monitoring Group (PMG), headed by Secretary (Coordination) in the 
Cabinet Secretariat, supports all mid and large sized Public, Private and ‗Public – 
Private Partnership‘ (PPP) Projects with fast -tracking of approvals, sectoral policy 
issues and r emoval of bottlenecks for expediting project commissioning. PMG helps 
in resolving issues in projects through regular coordination with the concerned State 
Governments. 
iii. Through Pro-Active Governance and Timely Implementation (PRAGATI) portal, the 
Hon‘ble Prime Minister reviews the issues. The PRAGATI portal ensures quick and 
effective decision making for swift resolution. 
iv. In addition to the aforesaid structural institutional arrangements, the issues affecting 
progress of the projects are also taken up with the State Government through letters 
from Secretary (HFW) to the Chief Secretary of the concerned State Government. 
Need-based directions are also given to Institutes/Executive Agencies/PSA for 
expeditious resolution of the issues between them.  
v. Further, site visits by senior officers of the Ministry and Hon‘ble Minister of Health 
and Family Welfare are also being undertaken to monitor the progress. 
vi. On the efforts being made to fill up posts, the Department stated that creation  of posts 
and recruitment of f aculty and non -faculty against vacant sanctioned posts is a 
continuous process.  The recruitment process in various new AIIMS is monitored at 
Ministry level on regular basis. During the year 2025, the approval was accorded for 
the creation of 4192 posts ac ross various new AIIMS, comprising 397 faculty posts, 

63 
 
936 Senior Resident posts, 911 Junior Resident posts, 824 Nursing Cadre posts and 
1124 other non-faculty posts. In order to expeditiously fill vacant sanctioned positions 
in various AIIMS, various steps have been taken: 
a) Standing Selection Committee has been constituted in each AIIMS as per the 
provision of the AIIMS Act to facilitate expeditious filling up of vacancies of 
Faculty. Further, instructions have been issued recently to conduct meeting 
of Standing Selection Committee at least four times in a year. 
b) Provision has been made for engagement of retired faculty at the level of 
Professor, Additional Professor and Associate Professor, on contract basis, up to 
the age of 70 years from Institutes of Natio nal Importance (INIs) and Government 
Medical Colleges (GMCs). 
c) Visiting Faculty Scheme is being implemented to allow Professors, Additional 
Professors and Associate Professors from Government institutions in India or 
academic institutions abroad to serve as  visiting faculty in AIIMS for teaching 
purposes.  
d) Centralized Nursing Officer Recruitment Common Eligibility Test (NORCET) for 
recruitment of nursing officers for various AIIMS, is conducted by AIIMS Delhi 
twice a year.  
e) For recruitment of Group B and Gro up C Non -faculty posts across various new 
AIIMS Common Recruitment Examination (CRE) has been conducted by AIIMS 
Delhi twice in 2025.  
f) Institute of National Importance Combined Entrance Test (INI -CET) for selection 
of Junior Resident (Academic) and Institu te of National Importance Super 
Speciality (INI -SS) exam for selection of Senior Residents (Academic) are 
conducted by AIIMS Delhi twice a year.
