3.6.10 The Committee e xpresses concern that allocations under the Pradhan Mantri 
Swasthya Suraksha Yojana (PMSSY) have witnessed a recurring pattern of reduction at 
the Revised Estimate stage, particularly under the Capital head, with the allocation 
declining from ₹2140 crore a t BE 2024 –25 to ₹1666 crore at RE stage and again from 
₹2140 crore at BE 2025 –26 to ₹1430 crore at RE stage. The Committee further observes 
that such reductions are largely attributable to delays in commencement and execution 
of major infrastructure projects, including AIIMS at Darbhanga, Madurai and Rewari, 
due to arising from issues such as non -availability of encumbrance -free land, delays in 
statutory and environmental clearances, and site -related constraints. The Committee, 
therefore, strongly recommend s that the Department must evolve a more robust pre -

65 
 
project preparedness framework in close coordination with State Governments to 
ensure that key prerequisites such as land availability, statutory approvals and basic 
infrastructure facilities are secured prior to budgetary provision. This would help align 
financial allocations with actual implementation capacity.  The Committee notes with 
concern that Regional AIIMS receive only a fraction of the allocation provided to 
AIIMS Delhi, yet they are expected to run all programmes on the lines of AIIMS Delhi 
despite significant budgetary constraints. The Committee recommends that a proper 
analysis be undertaken to ensure that the Regional AIIMS do not suffer due to 
inadequate budgetary allocations. 
 
3.6.11 The Com mittee observes that while the PMSSY has significantly expanded 
tertiary healthcare infrastructure in the country, with Six AIIMS approved in Phase I 
fully functional with all key hospital facilities and services; 12  AIIMS with either 
construction work com pleted (9) or major part of construction work completed (3) and 
teaching learning & research, IPD/OPD services operationalised; and several 
Government Medical College upgradation projects completed, however, a some projects 
remain pending or are progressin g slowly. In particular, AIIMS projects at Madurai, 
Awantipora, Bibinagar and Rewari are still under construction, while AIIMS 
Darbhanga is yet to commence the main civil works. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends that the Department must establish a clea rly defined time -bound 
implementation framework with periodic milestone -based monitoring for all ongoing 
projects so that the remaining AIIMS and upgradation works are completed within the 
stipulated timelines. The Committee also emphasises the need for st ronger coordination 
with executing agencies and State Governments to ensure that infrastructural and 
administrative bottlenecks do not delay the operationalisation of these institutions 
intended to improve access to tertiary healthcare and medical education. 
 
3.6.12 The Committee  takes note with satisfaction that although significant progress 
has been made under the upgradation component of PMSSY, with 71 out of 75 
Government Medical College projects completed, a few projects are still pending due to 
factors such as relocation of sites, delays in preparation of Detailed Project Reports and 
administrative approvals. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department 
should expedite the completion of the remaining projects, including those at Patna 
Medical College & Hospital, Government Medical College Surat, the proposed Super 
Speciality Block at Government Medical College Keonjhar in Odisha and the 
upgradation project at UCMS -GTB Hospital, Delhi. The Committee further 
recommends that greater attention be paid to timely preparation and approval of DPRs 
and early finalisation of executing agencies to avoid procedural delays in the future. 
 
3.6.13 The Committee is concerned to note the significant shortage of human resources 
across AIIMS institutions, with  faculty vacancies of about 37.75 per cent and non -
faculty vacancies of about 31.52 per cent across both established and new AIIMS. The 
Committee emphasises that such large vacancies may undermine the effective utilisation 
of the extensive infrastructure created under PMSSY and adversely affect the quality of 
healthcare services, medical education and research. The Committee, therefore, 

66 
 
recommends that the Department take time -bound measures to fill up these vacancies 
through accelerated recruitment process es, strengthening of existing mechanisms such 
as the Standing Selection Committees, NORCET and Common Recruitment 
Examinations, and by providing improved working conditions, competitive  pay scale 
career progression opportunities and adequate residential a nd institutional facilities to 
attract and retain qualified medical professionals, particularly in relatively remote 
locations. 
 
3.6.14 The Committee takes into account the fact that retention of faculty and 
healthcare personnel in newly established AIIMS,  particularly those located in remote 
or less developed regions, remains a persistent challenge. The Committee, in this regard, 
recommends that the Department must formulate a comprehensive human resource 
retention strategy for such institutions, including  incentives for serving in difficult 
locations, improved residential and transport facilities, enhanced academic and research 
opportunities, and appropriate career progression pathways. The Committee also 
emphasises the importance of reducing excessive dep endence on contractual staff and 
ensuring that regular appointments are prioritised so as to maintain institutional 
continuity, accountability and a stronger sense of belonging among the workforce. 
 
3.6.15 The Committee wishes to highlight that procurement  and timely installation of 
advanced medical equipment remains a challenge in several AIIMS due to issues such as 
single tenders, proprietary items and delays in import of specialised equipment. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department shou ld develop a streamlined 
and standardised procurement framework with advance planning and coordinated 
procurement support so that equipment is installed in a timely manner and the newly 
established AIIMS are able to deliver their full range of clinical, di agnostic and teaching 
services at the earliest. The Committee also recommends that greater use of digital 
technologies, artificial intelligence and ICT -based administrative tools must be 
promoted within these institutions to improve efficiency in hospital management, 
monitoring and research activities, while ensuring adequate training of staff for their 
responsible and effective use.  The Committee is given to understand that centralized 
procurement by HLL/HITES has, in several instances, resulted in the pro curement of 
inferior-quality equipment, which had to be returned or replaced. The Committee 
recommends strong oversight of the procurement process and the functioning of 
HLL/HITES to ensure that substandard critical healthcare equipment is not procured. 
 
3.6.16 On a specific query, the best practices being following by different AIIMS has been 
furnished by the Department. The First Edition of the Best practices Conclave of all new 
AIIMS was organized in AIIMS Nagpur on 13th and 14th June 2025 on the theme o f 
Improving Teaching Learning; Patient Care and Governance. In the minutes of the 
Conference the best practices listed are as follows: 
 
a) Hospital Administration & Patient Services (AIIMS Nagpur): Laundry Management & 
Patient Dietary Services on OPEX, outsourcing of HMIS-integrated laundry with color-

67 
 
coded linen, outsourcing of customised dietary services, provisioning of night -shelter 
with NGO-supported meals. 
b) Online Emergency Triage System (AIIMS Raipur): UNICEF -supported Online Triage 
System, enabling of paperless ER leading to attending 90% patient within 24 hours and 
improving public confidence, iECS AI model for STEMI diagnosis (>80% accuracy),  
c) System for AIIMS Human Resources & Accounting services (SAHAS) (AIIMS New 
Delhi): Integrated HR, finance, procurement and auditing system with unique employee 
codes leading to reduced paper usage and improved transparency (Pilot launched April 
2024 with C -DAC collaboration), SANTUSHT Patient Feedback Portal providing for 
QR-based grievance and feedback submission using UHID/mobile and real -time 
tracking and routing to responsible office 
d) Electronic Scrap Disposal (AIIMS Patna): Adoption of transparent e -auction disposal 
of e-waste leading to revenue generation as well as better environmental and infection 
control compliances. 
e) Inventory Lifecycle Management (AIIMS Bhopal): Having features like multiple 
procurement lanes and dedicated demand committees, bi -weekly maintenance review, 
clear categorization for repair/condemnation and improved pricing and cleaner campus. 
f) Cadaver Bone Preservation Protocol (AIIMS Jodhpur): Putting in place six -stage cost-
effective protocol preserving dignity and morphology helped in addressing cadaver 
shortages and supports teaching. 
g) Lifestyle Intervention Clinic (AIIMS Nagpur): Evidence-based counselling for lifestyle 
diseases led to 2,600 consultations with positive outcomes. 
h) Andrology Laboratory (AIIMS Nagpur): Male infertility diagnostics and treatment 
support, specialized training. 
i) Online Post -Mortem Report Tracking (AIIMS Nagpur): Websi te tab to track PM 
reports for police/relatives led to improved transparency and public satisfaction. 
j) Curriculum Governance Model (AIIMS Nagpur): Academic audit committees 
constituted and structured calendar put in place led to improved NEET/NEXT 
preferences, new courses (e.g., MCh Vitreoretinal Surgery, PDCC Lifestyle 
Modification). 
k) Tribal Health Projects (AIIMS Nagpur): ICMR community interventions increased 
screenings and awareness, DBT NIDAN Kendra for genetic/metabolic screening. 
l) Anaesthesia Informatio n Management System (AIIMS Jodhpur): Indigenous digital 
system integrating OT/ICU with 100% digital pre -anaesthetic check -ups, AI -
integration and cost-effective. 
m) Indoor Patient Navigation App (AIIMS Jammu): Multilingual mobile navigation with 
real-time tracking and display for waiting times, stretcher availability etc. 
n) Hospital Mentorship Yojana (AIIMS Deoghar): Mentoring state colleges/district 
hospitals, conducting workshops, facility sharing, reporting to state govt. 
o) Community Hands-Only CPR Programme (A IIMS Deoghar): 33,300+ people trained; 
made mandatory for MBBS students. 
p) Health & Demographic Surveillance System (AIIMS Gorakhpur): Covers 73,000 
population with GIS mapping, tracks MCH, NCDs, disease patterns, enables 
teleconsultation and real-time monitoring. 

68 
 
q) Incident Reporting System (AIIMS Guwahati): Mandatory reporting of adverse events 
within 2 hours and root-cause analysis of sentinel events. 
r) Pediatric Play & Art Therapy (AIIMS Patna): Child -friendly oncology/pediatric wards 
leading to better treatment response, reduced sedation. 
s) Digital Library (AIIMS Jammu): Two -floor smart library for 500 students, RFID 
books, e-resources, discussion rooms, children‘s corner. 
t) Early Neurodevelopmental Centre (AIIMS Jodhpur): Transdisciplinary care for autism, 
CP, genetic disorders. 
u) Centre of Excellence for Tribal Health (AIIMS Jodhpur): Hub -and-spoke model with 
telemedicine and outreach, drone-supported delivery and anaemia screening, Residents 
posted in tribal areas. 
v) Drone Transport of Radioactive Materials (AIIMS Bilaspur): Proposed cost -effective 
transport for nuclear medicine supplies by collaborating with AERB & IIT Mandi. 
   
3.6.17 On being asked w hether AIIMS Manglagiri has set up a high bench -mark in terms of 
overall cleanliness, OPD patient satisfaction and OPD including anti-microbial resistance, the 
Department stated that AIIMS Mangalagiri is performing well. To improve OPD patient 
satisfaction, the Institute has taken a number of steps viz., on -line OPD Registration, help -
desks and a dedicated helpline num ber, use of HMIS and proper signages to reduce the ques 
and better crowd management. Ramps and wheel -chair facilities are available for differently -
abled patients.  The Institute has been ensuring availability of clean drinking water, 
washrooms, clean toil ets and functional lifts. Directional sign -boards and adequate 
resting/accommodation facilities to the attendants of the patients are also being provided. 
Proper grievance redressal mechanism has been placing by the Institute. Overall, AIIMS 
Mangalagiri is providing accessible and affordable tertiary healthcare services while ensuring 
cleanliness in the hospital campus. Further, to promote high degree of cleanliness, hygiene 
and infection -control the MoHFW has evolved Kayakalp Scheme for the Tertiary Care 
Hospitals including new AIIMS. The hospitals which show exemplary performance in 
adhering to standard protocols of cleanliness and sanitation, are incentivized under the 
scheme.  
 
3.6.18 On being asked whether other regional AIIMS would be graded in compari son to each 
other in different parameters so that regular high standards are maintained in all regional 
AIIMS, the Department submitted that new AIIMS are at various stages of operationalization 
and need to be supported to attain full functionality. Any gr ading of regional AIIMS at this 
juncture appears premature.  
 
3.6.19 On being asked whether the Government has studied the model of Community Health 
Centre in Ghanerao, Rajasthan, recognized as the Number One CHC in terms of upkeep and 
cleanliness in the e ntire state of Rajasthan and as a mark of encouraging Community health 
participation, the Department stated that any healthcare facility meeting the certification 
norms as per the laid criteria by Central Quality Supervisory Committee (CQSC) can apply 
for quality certification under NQAS. However, as per our SaQsham database, NQAS 
application for CHC Ghanerao has not been received.  The Committee notes with concern 
the evasive response of the Department regarding a Community Health Centre (CHC) 

69 
 
that has esta blished high standards of cleanliness/treatment through a PPP model. 
Instead of encouraging such initiatives as role models, the Department has mentioned 
another procedural step, despite the fact that the CHC is not seeking any formal 
validation. The Commi ttee recommends for a study of the referred CHC as a best 
practice in Rajasthan and for issuing advisories to other State Governments to 
encourage adoption of similar models in more CHCs so as to engage larger society to 
enable it to contribute towards strengthening of the PHCs/CHCs. 
 
3.6.20 On a specific query, the Department further submitted that t here is no uniform or 
centrally notified mechanism under the Government of India for declaring any health 
institution as an Excellent Health Care Centre across  all specialties. Recognition of 
institutions as Centres of Excellence, where applicable, is programme -specific and considered 
within the framework of individual National Health Programmes based on prescribed 
technical and operational criteria. Institutions undertaking notable work may submit proposals 
through the respective State Governments/UT Administrations to the concerned National 
Programme Divisions. Such proposals are examined on the basis of infrastructure, human 
resources, service delivery capacity, training and research capabilities, accreditation status and 
public health impact, before any programme -specific recognition is accorded. There is no 
existing mechanism of declaring any privately owned centre of health as excellent. Only 
government inst itution with specific criteria are given the status of Regional Institute of 
Ophthalmology (RIO) under NPCBVI. However private institutions doing extraordinary work 
in eye care are partnering with NPCBVI by signing MoUs with states to provide eye care 
services in lieu of grant in aid being provided to them as per NPCBVI norms. They are also 
being recognized as training centres after due examination by committee and are also 
providing training to government eye surgeons in various sub specialties of ophthalm ology. 
Shankara Nethralaya and Aravind Eye Care System are non -governmental organisations 
empanelled with State Governments and programme authorities for delivery of eye care 
services. However, they have not been formally declared as Centres of Excellence by the 
Government of India.  
 
3.6.21 Further, Neonatology is not implemented as a standalone national programme. 
Newborn health interventions are addressed under the National Health Mission through 
establishment of Sick Newborn Care Units (SNCUs), Newborn Stabilization Units (NBSUs) 
and related initiatives. No separate declaration of Centres of Excellence in Neonatology has 
been notified under a distinct scheme. 
 
3.6.22 The Committee appreciates that several innovative and impactful best practices 
have been  developed across different AIIMS institutions in areas such as hospital 
administration, digital governance, patient care, community outreach and academic 
management. These include initiatives such as the Online Emergency Triage System at 
AIIMS Raipur, the SAHAS integrated administrative system and SANTUSHT feedback 
portal at AIIMS New Delhi, digital and AI -enabled systems in clinical services, 
community health programmes, and innovative patient support services. The 
Committee recommends that the Department  systematically document, evaluate and 
institutionalise these best practices and develop a formal framework for their 

70 
 
dissemination and replication across all AIIMS and other major public healthcare 
institutions. Establishing a structured knowledge -sharing mechanism and periodic 
conclaves would enable cross -learning among institutions and ensure that successful 
innovations are adopted more widely to enhance efficiency, transparency and quality of 
patient care. 
 
3.6.23 The Committee observes that many of the  best practices implemented by 
individual AIIMS institutions are leveraging digital technologies, artificial intelligence, 
health information management systems and mobile applications to improve hospital 
administration, patient services, emergency care, a cademic governance and grievance 
redressal. The Committee recommends that the Department formulate a comprehensive 
digital transformation strategy for all AIIMS and similar tertiary care institutions, with 
a view to integrating hospital management systems,  digital feedback platforms, clinical 
decision-support tools and real -time monitoring systems across the network. Such an 
integrated approach would not only strengthen governance and administrative 
efficiency but also improve patient experience, transparen cy and accountability in 
service delivery across these premier institutions. 
 
3.6.24 The Committee further notes that while several AIIMS have demonstrated 
notable achievements in areas such as hospital cleanliness, patient satisfaction, infection 
control and innovative community outreach initiatives, there is currently no uniform 
mechanism to benchmark or evaluate the performance of AIIMS institutions across key 
parameters. While recognising that many new AIIMS are still in the process of attaining 
full fu nctionality, the Committee recommends that the Department gradually evolve a 
transparent and objective performance assessment framework based on measurable 
indicators such as quality of patient care, infrastructure utilisation, academic output, 
research contribution, cleanliness standards, patient satisfaction and community health 
impact. Such a benchmarking mechanism, when implemented in a phased and 
supportive manner, would encourage healthy competition, promote accountability and 
help maintain uniformly high standards across all AIIMS institutions in the country. 
 
3.6.25 The Committee finds that while several healthcare institutions across the country 
demonstrate exemplary performance in areas such as cleanliness, patient care, 
specialised services and co mmunity outreach, there is presently no uniform or centrally 
notified mechanism under the Government of India for recognising health institutions as 
Centres of Excellence across all specialties. The Committee further observes that 
recognition of exemplary performance of institutions is presently programme -specific 
and largely confined to particular national health programmes. In this context, the 
Committee is of the view that establishing a structured framework for identifying and 
recognising outstanding he althcare institutions would encourage innovation, healthy 
competition and the adoption of best practices across the healthcare system. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department should explore the feasibility of 
developing a broad and transparent framework for recognition of Centres of Excellence 
in different areas of healthcare based on clearly defined parameters such as quality of 

71 
 
infrastructure, affordability and accessibility of quality healthcare services, service 
delivery standards, research output, training capacity and public health impact.  
 
3.6.26 The Committee observes that newborn health interventions in the country are 
presently implemented under the broader framework of the National Health Mission 
through facilities such as Sick N ewborn Care Units (SNCUs) and Newborn Stabilization 
Units (NBSUs), and that there is no standalone national programme or dedicated 
Centres of Excellence in the field of Neonatology. Considering the increasing importance 
of specialised neonatal care in redu cing infant mortality and addressing complications 
related to premature births, congenital disorders and high -risk deliveries, the 
Committee is of the  considered view that focused institutional strengthening in this 
domain is the need of hour. The Committ ee, therefore, recommends that the 
Department should examine the feasibility of developing a more structured approach 
for strengthening neonatal care services, including the identification and development of 
select tertiary care institutions, developing pu blic-private partnership models, and 
promoting such institutions as specialised referral and training hubs for advanced 
neonatal care, research and capacity building. The Committee recommends for 
introduction of advanced fellowship programs in neonatal car e besides specialised 
courses like DM etc.  
 
3.6.27 The Committee also notes that neonatal mortality can be significantly reduced by 
strengthening the capacity of local doctors, nurses and frontline health workers through 
targeted training in essential new born care, neonatal resuscitation, early identification 
of complications and timely referral. Continuous skill development of healthcare 
providers, particularly at peripheral health facilities, would ensure prompt and effective 
management of neonatal conditions and improve survival and quality of newborn care.  
 
3.7 NATIONAL AIDS AND STD CONTROL PROGRAMME (NACP)

3.7.1 Initiated in 1992 by the Ministry of Health & Family We lfare (MoHFW) to reduce 
HIV infections and reduce associated morbidity and mortality, the National AIDS and STD 
Control Programme (NACP) is currently in its fifth phase being implemented as a full -funded 
Central Sector Scheme. The national response has bee n augmented by landmark policies in 
last decade, including the HIV & AIDS (Prevention and Control) Act, 2017, the 'Test and 
Treat' strategy, and Universal Viral Load Monitoring.  
3.7.2 The programme goal is to attain Sustainable Development Goal (SDG) 3.3 of ending 
of AIDS epidemic as a public health threat 2030.  Elimination of Vertical Transmission of 

72 
 
HIV and Syphilis is a key component of SDG 3.3. World Health Organization has called for 
attainment of HIV incidence rate of 0.025 per 1000 uninfected population by 2030.     
 
3.7.3 The key strategies duly implemented are:  (A) Prevention including (i) Targeted 
Intervention for High Risk Groups (HRGs) and Bridge population, (ii) Integrated Counseling 
& Testing for HIV at 4571 ICTCs including prevention of Par ent to Child Transmission, (iii) 
Rural outreach through Link Worker Scheme, (iv) Information, Education and 
Communication activities for HRGs, vulnerable groups and general population in States and 
UTs. (B) Providing lifelong free treatment to people livin g with HIV / AIDS (PLHIV) 
through 840 Anti -retroviral Treatment (ART) centres and 1337 linked -ART centres, (C) 
Strategic Information Management, Surveillance of HIV and capacity building of personnel 
engaged under the programme for service delivery and (D)  Strategic Information 
Management.  
 
3.7.4 National Blood Transfusion Council (NBTC) is the policy formulating apex body for 
all matters pertaining to the organisation, operation, standards and training of a sustainable 
and safe blood transfusion service for the country. 
 
3.7.5 The total budget allocation under NACP was ₹2892.00 crore at BE 2024 –25. For FY 
2025–26, the allocation was increased to ₹3442.77 crore at BE stage but revised downward to 
₹2661.50 crore at RE stage, with an expenditure of ₹2540.67 c rore incurred up to 31.01.2026. 
In the BE 2026–27, the total allocation has been pegged at ₹3477.00 crore.  
 
3.7.6 During the year 2026 -27, budget allocation of ₹ 3200.00 crore has been made to 
National AIDS Control organization (NACO) for implementation o f the NACP. The 
appropriation of ₹ 3200.00 crore over the year 2026-27 is given below: 
Table – 3.7.2 
(₹ in Crore)

3.7.7 The Department submitted that the budget allocated for NACO in BE 2026 -27 to the 
tune of ₹3200.00 crore will not be   sufficient if the allocation is not increased at RE stage in 
2026-27. The demand of BE 2026 -27 was ₹ 3386.13 crore,  which has been reduced by ₹ 
186.13 crore. Further, there will be shortfall in FY 2025-26 as the Budget for NACO has been 

73 
 
drastically reduced. This shortfall and the reduction in BE 2026-27 is required to be taken into 
account at RE 2026-27 stage. 
3.7.8 On being asked about the course of action that can further be taken to achieve the 
objectives of the programme, the Department made a submission that the following course of 
action will be taken to achieve the objectives of the programme: 
 (i) Prevention 
a) Extensive awareness campaigns; 
 
b) Sustain focus on adolescent and youth  with a special attention to private 
institutions and the out of school/college youth. 
 
c) Strengthen awareness generation and screening  services in slums, semi-urban, 
peri-urban and rural population. 
 
d) Developing new age communication materials  for at-risk, high-risk and bridge 
populations on prevention. 
 
e) Increase coverage of preventive services to high-risk groups  
 
f) Collaboration with Drug De -Addiction Program (DDAP)  for increasing the 
coverage of Opioid Substitution Therapy (OST) among People Who Inject 
Drugs (PWID) through the existing structures of National Drug Dependence 
Treatment Centres, Regional Drug Dependence Treatment Centres and the 
Drug Treatment Centres.  
 
g) Convergence with Integrat ed Rehabilitation Centres for Addicts (IRCAs)  and 
Addiction Treatment Facilities (ATF). 
 
h) Universalise Sampoorna Suraksha Strategy  at ICTC and DSRCs for 
comprehensive package of preventive services to all At-Risk Clients.  
 
(ii) Care, Support and Treatment Services: Effective care, support and treatment services 
are critical to NACP-V goal of reducing AIDS-related mortalities.  
 
(iii) Capacity Building: Under the NACP, Capacity Building initiatives are regularly 
undertaken to enhance skills & knowledge and p rovide training to all cadres of NACP 
(MO, Lab Technicians, Counselors, DISHA staff, TI staff etc.) to improve service 
delivery at the field level. 
 
(iv)  Strategic Information Management:  
a) SOCH-MIS under NACP to be further strengthened with interoperabili ty with 
other information systems under National Health Mission (NIKSHAY Portal, 
RCH Portal etc) to be strengthened to facilitate comprehensive care. 
 

74 
 
b)  Strengthen case-base surveillance  allowing monitoring individual -level cohort 
data and detect emerging trends early. 
 
c) Evidence generation on newer bio-medical prevention kits and drugs  to strengthen 
the prevention approaches under NACP.     
3.7.9 The Committee expresses the concern that the most pressing risk to achieving the 
Sustainable Development Goal (SD G) 3.3 by 2030 is the low budgetary allocation and 
subsequent mid-year compression. The data reveals a drastic reduction in the Revised 
Estimate (RE) for FY 2025 -26, dropping from ₹3442.77 crore to ₹2661.50 crore, and a 
projected shortfall of ₹186.13 crore against NACO's demand for BE 2026-27. To prevent 
these recurring RE cuts, which are often a by-product of slow fund utilization in the first 
two quarters, the Committee recommends that the Department must implement a 
rigorous, month-on-month expenditure tracking mechanism, particularly for utilization 
of ₹2069.62 crore earmarked for Grants -in-Aid and ₹987.59 crore for Supplies and 
Materials. The Committee recommends that the Department should immediately begin 
compiling empirical data on the expanded Sampo orna Suraksha Strategy and the new 
NMC mandate for Anti-retroviral Treatment (ART)  centers in Medical Colleges to 
build an unassailable, evidence -based case for securing the required supplementary 
funds at the RE 2026-27 stage. 
3.7.10 The Committee believe s that to maximize the impact of the preventive and care 
strategies without proportionally increasing the budget, the Department must 
aggressively pursue structural convergence. The Committee, therefore, recommends 
that the proposed interoperability of the SOCH-MIS with the NIKSHAY (TB) and RCH 
(Reproductive and Child Health) portals should be fast -tracked into a unified, case -
based surveillance dashboard. This will allow for the tracking of individual -level cohort 
data, preventing duplication of efforts an d ensuring holistic care for co -infections. 
Additionally, the Committee is of the view that targeting high -risk populations, 
particularly People Who Inject Drugs (PWID), requires moving beyond isolated health 
initiatives. The Department should formalize a binding, inter -ministerial joint action 
plan with the Ministry of Social Justice and Empowerment (MoSJE) and the Drug De -
Addiction Program (DDAP) to pool resources, co -locate Opioid Substitution Therapy 
(OST) centers, and seamlessly connect vulnerable demo graphics to lifelong ART 
networks. 
3.7.11 The Committee is of the firm opinion that to achieve the ambitious SDG 3.3 
target of reducing the HIV incidence rate to 0.025 per 1000 uninfected population by 
2030, the Department must aggressively decentralize it s screening and prevention 
framework beyond traditional clinical settings. The Committee believes that the 
immediate priority should be the rapid universalization of the Sampoorna Suraksha 
Strategy across all 4,571 ICTCs, transitioning them from passive te sting centers into 
active hubs that offer a comprehensive preventive package for all at -risk clients. 
Because the programmatic focus must capture hard -to-reach demographics, such as 
out-of-school youth, and populations in slums, peri-urban, and rural areas, Information, 
Education, and Communication (IEC) campaigns need to pivot from generic national 

75 
 
broadcasts to hyper -localized, digital outreach involving regional social media 
influencers and community peer leaders. Furthermore, to complement these behavio ral 
interventions, the Committee recommends that the Department must expedite its 
evidence generation and rollout of "newer bio -medical prevention kits" (such as Pre -
Exposure Prophylaxis). The Committee recommends the Department to ensure that the 
cutting-edge biomedical tools are not restricted to urban Centers of Excellence but are 
seamlessly integrated into the grassroots Link Worker Scheme to ensure equitable 
access for the most vulnerable populations.

3.8.1 The COVID-19 Response Mechanism (C19 RM) was a specialized global initiative by 
the Global Fund aimed at supporting countries in their response to the COVID -19 pandemic 
and mitigating its impact. The gran t focuses on activities related to strengthening health 
systems for pandemic preparedness. The implementation period for the grant was till 31 st Dec 
2025 and has been extended for certain procurement and activities until 30th June 2026. 
3.8.2 For B.E. 2026 -27, under the Global Fund grant, Rs 1.91 crores is proposed under 
Revenue head and Rs 94.95 crores is proposed under the Capital head. Under the Covid 19 
Response Mechanism, the Ministry of Health and Family Welfare (MoHFW) was allocated 
a grant of Rs 458 .3 crore by the Global Fund in 2022 -23. The implementation period for the 
grant was till 31st Dec 2025 and has been extended for certain procurement and activities until 
30th June 2026. With the expected utilization of the grant by 2025 -26, only Rs 96.86 c rore is 
expected to be spent in 2026-27. 
3.8.3 The total allocation was ₹125.00 crore at both BE and RE stages in 2024 –25, against 
which an actual expenditure of ₹0.51 crore was incurred. For FY 2025 –26, the allocation was 
significantly increased to ₹355.0 7 crore at both BE and RE stages, with an expenditure of 
₹204.66 crore incurred up to 31.01.2026. On being asked about the reasons for low utilisation 
of funds allocated in the FY 2024 -25, the Department stated that the originally approved 
activities relat ed to Oxygen under the C19RM grant have largely been met by ECRP -2, 
PMCARES and PM -ABHIM. The COVID pandemic also  was at a minimal level with 
negligible requirements for oxygen. Hence it was decided to re -programme the grant proposal 
and make the funds ava ilable for utilization under NHM in October 2023. The re -
programming required a consultation with the State Governments and all relevant 
programme divisions under NHM. The re -programming was completed in December and 
after due approvals of competent author ity and India Country Coordination Mechanism, the 
proposal was submitted to the  Global Fund. After due processes, the  Global 
Fund communicated the approval in March 2024.  Of the total approved amount of Rs 458.3 

76 
 
crores, only 125 crores were proposed in BE  2024-25, considering the procurement lead time 
for the equipment proposed. Due to the lead time in procurement & approval required from 
Global Fund on the technical specifications, tender documents & other processes, the 
expenditure could not be booked within 2024-25 & carry forwarded to 2025-26. 
3.8.4 The Committee notes underutilization of allocated funds in FY 2024 –25, with 
only ₹0.51 crore spent against an allocation of ₹125 crore due to minimal level of 
COVID pandemic. The Committee observes that whil e the re-programming of activities 
was necessary due to overlapping support from ECRP -2, PMCARES, and PM-ABHIM, 
the delay in consultations, approvals, and procurement processes significantly 
compressed the expenditure window. This pattern highlights the ne ed for a more agile 
procurement and utilisation framework for externally funded grants. The Committee, 
desire that the Department should institutionalise a dedicated fast -track mechanism for 
Global Fund -supported programmes, including pre -approved technica l specifications 
and tender templates to reduce lead time, a standing coordination cell with State 
Governments and NHM divisions to expedite re -programming consultations, and 
quarterly utilisation reviews with escalation protocols to ensure improved fund 
absorption. Such measures will also enhance credibility with international partners, 
ensuring India remains a trusted recipient of global health financing. 
3.8.5 The Committee notes that while the allocation under the Global Fund grant was 
significantly enhanced in FY 2025–26 to ₹355.07 crore, with ₹204.66 crore utilised up to 
January 2026, the grant period is scheduled to conclude by June 2026, leaving only 
₹96.86 crore proposed for FY 2026 –27. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the 
Department should  consolidate the C19RM -funded activities into the broader 
framework of the National Health Mission and pandemic preparedness initiatives. This 
should include mapping all equipment and infrastructure procured under the grant to 
NHM facilities for sustained use, aligning residual funds with priority areas such as 
laboratory strengthening, surveillance, and workforce training, and documenting 
lessons from re -programming delays to inform future negotiations with global health 
financing partners. Such measures w ill ensure enduring value by transitioning from a 
pandemic-specific intervention to a systemic strengthening initiative aligned with India‘s 
National Health Policy targets.

3.9.1  The scheme provides for Swastha Nagrik Abhiyan (SNA), Population 
Research Centres, Health Surveys & Research Studies, procurement of Contraceptives for 

77 
 
Social Marketing and Free Distribution, Training of Doctors in No Scalpal Vasectom y 
/Recanalisation Technique etc. There are six components listed in the proposal under the 
Family Welfare Schemes which are crucial for attaining the goals and objectives laid out in 
National Health Policy 2017, and international commitments in the form of  Sustainable 
Development Goals.  
 
i. Mass Education - IEC (Information, Education and Communication) - Swasth Nagarik 
Abhiyan (SNA): SNA is an ongoing Central Sector scheme to carry out IEC activities 
across the country from the Central/Headquarter level. Such IEC activities are planned 
in consultation and coordination with the respective programme Divisions and as per 
their administrative requirements/priorities with the aim of spreading awareness and 
delivering eﬀective last mile outreach. Besides, SNA also leverages diﬀerent media 
platforms and partnership networks.  
ii. PNDT Act Awareness: Focuses on enforcing the Pre -Conception and Pre -Natal 
Diagnostic Techniques Act, 1994, to prevent sex selection and female foeticide.  As 
per the Sample Registration System (SRS) Report 2023, the Sex Ratio at Birth (SRB) 
of the country has shown a steady improvement, rising by 19 points from 898 (SRS 
2014–16) to 917 (SRS 2021–23) females per 1,000 male births. 
iii. Population Research Centers (PRCs): Objective of the PRCs are critical research -
based input to the Health and Family Welfare programmes and policies of the 
Government of India and states. 18 centers  (funded by MoHFW) conduct research on 
family planning, demographics, and population control, supporting government health 
policies and surveys like NFHS and LASI.  
iv. National Family Health Survey (NFHS): The National Family Health Survey (NFHS), 
conducted w ith IIPS, provides data on health and family welfare and associated 
domains like characteristics of the population; fertility and fertility preferences; family 
planning; infant and child mortality; maternal and child health; nutrition; morbidity 
and health care; women‘s empowerment, etc., enabling policy evaluation and 
benchmarking.  
v. Contraceptive Distribution: Provides free contraceptives (condoms, pills, IUCDs, etc.) 
to achieve population stabilization and reduce maternal/child mortality. As per NFHS-
5 (2019-21) data, 74.2% of currently married women (15 -49 years), have their need 
for family planning satisfied, with modern methods. The Government is utilizing 
NFHS-5 findings for evidence -based planning, strengthening programme 
implementation, and strategic resource allocation to address gaps and inter -state 
disparities, aiming to improve the programme. 
vi. WHO-NPS Support: WHO -supported National Polio Surveillance Project provides 
technical and operational support for vaccine -preventable disease surveillance, 
immunization, and other health initiatives. The ongoing activities on Vaccine 
preventable disease surveillance is being carried out and also actively supported by the 
Integrated Disease Surveillance Program (IDSP) under National Centre of Disease 
Control (NC DC) for complete management. The Polio free status of the country is 
maintained. 
 

78

3.9.3 The Committee observes that the overall allocation for Family Welfare 
Schemes has remained broadly sta ble over the last three financial years, with ₹620.48 
crore at BE 2025 –26, revised upward to ₹645.48 crore at RE stage, and ₹643.46 crore 
proposed at BE 2026 –27. However, expenditure of ₹462.95 crore up to 17.02.2026 
indicates that a considerable portion o f funds remained to be utilised during the 
remaining period of the financial year. At the component level, variations in utilisation 
are also observed, particularly under Information, Education and Communication on 
the PNDT Act and the WHO -supported Nation al Polio Surveillance Project. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department strengthen planning and 
monitoring mechanisms for these components to ensure timely and optimal utilisation of 
allocated resources. Greater emphasis may also be placed on outcome -oriented 
implementation of IEC campaigns, PNDT Act awareness activities, and surveillance 
initiatives so that financial allocations translate into measurable improvements in 
gender balance, population stabilisation, and disease surveillance outcomes. 
3.9.4 The Committee recognises that Accredited Social Health Activists 
(ASHAs) and other frontline health workers constitute the backbone of the effective 
implementation of Family Welfare Schemes, particularly in areas such as awareness 
generation, d istribution of contraceptives, community mobilisation, and facilitating 
access to reproductive and maternal health services. Their role is especially critical in 
ensuring last -mile delivery of services under initiatives such as mass IEC campaigns, 
population stabilisation programmes, and family planning interventions. In this context, 
the Committee is of the view that the sustained success of these schemes depends 
significantly on the motivation, retention, and capacity of these grassroots functionaries. 
The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department must take appropriate 

79 
 
measures, in coordination with States/UTs, to make the remuneration, incentives, and 
career progression avenues of ASHAs and other health workers more attractive and 
predictable. Strengthening their financial security, providing opportunities for skill 
upgradation, and creating clearer pathways for professional advancement would not 
only improve workforce retention but also enhance programme effectiveness and ensure 
better outcomes of Family Welfare initiatives at the grassroots level.

3.10.1 The scheme is envisaged as a centre -par-excellence to give impetus to the 
advancement of knowledge in the field of prevention and control of communicable/infectious 
diseases of public health importance and strengthening of other Health Initiatives. 
Establishment and Strengthening of NCDC Branches and One Health Initiatives – Program 
for Strengthening of Inter -sectoral coordination for Preven tion & Control of Zoonotic 
Diseases renamed as ―National One Health Program for Prevention and Control of 
Zoonoses‖, Surveillance of Viral Hepatitis, Containment of Antimicrobial Resistance is an 
umbrella and 100% Centrally Funded Central Sector Scheme. It  comprises six major 
programme components: 
1. National Programme on Antimicrobial Resistance (AMR) Containment 
2. National Rabies Control Programme (NRCP)

year. For 2025–26, the allocation was increased to ₹54.01 crore at BE stage but later revised 
downward to ₹35.00 crore at RE stage, against which an expenditure of ₹26.98 crore had been 
incurred up to 31.01.2026. For 2026–27, the allocation has been proposed at ₹60.00 crore 
under the Revenue head.  
 
3.10.3 On asking for the reasons for reduction of allocated fund at RE stage, the Department 
stated that under NCDC, a total Budget Estimate (BE) of ₹ 54.01 crore for 2025 –26 was 

80 
 
allocated for the Establishment and Strengthening of NCDC Branches and related health 
initiatives, including Inter -sectoral Coordination for Prevention and Control of Zoonotic and 
other Neglected Tropical Diseases, Surveillance of Viral Hepatitis, a nd Antimicrobial 
Resistance (AMR). The scheme provides financial support through Grants -in-Aid (GIA) to 
State Medical Colleges and other eligible institutions for establishing and strengthening 
laboratories under programmes on AMR, Rabies Control, Preventi on and Control of 
Leptospirosis, and Viral Hepatitis Surveillance, with funds released directly for timely 
implementation. Most institutions have progressed as per approved plans, though some faced 
operational and administrative constraints such as procure ment timelines, onboarding 
processes, and HR attrition, leading to partial utilization during the financial year. The 
implementation of the revised fund flow mechanism led to temporary disruptions in the 
allocation and disbursement of funds. The transition al adjustments impacted the pace of 
expenditure, resulting in comparatively lower spending during the period. Grants -in-Aid 
(GIA) is released in phased instalments of up to 25% based on Statements of Expenditure, but 
procedural requirements and repeated Ge M procurement attempts for reagents, consumables, 
and equipment have impacted the pace of expenditure. 
3.10.4 On a specific query, the Department provided programme -wise physical achievements 
for the year 2025-26 as below: 
a. The National One Health Programme for Prevention and Control of Zoonoses (NOHP-
PCZ) has undertaken several measures to operationalize the One Health framework 
across the country. State One Health Action Plans have been launched in Odisha and 
Rajasthan, while draft action plans have been p repared by the States of Telangana, 
Andhra, and Bihar. Zoonosis Committees have been formed in 35 out of 36 States to 
ensure intersectoral coordination and governance. The diagnostic capacity of States for 
endemic zoonoses has been enhanced through the est ablishment of 75 Sentinel 
Surveillance Sites, and 17 Regional Coordinators across the medical, veterinary, and 
wildlife institute sectors have been put in place to strengthen surveillance and 
coordination. The programme has also been successfully onboarded onto the Integrated 
Health Information Platform (IHIP). In addition, a systematic analysis of the digital 
architecture of surveillance platforms of other key stakeholder ministries and 
departments, such as MoFAHD and MoEFCC, has been undertaken, and a pil ot is 
planned in Tamil Nadu and Telangana. 
 
b. Under the National Rabies Control Programme (NRCP), as on date, 29 States have 
made rabies a notifiable disease. Consequent to the launch of the National Action Plan 
for Rabies Elimination (NAPRE) in 2021, State Action Plans for Rabies Elimination 
(SAPRE) have been launched for the States of Pondicherry, Meghalaya, Mizoram, 
Nagaland, Rajasthan, and Tamil Nadu, along with the ―Operational Guideline for 
Rabies Free City Initiative.‖ A network of 14 rabies diagnostic  laboratories has been 
strengthened, and joint multidisciplinary trainings of health, veterinary, and wildlife 
professionals at all levels are being conducted as a regular activity. 

81 
 
c. Under the Programme for Prevention & Control of Leptospirosis (PPCL), a ne twork of 
5 laboratories has been strengthened and a total of 22,900 samples have been tested for 
leptospirosis. Regular hands-on trainings on leptospirosis are being conducted, with 12 
such hands-on trainings conducted so far for all States, along with mas ter training of 
trainers through which more than 20,000 participants have been trained across all 
States/UTs. Diagnostic capacity has been further strengthened with the establishment 
of five Leptospirosis Regional Diagnostic Laboratories, complemented by 1 1 Regional 
Coordinators and 55 Sentinel Surveillance Sites under NOHP –PCZ, collectively 
facilitating testing of over 66,900 samples, and the case fatality rate has declined to 0 –
7%, remaining well below the acceptable threshold. All endemic States/UTs have  
adequate stock of doxycycline, kits, and consumables. 
 
d. The Establishment and Strengthening of New NCDC Branches: The scheme has been 
merged with PM -ABHIM, and all new NCDC State Branches are being constructed 
using PM -ABHIM funds. The present scheme suppo rts the 8 existing operational 
branches for renovation activities. 
 
e. Under the National Programme on Antimicrobial Resistance (AMR) Containment, 70 
state medical college laboratories have been strengthened for quality -assured testing 
for AMR detection, and surveillance of antimicrobial use has been established in 45 
state medical college hospitals. In addition, 12 States are being supported for the 
establishment of State AMR surveillance laboratory networks.  
 
f. The National Program for Surveillance of Viral H epatitis has strengthened laboratories 
in 29 medical colleges and institutions across the country to carry out surveillance of 
viral hepatitis, with the Viral Hepatitis Laboratory at NCDC functioning as the Centre 
of Excellence. The programme has been inte grated with population -level surveys and 
programmes for conducting surveillance of chronic viral hepatitis to ensure financial 
efficiencies, including the National Family Health Survey from round 4 onwards and 
HIV Sentinel Surveillance from round 17 onward s under the National AIDS Control 
Program for estimation of seroprevalence of Hepatitis B and Hepatitis C. As a result, 
the first ever nationally representative and state-wise seroprevalence of Hepatitis B and 
Hepatitis C has been obtained for evidence-based prioritization of inventions.  
 
3.10.5 On a specific query on functional NCDC branches in the country, the Department 
submitted that the scheme component has been subsumed under PM -ABHIM, and all new 
State NCDC branch buildings/establishments are being taken up under the PM -ABHIM.  
NCDC is the implementing agency for the component ―Strengthening Surveillance of 
Infectious Diseases and Outbreak Response‖, which includes the establishment of NCDC

a) Eight (08) NCDC branches are operational at Andhra Pradesh (Mangala Giri), 
Chhattisgarh (Raipur), Tamil Nadu (Chennai), Rajasthan (Jaipur), Uttar Pradesh 
(Varanasi), Bihar (Patna), Kerala (Kozhikode) and Karnataka (Bengaluru).

3.10.7 On a specific query, the Department stated that India‘s COVID -19 research response 
moved from reactive outbreak control to systemic preparedness building , including 
Diagnostics self -reliance, Vaccine R&D capacity, Genomic surveillance ecosystem, One 
Health integration & Early warning systems. The shift now is toward predict ed–prevent–
prepare, rather than respond after spread. COVID -19 highlighted the need for a One Health 
approach integrating human, animal, and environmental health. Key Areas of Ongoing & 
Emerging Research: 
a) One Health Surveillance through Integrated surveill ance of humans, livestock, 
wildlife and monitoring high -risk interfaces (wet markets, migratory corridors). 
Genomic sequencing networks (INSACOG) has also been established. 
b) Strengthening Laboratory Infrastructure: Expansion of BSL -3/BSL-4 labs, 
deployment of Mobile laboratories and Rapid diagnostic platform development for 
emerging pathogens. 
c) Pandemic Preparedness Platforms established such as mRNA and DNA vaccine 
platforms, Broad -spectrum antivirals research and Prototype pathogen approach 
(rapid vaccine development for virus families). 
d) Environmental & Wastewater Surveillance: Wastewater epidemiology as early 
outbreak detection. 
e) Wildlife & Spillover Research: Virus discovery programs in bats, rodents, and 
other reservoirs and Risk modelling for zoonotic spillover 
 
3.10.8 On a query related to inter-sectoral co-ordination of prevention and control of zoonotic 
diseases, viral hepatitis and Anti -Microbial Resistance (AMR), the Department provided that 
to further strengthen inter -sectoral coordination for preven tion and control of zoonotic 
diseases real-time data sharing between human, animal, and environmental sectors, expansion 
of sentinel surveillance and regional coordinators to strengthen surveillance, creation of a 
unified digital dashboard integrating IHIP , veterinary and environmental databases, 
strengthening implementation of the programme in States/UTs, and expansion of the number 
of Sentinel Surveillance Sites and Regional Coordinators along with strengthening the 
capacity of State medical colleges.  

83 
 
 
3.10.9 Under the National Programme on AMR Containment, the SFC 2026 –31 envisages 
strengthening of National Reference Laboratories (NRLs) for bacterial and fungal pathogens 
with enhanced capacity for molecular characterization of emerging AMR pathogens, Ext ernal 
Quality Assessment (EQAS), and AMR alert confirmation; expansion of the national AMR 
surveillance network to at least 200 tertiary care institutions across all States/UTs, along with 
parallel expansion of Healthcare -Associated Infection (HAI) and Ant imicrobial 
Consumption/Use (AMC/AMU) surveillance; establishment and strengthening of State AMR 
surveillance networks including creation of State Reference Laboratories for quality assurance 
and data validation; development of IEC material on AMR; capacity  building through 
standardized laboratory protocols, trainings, development of antibiograms, and strengthening 
of Infection Prevention and Control (IPC) practices; strengthening antimicrobial stewardship 
through systematic monitoring of antimicrobial consu mption and use; and establishing a 
dedicated Technical and Logistics Coordination Unit at NCDC to provide specialised 
technical oversight, expert guidance, and operational coordination for effective 
implementation of NAP -AMR 2.0. Under AMR, two National Re ference Laboratories are in 
place, namely the AMR -NRL for bacterial pathogens established at NCDC, Delhi, and the 
AMR-NRL for fungal pathogens established at Vallabhbhai Patel Chest Institute. 
3.10.10 The Committee finds that the allocation under the schem e for 
Establishment and Strengthening of NCDC Branches and related health initiatives has 
shown fluctuations during the last two financial years, with the Budget Estimate of 
₹52.00 crore in 2024–25 being reduced to ₹30.37 crore at RE stage and the BE of ₹5 4.01 
crore in 2025–26 being revised downward to ₹35.00 crore. The Committee observes that 
such reductions were primarily attributed to operational constraints including 
procurement timelines, onboarding delays, HR attrition and transitional challenges in 
the revised fund flow mechanism. While acknowledging the efforts of the Department in 
implementing multiple national programmes under the scheme, the Committee 
recommends that the Department streamline administrative and procurement 
processes, strengthen fi nancial planning, and ensure timely release and utilisation of 
Grants-in-Aid so that critical public health initiatives related to zoonotic diseases, 
antimicrobial resistance, and viral hepatitis surveillance are implemented without 
interruption. 
3.10.11 The Committee appreciates the progress made under the National One 
Health Programme for Prevention and Control of Zoonoses, particularly the formation 
of Zoonosis Committees in 35 States, the establishment of 75 sentinel surveillance sites, 
and the onboarding of the programme onto the Integrated Health Information Platform. 
Given the increasing risk of zoonotic disease outbreaks and the lessons learned from 
recent pandemics, the Committee emphasises the importance of strengthening the One 
Health framework th rough deeper integration of human, animal, and environmental 
health surveillance systems. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the 
Department accelerate the development of a unified digital surveillance architecture and 
expand sentinel surveillance si tes and regional coordination mechanisms so as to enable 

84 
 
real-time data sharing, early detection of emerging pathogens, and coordinated response 
across sectors and States/UTs. 
3.10.12 The Committee appreciates that the scheme plays a crucial role in 
strengthening disease surveillance infrastructure, including the expansion of 
laboratories for antimicrobial resistance detection, rabies diagnosis, leptospirosis 
testing, and viral hepatitis surveillance across several medical colleges and institutions. 
The Committee also observes that under the AMR containment programme, laboratory 
networks have been strengthened in 70 state medical colleges, while surveillance of 
antimicrobial use has been established in 45 hospitals. Considering the growing global 
threat of a ntimicrobial resistance and the need for robust diagnostic capacity, the 
Committee recommends that the Department further expand laboratory networks and 
surveillance systems, particularly in underserved regions, while strengthening national 
and state refer ence laboratories and ensuring regular training, quality assurance 
mechanisms, and antimicrobial stewardship practices across healthcare institutions. 
3.10.13 The Committee observes that the establishment of NCDC State Branches 
is a critical step towards s trengthening the country‘s infectious disease surveillance and 
outbreak response architecture. At present, eight NCDC branches are operational, while 
several others are at different stages of construction and are expected to become 
functional by 2027 –28 un der PM -ABHIM. While appreciating the progress achieved, 
the Committee stresses the importance of timely completion and operationalisation of 
these branches, which will play a vital role in real -time disease surveillance, outbreak 
investigation, and coordin ation with State health systems. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends that the Department intensify coordination with States/UTs and 
implementing agencies to expedite construction activities, ensure adequate staffing and 
laboratory infrastructure, and make  the upcoming NCDC branches fully functional 
within the stipulated timelines so as to strengthen the national public health 
preparedness and response system. 
3.10.14 The Committee notes that the scheme encompasses several emerging 
priority areas such as pandemic preparedness, genomic surveillance, environmental and 
wastewater surveillance, and research on zoonotic spillovers. The Committee further 
observes that the experience gained during the COVID -19 pandemic has highlighted the 
importance of predictive s urveillance systems, rapid diagnostics, and integrated 
research platforms to detect and contain emerging infectious diseases at an early stage. 
In this regard, the Committee recommends that the Department further strengthen 
research and innovation under th e scheme by promoting collaboration with premier 
global research institutions, medical colleges, and international public health bodies. 
Greater emphasis must also be placed on developing advanced diagnostic platforms, 
expanding genomic surveillance networ ks, and strengthening environmental and 
wildlife surveillance so that the country‘s public health system is better equipped to 
anticipate and respond to future infectious disease threats.

3.11.1 The allocation under the Revenue head for 2025–26 is ₹8.53 crore at BE stage but was 
slightly reduced to ₹8.10 crore at RE stage, with an expenditure of ₹6.26 crore incurred up to 
31.01.2026. For 2026–27, the allocation under the Revenue head has been proposed at ₹10.00 
crore.  
 
3.11.2 The National Pharmacovigilance Programme of India (PvPI) has been launched and 
implemented by the Ministry of Health & Family Welfare, Govt. of India since 2010 with the 
objective of ensuring patie nt safety by monitoring, assessing and preventing Adverse Drug 
Reactions (ADRs) associated with the use of medical products marketed in India. The key 
features and objectives of the Programme include: 
a. Create a Nation-wide system for patient-safety by ensuring drug-safety. 
b. Identify and analyse new signals from the reported cases. 
c. Analyse the benefit-risk ratio of marketed medications. 
d. Generate evidence-based information on safety of medicines. 
e. Support regulatory agencies in the decision-making process on use of medications. 
f. Communicate safety information on use of medicines to various stakeholders for 
preventing/minimizing the risk. 
g. Collaborate with other National Centres for exchange of information and data 
management. 
h. Provide training and technical support to other National Pharmacovigilance Centres 
across the globe. 
i. To organise and sensitize the stakeholders for celebration of National 
Pharmacovigilance Week from 17th September- 23rd September every year. 
j. Promote rational use of medicines 
k. Emerge as a National Centre of Excellence for Pharmacovigilance activities. 
 
Achievements so far: 
a. So far, 1,150 Adverse Drug Reaction Monitoring Centres (AMCs) have been enrolled 
across the country. 
b. There has been significant increase in reporting of Individual Case Safety Reports 
(ICSRs) under the Programme. As on 10.02.2026, a total of 10,79,698 ICSRs from 
India have been uploaded to VigiBase. 
c. India presently ranks as the 8th largest contributor of ICSRs globally, reflecting the 
growing maturity and robustness of the pharmacovigilance system. 
d. In addition to medicines, monitoring of adverse events related to medical devices has 
been strengthened through the Materiovigilance component of the Programme, 

86 
 
enabling reporting, assessment and regulatory follow-up of Medical Devices Adverse 
Events (MDAEs). 
e. By recognizing the strength and progressive journey of PvPI, WHO on 18.07.2017, 
bestowed upon India the honour of being WHO-Collaborating Centre for 
Pharmacovigilance in Public Health Programmes and Regulatory Services in South-
East Asia Regulatory Network (SEARN) Countries and is continuing, till date. 
f. As on 10.02.2026, a total of 13,657 training programmes have been conducted, 
covering 9,29,870 participants. 
3.11.3 The details of achievements of Pharmacovigilance Programme of Indi a relating to 
outputs /outcome of FY2025-26 (as on 10.02.2026) is given below: 
3.11.2

3.11.4 The Committee notes that the National Pharmacovigilance Programme has made 
commendable progress, with India now ranking as the 8th largest global contributor of 
Individual Case Safety Reports (ICSRs) and having established 1,150 Adverse Drug 
Reaction Monitoring Cent res (AMCs) across the country. The Committee appreciates 
the significant training outreach, with over 9.29 lakh participants sensitised, and the 
recognition accorded by WHO designating India as a Collaborating Centre for 
Pharmacovigilance in Public Health Programmes. The Committee, however, expresses 
concern that the actual expenditure in FY 2025 –26 stood at only ₹6.39 crore against an 
allocation of ₹8.10 crore, reflecting gaps in full fund absorption. Moreover, the uneven 
utilisation of resources risks slowing down the expansion of AMCs and the adoption of 
advanced data-mining technologies for signal detection. 
 
3.11.5 The Committee, further, recommends that the Department should strengthen the 
Programme by institutionalising regular pharmacovigilance inspections of Marketing 
Authorization Holders (MAHs) to ensure compliance, accelerating the expansion of 
ADR Monitoring Centres to cover underserved regions, and intensifying awareness and 
training programmes for healthcare professionals and consumers to promote timely 
reporting of adverse drug reactions. The Committee urges the Department to leverage 
modern data analytics and artificial intelligence tools for signal detection and risk 
assessment, thereby enhancing the robustness of India‘s pharmacovigilance system 
translating into improved patient safety, rational use of medicines, and sustained global 
leadership in pharmacovigilance.

3.12.1 The allocation under the Revenue head was ₹22.00 crore at both BE and RE stages in 
2024–25, against which an actual expenditure of ₹21.93 crore was incurred. In 2025 –26, the 
allocation was increased to ₹28.74  crore at both BE and RE stages, with an expenditure of 
₹20.50 crore incurred up to 31.01.2026. For 2026 –27, the allocation under the Revenue head 
has been proposed at ₹27.24 crore.  
 
3.12.2 The Central Sector scheme of Development of Nursing Services has been formulated 
considering the need to upgrade quality of nursing education. The scheme has been further 
continued for the period 2021 -22 till 2025 -26 with an outlay of ₹ 215.76 crore with the 
following components: 
 
(a) Training of Nurses - To update the knowledge and skills of the in -service nursing 
personnel funds to the tune of ₹2,28,500/ - per course of 7 days duration for 30 participants 
is provided for conducting short term training courses in three categories - Clinical 
Speciality, Education Technology Nursing Administration. 
 
(b) Strengthening of existing School of Nursing - In order to increase the availability of 
graduate nurses, one time assistance upto a maximum of ₹ 7.00 crores per institution (with 
recurring) is being provided for upgrading a School of Nursing into College of Nursing.  
 
(c) National Florence Nightingale Award for Nursing personnel - National Awards for 
nurses are given by Hon‘ble President of India on 12th of May each year, as mark of highest 
recognition for the meritorious servi ce of nurses and Nursing profession  in the country. 
Each award carries a citation, medal and cash Award of ₹ one lakh (₹1,00,000/-).  
 
3.12.3 On a query related to physical achievements under the scheme, the Department 
submitted that 52 training courses co nducted aimed at imparting training to 1560 nurses. 
Funds released to 07 Schools of Nursing (SoN) for upgradation into College of Nursing 
(CoN) under the Scheme.  In the FY 2026 -27, BE ₹ 27.24 has been proposed under the 
scheme for training of nurses and u pgradation of SoNs to CoNs. The amount of ₹27.24 crore 
is proposed to be utilized primarily for strengthening nursing education, infrastructure and 
professional capacity across the country.  The amount is considered adequate to meet the 
proposed activities  for the year, which are largely focused on upgradation of schools of 
Nursing, capacity building, training, and system strengthening.  
 
3.12.4 On a specific query, the Department further stated that total of 5253 nursing 
colleges/institutes are functional in India as on 31.10.2025 that provides nursing education. 

88 
 
Further, 176 Colleges/institutes have been established in the FY 2025 -26. Following steps 
have been taken to upgrade the education standard of nursing in the country to global level 
and improve quality of Nursing in Government and private institution in the country:  
 
(a) Revised B.Sc (N) Competency Based Syllabus: B.Sc.(N) syllabus has been revised 
in the year 2021 into competency -based syllabus with credit system at par with 
international standards. This semester-based syllabus has been successfully rolled out 
throughout the Country. 
 
(b) Nurses Registration and Tracking System - Nurses Registration and Tracking 
System (NRTS) has been implemented with aim to identify actual number of nurses 
(Live Register of Nurses) in working in India. It captures the specification of nurses 
and various other paramete rs Each nurse will be given Nurse Unique Identity Card 
(NUID) and Nurse passbook for facilitating reciprocal registration between the 
states.Renewal li cense is linked with 150 Hours of Continuing Nursing Education. 
13.66 Lakhs Nurses Live Data has been captured linked with the Aadhar. 
 
(c) Centrally Sponsored Scheme: The Government has sanctioned the Centrally 
Sponsored Scheme ―Augmenting Nursing Educati on - Establishment of new Colleges 
of Nursing in co -location with Medical Colleges‖ for establishment of 157 new 
nursing colleges in co -location with the existing medical colleges established since 
2014. 
 
(d) Dual Degree in Nursing - MoU between University of Brunswick, Canada and 
MAHE Manipal College of Nursing through INC collaboration with blended 
curriculum for Canada has been signed in 2022. Government has approved overseas 
blended model in nursing education to empower nursing students to work abroad th at 
includes country, hospital, professional and code of ethics which are included in 50 
hour of Canadian concepts in our B.Sc (N) syllabus. 
 
(e) Under the Central Sector Scheme ‗Development of Nursing Services‘ financial 
assistance is provided to States/UT s to upgrade Schools of Nursing into Colleges of 
Nursing.  
 
(f) Nurse Practitioner Programme: - INC has initiated post graduate level programme 
in residency mode in last one decade. NPCC is running in Govt. and Private 
Institutions since 2017 and till date  69 institutions with 975 seats is running the 
program successfully. 
 
(g) Doctor of Nursing Practice (DNP) - Indian Nursing Council has signed as MoU 
with the University of Houston in order to promote more evidence -based nursing 
practice. DNP programme is b eing started on pilot basis & candidates who have 
completed Nurse Practitioner in Critical Care are eligible for this DNP Programme. 
 

89 
 
(h) Indian Nursing Council has issued the notification with regard to Foreign 
Language as optional subject/ course in B.Sc.(N) & GNM program. All expenses to be 
borne by the nursing institution. 
 
(i) Minimum Pre -Requisites for Granting Suitability to Nursing Programs - Pre 
requisites have to be maintained for establishment of new Nursing 
institutions/Enhancement of seats for any Nursing programme. The minimum pre -
requisite related to student faculty ratio, infrastructure like classrooms, simulation 
labs, hostels, clinical facilities etc. and availability of 100 bedded parent hospital is 
mandatory. INC has notified the Minimum Pre-Requisites for Granting Suitability to 
Nursing Programs with effect from 12/03/2021. 
 
3.12.5 On a query on data about the shortage of nurses in the country, the Department 
furnished that as per the Indian Nursing Council, there are around 42.94 lakh nu rsing 
personnel registered in the country. Assuming 80% nursing personnel are active in the 
country, it is estimated that around 34.35 lakh nursing personnel are available for active 
services, which gives a Nurse: Population ratio of about 2.43 nurses per 1000 population.  
 
3.12.6 On being asked about the initiatives contemplating by the Government to promote 
nursing colleges and to make nursing education more affordable, the Department made a 
submission that the Government has undertaken several measures t o promote the growth of 
nursing institutions, including in the private sector, and to make nursing education more 
affordable and accessible to economically weaker and disadvantaged sections of society. The 
key initiatives are as follows: 
a. Strengthening the Regulatory Framework: The enactment of the National Nursing and 
Midwifery Commission Act, 2023 provides for a transparent, uniform and time -bound 
regulatory framework. The new framework aims to streamline approval processes, 
ensure accountability, and prom ote quality standards in the establishment and 
functioning of nursing institutions, including those in the private sector. 
b. Expansion of Government Nursing Institutions: Under the Centrally Sponsored 
Scheme titled “Augmenting Nursing Education – Establishment of new Colleges of 
Nursing in co -location with Medical Colleges” , the Government has sanctioned the 
establishment of 157 new Colleges of Nursing in co -location with medical colleges 
established since 2014. This expansion enhances access to quality nursi ng education 
across the country and reduces regional imbalances. 
c. Upgradation Support under Central Sector Scheme: Under the Central Sector Scheme 
“Development of Nursing Services”, financial assistance is extended to States/UTs for 
upgradation of Schools of Nursing into Colleges of Nursing, thereby increasing intake 
capacity and improving the quality of nursing education infrastructure. 
d. Minimum Pre -Requisites for Establishment of Institutions: The Indian Nursing 
Council has notified the Minimum Pre-Requisites for Granting Suitability to Nursing 
Programmes (effective from 12.03.2021), prescribing mandatory norms relating to 
student–faculty ratio, infrastructure (classrooms, simulation laboratories, hostels), 
clinical facilities, and the requirement of a 100 -bedded parent hospital. These 

90 
 
standards ensure quality assurance while enabling responsible expansion in both 
Government and private sector 
e. Rationalisation of Infrastructure and Clinical Facility Requirements: Further, with a 
view to facilitating establishm ent of nursing educational institutions, especially in 
urban, semi-urban and resource -constrained settings, the regulatory norms have been 
rationalised. The land requirement for Nursing Educational Institutions has been 
relaxed from the earlier stipulation of three acres to a constructed area of 54,000 sq. ft. 
for a School/College of Nursing, inclusive of hostel facilities. In addition, while a 100-
bedded parent hospital has been a mandatory requirement for opening GNM and B.Sc. 
(Nursing) programmes since 2 013–14 to ensure adequate clinical exposure, this 
requirement has been suitably relaxed for institutions located in hilly and tribal areas, 
taking into account local constraints and to promote equitable regional access to 
nursing education. 
3.12.7 The Comm ittee appreciates that the utilisation of funds under the scheme has 
been broadly satisfactory, with ₹21.93 crore spent against an allocation of ₹22 crore in 
FY 2024–25, and ₹20.50 crore utilised up to January 2026 against an allocation of ₹28.74 
crore in FY 2025 –26. However, the Committee expresses concern that despite the 
increased allocation, utilisation in FY 2025 –26 has lagged, potentially slowing down the 
pace of training and upgradation activities. The Committee, therefore, recommends that 
the Department should ensure timely release and absorption of funds by strengthening 
monitoring mechanisms and instituting quarterly utilisation reviews. Further, the 
Committee urges the Department to expand the scale of short -term training courses 
beyond the 52  courses conducted so far, so that a larger proportion of the 42.94 lakh 
registered nursing personnel can benefit from skill enhancement. This will improve the 
quality of nursing services, ensure uniformity in standards across states. The 
Committee, further, recommends that the Department should promote nursing services 
to bridge the gap in the nurse -to-population ratio, which currently stands at 2.43 nurses 
per 1,000 population. 
 
3.12.8 The Committee observes that significant reforms have been introduced in 
nursing education, including the competency -based B.Sc. (N) syllabus, the Nurses 
Registration and Tracking System (NRTS), the establishment of 157 new nursing 
colleges in co -location with medical colleges, and international collaborations for dual 
degree and  Doctor of Nursing Practice programmes. While these initiatives are 
commendable, the Committee is concerned that without a consolidated roadmap, the 
reforms risk remaining fragmented and unevenly implemented across institutions. The 
Committee, therefore, r ecommends that the Department should prepare a National 
Strategic Roadmap for Nursing Education and Services, integrating ongoing initiatives 
such as NRTS, Nurse Practitioner Programme ( NPCC), Doctor of Nursing Practice 
(DNP), and foreign collaborations in to a unified framework. This roadmap should set 
clear targets for expansion of nursing colleges, faculty development, infrastructure 
standards, affordability measures for disadvantaged groups, and to elevate nursing 
education in India to global standards w hile addressing domestic shortages and regional 
disparities. 

91 
 
3.12.9 The Committee notes that while 5,253 nursing colleges and institutes are 
functional across the country and 176 new institutions were established in FY 2025 –26, 
challenges remain in ensurin g equitable access to nursing education, particularly for 
economically weaker and disadvantaged sections of society. The Committee appreciates 
the measures undertaken by the Government, including the enactment of the National 
Nursing and Midwifery Commissi on Act, 2023, rationalisation of infrastructure 
requirements, and expansion of government nursing institutions. However, the 
Committee is concerned that rising costs of education and regional disparities may limit 
opportunities for aspiring nursing student s. The Committee, therefore, recommends 
that the Department should adopt a targeted affordability and inclusion strategy, which 
may include provision of scholarships and financial aid for disadvantaged groups, 
incentivising private institutions to reserve seats for economically weaker sections, and 
prioritising establishment of nursing colleges in underserved rural, tribal, and hilly 
areas. Such measures will not only expand the intake capacity but also ensure that the 
benefits of nursing education reforms reach all sections of society, thereby improving 
the nurse-to-population ratio in line with global standards.

3.13.1 The scheme (Health Sector Disaster Preparedness and Response and Human Resource 
Development for Emergency Medical Services) is being implemented by the Disaster 
Management Cell, under Ministry of Health & Family Welf are. Its objectives are in line with 
National Health Policy 2017, National Disaster Management Plan 2019 and other national 
policies and guidelines. The major objective of the scheme includes: 
 
a) Infrastructure development which includes: 
i. Setting up of Natio nal Emergency Life Support (NELS) Skill Centers in 
medical colleges/institutions under Central/State Governments 
ii. Setting up of secondary and tertiary level Chemical, Biological, Radiological 
and Nuclear (CBRN) medical management Centers 
 
b) Capacity building initiatives which includes a wide array of pan -India skill -based 
hands on trainings, including the following: 
i. Trainings of doctors, nurses and paramedics on National Emergency Life 
Support (NELS) 
ii. Hospital disaster preparedness and response for hospital administrators 
iii. Management of public health emergencies for district level health officers 

92 
 
iv. Psycho-social care in disaster settings for psychiatrists, psychologists and 
psycho-social workers 
v. Medical management of radio-nuclear emergencies for medical officers 
 
3.13.2 The scheme is intended to augment capacities for health sector preparedness and 
response based on an ‗all-hazard‘ approach, with the objectives: a) To strengthen capacities of 
the health sector to prevent, mitigate and remain prepared to respond to the adverse outcome 
of disasters and other public health emergencies; and b) To strengthen human resource 
capacities for providing emergency medical services. Under HSDPR, two central sector 
schemes (100% funded by central government) –Health Sector Disast er Preparedness & 
Response   and Human Resource Development for Emergency Medical Services are 
implemented under one scheme code.
