3.13.6 The Committee finds that despite allocations of  ₹94 crore in FY 2025 –26, actual 
expenditure stood at ₹63.85 crore as of February 2026, with significant reductions in 
capital outlay in both FY 2024–25 and FY 2025–26 due to delays in land availability and 
lower demand from the Project Management Consulta nt. The Committee expresses 
concern that such delays in execution of secondary and tertiary -level CBRN centres and 
NELS skill centres risk undermining the objectives of disaster preparedness and 
emergency medical response. The Committee, therefore, recomme nds that the 
Department should establish a time -bound execution framework for infrastructure 
projects, including firm timelines for land acquisition, prototype finalisation, and 
construction milestones. The Committee urges the Department to strengthen 
coordination with State Governments and Project Management Consultants to avoid 
underutilisation of funds and ensure that the proposed ₹95 crore allocation for FY 
2026–27 is absorbed effectively for intended purpose i.e. accelerating the 
operationalisation of CBRN centres and expand NELS skill centres, thereby enhancing 
national preparedness for chemical, biological, radiological, and nuclear emergencies. 
 

94 
 
3.13.7 The Committee observes that while the Health Sector Disaster Preparedness & 
Response component has shown reasonable progress, the Human Resource Development 
for Emergency Medical Services (HRDEMS) component reflects stark under -utilisation, 
with only ₹2.33 crore spent against a revised allocation of ₹8.27 crore in FY 2025 –26. 
The Committee is concerned that such under -utilisation directly impacts the scale of 
training programmes for doctors, nurses, paramedics, and administrators , which are 
critical for building national capacity in emergency medical response. The Committee, 
therefore, recommends that the Department should adopt a structured capacity -
building plan, with annual targets for the number of personnel to be trained unde r 
NELS, hospital disaster preparedness, psycho -social care, and radio -nuclear emergency 
management. The Committee further recommends leveraging digital platforms and 
simulation-based training to expand reach and reduce costs, while ensuring that trained 
personnel are equitably distributed across states, including disaster -prone and 
underserved regions to develop a robust cadre of emergency medical professionals 
capable of responding to all-hazard public health emergencies. 
 
3.14 NATIONAL ORGAN TRANSPLANT PROGRAM (NOTP)

3.14.1 The total allocation under the scheme was ₹15.00 crore at BE 2024 –25, which was 
reduced to ₹10.00 crore at RE stage, with an actual expenditure of ₹7.05 crore during the 
year. In 2025 –26, the allocation was resto red to ₹15.00 crore at both BE and RE stages, 
against which an expenditure of ₹9.03 crore had been incurred up to 31.01.2026. For 2026 –
27, the allocation has been maintained at ₹15.00 crore, comprising ₹14.38 crore under the 
Revenue head and ₹0.62 crore under the Capital head. 
 
3.14.2 The National Organ Transplant Programme (NOTP) is a Central Sector Scheme which 
aims at strengthening the national ecosystem for organ and tissue donation and transplantation 
in accordance with the Transplantation of Human Org ans and Tissues Act (THOTA), 1994 
(as amended in 2011). The Programme seeks to bridge the critical gap between the increasing 
demand for life -saving organ transplants and the limited availability of donor organs by 
establishing a structured, transparent and equitable national framework. 
 
3.14.3 The basic objective of NOTP is to promote Deceased Organ Donation across the 
country with a view to increase availability of organs for transplantation, to organize an 
efficient mechanism for organ and tissue procure ment/retrieval especially from deceased 
donors and their distribution for transplantation, to establish new and strengthen the existing 
organ and tissue retrieval and transplant infrastructure facilities especially in public sector 

95 
 
hospitals/institutions a nd to monitor organ and tissue transplant services and bring about 
policy and Programme corrections/changes whenever needed.  
 
3.14.4 The Expected outcomes of the programme have been reported as (a) Increase in 
deceased organ donation rate per million popu lation; (b) Enhanced transparency and 
efficiency in organ allocation; (c) Strengthened public sector transplant infrastructure; (d) 
Improved access to life -saving organ transplants for patients on the national waiting list; and 
(e) Creation of a sustainable, ethical and equitable national transplantation ecosystem. 
 
3.14.5 During the year 2025, significant physical progress has been achieved under the 
National Organ Transplant Programme (NOTP), as detailed below: 
 
A.  Institutional Expansion and Networking 
a) Four new SOTTOs in North -Eastern States and one SOTTO in Delhi have been 
established, further strengthening the national network. 
b) The three -tier structure now comprises one NOTTO at national level, five 
ROTTOs at regional level, and 26 SOTTOs at State/UT level. 
c) During 2025, an additional 126 transplant centres, 20 NTORCs, and 66 
Biomaterial Centres were linked with the NOTTO portal. 
d) The network is currently linked with more than 1,100 hospitals, including 782 
transplant centres, 154 Non -Transplant Organ Retrie val Centres (NTORCs), and 
182 Tissue Banks (Biomaterial Centres). 
 
B.  Digital Strengthening and Registry 
a) A total of 2,58,695 new Aadhaar -verified pledges were registered on the NOTTO 
digital pledge portal during 2025. 
b) CDAC has been engaged for development of  a new robust NOTTO website, 
mobile application, and upgraded National Digital Registry for donors, recipients 
and hospital networking. 
 
C. Organ Donation Promotion and Public Awareness 
a) The 15th Indian Organ Donation Day (IODD) was observed on 2nd August 2025 , 
during which States, hospitals and professionals were felicitated and families of 
deceased donors were honoured. 
b) A nationwide campaign ―Angdaan – Jeevan Sanjeevani Abhiyaan‖ was 
undertaken to promote organ donation. 
c) Various publications were released, in cluding Organ Donation Awareness 
Booklet, Booklet on promotion of organ health through Ayurveda and Yoga, 
Annual Report 2024 –25, Compilation of best practices of States and NOTTO e -
Newsletter. 
 
D. Capacity Building and Technical Initiatives 
a) Multiple training programmes were conducted for Transplant Coordinators (TCs) 
and other manpower for capacity building. 

96 
 
b) Organ Retrieval Workshops were organized in coordination with skill centre 
including private centres like Amrita Medical Institute, Faridabad. 
c) States and hospitals were advised to promote Swap Kidney Exchange 
Transplantation, with detailed procedural guidance issued. 
d) Amendments to THOT Rules, 2025 were notified to strengthen cornea 
transplantation facilities especially in rural areas. 
 
E.  Transplant Performance and National Standing 
a) India continues to rank third globally in total organ transplants and first in living 
donor transplantation. 
b) Total transplants increased from 4,990 in 2013 to 18,911 in 2024. 
c) Deceased donor transplants increased from 837 in 2013 to 3,403 in 2024. 
d) More than 4.7 lakh citizens have registered Aadhaar -authenticated pledges since 
launch of the digital portal in September 2023. 
 
3.14.6 Overall, the year 2025 witnessed expansion of institutional infrastructure, 
strengthening of digital systems, enhanced public awareness, capacity building of manpower, 
and sustained growth in organ donation and transplantation activities across the country. The 
earmarked allocation of ₹15 crore in BE 2026–27 will be appropriated primarily as Grants-in-
Aid to Regions, States & Institutions towards (i) Establishment and strengthening of office 
infrastructure; (ii) Meeting recurring operational expenditure and remuneration of engaged 
manpower; (iii) Capacity building and structured training of transplant coordin ators and 
hospital-based teams; (iv) Intensified public awareness campaigns and IEC activities; and (v) 
Strengthening organ allocation mechanisms and National Digital Registry systems. 
 
3.14.7 On being asked for the reasons of reduction of funds from ₹ 15.00 crore in BE 2024 -
25 to ₹ 10.00 crore in RE 2024 -25 and even low actual expenditure of ₹ 7.05 crore, the 
Department submitted that the scheme is implemented through States and organizations. All 
states are regularly requested and followed up through meetings to send proposals for availing 
the grants available in Program like Infrastructure development, capacity building, training, 
hiring of transplant coordinators, providing immunosuppressant medicines to BPL post -
transplant recipients, disbursing grants for dignified funeral and maintaining deceased donors 
and carrying out awareness activities.  
 
3.14.8 The Committee notes that despite allocations of ₹15 crore in FY 2024 –25 and FY 
2025–26, actual expenditure stood at only ₹7.05 crore and ₹9.03 crore (till January 2026) 
respectively, reflecting persistent under -utilisation of fund s under NOTP. The 
Committee expresses concern that delays in submission of proposals by States and 
institutions, coupled with limited uptake of grants for infrastructure development, 
training, and awareness activities, are constraining the full potential of the Programme. 
The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department should establish a 
structured fund utilisation framework, including mandatory annual action plans from 
States, time -bound release of grants, and quarterly monitoring of expenditure against 
physical targets. Further, the Committee urges the Department to incentivise States to 
strengthen transplant infrastructure in public hospitals, hire transplant coordinators, 

97 
 
and expand awareness campaigns. Such measures will ensure that the earmarked ₹15 
crore alloca tion for FY 2026 –27 is absorbed effectively, thereby enhancing 
transparency, efficiency, and inter-region equity in organ transplantation services. 
 
3.14.9 The Committee observes that while India has made significant progress in organ 
transplantation, rank ing third globally in total transplants and first in living donor 
transplants, the deceased donor rate remains relatively low, with challenges such as non-
declaration of brain death, lack of sensitisation among doctors, and reluctance of 
families even afte r pledges by the deceased. The Committee is concerned that these 
barriers undermine the objective of creating a sustainable and equitable transplantation 
ecosystem. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department should intensify 
efforts to promote deceased organ donation by mandating training for doctors on brain 
death certification, strengthening the role of transplant coordinators in family 
counselling, and expanding nationwide IEC campaigns such as Angdaan – Jeevan 
Sanjeevani Abhiyaan. These steps will help increase the deceased donor rate per million 
population and ensure equitable access to life -saving transplants for patients on the 
national waiting list. 
 
3.14.10 The Committee further recommends closer inter -sectoral coordination 
among diffe rent agencies to ensure timely retrieval and transplantation of organs. 
Preventive measures such as early diagnosis and management of non -communicable 
diseases like diabetes and hypertension should also be integrated into awareness 
campaigns to reduce orga n damage. The Committee is also concerned about the high 
cost of organ transplantation and the financial burden it places on patients and their 
families. The Committee  therefore, recommends that the Department should take steps 
to make transplantation mor e affordable by ensuring inclusion of all transplant 
procedures under Government social insurance schemes like PMJAY, reducing the cost 
of immunosuppressant drugs through measures like reduction in taxes and supporting 
manufacturing of quality generic drug s in India, and considering their coverage under 
insurance schemes at least for the first month post -surgery. These measures, alongside 
improved coordination and preventive strategies, will help create a more equitable and 
accessible transplantation ecosystem in the country. 
 
3.14.11 The Committee strongly recommends that organ transplantation facility 
should be encouraged / developed in rural areas of the country. Centers/ Medical 
Colleges providing affordable transplant services be designated/ encouraged as Centre 
for Excellence and be provided with additional autonomy. 
 
3.15 NATIONAL TELE MENTAL HEALTH PROGRAMME  
 
3.15.1 Mental health disorders constitute a significant public health challenge in India, with a 
substantial treatment gap due to stigma, shorta ge of trained professionals, geographical 
barriers, and limited access to services, particularly in rural and remote areas. Strengthening 
accessible, affordable, and quality mental health services is a national priority under the 
National Mental Health Pro gramme (NMHP) and the District Mental Health Programme 
(DMHP).  

98 
 
3.15.2 The Committee recommends for introduction of the Indian Knowledge System 
programme on lines of the UGC mand ated programmes in all Medical/ Dental/ 
Pharmacy / Nursing / Allied Healthcare councils/institutes of the country to address the 
burden of Mental Health. This should be integrated with the National Tele Mental 
Health Programme. 
 
3.15.3 In this context, the National Tele Mental Health Programme (NTMHP), branded as 
Tele MANAS (Tele Men tal Health Assistance and Networking Across States), was launched 
on 10th October 2022 to provide free, round -the-clock mental health support through 
telecommunication technologies. Tele MANAS functions as a digital extension of the District 
Mental Health Programme and aims to bridge the treatment gap by leveraging technology -
enabled service delivery. The objectives of the program are: - 
 
i. To exponentially scale up the reach of mental health services to anybody who 
reaches out, across India, any time, by set ting up a 24x7 tele -mental health 
facility in each of the States and UTs of the country.  
 
ii. To implement a fully -fledged mental health service network that, in addition to 
counselling, provides integrated medical and psychosocial interventions 
including vid eo consultations with mental health specialists, e -prescriptions, 
follow-up services and linkages to in-person services.  
 
iii. To extend services to vulnerable groups of the population and difficult to reach 
populations.

3.15.4 The allocation under the Revenue head was ₹90.00 crore at BE 2024 –25, which was 
reduced to ₹45.00 crore at RE stage, with an actual expenditure of ₹41.73 crore during the 
year. In 2025 –26, the allocation was ₹79.60 crore at BE sta ge and was again revised 
downward to ₹45.00 crore at RE stage, against which an expenditure of ₹28.82 crore had been 
incurred up to 31.01.2026. For 2026 –27, the allocation has been proposed at ₹51.14 crore 
under the Revenue head. The budget outlay 2025 -26 till date included budget for 36 
States/UTs, Mentoring Institutes and Apex Institutes, however, the budget outlay for 2026 -27 
will be appropriated for Apex Institute and 22 Mentoring Institutes for the budget allocation 
in the heads mentioned in the operational guidelines.  
 
3.15.5 On being asked for the reasons of reduction in fund allocation from ₹ 79.60 crore (BE 
2025-26) to ₹ 45 crore (RE 2025 -26) and reduced budget outlay of ₹ 51.14 crore in BE 2026 -
27, the Department reported that the fund allocation was reduced from ₹ 79.60 Crore to ₹ 45 
Crore due to transition of States/UTs component to Centrally sponsored scheme from Central 
scheme. The reasons behind the reduction in the allocation due to low expenditure in the 

99 
 
initial years and savings under the s cheme are (i) delay in recruitment process of Human 
resources under NTMHP by States; (ii) the expenditure under AMC, was factored on the basis 
of peak capacity of cells to handle calls. However initially the number of calls was less and 
has grown exponentially since then; and (iii) all the States / UTs / Institutes were mapped as 
Central Nodal Agency (CNA) which took considerable time and effort causing delay in 
release of funds to States. The trend reflects a reduction of funds at the RE stage because 
expenditure is aligned more closely with realistic operational needs which is anticipated 
accurately in the mid -year review. The budget outlay has been reduced to 51.14 crores 
because only the budget for Apex Institute i.e. NIMHANS and 22 mentoring Institutes will be 
taken care and the States/UTs part will be funded through NHM under Centrally sponsored 
scheme. 
 
3.15.6 Current Status of the program as on 31.12.2025: - Tele MANAS is Operational in all 
36 States/ UT‘s through 53 functional Tele MANAS cells (inclu ding 2 additional cells at 
AFMC Pune and at Nagpur in collaboration with Aditya Birla Empower Initiative Group). 
Nearly 32 lakh calls have been handled through the helpline number (14416) since launch 
of the program with Counsellors engaging in more than 2 0 different languages. The total 
number of calls handled by the Tele MANAS cells are projected to increase by 5500 calls 
each month.  
 
3.15.7 Tele MANAS services include Tier -1 comprising the State Tele MANAS cells, 
which includes trained counsellors and me ntal health specialists. Tier 2 which comprise 
specialists at District Mental Health Programme (DMHP)/Medical College resources for 
physical consultation and/or Video calling feature for audio visual consultation. Video 
consultations through e -Sanjeevani i nclude e -Prescription services, enabling secure, 
personalised treatment without in -person visits.  Steps taken for Tele MANAS Application 
enhancements -  
a. Expansion of multilingual interface to 10 additional regional languages (Assamese, 
Bengali, Gujarati, Kannada, Malayalam, Marathi, Tamil, Telugu, Odia, Punjabi), apart 
from existing English and Hindi. 
b. Accessibility improvements for visually impaired users - Enhanced design and 
navigation for persons with disabilities, including screen reader compatibi lity and user-
friendly features for an inclusive digital experience. 
c. Chat interface integration for improving usability and user experience - AI-enabled 
chatbot has been introduced to provide users with pre -defined, easy-to-access responses 
to common queries, designed based on feedback from Tele-MANAS users. 
d. Emergency response content has been incorporated to ensure timely guidance and 
support during crises - A dedicated module within the Tele -MANAS app addressing 
mental health challenges during natio nal or state emergencies. Includes guidance for 
civilians as well as families of Armed Forces personnel suggesting methods to cope with 
the emergency situations.  
3.15.8 The Committee notes that despite allocations of ₹90 crore in FY 2024 –25 and 
₹79.60 cro re in FY 2025 –26, actual expenditure stood at only ₹41.73 crore and ₹28.82 

100 
 
crore respectively, reflecting persistent under -utilisation of funds. The Committee 
expresses concern that delays in recruitment of human resources, slow mapping of 
States/UTs as Ce ntral Nodal Agencies, and initial low call volumes have constrained 
the pace of implementation. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the 
Department should institute a time -bound recruitment and deployment framework 
for counsellors and mental health pr ofessionals, ensuring adequate staffing across all 
Tele MANAS cells. The Committee further urges the Department to strengthen 
monitoring mechanisms through a real -time digital dashboard, linking fund release to 
measurable outputs such as call volumes, coun selling sessions, referrals, and follow -
ups. Special emphasis should be placed on expanding accessibility of Tele MANAS 
services to rural and remote populations by identifying underserved districts and 
leveraging community health workers, thereby expanding  the reach of Tele MANAS 
and bridging the treatment gap in mental health services.  
 
3.15.9 The Committee observes that Tele MANAS has handled nearly 32 lakh calls 
since its launch, with multilingual counselling support and integration of video 
consultation s through e -Sanjeevani. While these achievements are commendable, the 
Committee is concerned that affordability, inclusivity, and emerging mental health 
challenges require further attention. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the 
Department should e xpand the scope of Tele MANAS by integrating primary mental 
healthcare services into Ayushman Arogya Mandirs, introducing peer education 
programmes for mental health in schools and colleges, and addressing digital 
addiction through dedicated counselling mo dules. The Committee further 
recommends strengthening affordability by ensuring coverage of mental health 
consultations under Government insurance schemes like PMJAY, and by promoting 
workplace-based occupational mental health initiatives. Interdisciplinar y research on 
the linkages between lifestyle factors, such as ultra -processed food consumption 
highlighted in the Economic Survey 2025 –26, and mental health disorders should also 
be supported to inform preventive strategies. These measures will ensure that  Tele 
MANAS evolves into a comprehensive, inclusive, and sustainable national mental 
health support system.  
 
3.16 NATIONAL DIGITAL HEALTH MISSION (NDHM)/ AYUSHMAN BHARAT 
DIGITAL MISSION (ABDM): 
3.16.1 Ayushman Bharat Digital Mission (ABDM), earlier known a s the National Digital 
Health Mission (NDHM), was launched by the Government of India in September 2021 to 
support the development of an integrated, citizen -centric national digital health ecosystem. 
Using ABDM -enabled software, health facilities can creat e digital health records linked to 
Ayushman Bharat Health Account (ABHA). Also, they can access the previous records of the 
patient digitally after taking the patient‘s consent. This enables seamless interoperability 
across the ecosystem. The core componen ts of ABDM‘s technological architecture include 
three registries for providing a trustable identity across healthcare stakeholders in the 
ecosystem: Ayushman Bharat Health Account (ABHA) for citizens, Healthcare Professional 
Registry (HPR) for healthcare p rofessionals, and Health Facility Registry (HFR) for health 
facilities. Additionally, three gateways facilitate seamless healthcare information exchange 

101 
 
ensuring interoperability: Health Information Consent Manager (HIE -CM), the National 
Health Claims Exchange (NHCX), and Unified Health Interface (UHI). 
 
3.16.2 ABDM is improving access to healthcare services and the management of health 
records through the creation of longitudinal health records. This mission seeks to enable 
individuals/ patients to build a  continuous and comprehensive recording of their health 
information over time using their Ayushman Bharat Health Account (ABHA) numbers. As on 
12th Feb 2026, a total of 85,82,71,270 (~85.82 Cr) Ayushman Bharat Health Accounts 
(ABHAs) have been created, 4,6 1,271 (~4.61 Lac) health facilities have been registered on 
HFR, 8,05,309 (~8.05 Lac) healthcare professionals have been registered on HPR and 
87,63,76,511 (~87.63 Cr) health records have been linked with ABHA. 
 
3.16.3 The infrastructure required for devel opment of interoperable digital platforms & 
certain modules under ABDM has broadly been assessed and identified in line with the 
objectives of ABDM. For administrative support, various policy frameworks such as ‗Health 
Data Management policy‘, ‗Sandbox Gui delines‘, ‗Health Information Provider Guidelines‘ 
etc. have been prepared and issued for smooth implementation of ABDM. National Health 
Authority (NHA) provides necessary support including technical assistance and training for 
all stakeholders to integrat e their solutions under the ABDM ecosystem. ABDM is 
implemented in all States as a Central Sector Scheme under which States are provided funds 
for supporting the human resources deployed for implementation of ABDM, capacity building 
of various stakeholders and IEC (Information, Education & Communication).

increased to ₹225.00 crore at RE stage, with an actual expenditure of ₹212.58 crore during the 
year. In 2025–26, the allocation was significantly enhanced to ₹340.11 crore at BE stage and 
slightly reduced to ₹324.26 crore at RE stage, against which an expenditure of ₹202.77 crore 
had been incurred up to 31.01.2026. For 2026 –27, the allocation has been proposed at 
₹350.00 crore under the Revenue head.  
 
3.16.5 The achievements during FY 2025‑26 (as on 14.02.2026) are (i) 85.83 crore ABHA 
IDs created; (ii) 87.66 crore health records linked; (iii) 4.61 la kh facilities in HFR; (iv) 8.05 
lakh professionals in HPR; and (v) 2.53 lakh facilities use ABDM enabled software. A public 
dashboard, https://dashboard.abdm.gov.in  is available for National Health Authority (NH A), 
the implementing body of ABDM, and the states/union territories to assess the different 
indicators reflecting the progress of the scheme.  
 
3.16.6 For the FY 2026 -27, the earmarked funds of ₹ 350 crores is planned to be used for 
enhancing the IT tools built under ABDM and accelerated adoption of ABDM. These would 

102 
 
comprise activities such as development of use cases, expansion of ABHA, strengthening 
registries, upgrading software, interoperability, awareness and cybersecurity, capacity 
building of health care ecosystem, partnering with academic institutions, leveraging AI etc. 
ABDM‘s aim is primarily to create a digital ecosystem which includes Software platforms, 
Registries, Digital services, Interoperability frameworks. Such expenditure falls under 
Revenue head. 
 
3.16.7 The Department furnished the State -wise and year -wise figures for consolidated 
allocation to States/UTs and funds released under ABDM for FY 2021 -22 to 2025 -26 (as on 
31.12.2025). The State/UT -wise allocation and release pattern during FY 2021–22 to FY 
2025–26 indicates significant variations between allocations and actual fund releases, with 
several States/UTs receiving partial or no releases in certain years. Notably, in FY 2021 –22, 
many States/UTs including Delhi, Odisha, Telangana, Utta r Pradesh and several UTs 
recorded zero releases despite allocations. Similar instances of nil releases are observed in 
subsequent years for some States such as Delhi (no fund released from 2021 -22 to 2024 -25 
and the first tranche of fund amounting to ₹ 0.76 crore released only in 2025 -26), Odisha (nil 
fund released from 2021 -22 to till 31.12.2025) and Telangana. Although the overal l releases 
have shown a gradual increase from ₹4.20 crore in FY 2021 –22 to ₹61.94 crore in FY 2025 –
26, the pattern reflects uneven fund flow across States/UTs and intermittent years of zero fund 
release in certain cases. The total details for all States/UTs are reproduced as under: -

3.16.8 On a specific query related to inter -operability and privacy, the Department stated that 
ABDM ensures  privacy by design so that health data is exchanged between the intended 
stakeholders on ABDM network only after the patient's consent. Also, there is no centralised 
repository of health data. These measures collectively ensure that interoperability does n ot 
compromise citizens' privacy and data protection. Health Data Management Policy (HDM 
Policy) was released on 14th Dec 2020 by the Ministry of Health and Family Welfare, 
Government of India. It is a guidance document that sets out the minimum standards f or 
privacy and data protection that should be followed by all the participants/stakeholders of the 
Ayushman Bharat Digital Mission (ABDM) ecosystem. Health data management policy 
specifies that no data shall be shared with any other entity, including insur ance and 
pharmaceutical companies without consent of the individual. 
 
3.16.9 The Department further submitted that using an ABDM enabled software, government 
hospitals can create digital health records linked to Ayushman Bharat Health Account 
(ABHA). Also, they can access the previous records of the patient digitally after taking the 
patient‘s consent. This enables seamless interoperability across the ecosystem. In addition, 
several national health programme platforms including Pradhan Mantri Jan Arogya Yoj ana 
(PM-JAY), Nikshay portal, Reproductive and Child Health (RCH) and Non -Communicable 

103 
 
Diseases (NCD) are integrated with ABDM enabling government hospitals in the States/UTs 
to create inter-operable health records. ABDM actively promotes awareness about the benefits 
of ABHA and the aforesaid privacy preserving measures among healthcare providers and 
institutions, including the private sector, through participation in events such as medical 
conferences, technology events etc. encouraging the creation of ABH A and adoption of 
digital healthcare. States/UTs also undertake targeted IEC activities and capacity building to 
enhance adoption among the various healthcare stakeholders, including the private sector. As 
a result of the aforesaid efforts, over 87 Crore i nteroperable health records have been created 
through ABDM enabled software, enabling these records to be securely and seamlessly 
exchanged among different healthcare providers of the healthcare ecosystem. 
 
3.16.10 On being asked about the measures taken f or ensuring the execution of the 
Mission in rural and tribal areas which lack both digital connectivity and literacy, the 
Department replied that the Government has taken various steps to ensure that the benefits 
of the mission reach every citizen. Inclusi on is one of the key principles of ABDM. The 
digital health ecosystem created by ABDM supports continuity of care across primary, 
secondary and tertiary healthcare in a seamless manner. It aids availability of health care 
services particularly in remote an d rural areas through various technology interventions 
like telemedicine etc. ABDM has provisions for assisted mode in places where internet 
connectivity might be poor. For instance, offline mode for creation of ABHA has been 
enabled wherever internet conn ectivity might be poor or there would be unavailability of 
hardware or both.  
 
3.16.11 The Committee notes with serious concern the glaring discrepancies 
between the consolidated budgetary allocations and the actual fund releases to 
States/UTs under the Ayu shman Bharat Digital Mission (ABDM). The data reveals a 
pattern of intermittent or complete stagnation in fund flow, most notably in States 
like Odisha, which recorded absolute zero releases from FY 2021 -22 through 
December 2025, and Delhi, which received its first marginal tranche only in FY 2025 -
26. Even in 2025 -26, fund release to Telangana and Odisha is nil as on 31.12.2025. The 
total fund released to all States/UTs in FY 2021 -22 was 25% of the total allocation, 
which has been improved to 53% in FY 2025 -26 (as on 31.12.2025). S uch disparities 
undermine uniform adoption of ABDM across the country. The Committee is of the 
view that such persistent bottlenecks indicate deep -rooted administrative challenges 
at the State level, likely stemming from delays in submitting Utilization Certificates 
(UCs) or a lack of technical capacity to implement NHA guidelines. The Committee, 
therefore, recommends that the Department should constitute a dedicated 'State -
Level Facilitation and Capacity Building Taskforce.' This t askforce should 
proactively engage with lagging States to resolve procedural deadlocks, streamline the 
submission of UCs, provide targeted technical assistance and capacity building for 
lagging States  and ensure that the allocated human resources and Infor mation, 
Education & Communication (IEC) funds are efficiently utilized to drive the mission 
at the grassroots level.  
 
3.16.12 The Committee believes that while the creation of nearly 85.83 crore 

104 
 
Ayushman Bharat Health Accounts (ABHAs) and the linking of 87 .66 crore health 
records are highly commendable achievements, there remains a critical lag in the 
corresponding adoption of the ecosystem by healthcare providers. With only 4.61 lakh 
facilities registered on the Health Facility Registry (HFR) and a mere 2. 53 lakh 
facilities actively utilizing ABDM -enabled software, the foundational goal of seamless 
interoperability is at risk of underutilization. The Committee recommends that the 
₹350.00 crore Revenue allocation for FY 2026 -27 be strategically front -loaded toward 
incentivizing provider onboarding. The Department should accelerate the deployment 
of the Unified Health Interface (UHI) and the National Health Claims Exchange 
(NHCX), demonstrating tangible administrative and financial benefits, such as 
expedited insurance claim settlements to private and public health facilities. By 
shifting the focus from mere citizen enrollment to driving active software adoption at 
the clinic and hospital level, the Department can truly operationalize the longitudinal 
health records ecosystem.  
 
3.16.13 The Committee observes that while ABDM has achieved remarkable 
progress with over 85 crore ABHA IDs and 87 crore linked health records, challenges 
remain in extending services to rural and tribal populations due to poor digital 
connectivity and limited digital literacy, alongside rising concerns about privacy and 
cybersecurity. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department should 
prioritise offline and assisted modes of ABHA creation, strengthen telemedicine 
services, an d leverage Ayushman Arogya Mandirs and community health workers to 
expand accessibility in underserved areas. At the same time, the Committee urges the 
Department to establish a robust national cybersecurity framework for digital health, 
including regular audits, compliance standards, and intensified IEC campaigns to 
build public trust by clearly communicating privacy safeguards and citizens‘ rights 
under the Health Data Management Policy.  
 
3.17 SCHEME FOR ALLIED HEALTH CARE PROFESSIONALS (AHPs)  
 
3.17.1 Allied Health Care Professionals (AHPs) scheme has been introduced in Budget 2026-
27 to support up -gradation of existing institutions, establish new Allied Healthcare 
Professionals Institutions (AHPIs) to trains Allied Health Professionals (AHPs) etc. in pri vate 
and Government sectors. This will cover 10 selected disciplines, including optometry, 
radiology, anesthesia, OT Technology, Applied Psychology and Behavioural Health and add 
100,000 AHPs over the next 5 years. 
 
3.17.2 The Department , in its submission , stated that modern healthcare is a team -based 
work. While doctors and nurses remain indispensable, it is the Allied and Healthcare 
Professionals; the technicians, technologists, therapists;, and others, who form the backbone 
of a functional, responsive, and efficient healthcare system. These professionals are critical to 
diagnostics, therapeutics, rehabilitation, and preventative care. India is expected to have ~20% 
population over 60 years by 2050. 65% of disease burden now arises from Non -
Communicable D iseases requiring diagnostics, imaging, pathology, rehabilitation, 
behavioural health, and long-term care. India now has over 70,000+ private hospitals, 1.5 lakh 

105 
 
labs, and 30,000 imaging centers creating continuous demand. MRI, CT, molecular 
diagnostics, genomics, and digital pathology require highly trained technologists. 
 
3.17.3 India has estimated over 500 government AHP institutes having 47,930 seats vs 
~3,800 private institutes with 3,63,742 seats across various disciplines. However, there are 
significant variations across institutes in physical infrastructure, laboratories and equipment, 
trained faculty and other facilities. This inconsistency affects workforce readiness and 
national standards being laid down by NCAHP. The operationalization of the Nat ional 
Commission for Allied and Healthcare Professions (NCAHP) under the NCAHP Act, 2021 
has now filled this regulatory gap. With 26 States/UTs having established their respective 
Allied and Healthcare Councils, the ecosystem is ready for a coordinated nat ional expansion 
in this vital sector. NCAHP has already launched 15 curriculums covering 24 professions 
from among the 57 allied and healthcare professions regulated by the NCAHP Act, 2021. 
These curriculums are mandatory for the institutes from the academic year 2026-27.  
 
3.17.4 The establishment of new allied and healthcare institutes in the government sector will 
motivate prospective students to consider this field as a respectable and promising career 
option. It will also expand opportunities for stude nts from marginalized and disadvantaged 
backgrounds. Further, the upgradation of existing allied and healthcare institutions to comply 
with the regulatory standards of NCAHP, along with enhancement of intake capacity or 
introduction of new courses, will en sure optimal utilization of existing resources and promote 
the sustainable and cost -effective growth of allied and health education. Over time, private 
institutions are expected to align with the standards prescribed by the National Commission 
for Allied a nd Healthcare Professions (NCAHP), thereby contributing to the achievement of 
the target of producing 1,00,000 Allied Health Professionals within five years. The Ministry 
of Health and Family Welfare is drafting a Central Sector Scheme for ‗Strengthening o f allied 
and healthcare professions‘ involving two components: (i) Establishing of new allied and 
healthcare institutes; and (ii) Upgradation of existing medical/ allied and healthcare institutes 
for starting of a new allied and healthcare course or for in crease of seat capacity. The Scheme 
will be spread over a period of 5 years The Scheme will cover State Govt. Medical/Allied and 
Healthcare Institutes, Central Govt. medical/Allied and Healthcare Institutes and any allied 
healthcare/ medical institute in the UT with 100% funding. 
3.17.5 In the FY 2026 -27, the budget of ₹ 1000 Crore has already been allocated. The gap 
analysis of existing institutions in terms of the norms specified by NCAHP is already in 
process. This will also include consultation with all the State Governments to  understand the 
present situation of allied health institutes, the courses being run, the seat capacity and allied 
health courses which the States are interested in from among the 10 disciplines which were 
announced in the Budget. Subsequently, based on th e proposals that will be received from the 
states, few states will be selected this year for initiation of the scheme. The funds will be 
allocated in the two components depending upon the proposal from those States. 
 
3.17.6 The Committee believes that the introduction of the new Scheme for Allied 
Health Care Professionals (AHPs) with a ₹1000 Crore allocation for FY 2026 -27 is a 
landmark step toward addressing the shifting disease burden, where 65% of cases now 

106 
 
arise from Non-Communicable Diseases requiring specialized diagnostics and long-term 
care. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department prioritize the 
establishment and upgradation of government -led Allied Healthcare Professionals 
Institutions (AHPIs) to balance the current disparity where ~88% of seats are currently 
concentrated in private institutes. To ensure the target of 100,000 AHPs within five 
years is met with clinical excellence, the Department must move beyond mere 
infrastructure development and focus on the strict implementation o f the 15 mandatory 
National Commission for Allied and Healthcare Professions ( NCAHP) curriculums 
across all selected  disciplines, including high -demand areas like Molecular Diagnostics, 
Genomics, OT Technology and Digital Pathology. The Committee is furthe r of the view 
that the proposed "Gap Analysis" of existing institutions in terms of norms specified by 
NCAHP must be concluded in a time -bound manner to ensure that the 100% funding 
for State and Central institutes is utilized for high -fidelity laboratorie s and advanced 
imaging equipment that mirror the requirements of modern private -sector labs and 
hospitals.  
 
3.17.7 The Committee recommends that the Department ensure timely and effective 
implementation of the proposed Central Sector Scheme for strengthen ing allied and 
healthcare professions. While the scheme rightly focuses on establishing new institutes 
and upgrading existing ones with 100% funding over five years, the Committee urges 
the Department to adopt a phased rollout plan with clear annual target s to expand seat 
capacity and introduce new courses in line with national health workforce requirements. 
Further, the Committee recommends prioritizing geographic equity by ensuring that 
Tier-2 and Tier-3 cities, as well as underserved regions, benefit fro m new institutes and 
upgraded facilities. Strong monitoring mechanisms, integration with State Programme 
Implementation Plans, and alignment with Indian Public Health Standards ( IPHS) 
norms will ensure that the scheme not only expands educational capacity but also builds 
a sustainable pipeline of skilled allied and healthcare professionals to strengthen India‘s 
health system. 
3.18 STATUTORY AND REGULATORY BODIES  
 
3.18.1 The following statutory and regulatory bodies come under the purview of the 
Department of Health and Family Welfare: -  
 
i. Food Safety and Standards Authority of India (FSSAI)  has been established under 
the Food Safety and Standards Act, 2006 for laying down the science based 
standards for articles of food and to regulate their manufacture, sto rage, 
distribution, sale and import, to ensure availability of safe and wholesome food for 
human consumption.  
 
ii. Indian Pharmacopeia Commission ( IPC) provide for performing activities to 
Publish Indian Pharmacopeia and its Addendum, to develop and validate the 
Indian Pharmacopeia Reference Substances (IPRS) and Impurity Standards, Skill 
Development of Drugs Analyst, Drugs Inspectors and Stakeholders etc.  
 

107 
 
iii. National Medical Commission (NMC) is a statutory body with the responsibility 
of establishing and maint aining high standards of medical education and 
recognition of medical qualifications in India. It registers doctors to practice in 
India, in order to protect and promote the health and safety of the public by 
ensuring proper standards in the practice of medicine.  
 
iv. Dental Council of India (DCI) is a Statutory Body incorporated under an Act of 
Parliament viz. The Dentists Act, 1948 (XVI of 1948) to regulate the Dental 
Education and the profession of Dentistry throughout India.  
 
v. Pharmacy Council of India (PC I) is a statutory body governed to regulate 
Pharmacy education and profession upto graduate level and prescribe minimum 
standard of education required for qualifying as pharmacist.  
 
vi. Indian Nursing Council (INC) is a statutory body constituted by the Centr al 
Government under section 3(1) of the Indian Nursing Council Act, 1947 to 
establish a uniform standard of training for nurses, Midwives and health visitors.  
 
vii. NAMS is a unique institution which fosters and utilizes academic excellence as its 
resource to meet medical and social goals, established under Societies Registration 
Act XXI of 1860.  
 
viii. National Board of Examinations (NBE) is an independent autonomous body of its 
kind in the country in the field of medical sciences with the prime objective of 
improving the quality of the medical education by elevating the level and 
establishing standards of post graduate examinations in modern medicine on all 
India basis. 
 
ix. National Commission for Allied and Healthcare Professions (NCAHP) Act, as a 
common regulatory body, provides for regulation and maintenance of standards of 
education and services by Allied and Healthcare professionals, assessment of 
institutions, maintenance of a Central Register and State Register, and creation of a 
system to improve access, resear ch and development and adoption of latest 
scientific advancement and connected matters.

3.18.3 The total allocation for Regulatory Bodies in 2024 –25 was ₹640.71 crore at BE stage 
and reduced to ₹529.97 crore at RE stage, with actual expenditure of ₹518.08 crore. For 
2025–26, the allocation was ₹547.45 crore at BE stage and ₹493.15 crore at RE stage, against 
which ₹456.31 cr ore had been spent up to 17.02.2026. For 2026 –27, the allocation has been 
proposed at ₹494.92 crore. 
 
3.18.4 The Committee observes that several statutory regulatory bodies under the 
Department, such as the National Medical Commission  (NMC), Dental Council of India 
(DCI), Pharmacy Council of India  (PCI), Indian Nursing Council  (INC), and the 
National Board of Examinations  (NBE), receive only nominal budgetary allocations 
from the Union Government, whereas emerging bodies such as the National Commission 
for Allied and Healthcare Professions (NCAHP) have been provided comparatively 
higher allocations for establishing regulatory frameworks and institutional systems. 
Considering the increasing demand for quality healthcare professionals and the need to 
maintain uniform standards in medical, dental, pharmacy, nursing, and allied health 
education, the Committee recommends that the Department strengthen coordination 
among these regulatory bodies and promote greater convergence in regulatory 
practices, accreditation mechanisms, and digital registries of professionals. The 
Committee emphasises the need to support the operationalisation of the National 
Commission for Allied and Healthcare Professions so that the large and diverse allied 
health workforce in the country can be effectively regulated, trained, and integrated into 
the healthcare delivery system. 
3.18.5 The Committee observes a concerning, systemic trend of downward budgetary 
revisions at the Revised Estimate (RE) stage across regulatory bodies, culminating in  a 
reduction from ₹547.45 crore (BE) to ₹493.15 crore (RE) in FY 2025 -26. The Committee 
notes with specific concern the chronic underutilization of funds by the National 
Commission for Allied and Healthcare Professions (NCAHP), which recorded an actual 
expenditure of merely ₹0.75 crore against a BE of ₹20.00 crore in 2024 -25. The 
Committee believes that such financial inertia severely cripples the standard -setting 
mandates of these nascent statutory bodies. The Committee, therefore, recommends that 
the Department institute a mandatory, quarterly expenditure review mechanism for all 
statutory councils. This oversight is critical to ensure that the ₹494.92 crore allocated for 
FY 2026-27 is fully absorbed and translated into actionable regulatory outcomes rathe r 
than being surrendered. 

109 
 
3.18.6 The Committee is concerned to note that the bodies like NMC/PCI/ DCI/ Central 
Drugs Standard Control Organisation ( CDSCO) have been collecting hundred of cores 
of Rupees in the name of licensing/ regulation. The Committee r ecommends for proper 
audit, rationalisation of the ir expenses. The Committee further recommends that the 
licensing fees by CDSCO for Medical Devices need to be reduced by at least 75 % to 
make India Competitive as a low cost ma nufacturing supplier  in compa rison to 
countries like China. 
Food Safety and Standards Authority of India (FSSAI) 
 
3.18.7 The allocation for FSSAI in 2024 –25 was ₹620.00 crore at BE stage and ₹520.00 
crore at RE stage, with actual expenditure of ₹516.64 crore. For 2025 –26, the allocation was 
₹525.00 crore at BE stage and ₹470.56 crore at RE stage, with expenditure of ₹440.00 crore 
up to 17.02.2026. For 2026–27, the allocation is ₹465.02 crore. 
 
3.18.8 The Department in its submission stated that there are approximately 62 lakh Food 
Business Operators (FBOs) across the country, of which nearly 61 lakh operate under the 
jurisdiction of State Authorities. Therefore, state authorities perform pivotal role in regulating 
and enforcement of Food Safety and Standards Act, 2006 on field. As on 30 -11-2025, a total 
12,43,620 licenses and 57,12,694 registrations are active. The Continuous steps are being 
taken to simplify the procedure of licensing and registration of food businesses and 
digitisation of enforcement activities. Since 1st April 2025, 1 7 primary labs were notified 
increasing the number from 243 to 252. Further, 2 referral labs were notified increasing 
referral laboratories from 22 to 24. As of now, total 33 Microbiological Laboratories have 
been established with financial assistance from  FSSAI. In 2025 -26, 571188 food handlers 
were trained by FSSAI on safe food & hygiene practices. 
 
3.18.9 For Capacity Building of Laboratory Personnel, Training Programs and Participant 
Engagement are organised regularly. In the FY 2025 -26, 18 comprehensive training programs 
were conducted, training 507 participants from food testing laboratories across India. To 
strengthen the understanding of the food testing workflow and promote effective utilization of 
the FSSAI InFoLNeT (Indian Food Laboratory Network)  portal, six hands -on training 
programmes and 2 virtual trainings were organized for FSSAI Notified Food Testing 
Laboratories. FSSAI has provided financial grant to 10 Research Institutes under National 
Reference Laboratories scheme for undertaking researc h projects in their specified expertise 
domain. 
3.18.10 The Department further stated that to enhance food safety, transparency, and 
ease of doing business at all Points of Entry, FSSAI and CBIC have jointly implemented a 
Single Sign-On (SSO) functionality enabling Customs Officers notified as FSSAI Authorised 
Officers to access and operate the FICS platform seamlessly. As of date, the SSO facility has 
been implemented at all Points of Entry(PoE). FSSAI has successfully integrated its Food 
Import Clearance System (FICS) with the CBIC‘s ICEGATE platform under SWIFT 2.0, 
further strengthening the digital ecosystem for food import clearance.  

110 
 
3.18.11 FSSAI is extending technical as well as financial support to the States/UTs for 
strengthening the food safety ecosystem in the country through Memoranda of Understanding 
(MoUs). During FY 2025 –26, as on 22.12.2025, work plan proposals have been received 
from 33 States/UTs. Funds amounting to ₹306.31 crore have been approved for 21 
States/UTs, out of which an amount of ₹110 crores has been released to the States/UTs as the 
first tranche against the finalized work plans.  On a specific query, the Department provided 
the measures proposed to be undertaken in 2026-27 as follows: - 
a) Increase in threshold limit for registrat ion: T here are approximately 67 lakh 
Registration/Central/State Licenses issued to FBOs. Out of the 67 lakh around 55 
lakh registrations i.e. FBO having turnover up to 12 lakhs. The turnover was 
notified in FSSAI (Licensing and Registration of Food Busines ses) Regulations, 
2011. Thus, it is almost 15 years since the turnover was notified. Therefore, there 
is a need to revise this limit. Accordingly, keeping in view the limits prescribed 
under GST, it is proposed to revise these limits from 12 Lakh to 1.5 Cr.  
b) Perpetual license to all food business operator under the FSS Act, 2006: The 
provision for issuance of perpetual registration/licenses is under consideration. The 
draft amendment provides that a license/registration shall be valid and subsisting, 
unless otherwise suspended or cancelled . Renewal cycles of 1 –5 years under the 
2011 regulations are thus replaced with perpetual validity. The Food Authority is 
empowered to specify conditions/procedures in this regard by order.  
c) Digital integration with Nationa l Single Window System: FBOs have to obtain 
license/NOC from several departments in order to start a business. e.x. FSSAI 
license, Fire NOC, factory license, NOC from Pollution Control Board, Ground 
water NOC, Municipal license, GST, IEC, export/ import ce rtifications etc. 
depending upon kind of business. This requires submission of similar/ overlapping 
information/ data/ documents on various online portals causing involvement of 
consultants, time consuming, increased compliance burden, operational delays. 
FSSAI is already on -board with DPIIT for integration for few services. However, 
it has now been decided for complete integration of all online portals enabling 
one-time submission of common documents/ information by FBOs to obtain any 
license.  
d) Single Sign-On (SSO) system for Customs Officers notified as FSSAI Authorised 
Officers 
3.18.12 The Committee notes that the bulk of the allocation under Statutory and 
Regulatory Bodies is accounted for by the Food Safety and Standards Authority of India 
(FSSAI), whic h plays a critical role in regulating food safety standards across the 
country. The Committee observes that out of the 62 lakh Food Business Operators 
operating in the country, nearly 61 lakh operate squarely under the jurisdiction of State 
Authorities, making state-level enforcement the absolute linchpin of the Food Safety and 
Standards Act. The Committee observes that while ₹306.31 crore was approved under 
MoUs for 21 States/UTs in FY 2025 -26, only ₹110 crore has been disbursed as the first 
tranche. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department immediately 
expedite the release of the remaining funds.  

111 
 
3.18.13 The Committee observes the effective implementation of the Food Safety 
and Standards Act, 2006 requires strong coordination between the Centre and the 
States, along with adequate laboratory infrastructure and trained manpo wer. While the 
Department has taken several steps such as expansion of food testing laboratories, 
strengthening the Food Import Clearance System, and training of food handlers, the 
Committee recommends that the Department further augment laboratory capacit y, 
ensure uniform enforcement standards across States/UTs, and expand training 
programmes for food safety officials and food handlers. The Committee is of the view 
that FSSAI must strategically direct state -level grants toward expanding its current 
network of 252 primary and 24 referral laboratories. Funding should be specifically 
earmarked to establish high -tech microbiological laboratories at the district level to 
ensure robust, grassroots safety screening. 
3.18.14 The Committee believes that the proposed  regulatory amendments by 
FSSAI, specifically increasing the registration turnover threshold from ₹12 lakh to ₹1.5 
crore (to align with GST norms) and transitioning to perpetual licenses, are highly 
progressive steps necessary to reduce the compliance burd en on small Food Business 
Operators (FBOs). The Committee, therefore, recommends the swift gazette notification 
and implementation of these amendments. Furthermore, the Committee is of the view 
that the proposed complete digital integration of FSSAI's lice nsing system with the 
National Single Window System (DPIIT) must be executed in a strictly time -bound 
manner. This integration will eliminate the redundant submission of documents across 
various municipal and central departments, thereby truly actualizing a seamless 
operational environment for the country's 67 lakh registered food businesses. 
3.18.15 On a query about the Mobile Food Testing Labs, the Department provided that 
FSSAI is allocating funds to States/UTs for the procurement of fully equipped Mobil e Food 
Testing Labs (MFTLs). Out of 541 sanctioned MFTLs, 305 MFTLs are deployed across the 
nation in 35 States/UTs. During 2025 -26, MFTLs have facilitated 232,017 food testing, 
alongside conducting 9480 awareness campaigns and 9027 training sessions. Furt her, a total 
fund of 33.64 Cr. has been earmarked for the operational expenses of already deployed 
MFTLs through annual work plans established via Memoranda of Understanding (MoUs) 
with State/UT authorities based on the proposals received from State/UT. FS SAI is in process 
of finalising standard/model for next generation MFTLs and expected to be completed by 
May, 2026. Once finalized, procurement of 236 MFTLs will be completed in next 2 years.  
3.18.16 On a specific query, the Department provided the detail s of NABL accredited 
food testing laboratories nationwide and stated that FSSAI has notified 252 NABL -accredited 
primary food testing laboratories under the Food Safety and Standards Act, 2006 for the 
testing of food samples. In addition, 24 Referral Food Laboratories, all owned by Government 
departments and institutions, have been notified for the analysis of appellate samples. 
Accounting for 11 laboratories functioning as both primary and referral laboratories, the total 
number of FSSAI-notified laboratories stands at 265 across the country. 
3.18.17 From scrutiny of the details provided by the Department, the Committee noted 
that the total 252 primary laboratories notified by FSSAI, comprises of 61 State Government -
owned primary laboratories, 2 FSSAI -owned laboratories, 3 FSSAI laboratories operating in 

112 
 
PPP mode, 28 laboratories owned by other Government institutions, and 158 private 
laboratories. The distribution indicates higher concentration of labs in States such as 
Maharashtra, Tamil Nadu, Karnataka, G ujarat, Haryana and Delhi, whereas several smaller 
States and UTs like Arunachal Pradesh, Mizoram, Sikkim, Andaman & Nicobar Islands, 
Chandigarh, Ladakh, Puducherry and Lakshadweep have no notified laboratories. Further, 
Referral Food Laboratories are avai lable only in 11 states namely, Andhra Pradesh, Gujarat, 
Haryana, Karnataka, Kerala, Maharashtra, Punjab, Tamil Nadu, Telangana, Uttar Pradesh and 
West Bengal. A brief of the details provided by the Department is presented below:

3.18.18 On further query, the Department provided that a ll the 36 State/UTs have 
separate Food Safety department and 31 State/UTs have Food Safety Appellate Tribunal. 
Scrutiny of the details provides that two States/UTs namely, Andhra Pradesh and Dadra and 
Nagar Haveli & Daman & Diu, have no Food Safety Appellate Tribunal. The institution of 
tribunal is under process in Bihar, Karnataka and Mizoram.  
 
3.18.19 On subsequent query on work strength of FSSAI, the Department provided that 
as on 10.02.20 26, out of the 822 sanctioned posts, 279 posts are vacant. 72 persons are 
working on a contract basis in various positions.  
 
3.18.20 The Department, in its submission on legislative reforms, provided that i t is 
proposed to bring following amendments in the Food Safety and Standards Act, 2006 for Ease 
of Doing Business: - 
 
a. Modify definition of unsafe food to avoid contradictory implementation of provisions 
of the Act: The term Mis -Branded and Sub-standard as defined in FSS Act, 2006 says 
that it does not render the article of food unsafe. Selling of Sub- standard/ Mis- branded 
food may lead to adjudication (civil court) while selling unsafe food may lead to 
prosecution. Such contradictory/ambiguous definition of unsafe food leads to 
individual discretion an d regulatory unpredictability for FBOs in case of non -
compliance.   
 

113 
 
b. Exemption to retailers/ distributors from penalty for non -compliances beyond their 
control: As per the Section 49 of FSS Act, 2006 on General Provisions relating to 
penalty, in case of no n-compliance, penalty is applicable to all FBOs such as seller, 
distributor, manufacturer etc.  But, the retailers/ aggregators/ distributors of pre -
packaged food products have limited role in ensuring the kind of additives/ ingredients 
used and the level at which they are used.  
 
c. Provision for compounding of offences: As per Section 69 of FSS Act, 2006, power to 
compound offences is presently applicable only to registered Food Business Operators 
(FBOs). However, licensed FBOs committing minor offences are s till required to 
undergo adjudication proceedings, as no provision for compounding is currently 
available to them.  
 
d. Enabling use of all food grade solvents for plants and botanical extract: As per Section 
22 of FSS Act, 2006, plants or botanicals extracts are allowed in only 3 solvents (water, 
ethyl alcohol or hydro alcoholic extract)  and/or its combination. The Act was passed in 
2006. Since then, significant technological advancement has taken place necessitating 
use of wide range of solvents. This provis ion is now restricting the scope of innovation 
by the industry.  
 
3.18.21 The Committee notes with serious concern the stark regional disparity in 
the availability of FSSAI -notified testing infrastructure. While states like Maharashtra, 
Tamil Nadu, and Gujarat have a high concentration of facilities, some States and Union 
Territories, namely, Arunachal Pradesh, Mizoram, Sikkim, Andaman & Nicobar 
Islands, Chandigarh, Ladakh, Puducherry and Lakshadweep lack even a single notified 
laboratory. Furthermore, Refe rral Food Laboratories are restricted to just 11 states 
namely, Andhra Pradesh, Gujarat, Haryana, Karnataka, Kerala, Maharashtra, Punjab, 
Tamil Nadu, Telangana, Uttar Pradesh and West Bengal. The Committee believes that 
such skewed distribution severely un dermines the equitable enforcement of food safety 
standards nationwide. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department 
immediately prioritize the unrepresented States and UTs for the deployment of the 
pending 236 "next -generation" Mobile Food Tes ting Labs (MFTLs) once their 
specifications are finalized by May 2026. Concurrently, the Department must allocate 
targeted financial assistance to establish permanent, state -owned primary and referral 
laboratories in the North -Eastern states and UTs to eli minate their dependency on 
distant facilities. 
 
3.18.22 The Committee is of the view that robust regulatory frameworks are 
rendered ineffective without adequate manpower and institutional grievance 
mechanisms. The Committee observes that out of FSSAI's 822  sanctioned posts, 279 
(nearly 34%) remain vacant, perpetuating an undesirable reliance on contractual staff. 
Additionally, the complete absence of Food Safety Appellate Tribunals in Andhra 
Pradesh and Dadra and Nagar Haveli & Daman & Diu, along with the d elayed 
constitution in Bihar, Karnataka, and Mizoram, deprives citizens and Food Business 
Operators (FBOs) of essential, timely legal recourse. The Committee, therefore, 

114 
 
recommends that the Department initiate a fast -track, time-bound recruitment drive in 
coordination with the relevant recruiting agencies to fill the 279 vacancies at FSSAI. 
Furthermore, the Department must proactively engage with the defaulting State/UT 
governments to ensure the immediate constitution and operationalization of their 
respective Food Safety Appellate Tribunals. 
 
3.18.23 The Committee believes that the proposed legislative amendments to the 
Food Safety and Standards Act, 2006, are critical for modernizing the regulatory 
landscape. The current statutory ambiguities, such as pena lizing retailers for 
manufacturing-level non-compliances beyond their control, restricting the compounding 
of minor offences only to registered (and not licensed) FBOs, and limiting botanical 
extractions to outdated solvent technologies, create unnecessary  compliance burdens 
and stifle industry innovation. The Committee is of the view that redefining "unsafe 
food" to remove contradictory interpretations and expanding permissible food -grade 
solvents will significantly enhance operational predictability. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends that the Department expedite the drafting and parliamentary introduction 
of these legislative amendments to ensure that the statutory framework aligns with 
contemporary technological realities and fair regulatory practices. 
 
National Medical commission 
 
3.18.24 National Medical Commission (NMC) was established under the provisions of 
the National Medical Commission Act, 2019, superseding the erstwhile Medical Council of 
India (MCI). The Commission is entrusted with prescribi ng policies and standards for 
undergraduate and postgraduate medical education, regulating medical institutions, 
maintaining the national medical register, and ensuring ethical conduct among registered 
practitioners. Its functioning is carried out through autonomous boards namely: (a) 
Undergraduate Medical Education Board (UGMEB); (b) Postgraduate Medical Education 
Board (PGMEB); (c) Medical Assessment and Rating Board (MARB); and (d) Ethics and 
Medical Registration Board (EMRB). 
3.18.25 On being asked abou t achievements, the Department submitted that during the 
financial year 2025 -26, National Medical Commission (NMC), recorded substantial physical 
achievements in expansion of medical education capacity across the country with an addition 
of 11,169 UG seats and 8,416 PG seats, culminating in a total increase of 19,489 seats during 
the FY 2025-26. As a result of these expansions, the total number of medical colleges in the 
country reached 818, with an aggregate of 1,28,976 Undergraduate seats and 85,020 
Postgraduate seats. The expansion in the number of medical colleges and seats will 
significantly strengthen the healthcare delivery system of the country. The substantial increase 
in Undergraduate and Postgraduate seats will facilitate the availability of a lar ger pool of 
trained medical professionals, specialists and super -specialists, improving tertiary care 
services thereby addressing regional imbalances, reduce dependence on limited high -burden 
institutions and the persistent shortage of doctors, particularly in underserved and rural areas.

Apart from the above, 66 consultants and 170 DEOs, MTS & Drivers have been outsourced

3.18.27 The Committee observes that while the National Medical Commission 
(NMC) has successfully overseen a massive expansion of medical education capac ity, 
reaching 818 colleges, its own regulatory architecture is suffering from acute personnel 
deficits. The Committee notes with serious concern the absolute vacancy in the crucial 
category of Whole -Time Members in Autonomous Boards, with 0 out of 8 sancti oned 
posts filled, alongside significant vacancies in encadred administrative posts. The 
Committee believes that the consequent heavy reliance on 236 outsourced and 
contractual staff members compromises institutional accountability, dilutes a sense of 
belongingness, and raises the risk of unethical practices to secure continued engagement. 
The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department urgently initiate a time -
bound recruitment process to fill all statutory, ex-officio, and encadred vacancies.  
 
3.18.28 The Committee also recommends that in order to further strengthen the 
quality and consistency of postgraduate medical education in the country, it may be 
considered to ensure uniformity in faculty –student and patient–student ratios across all 
medical institutions, irrespective of ownership. Such uniformity would help promote 
equitable clinical exposure, adequate academic mentorship, and consistent training 
opportunities for postgraduate students nationwide. As graduates from all recognized 
institutions contribute to the same national healthcare system, maintaining uniform 
standards would further reinforce confidence in the competence and training of medical 
specialists produced across the country.

This Ministry vide letter dated 31.05.2023 had instructed DCI to not make any fresh regular

3.18.31 National Dental Commission (NDC) Act, 2023 has been notified on 11 th 
August 2023. This will replace the Denta l Council of India (DCI) Act 1948 and intends to 
regulate the profession of dentistry in the country, provide quality and affordable dental 
education and make high-quality oral healthcare accessible. 
3.18.32 On a specific query on any provision mandating d ental professionals to obtain 
multiple registrations with State Dental Councils for practicing in more than one State, the 
Department replied that as per the Dentists Act, 1948 under Section 46(5), ―Any person who 
is a registered dentist, registered dental hygienist or registered dental mechanic in a State may 
practise as such in any other State.‖ However, DCI received emails from the dentists therein 
stating that states have mandated registration in their respective State, before applying for 
permanent vacancy in their state.  
3.18.33 On being asked about the number of complaints/representations received 
during the last five years, the Department submitted that as per records, DCI has received 
around 38 representations from candidates against imposition of mandatory registration in 
State Dental Council while applying for vacancy prior to exam. The DCI has forwarded such 
representations to respective Health Secretary and Directorate of Medical Education to take 
cognizance on DCI‘s Circulars dated 20.03.2025 &  01.02.2019 and consider the inter-state or 
intra-state registration of the dental surgeons with the State Dental Council as per section 
46(5) of the Dentists Act, 1948. The concerned organization/s, after final selection may ask 
the concerned applicant to  get his/her name transferred u/s 46A of the Dentists Act, 1948, 
within stipulated time. The applicants have informed that the following states have mandated 
the registration in their state before applying for vacancy, Bihar, Madhya Pradesh, Telangana, 
Uttar Pradesh, and Delhi. The registration fees and renewal charges levied by the States 
Dental Councils comes under the purview of State Government (as per Dentists Act, 1948). 
3.18.34 The Committee notes with concern the persistent violation of Section 46(5) 
of the Dentists Act, 1948, which explicitly allows registered dental professionals to 

117 
 
practice in any State. Despite established legal provisions and subsequent circulars 
issued by the Dental Council of India (DCI), several States, including Bihar, Madhy a 
Pradesh, Telangana, Uttar Pradesh, and Delhi, continue to mandate local state 
registration as a prerequisite for applying to permanent vacancies. The Committee is of 
the view that such restrictive practices act as a significant barrier to the mobility of  
skilled healthcare professionals and create an unnecessary financial and administrative 
burden on candidates. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department, in 
coordination with the upcoming National Dental Commission (NDC), issue a mandatory 
directive to all State Health Secretaries and Directorates of Medical Education ensuring 
that inter-state registration is recognized during the application and examination stages, 
with the provision that successful candidates may transfer their registration  under 
Section 46A after final selection. 
3.18.35 The Committee observes a critical depletion in the human resource 
capacity of the Dental Council of India, with 47 out of 95 sanctioned posts currently 
vacant, representing a nearly 50% vacancy rate. The Co mmittee believes that the 
instruction in the year 2023 to halt fresh regular recruitment, while intended to facilitate 
the transition to the National Dental Commission (NDC), has resulted in an 
administrative vacuum that threatens the quality of regulatory  oversight and the 
processing of Letters of Permission (LOPs) for BDS and MDS courses. The Committee, 
therefore, recommends that the Department prioritize the early constitution and 
operationalization of the National Dental Commission (NDC) as mandated by the NDC 
Act, 2023. The Committee is of the view that the swift establishment of the 
Commission‘s permanent organisation al and administrative structure is the only 
sustainable way to fill this regulatory void and resolve the current staffing crisis, 
ensuring that the quality and affordability of dental education are not compromised 
during this prolonged transitional phase. Till the constitution of NDC, the Department 
may consider a special "Transition Recruitment Plan" or the deputation of personnel 
from other health departments.

3.19.1 The Committee, on examination of Demands for Grants, finds the utilisation of

is Revenue in nature, with no provision under the Capital head. The Budget Estimate (BE) for 
2025–26 was ₹5200.00 crore, later revised to ₹5238.70 crore, with actual expenditure of 
₹4953.36 crore as on 31.01.2026. For 2026–27, the BE has been further enhanced to ₹5500.92 
crore, indicating a gradual increase in budgetary allocation. In its submission, the Department 
stated that projection of the institute to meet the requirements for 2026-27 was ₹5758 crore.

approximately 190 acres of AIIMS campus spread over five lands parcels. The EFC 
Memorandum was submitted by AIIMS for implementation in phases spread over a period of 
07 years.   After resolution of land related issues and subsequent amalgamation of all land 
parcels allotted to AIIMS, New Delhi by Land & Development Office of MoHUA and Delhi 
Development Authority and requisite payment by AIIMS, MoHUA supported the proposal 
contained in the EFC Memo in July  2024. In the meanwhile two components of Master Plan 
viz. (i) construction of new OPD Block at Masjid Moth in AIIMS, New Delhi and (ii) 
construction of Hostel Accommodation at Trauma Centre Extension have been undertaken 
separately on urgent need basis.
