#The pre -construction activities of obtaining approvals of local bodies is pending on 02 
counts, as under: 
 
(i) Tree cutting/ transplantation approval 
 
The Central Empowered Committee (CEC) vide its Order dated 19 .11.2025 has approved 
felling/transplantation of 72 trees (out of proposed 75) and the Institute is in the process of 
complying with the terms and conditions received from Department of Forest & Wildlife, 
GNCTD. 
 
(ii) Environmental clearance 
 
The process o f seeking environmental clearance (EC) was started on 5 th March 2024 . The 
matter was  shifted from State Environment Impact Assessment Authority to the Expert  
Appraisal Committee (EAC) of Ministry of Environment, Forest &  Climate Change 
(MoEF&CC). EAC reverted the proposal and a fresh application was filed under Clause 8(b) 
of EIA Notification 2006. The process of preparing certified compliances to previous ECs is 
under final stages. The matter has been formally taken up with DG CPWD to issue necessary 
directions for prompt and timely action. 
 
@The project involves redevelopment of 02 residential colonies in place of the existing 
dilapidated hous ing units on self -finance basis (through sale of commercial area) a t West 
Ansari Nagar (WAN) and Ayur Vigyan Nagar  Campuses of AIIMS, New Delhi. However, 
sale of commercial spaces at WAN campus was not allowed by DDA citing that the land use 
is public/semi-public and hence cannot be monetized. Since the dynamics of the project and 
the financial model have changed cons iderably and therefore project requires fresh appraisal 
and approval of the Cabinet. Meanwhile, construction of Type IV spl quarters comprising 3 
Towers (117 Units) at WAN is likely to complete by 31.10.2026. The work for Type V 
quarters comprising 4 Tower s (156 Units) is also underway and likely to complete by  
31.03.2027. 
 
3.19.4 The Committee has been given to understand that AIIMS, New Delhi has also initiated 
the process for obtaining necessary approvals for some major projects, including: (i) 
establishment of a CBRNE facility and expansion of JPNATC with an estimated cost of 
₹494.40 crore; (ii) construction of a Centre of Excellence in Child Health, including 
residential facilities at Jhajjar, with an estimated cost of ₹1,869.75 crore; and (iii) construction 
of hostel accommodation at the Trauma Centre Extension with an estimated cost of ₹876 
crore. These proposals are under examination at different stages in the Ministry/DoE.  
 

121 
 
3.19.5 The Committee notes with appreciation the satisfactory utilization of  budgetary 
funds by AIIMS, New Delhi, and acknowledges the gradual increase in the Budget 
Estimate (BE) to ₹5500.92 crore for FY 2026 -27. However, the Committee observes that 
this allocation falls short of the Institute's projected requirement of ₹5758 cro re. The 
Committee believes that fully funding the Institute is non -negotiable, especially as 
AIIMS is executing a complex, 190 -acre integrated Master Plan while simultaneously 
maintaining unhindered patient care services. The Committee, therefore, recommen ds 
that the Department proactively review and enhance the allocation at the Revised 
Estimate (RE) stage to bridge this ₹257 crore deficit. Ensuring adequate liquidity is 
critical to support not only the ongoing phased redevelopment but also the smooth 
initiation of upcoming mega -projects, such as establishment of a CBRNE facility and 
expansion of JPNATC and Centre of Excellence in Child Health at Jhajjar. 
 
3.19.6 The Committee views with serious concern the severe procedural bottlenecks 
stalling vital patie nt-care infrastructure. Specifically, the Committee notes that the 
Super Specialty OPD Block at Masjid Moth is caught in a prolonged DoE reappraisal 
loop due to tender costs exceeding the ₹500 crore threshold, while the 150 -bedded 
Critical Care Block is se verely delayed by pending tree transplantation approvals and 
shifting Environmental Clearance (EC) goalposts by the MoEF&CC. The Committee is 
of the view that such bureaucratic delays defeats the urgent mandate of these projects. 
The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department immediately constitute an 
empowered Inter-Ministerial Steering Group to aggressively liaise with the Department 
of Expenditure (DoE), MoEF&CC, and the Delhi Government. This group must secure 
a specialized, fast -track clearan ce pathway for all AIIMS infrastructural projects to 
prevent further cost escalations and time overruns. 
 
3.19.7 The Committee observes a significant failure in the initial planning and inter -
agency coordination regarding the ₹4441 crore Redevelopment of Residential Colonies 
project. The Committee believes that the Delhi Development Authority's (DDA) 
rejection of the commercial monetization of the West Ansari Nagar (WAN) campus, 
which formed the very basis of the project's self -financing model, has severely derailed 
the initiative. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department urgently 
finalize and submit the revised Cabinet Note with a realistic, updated financial structure 
that does not rely on contested land -use conversions. Furthermore, the Committee is of 
the view that while the revised financial model is being approved, the Department must 
ensure uninterrupted funding so that the ongoing construction of the Type IV and Type 
V quarters (costing ₹118.92 crore and ₹143.94 crore, respectively) meets their stipulated 
completion deadlines of 2026 and 2027. 
 
3.19.8 The Committee notes with concern the p rofound lack of progress in critical 
projects, specifically the Expansion of the National Drug Dependence Treatment Centre 
(NDDTC) and the establishment of the ₹358.19 crore IT and Network Security 
Infrastructure. Despite the project‘s official commencemen t dated of 18 th March 2024 
with 18 -month completion timeline and an advance payment of ₹8.60 crore to the 
CPWD for the NDDTC, physical progress remains at 'Nil', while the ambitious IT 

122 
 
modernization project faces an exceptionally tight completion deadline of September 
2026, given the complexity of digitizing a massive healthcare ecosystem. The Committee 
believes that such delays in both civil works for vulnerable populations and the digital 
resilience of the Institute‘s data systems are unacceptable in a pr emier national 
institution. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department institute a 
unified "High-Level Project Monitoring Cell" to hold both civil executing agencies (like 
CPWD) and technical consultants (like BEL) strictly accountable throug h monthly 
milestone tracking. The Committee is of the view that the Department must formulate 
and enforce penalty clauses in MoUs to held executing agencies financially accountable 
for construction inertia. The Committee further urges the Department to est ablish a 
dedicated Cyber -Security Cell within AIIMS to manage and protect the new digital 
infrastructure from the increasing global threats to healthcare data. 
 
3.19.9 The Committee observes that while AIIMS, New Delhi is undergoing a massive 
internal redevelopment, its role in decompressing tertiary care through satellite facilities 
remains behind schedule. Specifically, the construction of the 30 -bedded Community 
Health Centre at Fatehpur Billoch and the New Ward Block at CRHSP Ballabhgarh are 
both trailing their original stipulated completion dates, with the latter showing a dismal 
physical progress of only 15% nearly a year after its start. The Committee believes that 
the chronic overcrowding at the main Ansari Nagar campus can only be permanently 
mitigated if these peripheral and community -based facilities are operationalized to 
handle primary and secondary workloads. The Committee, therefore, recommends that 
the Department institute a "Satellite Project Acceleration Taskforce" to oversee these 
specific expansions and ensure their timely completion, thereby guaranteeing that rural 
and semi-urban populations gain access to AIIMS -standard care without necessitating 
travel to the high-burden New Delhi facility.

Department submitted the details of faculty posts as under:

 3.19.11 The following efforts to fill up the vacancies submitted by the Department:  
a) Efforts for filling up of faculty posts: 328  posts of Faculty have been advertised on 
regular basis (265 posts advertised in April 2025 and 63 posts advertised in September 
2025) against which interviews and screening of applications respectively are going 
on. 
b) Efforts to fill up JR/SR positions: The  posts of JRs/SRs are tenure posts. 
Recruitment/Admission to these posts are conducted twice a year i.e Jan/July Session. 
Therefore, the vacant seats are advertised in the subsequent session. 
c) Efforts to fill up non -faculty positions: Recruitment for fillin g up the vacant posts 
under the mode of Direct recruitment is being done on continuous basis through 
centralized recruitment examinations such as Common Recruitment Examination 
(CRE), Nursing Officer Recruitment Common Eligibility Test (NORCET) and 
Recruitment Examination for Group A (REGA).  For filling the posts of Nursing 
Officer, NORCET exam is held twice a year. AIIMS, New Delhi has 
started conducting Common Recruitment exam (CRE) also twice a year. 
3.19.12 The Department provided that the Key concern a rea for optimum functioning 
of the institute are managing high patient load on OPD, IPD and Emergency services, 
Utilizing, upgrading and maintaining the hospital infrastructure, Ensuring low waiting time 
for OPD consultation, investigations, and surgeries,  Ensuring optimum and uninterrupted 
supply of drugs and consumables, and Ensuring availability and retention of faculty, resident 
doctors, nursing and paramedical staff. The strategic measures suggested for efficient 
functioning of the hospital to cater large number of patients:  
a. Digital Command and Control Systems: Implementation of a centralized dashboard 
for real-time tracking of patient flow, inventory, HR metrics, and financials. Use of 
AI-driven analytics to forecast demand, optimize scheduling, and reduce bottlenecks. 
 
b. Performance-Based Accountability: Link departmental performance to measurable 
outcomes like patient satisfaction, academic output, and operational efficiency. 
 
c. Referral System Strengthening: Use digital triage tools to redirect non -tertiary cases 
to appropriate centres. 
 Performance indicators that are being used to evaluate hospital -level allocations, utilization 
and service-delivery are:  
a) Hospital utilization statistics including bed occupancy rate, average length of stay, bed 
turnover rate etc are being captured regularly to evaluate hospital-level allocation. 
b) Statistics on the number of OPD patients, in -patient admissions, ICU admissions, 
number of lab tests done, radiological investigations and total number of major and 
minor surgeri es done is captured regularly to assess the utilization and service 
delivery. 
c) Hospital infection rates, including healthcare associated infections (HAIs) captured 
regularly to monitor and plan strategies to reduce the incidence of HAIs.

3.19.14 With regard to the 'Application -to-Selection Time Ratio' and the 'Offer -to-
Joining Ratio', the Department submitted that for the last two recruitment cycles during 2025, 
the process of recruitment is presently underway.  With regard to faculty recruitment 
advertised during 2021 -22 for which the joining process continued up to December 202 3, 
2325 candidates applied, out of which 203 candidates were selected. Accordingly, offer letters 
were issued to 203 candidates, against which 176 candidates joined. 
3.19.15 The Committee observes with concern a staggering 35% vacancy rate (452 
vacancies) in faculty positions, along with J unior/Senior residents (700+ vacancies), and 
non-faculty posts (2,540 vacancies) which constrain service delivery and strain existing 
staff. Most alarmingly, the data reveals that while 32 faculty members resigned or 
superannuated in the years 2024 -25 and 2025 -26 (till January 2026), zero new faculty 
members joined the institute during this period. The Committee believes that this 
recruitment paralysis severely jeopardizes the academic and clinical standards of India's 
premier medical institution. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the 
Department launch a focused, time -bound recruitment drive with clear milestones, for 
example, target filling (at least 85%) of sanctioned posts by fast -tracking advertised 
vacancies, conducting rolling interview panels, and decentralising selection timelines to 
avoid protracted offer -to-joining delays. T he 328 currently advertised faculty positions 
may be finalised within the first two quarters of FY 2026 -27. Furthermore, the 
Committee is of the view that the persistent reliance on contractual faculty (currently 73 
Assistant Professors) must be phased out in favor of permanent appointments to ensure 
institutional belongingness and long -term accountability, as contractual arrangements 
should serve only as temporary stop -gap measures. L ong-standing contractual 
incumbents meeting eligibility criteria should be offered a regularisation/absorption 
pathway. The Department should introduce retention incentives tied to hard -to-fill 
specialties and underserved postings to improve the offer-to-joining ratio. 
3.19.16 The Committee is concerned about excessive continuous duty hours for 
Junior and Senior Residents and the risk of clinical errors and burnout, thereby 
compromising patient safety . The Comm ittee, therefore, recommends that the 
Department formulate and strictly enforce a "Clinical Duty Hours Regulation" policy 
with mandatory rest periods, and monitored rosters , drawing a direct analogy from 
other safety-critical professions  like civil aviatio n to prevent fatigue -induced accidents.  

125 
 
The presence of senior faculty should be ensured in wards and in operating theatres 
during peak hours by aligning faculty schedules with clinical demand and by appointing 
designated supervisory rosters. Structured m entorship and protected teaching time 
should be introduced so that trainees gain supervised clinical experience rather than 
learning by trial. Additionally, to ensure the retention of high -quality talent, the 
Committee recommends that medical professionals  be provided with improved working 
conditions, including accommodation, transportation, and administrative support, on 
par with the facilities afforded to senior civil servants, and transparent career 
progression with time -bound promotions. The Committee a lso suggests the integration 
of Yoga and the Indian Knowledge System (IKS) within the workforce to address the 
rising incidence of depression and psychiatric issues among the medical community. 
3.19.17 The Committee notes that AIIMS, New Delhi, continues t o struggle with 
high patient loads in OPD and Emergency services, leading to long waiting times (may 
extended to several months) for investigations and surgeries; and optimum and 
uninterrupted supply of drugs and consumables. The Committee believes that ph ysical 
infrastructure alone cannot solve the congestion at a tertiary care center of this scale. 
The Committee, therefore, recommends the immediate implementation of the proposed 
"Digital Command and Control System" and ―Performance -Based Accountability‖, 
utilizing AI -driven analytics to forecast patient demand and optimize surgical 
scheduling, and identifying bottlenecks so that human resources match infrastructure 
growth. Furthermore, the Committee is of the view that a robust digital triage tool must 
be deployed to strengthen the referral system, effectively redirecting non -tertiary cases 
to appropriate secondary centers. This will ensure that the specialized resources of 
AIIMS are reserved for the most complex clinical cases, thereby improving overall 
operational efficiency and patient satisfaction. 
3.19.18 The Committee believes that while AIIMS, New Delhi, currently captures 
extensive hospital utilization and service -delivery statistics, the existing internal 
monitoring mechanism lacks the necessary ext ernal validation to drive systemic 
improvements. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department facilitate 
an independent audit to evaluate hospital -level allocations, utilisation and service 
delivery, and key performance indicators like bed occu pancy rates, bed turnover rates, 
and average length of stay, ICU admissions, surgical volumes, laboratory and 
radiological investigations, hospital infection rate including healthcare associated 
infections, on quarterly basis. The Committee is of the view that the outcomes of these 
audits must be subjected to public scrutiny to ensure absolute institutional transparency 
with audit results directly informing the strategic allocation of resources and the 
planning. 
3.19.19 The Committee believes that the gover nance of a massive, multi -specialty 
ecosystem like AIIMS requires a blend of clinical expertise and formal administrative 
training. The Committee, therefore, recommends that doctors with specialized 
managerial qualifications be prioritized for top -tier pol icy-making and administrative 
positions within the Institute. Additionally, the Committee is of the view that the medical 

126 
 
and nursing curriculum must be reformed to include an introductory syllabus on Ayush 
and the Indian Traditional Medicinal Systems. Thi s sensitisation will not only 
complement allopathic treatments through palliative and preventive care but will also 
enhance the affordability and accessibility of the healthcare system. The Committee 
concludes that this multidisciplinary approach is essent ial for producing a more holistic 
and resilient healthcare workforce. 
3.19.20 On scrutiny of the submission related to OPD and IPD services, it is noted that 
AIIMS, New Delhi, along with its extended establishments such as JPNATC, NCI Jhajjar and 
CRHSP Bal labgarh, catered to 5,42,59,860 outpatients (including casualty cases) and 
recorded 3,92,518 admissions during the period 01.01.2025 to 31.12.2025. For ease of access 
to OPD services, the Institute continues to accept online appointments through the ORS po rtal 
and a token system based on a first -come, first-served basis to manage flow for walk -ins: (a) 
50% slots for online appointments; (b) 30% slots for walk -ins; and (c) 20% slots for cross -
referrals. Web based SANTUSHT Portal of AIIMS, New Delhi efficient ly collects and 
process feedback of OPD and IPD for continuous improvement. Feedback forms for assessing 
patients‘ satisfaction after discharge from hospital regarding food, hygiene, linen and staff 
behaviour is also brought in place. Feedback forms and su ggestion boxes placed in OPD and 
IPD areas. There is a mechanism in place to improve and build on the practices of capturing 
public feedback as per KRA -8 of the Kaya Kalp guidelines. To assist patients and their 
attendants, Shelter Homes/Vishram Sadans are  operational at five locations with provision of 
over 1500 beds with facilities for clean drinking water, toilets, e -vehicles etc. In addition, to 
cope with extreme cold weather, Delhi Urban Shelter Improvement Board (DUSIB), 
Government of NCT of Delhi has  set up and operationalized 10 -bedded 40-night shelters for 
patients and their attendants.   
3.19.21 On scrutiny of the data regarding available and functional Operation Theatres 
(OTs), it is noted that the Main Hospital and some blocks/centres have 100% o f the available 
OTs functional. However, the following blocks/centres do not have full operationalisation of 
OTs, as the number of functional OTs is less than the total available OTs, as detailed below: 
Table – 3.19.6 
 
It is further noted that NDDTC has no Operation Theatre (OT) available, while CRHSP, 
Ballabgarh has only one OT. The Committ ee assumes that the existing OT capacity at these 
centres is commensurate with their functional requirements. 
 
3.19.22 The Committee notes that AIIMS, New Delhi, along with its extended 
establishments including JPNATC, NCI Jhajjar and CRHSP Ballabgarh, han dled an 
exceptionally large patient load of 5.42 crore OPD cases (including casualty) and 3.92 
lakh IPD admissions during the period 01.01.2025 to 31.12.2025. While the Committee

127 
 
appreciates the measures adopted by the Institute to streamline patient acces s through 
the Online Registration System (ORS), token -based walk -in system, patient feedback 
mechanisms such as the SANTUSHT portal, and the provision of Vishram 
Sadans/shelter homes, it observes that the extremely high patient inflow continues to 
place considerable pressure on hospital infrastructure and services. The Committee also 
notes that several blocks and centres do not have full operationalisation of available 
Operation Theatres (OTs) and that patients continue to face long waiting periods for 
diagnostic tests, surgeries and procedures. The Committee, therefore, recommends that 
the Department take urgent steps to ensure that all sanctioned OTs across various 
blocks are made fully functional at the earliest through adequate staffing, equipment 
provisioning and maintenance support, and also augment diagnostic and surgical 
capacities to reduce waiting periods for critical procedures. The Committee further 
recommends that the feedback received through various mechanisms should be made 
public and the rele vant feedbacks should be considered for identifying the genuine 
problems and their redressal. 
 
3.19.23 The Committee further observes that the heavy patient load at AIIMS, 
New Delhi is partly due to the absence of adequate tertiary care facilities in sever al 
surrounding regions. While satellite institutions such as AIIMS Jhajjar and the facilities 
at Ballabgarh, Faridabad and Dakshinpuri have helped serve populations in the 
southern outskirts of Delhi, the northern periphery of the National Capital Region —
including North Delhi, parts of northern Haryana and adjoining areas of western Uttar 
Pradesh, continues to remain comparatively underserved in terms of access to advanced 
tertiary healthcare. In view of the persistent demand for specialised medical service s 
and the continuing pressure on the main AIIMS campus, the Committee recommends 
that the Department examine the feasibility of establishing an additional satellite centre 
of AIIMS in the northern outskirts of Delhi to expand access to tertiary healthcare,  
reduce patient congestion at the main institute and provide timely treatment to 
populations in the surrounding regions. 
 
3.19.24 On a query on research work related to antimicrobial resistance, the 
Department submitted that AIIMS New Delhi is involved in multiple research studies on 
antimicrobial (AMR) both at hospital resistance as well as at community level. AIIMS, New 
Delhi is a designated Infectious Disease Research & Diagnostic Laboratory (IRDL) centre 
from ICMR. It is also a Nodal Coordinating Centre  for surveillance network of antimicrobial 
resistance (AMRSN) in Salmonella enterica serotype Typhi and Paratyphi and other bacterial 
pathogens. The institute is also involved in research on multiple aspects of AMR, including 
studies on sexually transmitte d infections, sepsis, meningitis, urinary tract infections, 
diarrhoeal infections, and tuberculosis, focusing on areas such as rapid diagnostics, genomic 
profiling of resistant pathogens, biomarker identification, molecular epidemiology, 
development of bacteriophage and monoclonal antibody therapies, CRISPR-based therapeutic 
approaches, and point-of-care test development, etc. 
 
3.19.25 On being asked whether  any bond is filled by the students (Degree/PG/Super -
specialty) passed out from the Institute to serv e for an initial period in the country and 

128 
 
whether any study carried out by the Institute to record how many students passed out from 
the Institute during the last decade are presently serving in the country, the Department 
replied that as far as AIIMS, Ne w Delhi is concerned, there is no bond filled by the students 
passed out from the institute to serve for an initial period in the country. Further, no data of 
the passed out candidate is maintained who are currently serving in the country. 
 
3.19.26 On a sp ecific query, the expenditure incurred on annual consumption of 
Electricity and Water during the FY 2024 -25 is stated as  ₹108.86 crore and ₹3.97 crore 
respectively. On initiatives taken for alternative source of electricity and conservation of 
electricity and water, the submission stated that the Institute has enhanced the Solar Power 
Generation Capacity during 2025-26 from 703 KWp to 1108 KWp and saving of expenditure 
recorded on this account till date is ₹44,62,000/ - (approx.). The following works of Roo f Top 
Solar Power Generation System are in process, JPNA Trauma Centre – 118 KWp; New RAK 
OPD – 200 KWp; MCH,NCA Block – 225KWp; CRHSP Ballabhgarh, Haryana and 
associated Sub Centres -  150KWp; and NCI Jhajjar -100 Kwp. In respect of Water 
Harvesting, AIIMS recharge harvest pits regularly. Water treated through STP is being used 
for horticulture purposes. 
 
3.19.27 On being asked whether the pathology labs in the Institute are run by in -house 
staff/technicians or staff/technicians outsourced through external  agencies, the Department in 
its submission stated that the pathology laboratories at AIIMS, New Delhi are run by the 
regular in-house faculty members supported by regular/outsourced staff/ technicians. This is 
to meet the requirement of high patient load and to ensure uninterrupted services. The 
diagnostic services are supervised and controlled by the regular faculty of the Institute to 
ensure strict adherence to high quality standards and for compliance with institutional 
protocols. 
3.19.28 The Committee notes that AIIMS, New Delhi is actively engaged in 
extensive research on antimicrobial resistance (AMR) at both hospital and community 
levels and functions as a designated Infectious Disease Research & Diagnostic 
Laboratory (IRDL) as well as a nodal coordi nating centre for the Antimicrobial 
Resistance Surveillance Network (AMRSN). While the Institute‘s work in areas such as 
rapid diagnostics, genomic profiling, biomarker identification, bacteriophage therapy, 
CRISPR-based approaches and point -of-care diagno stics is commendable, the 
Committee emphasises that the outcomes of such research should translate into scalable 
public health solutions. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department 
promote stronger collaboration between AIIMS, ICMR laboratori es, State medical 
colleges and public health institutions, and establish a national knowledge -sharing 
platform to disseminate research findings and innovations to strengthen AMR 
surveillance, diagnostics and antimicrobial stewardship across the country. 
3.19.29 The Committee observes that AIIMS, New Delhi does not require students 
graduating from its Degree, Postgraduate or Super -specialty programmes to undertake 
any service bond, nor does the Institute maintain data on the current professional 
engagement of its alumni within the country. Considering that the Institute represents 

129 
 
one of the most prestigious publicly funded medical institutions and produces a large 
pool of highly trained medical professionals each year, the Committee is of the view that 
systematic tracking of alumni and their professional contributions would provide 
valuable insights into the national healthcare workforce. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends that the Department consider developing a structured database to assess 
the extent to  which trained specialists contribute to the domestic healthcare system, 
identify gaps in specialist availability across regions, and inform future policy decisions 
relating to medical education, workforce planning and retention of highly skilled 
professionals within the country. 
3.19.30 The Committee notes that the Institute incurred substantial expenditure 
on electricity (₹108.86 crore) and water (₹3.97 crore) during FY 2024 –25, reflecting the 
scale of its operations and infrastructure. The Committee appreciates the steps taken b y 
the Institute to enhance solar power generation capacity from 703 KWp to 1108 KWp, 
along with ongoing rooftop solar projects across various blocks and centres, as well as 
measures such as rainwater harvesting and reuse of treated water through sewage 
treatment plants (STPs) for horticulture purposes. While these initiatives are 
encouraging, the Committee is of the view that there remains considerable scope to 
further strengthen sustainable energy and water management practices within the 
Institute. The Co mmittee, therefore, recommends that the Department encourage 
expansion of renewable energy capacity, adoption of energy -efficient technologies, and 
systematic water conservation measures, with clearly defined targets for reducing 
dependence on conventional  electricity and fresh water consumption. Such measures 
would not only reduce operational expenditure in the long term but also promote 
environmentally sustainable practices in large public healthcare institutions.

3.20.3 In its submission, the Department provided that the delay in execution of t he projects 
has been mainly attributed to multiple administrative, technical and external factors, including 
time taken in appointment of CPSU/Executing Agency and preparation of DPR, delays in 
approval of building plans, statutory and environmental cleara nces, and late handing over of 
the site by the State Government. Additional constraints included revision of building plans, 
requirement of additional works and modifications, delays in approval of furniture samples 
and special services tenders, and time t aken for planning and designing of service blocks. The 

131 
 
project also faced infrastructure -related challenges such as shifting of existing utilities and 
municipal water pipelines, congestion of traffic, excavation constraints and rains.  
 
3.20.4 On being ask ed about new projects to be undertaken during FY 2026 -27, the 
Department submitted that development of new campus of PGIMER at Sarangpur, 
Chandigarh (Phase-I) is proposed with budget of ₹472.00 crore.  
 
3.20.5 On a query related to key concern areas of the Institute, the Department submitted that 
the key concern areas of PGIMER, Chandigarh for optimum functioning are Patient load, 
Computerization/digitization, and Manpower. Strategic  measures for efficient functioning of 
the hospital are as follows: 
a) Patient load – PGIMER is in the process of commissioning two new centres namely 
Neurosciences Centre and Mother & Child Health Care Centre alongwith three Satellite 
Centres at Sangrur, Ferozepur and UNA to de-congest the current infrastructure. 
b) The peripheral health care facilities need to be upgraded and a scientific referral system 
be put into place so as to ensure that only genuine patients are referred to PGIMER.   
This will help in de-congesting PGIMER and also hugely benefit the patients.   
c) The current Hospital Information System (HIS) needs to be upgraded. This will help in 
digitizing several manual processes and also the record keeping. 
d) Manpower recruitment to be done to successfully commission the new Centres and fill 
gaps in the current status. 
Several steps are taken to evaluate the service delivery like Mortality meetings, Hospital 
Infection Control Committee meetings, Antibiotic Stewardship Programme, and Patient 
feedback. 24x7 Control Room by Department of Hospital Administration to monitor services. 
 
3.20.6 The Committee notes with concern the protracted delays in operationalizing 
critical facilities, specifically the 300 -bedded Advanced Neurosciences Centre and the 
Advanced Mother & Child Centre, which have been under construction since 2019 and 
2021 respectively, yet remain stalled at 99% and 97% completion. The Committee 
believes that the cited administrative bottlenecks, such as delayed statutory clearances, 
late site handovers, and utility shifting disputes, must be aggressively resolved through a 
dedicated inter -agency task force to prevent further cost escalations. The Committee, 
therefore, recommends that the Department immediately facilitate the final 
commissioning of thes e near -complete projects to directly benefit patient care. 
Furthermore, the Committee is of the view that the budgetary allocation of ₹2504.65 
crore for FY 2026 -27, which falls significantly short of the Institute's projected demand 
of ₹2865.00 crore, must be augmented at the Revised Estimate (RE) stage. This financial 
reinforcement is vital to ensure the smooth initiation of the proposed ₹472.00 crore 
Sarangpur campus (Phase-I) without financially starving the ongoing satellite projects. 
 
3.20.7 The Commit tee observes that the main campus of PGIMER is severely 
overburdened, compromising the quality of specialized tertiary care. The Committee is 
of the view that merely constructing physical satellite centres at Una, Ferozepur, and 

132 
 
Sangrur is insufficient unl ess coupled with a formalized, policy -driven network linking 
these facilities. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department mandate 
the immediate development and implementation of a digitized "scientific referral 
system" across the neighboring states. This system must actively upgrade peripheral 
health facilities to act as primary gatekeepers, utilizing strict digital triage protocols to 
filter and redirect non -tertiary cases. By doing so, the highly specialized resources and 
personnel of PGIMER  will be exclusively reserved for genuine, complex clinical 
interventions, thereby effectively decongesting the institute. 
 
3.20.8 The Committee believes that the successful integration of the five new major 
clinical and satellite centres relies entirely o n concurrent advancements in digital 
infrastructure and human resources. The current reliance on manual processes and an 
outdated Hospital Information System (HIS) hampers both clinical efficiency and data -
driven decision-making. The Committee, therefore, recommends the immediate funding 
and expedited rollout of a modernized, comprehensive HIS to fully digitize record -
keeping, streamline patient flow, and enhance real -time monitoring through existing 
mechanisms like the 24x7 Control Room. The Committee is f urther of the view that the 
Department must authorize a dedicated, time -bound recruitment drive to be executed 
prior to the final handover of the new Neurosciences and Mother & Child centres. It is 
imperative that no newly constructed, state -of-the-art fac ility remains non -operational 
or underutilized due to a preventable shortage of specialized medical and paramedical 
staff.

3.20.10 The per day average OPD for various departments during the period April 2025 to Jan 2026 is 
reported as 10918. Out of the 75 OT Tables across various departments, 3 OT tables in NEOT, 02 in 

133 
 
NHE OT and 02 in Day Care OT are reported as non -functional. Some of the best practices

3.20.11 On being asked for the mechanism established by the Institute to record sat isfaction 
and feedback of OPD and IPD patients, the following measures reported: - 
 
1. Feedback Performas at Reception Counters- Feedback performas in both English and Hindi 
are placed at the reception counters of all Centres of PGIMER, Chandigarh. Patient s and their 
attendants who wish to provide suggestions or register grievances can fill these forms.  
2.  Written Complaints to Director/Medical Superintendent - In case of any serious or severe 
complaint, the patient or attendant may submit a written compla int addressed to the Director 
or the Medical Superintendent of PGIMER. 
3.  Mera Aspataal Feedback System - PGIMER is part of the Government of India's "Mera 
Aspataal" initiative under the Ministry of Health & Family Welfare. Patients (OPD and IPD) 
are invi ted to provide feedback after availing services. Feedback can be submitted through 
SMS and web portal. 
4. Online feedback application of SARATHI -Feedback is obtained through the application 
from patients who have sought assistance from Sarathi and as a fo llow-up, daily random calls 
are being made on the mobile number of the patients & their feedback is obtained. 
3.20.12 The Committee notes with serious concern the glaring human resource 
deficit at PGIMER, Chandigarh, particularly the 1,848 vacant non -faculty positions 
against a sanctioned strength of 7,004. Coupled with 139 faculty vacancies and nearly 
300 vacant resident posts, the Committee believes that managing an overwhelming daily 
average of 10,918 OPD patients with a depleted workforce severely degra des the quality 
of tertiary care and overwhelms the existing staff. Furthermore, the Committee is of the 
view that the existence of 7 non -functional operating tables (in NEOT, NHE OT, and 
Day Care OT) out of a total of 75 is unacceptable in a premier natio nal institute where 
surgical waitlists are chronically long. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the 
Department immediately sanction a targeted, time -bound recruitment drive prioritizing 
the massive non -faculty and resident vacancies. Concurrently, t he Institute must be 
directed to utilize emergency maintenance funds to repair or replace the non -functional 
OT tables within the current quarter, ensuring that the hospital's surgical capacity 
operates at 100% efficiency. 

134 
 
3.20.13 The Committee observes th at while PGIMER has initiated commendable 
patient-centric practices, such as the SARATHI initiative, online lab reports, and 
integration with the "Mera Aspataal" portal, many of these interventions remain highly 
fragmented. For instance, the token system i s currently restricted only to the Cardiac 
Centre, and the advanced Queue Management System remains merely "planned" under 
HIS-II. The Committee believes that managing a staggering daily footfall of nearly 
11,000 patients requires universal, standardized d igital crowd control across all 
departments. The Committee is of the view that the feedback mechanisms (physical 
performas, written complaints, Sarathi app, and online portals) must be synthesized to 
yield actionable administrative insights. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the 
Department mandate the expedited, institute -wide rollout of the token and Scan -and-
Share systems across all OPDs without waiting for the full HIS -II deployment. 
Furthermore, the Committee recommends the establishment of a un ified 'Patient 
Experience Cell' to consolidate data from all feedback streams into a single monthly 
analytical report for the Director, which may be available for public view also, ensuring 
that patient grievances systematically drive operational improvements.

3.21.3 On being asked about new projects to be undertaken during FY 2026 -27, the 
Department submitted that Construction of 28 Nos. Type -V Residential Quarters (Block B & 
C, S+7) at JIPMER campus, Puducherry is proposed at a total estimated cost of ₹ 
37,47,75,000/-. The Standing Finance Committee has recommended the proposal and the 
same will now be processed for approval of the Department of Expenditure (DoE). 
 
3.21.4 On a query related to key concern areas of the Institute, the Department submitted tha t 
the key concern areas of JIPMER for optimum functioning are:  
 
a) Disproportionate Patient Load (NCD Burden): The hospital faces a massive influx of 
outpatients which overwhelms the existing infrastructure. There is a specific, critical 
bottleneck in Non -Communicable Diseases (NCDs), particularly Cancer and 
Cardiovascular diseases. The current facilities are insufficient, leading to huge 
waitlists for chemotherapy, radiation, and cardiac interventions.  

136 
 
b) Inpatient Bed Deficit: There is a severe mismatch betwe en the large number of 
outpatients and the inadequate number of inpatient beds and this results in 
overcrowded wards and the inability to admit patients requiring critical care in a 
timely manner. The number of OT tables for cancer and heart surgery and Ca th lab 
slots for cardiac interventions are not sufficient. Waitlists for surgical and 
interventional treatments for cancer and cardiac diseases is a matter of concern.  
c) Gaps in Specialized Care (Transplant & Trauma): Despite having clinical expertise, 
the transplant volumes are inadequate compared to the national need. Furthermore, the 
high burden of trauma cases (road traffic accidents, etc.) strains the general emergency 
services, often diluting the focus required for elective surgeries.  
d) Deficiency in Pa tient Support Amenities: For a hospital with such high footfall, 
support services have been historically lacking. Key concerns include the lack of 
adequate waiting areas (leading to crowding in corridors), insufficient canteen 
facilities for patients/atten dants, and a lack of overnight stay options for those 
traveling from distant regions. 
 
3.21.5 Strategic measures for efficient functioning of the hospital are as follows: 
 
a) Expansion of Specialized NCD lnfrastructure: i. To address the oncology waitlist, a 
dedicated additional block for the Regional Cancer Centre (RCC) is being inaugurated. 
That will increase bed capacity, chemotherapy stations and surgical suits; ii. A 
dedicated Cardiac Centre building is planned to segregate cardiac care from general 
medicine, allowing for higher throughput of angioplasties and cardiac surgeries.  
b) Decongestion via Satellite Campuses (Sedrapet): i. To reduce the load on the main 
campus and provide hyper -specialized care, JIPMER is developing the Sedrapet 
Campus (50 acre, 5 k m from the main campus); ii. A dedicated institute will be 
established here to streamline and increase transplant volumes (Liver, Kidney, Heart 
etc.) without competing for OT space in the main hospital; iii. Moving trauma care to a 
dedicated centre in Sedr apet will allow for specialized trauma management while 
freeing up the main campus resources for elective and other medical cases. 
c) Enhancement of Patient Welfare Amenities: i. Recognizing that clinical care extends 
beyond the doctor's office, strategic mea sures are being taken to rectify amenity gaps; 
ii. lnfrastructure upgrades include constructing additional designated waiting areas to 
manage crowds, opening new food outlets/canteens, and establishing low -cost night 
shelters (Dharamshalas) to support outstation patients and their attendants. 
 
3.21.6 Key Performance Indicators to evaluate the effectiveness of allocations and service 
delivery: 
 
a) Evaluating Hospital -Level Allocations (Resource Efficiency) i. Budget Utilization 
Rate; ii. Equipment Downtime vs. Uptime: Specifically for high -value assets like 
Linear Accelerators (Cancer) or Cath Labs (Cardiac). 
b) Evaluating Utilization (Capacity Management) i. Bed Occupancy Rate (Target: 80 -
90% for efficiency without overcrowding); ii. Average Length of Stay (Monito ring to 
ensure faster bed turnover, especially in the new Cardiac and Trauma centers); iii. 

137 
 
Operating Theatre (OT) Utilization Rate; iv. Bed Turnover Rate (The number of 
patients treated per bed per year). 
c) Evaluating Service-Delivery (Quality & Access) i. Waitlist Reduction Rate; ii. Patient 
Satisfaction Index; iii. Outpatient-to-Inpatient Conversion Rate.  
 
3.21.7 The Committee notes that the Budget Estimate (BE) for 2024 –25 was ₹1440.00 
crore, which was reduced to ₹1370.00 crore at the RE stage, with an a ctual expenditure 
of ₹1351.96 crore, while for 2025 –26 the BE of ₹1450.00 crore was revised to ₹1460.79 
crore, against which the expenditure as on 31.01.2026 stands at ₹1087.52 crore, 
indicating comparatively lower utilisation during the year. Although the  BE for 2026–27 
has been increased to ₹1496.46 crore, against projected requirement of ₹1546.46 crore, 
reflecting growing operational and infrastructure demands. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends that the Department ensure timely release and optimal uti lisation of funds, 
while also strengthening internal monitoring mechanisms for infrastructure projects and 
institutional expenditure so that sanctioned allocations translate into timely completion 
of projects and improved healthcare delivery capacity. 
 
3.21.8 The Committee notes the progress of several infrastructure projects at 
Jawaharlal Institute of Postgraduate Medical Education and Research, Puducherry, 
including the Regional Cancer Centre annexe building, critical care hospital block, 
residential quar ters and the proposed 476 -bedded teaching hospital at the Karaikal 
campus. While some projects are nearing completion, others are still at relatively early 
stages of physical progress. Given the rising burden of non -communicable diseases 
(NCDs), particular ly cancer and cardiovascular diseases, and the existing shortage of 
inpatient beds, operation theatre capacity and cardiac intervention facilities, the 
Committee stresses the need for expedited completion of ongoing infrastructure projects 
and timely approval of new residential and clinical facilities. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends that the Department prioritize the immediate operationalization of the 
Regional Cancer Centre Annexe, which currently stands at 99% physical progress. 
Furthermore, the Com mittee believes that the Institute must aggressively fast -track the 
development of the Sedrapet Campus. Shifting trauma and transplant cases to that 
dedicated 50 -acre satellite facility is paramount to effectively decongesting the main 
campus and freeing u p critical operating theatre (OT) space for elective and specialized 
NCD surgeries. 
 
3.21.9 The Committee notes that while JIPMER has outlined comprehensive structural 
expansions to manage its disproportionate NCD burden and trauma influx, the mere 
addition of physical infrastructure at the main and Sedrapet campuses will not 
autonomously resolve the systemic waitlists and bed deficits. The Committee believes 
that these expansions must be inextricably linked to rigorous, real -time performance 
auditing to pr event the new specialized centres from succumbing to the same 
operational bottlenecks currently congesting the existing facilities. 
 
3.21.10 The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department mandate the 
immediate deployment of a centralized, digital  monitoring system explicitly tied to the 

138 
 
newly defined Key Performance Indicators (KPIs). The Committee is of the view that 
metrics evaluating resource efficiency, such as the downtime versus uptime of high-value 
life-saving assets like Linear Accelerator s and Cath Labs, must be strictly audited on a 
monthly basis. Furthermore, the Committee recommends that the Department enforce 
strict accountability regarding capacity management and service delivery targets. 
Indicators such as the Outpatient -to-Inpatient Conversion Rate, the 80 -90% Bed 
Occupancy Rate target, and the Trauma Survival Rate must be actively utilized to 
adjust administrative strategies dynamically, ensuring that the heavy investments in the 
Regional Cancer Centre and the new Advanced Trauma Ce ntre translate directly into a 
measurable reduction in patient waitlists and improved clinical outcomes.

positions, with 85 posts lying vacant out of a sanctioned strength of 412, and the 
continued reliance on contractual s taff in several service areas. While acknowledging 
that recruitment is underway through centralised mechanisms such as AIIMS CRE and 
NORCET, the Committee emphasises that timely filling of vacancies for doctors, nurses, 
technicians and other healthcare per sonnel is essential to match the expanding 
infrastructure and rising patient load. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the 
Department take expedited measures to fill all vacant positions on a regular basis. 
Concurrently, the Committee is of the view that the Department should take measures 

139 
 
to improve retention like providing better working conditions and career progression 
opportunities for medical professionals.

3.22.4 The Committee notes that for 2025 –26, the BE of ₹860.00 crore was revised to 
₹866.40 crore, against which ₹693.44 crore has been utilised as on 12.02.2026, indicating 
relatively slow pace of utilisation. While the BE for 2026 –27 has been enhanced to 
₹917.21 crore, the Committ ee is of the view that effective utilisation of available 
resources is equally important for strengthening institutional capacity. The Committee, 
therefore, recommends strengthening financial planning and monitoring mechanisms so 
that funds allocated to pr emier institutions like National Institute of Mental Health and 
Neuro Sciences are utilised efficiently. 
 
3.22.5 The Committee notes the progress of infrastructure projects at NIMHANS, 
Bengaluru, including the completion of the Common Laboratory Complex an d 
Psychiatry Specialty Block, while major projects such as the OPD Complex and the 300 -
bedded Poly Trauma Centre are at preliminary stages of design and project 
management consultancy. Considering the growing burden of mental health and 
neurological disorders and the need for specialised trauma and rehabilitation services, 
the Committee emphasises the importance of timely execution of these critical 

141 
 
infrastructure projects. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department 
closely monitor the progress of these projects, expedite the approval and appointment of 
project management consultants, and ensure time -bound completion of the OPD 
complex and poly trauma facility, so that the Institute can significantly expand its 
clinical, research and patient care capacities.

staff, especially Hospital/Office Assistants where 303 posts remain  unfilled. Such 
shortages inevitably affect clinical services, academic activities, research output, patient 
flow management, and the quality of care. The Committee, therefore, recommends that 
the Department take time -bound measures to fill the large numbe r of vacancies,

3.22.9 The Committee observes that demanding work conditions and comparatively 
limited s upport infrastructure often contribute to attrition among doctors and other 
critical healthcare staff. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department 
take focused measures to improve working conditions and introduce structured 
retention policies aimed at sustaining a stable and motivated workforce. Such measures 
may include provision of adequate residential accommodation near the campus, reliable 
transportation facilities, availability of support staff and helpers, safe and well -equipped 
working environments, and clearly defined career progression pathways.  
 
3.22.10 The Committee further notes that the Institute has identified several systemic 
challenges including overcrowded facilities, fragmented patient flow, limited 
technological integration, and increasing demand for specialized services. The 
Committee recommends accelerated adoption of digital health technologies including 
Hospital Information Systems, Electronic Medical Records, telemedicine platforms, and 
AI-enabled monitoring tools to streamline patient management, reduce waiting times for 
diagnostics and consultations, and improve operational efficiency of operation theatres 
and hospital beds.  
 
3.22.11 The Committee also underscores the importance of institutionalising a robust 
patient feedback mechanism through both digital and offline channels so that patients 
from all backgrounds can share their experiences; such feedback should be analysed 
periodically and transparently displayed on institutional platforms to drive continuous 
quality i mprovement. Further, in view of the growing recognition of integrative 
healthcare, the Committee suggests that sensitisation regarding traditional Indian 
systems of medicine may be incorporated within medical, nursing, and allied health 
training to complem ent preventive and palliative care approaches and improve 
affordability and accessibility of healthcare services. 
 
3.22.12 On being asked about the contribution of the Institute in alleviating the mental health 
burden especially during last three years, it  is submitted that The National Institute of Mental 
Health and Neurosciences (NIMHANS) Bengaluru, has played a central role in strengthening 
India‘s mental health system through digital innovation, decentralised service expansion, 
structured suicide preven tion initiatives, capacity building of primary care providers, and 
evidence-based task-sharing models. These efforts have directly addressed the longstanding 
treatment gap, improved access in rural and underserved areas, strengthened crisis response 
systems, and enhanced continuity o f care. The key focus areas are:  Digital Innovation & 
Capacity Building; Community & Rural Outreach viz. NAMAN programme in Karnataka & 
Uttarakhand for lifespan -based mental health care, Taluk -level schizophrenia intervention 
reduced treatment gap to ~7%.; Youth & Child Mental Health viz Yuva Spandana: Reached 
8.3M youth, counselling for 28,000+, SAMVAD: National child protection and psychosocial 
care centre, trained 1.4 lakh duty -bearers, USHAS –YASHAS: Youth self -harm prevention , 
reduced re -attempt rates to ~1.2%; Specialised Services viz. Vayomanas Programme: 
Geriatric psychiatry with family and caregiver support and Perinatal Mental Health and 

143 
 
National Systems Strengthening viz Tele -MANAS: 24×7 national helpline (14416) & 
Integration with DMHP and NHM for continuity of care. 
 
3.22.13 The impact highlights submitted are (i) Reduced treatment gaps in rural 
schizophrenia care; (ii) Scaled digital training to tens of thousands of frontline workers; (iii) 
Youth and child -focused prog rammes addressing stress, safety, and self -harm; (iv) 
Strengthened perinatal and geriatric mental health services; and (v) Tele -MANAS established 
as India‘s national digital mental health gateway. 
 
3.22.14 On being asked about the efforts made and can furt her be made by the Institute 
to reduce the mental illness cases and ensuring access to treatment facilities across the semi -
urban, rural, tribal and remote areas of the country, it is furnished that Over the past decade, 
NIMHANS has developed and implement ed scalable models that combine epidemiological 
evidence, task -sharing, tele -mentoring, community engagement, and rehabilitation 
frameworks. These efforts have significantly improved early identification, treatment 
initiation, continuity of care, and stigm a reduction in underserved populations. The core 
approach of the NIMHANS - Community & Digital Mental Health Models are Decentralised 
services in rural, tribal, semi -urban areas; Capacity building via tele -mentoring, blended 
training, and task-sharing; and Integration of mental health into primary care and community 
systems. The key models and achievements have been (i) Sakalawara Rural Psychiatry;  (ii) 
Tele-Mentoring (ECHO Model);  (iii) Diploma in Primary Care Psychiatry (DPCP);  (iv) 
CHaMP & TORENT (Chhatti sgarh); (v) NAMAN (Taluk -Level Model);  (vi) Community 
Manuals & Toolkits; and (vii) Tele-MANAS (National Helpline 14416). 
 
3.22.15 The overall impact has been submitted as (a) Reduced treatment gap through 
localised care; (b) Empowered non -specialist provi ders; (c) Increased early detection & 
intervention; (d) Strengthened referral pathways; (e) Promoted stigma reduction via 
community engagement; and (f) Enhanced digital access and continuity of care. 
 
3.22.16 On being asked about the measures likely to be taken to implement the policy 
announcement related to mental health made in Budget 2026 -27, the submission stated that 
The Union Budget 2026 –27 includes significant announcements aimed at strengthening the 
mental health ecosystem of the country. These include the establishment of a second national-
level mental health institute in North India, the up -gradation of regional mental health 
institutes, expansion of emergency and trauma care centres with mental health components, 
and strengthening of the allied he alth workforce. NIMHANS, Bengaluru as an Institute of 
National Importance and apex technical body in mental health, is likely to play a central role 
in operationalising these announcements through academic leadership, technical guidance, 
training, digital integration, and systems strengthening. The key announcements have been 
stated as below: - 
 
a) NIMHANS-2 in North India: New apex institute to correct regional imbalance. 
b) Upgradation of Regional Institutes : Strengthen Ranchi, Tezpur, others for decentralised 
specialist care. 
c) Emergency & Trauma Centres: Integrate psychiatric crisis management into district hospitals. 
d) Workforce Expansion: More seats, training hubs, tele -mentoring for doctors, nurses, CHOs, 
ASHAs. 

144 
 
e) Digital Integration: Strengthen Tele-MANAS, electronic records, multilingual tele-support. 
f) Research & Policy Guidance : National standards, region -specific studies, technical assistance 
to states. 
 
3.22.17 The role of NIMHANS would be (i) Academic leadership: curricula, faculty 
mentoring, postgraduate exp ansion; (ii) Technical guidance: service protocols, quality 
benchmarks, referral pathways; (iii) Training: blended learning, tele -mentoring hubs, CME 
systems; (iv) Digital integration: national dashboards, tele -support networks; and (v) Policy 
support: evidence-based guidelines, monitoring, evaluation. 
 
3.22.18 The expected impact has been stated as (i) Reduced treatment gap nationwide; 
(ii) Greater geographic equity in specialist care; (iii) Strengthened district -level and primary 
care services; (iv) Impro ved suicide prevention and crisis response; (v) Expanded trained 
workforce across disciplines; (vi) Enhanced digital access and data -driven systems; (vii) 
Lower out-of-pocket costs via decentralization; (viii) Early detection across life stages; (ix) 
Integration of mental health into public health; and (x) Increased public confidence in 
services. 
 
3.22.19 The Committee takes into consideration the significant contribution of the 
NIMHANS, Bengaluru in strengthening the country‘s mental health ecosystem throu gh 
innovative digital platforms, decentralised service delivery, and large -scale capacity 
building initiatives. Programmes such as Tele -MANAS, the Digital Academy, and tele -
mentoring models for primary healthcare providers have demonstrated the potential o f 
technology-enabled systems to bridge the long -standing treatment gap, particularly in 
rural and underserved areas. However, considering the rising burden of mental health 
disorders and the limited availability of specialised professionals across the coun try, the 
Committee recommends that the Department further institutionalise and scale up such 
digital mental health platforms across all States and Union Territories. Strengthening 
Tele-MANAS with enhanced multilingual support, integration with district hos pitals 
and primary health centres, and linkage with the District Mental Health Programme 
(DMHP) would ensure timely crisis response, improved referral pathways, and 
continuity of care, thereby significantly expanding access to mental health services in 
remote and underserved regions. 
 
3.22.20 The Committee notes that community -based mental health models 
implemented by NIMHANS, including the Sakalawara Rural Psychiatry model, 
NAMAN programme, and tele -mentoring initiatives such as the ECHO model and 
Diploma in Primary Care Psychiatry (DPCP), have shown encouraging results in 
reducing treatment gaps, improving early detection, and empowering non -specialist 
healthcare providers. As such initiatives demonstrate that task -sharing approaches, 
combined with structu red training and community engagement, can effectively extend 
mental health services beyond tertiary care institutions. The Committee therefore 
recommends that the Department adopt these models more widely through integration 
with the National Health Missi on and the District Mental Health Programme, ensuring 
that trained primary care doctors, community health officers, ASHAs, and other 
frontline workers are equipped to identify and manage common mental health 

145 
 
conditions at the community level. Such decentra lised service delivery models would 
significantly reduce the burden on tertiary institutions while making mental healthcare 
more accessible and affordable for the population. 
 
3.22.21 The Committee further notes that several targeted interventions 
undertaken by NIMHANS, such as Yuva Spandana for youth mental health, SAMVAD 
for child protection and psychosocial care, USHAS –YASHAS for self -harm prevention, 
and specialised programmes in geriatric and perinatal mental health that have 
addressed critical and oft en under -recognised segments of the population. Given the 
growing prevalence of stress, anxiety, and self -harm among youth and vulnerable 
groups, the Committee recommends that the Department prioritise expansion of these 
life-cycle-based mental health prog rammes across the country through collaboration 
with educational institutions, community organisations, and state governments. 
Particular emphasis may be placed on strengthening school and college -based mental 
health support systems, expanding counselling services, and integrating psychosocial 
support within maternal and geriatric healthcare services.  
 
3.22.22 The Committee takes note of the policy announcements made in the Union 
Budget 2026 -27 regarding the establishment of a second national -level mental health 
institute in North India, the up -gradation of regional mental health institutes viz. 
Ranchi, Tezpur , other for decentralized specialist ca re, and the strengthening of 
emergency and trauma care centres with integrated psychiatric services. While thes e 
initiatives are expected to significantly enhance the country‘s mental healthcare 
infrastructure, the Committee emphasises the need for a coordinated implementation 
framework under the technical leadership of NIMHANS. The Committee recommends 
that the De partment ensure timely operationalisation of the proposed institute and 
regional upgrades while establishing strong academic, training, and digital linkages with 
NIMHANS to maintain uniform standards of care, research, and training across the 
network. In a ddition, NIMHANS may be entrusted with developing national protocols, 
quality benchmarks, and monitoring systems to guide the expansion of mental health 
services.  
 
3.22.23 The Committee recommends for integration of the Indian Knowledge 
System with modern medicine in a scientific and evidence -based manner to address the 
growing challenges of mental health in the country. Such integration, including the 
incorporation of traditional wellness practices, may complement existing treatment 
approaches, strengthen  preventive mental healthcare, and contribute to reducing the 
rising burden of mental health disorders.

3.23.4 The Committee finds that the North Eastern Indira Gandhi Regional Institute of 
Health and Medical Sciences (NEIGRIHMS), Shillong has demonstra ted efficient 
utilisation of its allocated funds in 2024 –25, with 99.71% utilisation of the Revised 
Estimate, however, in 2025 -26, the 80.91% utilisation of the Revised Estimate as of 
10.02.2026 indicates moderate utilisation. Further, the Committee observ es a 
considerable gap between the projected demand of ₹704.30 crore for 2026 –27 and the 
allocated amount of ₹565.26 crore, particularly under certain revenue components and 
capital expenditure. Given the expanding scope of services, the proposed establishm ent 
of the Regional Cancer Centre, and the increasing operational requirements of the 
Institute, the Committee recommends that the Department undertake periodic 
assessment of the Institute‘s financial needs and ensure adequate and timely budgetary 
support so that critical activities relating to patient care, procurement of medicines and 
consumables, recruitment of manpower, and maintenance of infrastructure are not 
adversely affected. Adequate capital investment should also be prioritised to support 
modern medical equipment and infrastructure expansion in the region. 
 
3.23.5 The Committee has been given to understand that  the ongoing construction of 
the 150 -bedded Critical Care Block in NEIGRIHMS under PM -ABHIM, which has 
achieved over 90% physical progress  and 85.50% financial progress, although the 
project has experienced delays due to initial site -related constraints, forest clearance 
issues, and local dis turbances. The Committee desires  the Ministry should strive to 
accelerate construction and mobilise additional resources to meet the revised completion 
timeline of March 2026. The Committee recommends , in this regard, the Department to 
strengthen project monitoring and coordination mechanisms to ensure timely 
completion of critical healthcare infrastructu re projects. The Committee further notes 
the proposed new projects such as the Drug De -addiction Centre, NELS facility, and 
boundary wall construction, which are important for strengthening healthcare services 
and campus infrastructure. The Department must  ensure timely approval and funding 
of these projects while adopting robust project management practices to avoid delays 
and to enhance the overall capacity of the Institute to provide specialised healthcare 
services in the North Eastern region.

3.23.7 The key areas of concern  are the Shortage of manpower (faculty and para medical 
staff). The allocation utilisation and service delivery indicators are stated as Reduction in 
patient waiting time; Bed turnover rate; Average length of stay; Equipment utilisation; 
Emergency service wait time; and Availability of speciality care. The Quality indicators are 
stated as Re -admission rates; Infection rates; Mortality rates; Patient satisfaction score; and 
Research. 
 
3.23.8 The Committee notes with concern that 399 posts remain vacant out of the 
sanctioned strength of 1,977  in the Instit ute, including 56 faculty posts, 205 Group ‗B‘ 
posts, 44 Group ‗C‘ posts and 49 Senior Resident Doctor posts , which may significantly 
affect the Institute‘s capacity to deliver timely and quality healthcare services in the 
North Eastern region. Persistent shortages of faculty, resident doctors and paramedical 
staff can adversely impact key service delivery indicators such as patient waiting time, 
bed turnover rate, utilisation of medical equipment, emergency service response time, 
etc., besides affecting critical quality indicators including infection rates, mortality rates, 
patient satisfaction, research output, etc . The Committee recommends that the 
Department should adopt proactive measures to widen the pool of applicants through 
national outreach, academic collaborations, and incentives for specialists to serve in the 
North Eastern region.

3.24.3 The Committee has been given to understand that while RIMS, Imphal 
demonstrated an exemplary fund utilizati on rate of 100% of its Revised Estimate in FY 
2024-25, there is, however, slow utilisation of 74.45% of the RE allocation till 31.01.2026 
in FY 2025 -26. The Committee observes a notable shortfall between the Institute's 
projected demand of ₹749.65 crore an d the actual Budget Estimate allocation of ₹693.24 
crore for FY 2026-27. The Committee is of the view that the allocated Capital budget of 
₹112.00 crore may prove insufficient to comfortably procure and install high -end, life-
saving medical assets, such as  the planned LINAC machine for radiation oncology 
(costing approximately ₹30 crore), Biplane systems, and 4K 3D Laparoscopy sets, 
alongside the necessary civil works. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the 
Institute urgently overhaul its internal pr ocurement and administrative timelines to 

150 
 
accelerate fund utilization in the final quarter. The Department must proactively re -
evaluate the Capital allocation at the Revised Estimate stage and provide the additional 
funds required so that the acquisition o f this critical diagnostic and therapeutic 
equipment is not hindered by foreseeable financial constraints. 
 
3.24.4 The Committee acknowledges the severe geo -political and climatic challenges, 
including ongoing communal violence, curfews, internet bans, and  landslides, that have 
plagued Manipur since May 2023, severely disrupting the supply chains for manpower 
and construction materials. Despite these formidable hurdles, the Committee believes 
that the timely completion of the 150 -bedded Critical Care Block (currently at 50% 
progress) and the 1350 KLD -STP/250 KLD -ETP (at 40% progress) is absolutely non -
negotiable for the region's health security. The Committee, therefore, recommends that 
the Department establish a dedicated coordination mechanism with the Sta te 
Government and central security agencies to facilitate a protected logistics corridor for 
essential medical and construction supplies. The Committee is of the view that such 
strategic, inter -agency interventions are imperative to ensure the Executing Ag ency 
(HSCC Ltd.) meets the June 2026 deadline for the Critical Care Block, and to expedite 
the immediate functional handover of the completed Maternity Block. 
 
3.24.5 The present and cadre wise (both faculty and non -faculty) sanctioned vis -à-vis in 
position strength and vacant position in RIMS, Imphal, is placed below in tabular form . At 
present, there are 550 vacancies in RIMS, Imphal, out of these vacancies, a total of 325 
vacancies viz 130 posts of MTS (2023); 54 posts of Nursing Officer (2023);134 posts  of MTS 
(CRE-2025); and 7 posts (Lab. Technician -5 and Lab. Assistant -2) (CRE-2025) are pending 
before Hon‘ble High Court of Manipur. Further, 68 of the existing vacancies are promotional 
posts and another 69 posts have already been advertised for recruit ment (36 under CRE -2025 
and 33 under NORCET-2025). In addition to the above, 22 vacancies (2024) and 3 vacancies 
(2025) have been earmarked for Compassionate appointment. Accordingly, 63 vacancies are 
left for being taken up in the recruitment in the near future. The up -gradation of RIMS, 
Imphal, into a Tertiary Cancer Care Centre is being undertaken with Central Government 
financial assistance under the Ministry of Health & Family Welfare. Funds are being provided 
in a phased manner for infrastructure deve lopment, procurement of equipment and 
strengthening of Oncology. Dedicated infrastructure for oncology services, including Medical 
Oncology, Radiation Oncology, Surgical Oncology, Preventive Oncology and palliative 
Medicine Department has been planned and is being developed in a phased manner. As of 
now Radiation Oncology is also looking after  Medical Oncology and Palliative care services. 
Interim arrangements and referrals are in place to ensure continuity of cancer care. At present, 
no Public -Private-Partnership (PPP) has been finalized, though the option remains under 
consideration for development of Nuclear Medicine.  The present status of vacancy in the

3.24.6 The key concern areas of RIMS, Imphal are submitted as under: - 
i. Streamline OPD and IPD patient flow through revised schedules, digital 
management, and token systems. 
ii. Recruit additional medical, nursi ng, and support staff in a phased manner to meet

3.24.7 On being asked about the new projects, the Institute has taken/plans to take to make 
healthcare services accessible in the North Eastern Region, the Department stated that the 
Institute has undertaken several major infrastructure upgradat ion projects. A separate 
Maternity Block has been constructed and is ready for operation, while a Critical Care Block 
is currently under construction to strengthen health services and advanced emergency care. In 
addition, modernization of diagnostic facili ties, ICU expansion, and emergency service 
enhancement are being prioritized. RIMS, Imphal, initiated renal transplantation services in 
2024 and has also commenced open heart surgery following the establishment of the Regional 
Pediatric Cardiac Unit under the Rashtriya Bal Swasthya Karyakram (RBSK). The institute 
was declared the 13 th Center of Excellence for Rare Diseases in 2025 and has since started 
providing treatment for rare disease cases. A separate 40 -bedded Nephrology Department 
with 20 dialysis units has been established to strengthen kidney care services. Additionally, a 
dedicated 20 -bedded Cardiac Unit is currently being set up to enhance cardiac treatment 
facilities. Efforts are on the modernization of the Campus with the latest facilities to au gment 
patientcare service. The Master Plan for Redevelopment of RIMS, Imphal has been proposed  
 to be executed in three phases, over a period of 8 years, at a total project cost of ₹ 3642.91 
crores. 28 faculty posts have been recently created with the app roval of the Department of 
Expenditure. Some more posts are proposed to be created to further strengthen the human 
resource to meet the increasing patient load on the institute. 
 
3.24.8 On a query related to performance of RIMS Manipur which was upgraded t o Regional 
Cancer Centre in 2006, the submission stated that  RIMS, Imphal, was upgraded to a Regional 
Care Centre (RCC) in 2006 to strengthen cancer care services in the North Eastern Region. 
The Institute provides radiotherapy, chemotherapy, onco -surgery, pathology (including IHC), 
diagnostic imaging and palliative care services.  The Institute has essential infrastructure 
including radiotherapy equipment, chemotherapy day -care facilities, oncology wards and 
diagnostic support. However, there are shortages in certain specialist manpower and technical 
staff. The major issues faced include shortage of oncology specialists especially Medical 
Oncologists, Surgical Oncologists, Nuclear Medicine, Palliative Medicine Physician, 
Preventive Oncology clinicians/sectio n, adequate nursing staffs and unit expansion 
of Radiation Oncology, increasing patient load from the region, infrastructure constraints, 
delays in procurement of advanced equipment and need for enhanced funding support. 
  

152 
 
3.24.9 RIMS, Imphal, has taken va rious steps towards its upgradation as a Tertiary Cancer 
Care Centre. Its Radiation Oncology Department is already running as a full fledge 
Department at RIMS, Imphal. Further, the process for post creation of posts for the Surgical 
Oncology and Medical On cology is underway. In addition, the proposal for opening of 
Nuclear Medicine Department is being submitted in the next meeting of the (FC) Finance 
Committee of RIMS, Imphal. In addition, a proposal for development of a 150 bedded Cancer 
Hospital in RIMS, Imphal, is proposed to be executed under Re -development plan of the 
Institute.  
 
3.24.10 The Committee notes that while RIMS, Imphal was designated as a 
Regional Cancer Centre in 2006, its transition into a full -fledged Tertiary Cancer Care 
Centre remains constrained by significant gaps in specialized personnel and 
infrastructure. The Committee believes it is concerning that the Radiation Oncology 
Department is currently forced to oversee Medical Oncology and Palliative care services 
due to a lack of dedica ted specialists. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the 
Department fast -track the creation and recruitment of specialized faculty posts for 
Surgical Oncology, Medical Oncology, and Nuclear Medicine. Furthermore, the 
Committee is of the view that the  proposal for the 150 -bedded Cancer Hospital under 
the Institute's Redevelopment Plan must be prioritized for financial clearance to 
eliminate existing infrastructure bottlenecks and reduce the reliance on interim referral 
arrangements for the North Eastern population. 
 
3.24.11 The Committee observes with grave concern that out of 550 total 
vacancies at RIMS, Imphal, a staggering 325 posts, including the crucial Nursing Officer 
cadres, are currently stalled due to pending litigation before the Hon‘ble High Court of 
Manipur. The Committee believes that a 45% vacancy rate in Group C positions 
severely undermines the Institute‘s ability to operationalize new facilities, such as the 
newly constructed Maternity Block and the upcoming 150 -bedded Critical Care Bloc k. 
The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department, in coordination with the 
Institute‘s legal counsel, move for an expedited hearing or interim resolution of these 
court cases to release these vital posts for recruitment. The Committee is, additi onally, 
of the view that the 63 vacancies currently available for immediate recruitment should 
be filled without further delay to support the Institute‘s recent expansion into renal 
transplantation and rare disease treatment. 
 
3.24.12 The Committee notes the ambitious Master Plan for the Redevelopment of 
RIMS, Imphal, estimated at ₹3,642.91 crore over an eight -year period. The Committee 
believes that such a large -scale modernization is essential for RIMS to function 
effectively as the 13 th Center of Excelle nce for Rare Diseases and to sustain its newly 
initiated open-heart surgery and pediatric cardiac programs. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends that the Department provide a clear, multi -year funding commitment for 
Phase-I of this redevelopment to ensure that diagnostic modernization, ICU expansion, 
and the establishment of the dedicated 20 -bedded Cardiac Unit proceed as scheduled. 
The Committee is further of the view that the Department should seriously explore the 
proposed Public -Private-Partnership (PPP ) model for the development of the Nuclear 

153 
 
Medicine Department to ensure that advanced diagnostic capabilities are made available 
to patients in the North Eastern Region in a time-bound manner.
