3.25.4 The Key Concern Areas of RIPANS, Aizawl for optimum functioning are stated as 
under: 
a) Geographical concerns (hilly terrain/remote area): Logistics of timely delivery of 
equipment/consumables; Incessant rainfalls in certain months leading to landslide 
which can effect; Major electric stations and wires causing electricity shortage and 
poor internet connectivity; Blockage of supply chains of essential goods (eg. Fuel, 
food, medicine). 
 
b) Absence of servicing centre within Mizoram which negatively impact the lead time for 
maintenance and servicing of major and minor equipment malfunctions. 
 
c) Site saturation, resulting in constrained physical capacity for augmentation of 
auxiliary facilities and future upgradation initiatives. 
 
d) Water supply shortage in the whole state which affects the hospital services. 
 
3.25.5 The Committee observes that 19 posts remain vacant out of the sanctioned 
strength of 64 Group ‗A‘ posts, including key academi c positions such as Professors, 
Associate Professors and Assistant Professors. Given that RIPANS plays an important 
role in strengthening the paramedical and nursing workforce in the North Eastern 
region, the Committee recommends that the Department take proactive steps to expedite 
recruitment and address structural issues affecting faculty availability. Measure s to be 
undertaken include timely completion of promotional processes, review of recruitment 
rules where necessary, wider national outreach for facu lty recruitment, and provision of 
incentives to attract qualified professionals to serve in remote and geographically 
challenging regions. Ensuring adequate faculty strength is essential for maintaining 

155 
 
academic standards, expanding training capacity, and supporting the functioning of the 
newly operational hospital facilities. 
 
3.25.6 The Committee also takes note of several operational challenges faced by 
RIPANS owing to its location in a remote and hilly region, including difficulties in 
logistics and sup ply chains, frequent disruptions due to heavy rainfall and landslides, 
lack of local servicing centres for medical equipment, limited physical space for future 
expansion, and recurring issues relating to water supply and power connectivity. These 
factors c an adversely affect the timely availability of essential medical supplies, 
maintenance of equipment, and the overall efficiency of healthcare delivery. The 
Committee therefore recommends that the Department, in coordination with the State 
Government and ot her concerned agencies, explore measures to strengthen logistical 
support systems, establish regional maintenance and servicing facilities for medical 
equipment, and develop contingency mechanisms for uninterrupted supply of essential 
utilities such as water, electricity and internet connectivity.

3.26.6 Key Concern Areas Affecting Optimum Functioning of LGBRIMH 
a) Inadequate number of faculty and trained professionals to provide the range of 
services required. 
b) Establishment of a dynamic functional model for especially developing the mental 
health services in the NER of our country. 
c) Autonomy to design implement and develop training models for mental health 
professionals at all levels w ith freedom to confer degrees and diplomas like the 
AIIMS, NIMHANS, PGIMER JIPMER etc. 
d) Management of co-morbid illnesses for the admitted patients in a regular manner. 
e) Developing centres with domain expertise to deal with issues requiring multi -
disciplinary team work. 
f) Attrition of the staff due to lack of creating an attractive ecosystem for the 
professionals and faculty. 
g) Though the LGBRIMH is the only tertiary care stand -alone mental health setup in the 
entire NER and the only 3rd one under the GOI it is n ot yet comparable in terms of 
opportunities and facilities with the similarly mandated GOI institutes of the country. 
3.26.7 The Committee finds that the Lokpriya Gopinath Bordoloi Regional Institute of 
Mental Health (LGBRIMH), Tezpur has demonstrated sati sfactory utilisation of the funds 
allocated to it, with 99.40% utilisation of the Revised Estimate in FY 2024 –25 and over 82% 
utilisation of the Revised Estimate in FY 2025 –26 up to January 2026. However, the 
Committee observes that the Budget Estimate of ₹69.32 crore for FY 2026–27 is significantly 
lower than the Institute‘s projected requirement of ₹93.48 crore, resulting in a shortfall of 
about ₹24.16 crore. The Committee further notes with concern that after meeting the 
committed expenditure under GIA ( Salary) and GIA (General), only a nominal amount of 
₹0.50 crore remains under the Capital head, which is insufficient for procurement of 
essential hospital equipment and infrastructure development, despite the recommendation of 
₹11.70 crore by the SFC for capital expenditure. Considering that LGBRIMH is a key 
tertiary care mental health institution serving the entire North Eastern region, the 
Committee recommends that the Department review the Institute‘s financial requirements 
and provide adequate capital support in subsequent budget allocations so that critical 
equipment procurement, infrastructure development, and service expansion are not 
adversely affected. 
 
3.26.8 The Committee also notes that 57 posts remain vacant out of a sanctioned strength of 
323, including 20 Group ‗A‘ posts, along with substantial shortages of Senior Resident 
Doctors and Junior Residents. The Committee further observes that recruitment could not 

158 
 
be undertaken during FY 2025 –26 due to insufficient budgetary provision under the sal ary 
head, which has affected the Institute‘s ability to expand services and maintain adequate 
clinical, academic, and research capacity. Given that LGBRIMH serves as the only tertiary -
level standalone mental health institute in the North Eastern region, su ch manpower 
shortages can significantly constrain its ability to address the growing burden of mental 
health disorders in the region. The Committee therefore recommends that the Department 
ensure adequate financial provision for manpower requirements and e xpedite recruitment 
processes to fill vacant posts in a time -bound manner. Simultaneously, efforts should be 
made to create a more attractive professional ecosystem through improved facilities, 
research opportunities, and academic incentives in order to re duce attrition and attract 
qualified mental health professionals to serve in the Institute. 
 
3.26.9 The Committee further notes that LGBRIMH continues to face several structural and 
institutional challenges, including limited faculty strength, the need for  specialised 
multidisciplinary centres, constraints in managing co -morbid medical conditions among 
psychiatric patients, and the absence of adequate autonomy to design and expand training 
programmes comparable to other premier national institutes. The Comm ittee, therefore, 
recommends that the Department consider strengthening the institutional capacity of 
LGBRIMH through enhanced academic and administrative autonomy, enabling it to 
develop specialised training programmes, expand multidisciplinary services, and confer 
advanced qualifications in mental health disciplines. Additionally, the Committee suggests 
that the Department expedite the preparation and approval of estimates for pending capital 
and maintenance works through CPWD and other executing agencies  so that essential 
infrastructure development and campus facilities can be completed in a timely manner. 
Strengthening the institutional and infrastructural capacity of LGBRIMH will be critical for 
improving access to specialised mental healthcare and trai ning of professionals across the 
North Eastern region.

CHAPTER IV 
 
NATIONAL HEALTH MISSION 
4.1 National Health Mission 
 
4.1.1   National Health Mission (NHM) aims for attainment of universal access to 
equitable, affordable and quality hea lth care services, accountable and responsive to people‘s 
needs, with effective inter -sectoral convergent action to address the wider social determinants of 
health. NHM enables the country to advance towards meeting Sustainable Development Goal 3, 
i.e. "Ensure healthy lives and promote well -being for all at all ages", including Universal Health 
Coverage. NHM consists of two Sub -Missions, the National Rural Health Mission (NRHM) and 
the National Urban Health Mission (NUHM).  
 
4.1.2   The major objectives of NHM are as under:  
 
(i) Reduction in child and maternal mortality.  
(ii) Prevention and control of communicable and non -communicable diseases, 
including locally endemic diseases.  
(iii) Access to integrated comprehensive primary health care.  
(iv) Population stabilisation, gender equality and demographic balance.  
(v) Universal access to public services for food and nutrition, sanitation and hygiene 
and universal access to public health care services with emphasis on services 
addressing women‘s and children‘s health and universal immunisation.  
(vi) Promotion of healthy life styles.  
 
4.1.3   The main programmatic components include Health System Strengthening in 
rural and urban areas, Reproductive -Maternal- Neonatal-Child and Adolescent Health 
(RMNCH+A), an d Communicable and Non -Communicable Diseases. Under the NHM, 
technical and financial support is provided to State / UTs for strengthening of their healthcare 
systems based on the proposals submitted by them in their Programme Implementation Plans 
(PIPs). 
 
4.1.4 Under NHM, the performance of various health programmes including target sets 
under NHP are regularly monitored in all the States/UTs, through review meetings, mid -term 
reviews of key deliverables, field visits of senior officials, promoting performa nce by setting

4.1.5 Various new initiatives carried out under NHM  by the Government of India in the 
country include operationalization of Ayushman  Arogya Mandirs, National Ambulance 
Services, Mobile Medical Units, ASHAs, 24 x 7 Services and First Referral Facilities, Prime 
Minister's National Dialysis Programme, Free Diagnostics Service Initiative and Free Drugs 
Service Initiative, activities under Reproductive & Child Health, Anaemia  Mukt Bharat 
(AMB) strategy, TB Mukt Bharat Abhiyaan and Universal Immunization programme.

Urban Local Bodies (ULBs). Physical progress as well as the service delivery achievements

4.1.7 The Committee notes that the National Health Mission (NHM) has played a 
pivotal role in strengthening India‘s public health system and advancing progress 
towards the targets set under the National Health Policy, 2017 and Sustain able 
Development Goal -3. Significant improvements have been recorded in several health 
indicators, including an increase in life expectancy to 70.3 years, reduction in Infant 
Mortality Rate to 25 per thousand, Maternal Mortality Ratio to 88 per lakh live b irths, 
and high levels of immunisation coverage and antenatal care. The Committee 
appreciates the progress made in elimination of diseases such as Kala -Azar and 
sustained control of leprosy, along with improved TB treatment coverage. However, the 
Committee observes that certain targets, such as further reduction in under -five 
mortality, neonatal mortality, and improvement in family planning coverage, still 
require sustained attention. The Committee therefore recommends that the Department 
strengthen targeted interventions under NHM, particularly in States and districts that 
continue to lag behind national averages, through enhanced monitoring, data -driven 
planning, and focused support under Programme Implementation Plans (PIPs). Greater 
emphasis may also be placed on preventive healthcare, early detection of diseases, and 
behavioural change communication to consolidate the gains already achieved and 
accelerate progress towards remaining health targets. 
 
4.1.8 The Committee observe s the steady expansion of ser vices under the National 
Urban Health Mission (NUHM), with a significant increase in the number of Urban 
Primary Health Centres, polyclinics, Ayushman Arogya Mandirs, and healthcare 
personnel over the last three years, which has contributed to rising OPD a ttendance and 
improved service delivery in urban areas. While these developments indicate progress in 
strengthening urban health infrastructure, the Committee emphasises that rapid 
urbanisation, migration, and the growth of informal settlements continue to  place 
considerable pressure on urban public health systems. The Committee therefore 
recommends that the Department should strengthen the NUHM framework by ensuring 
adequate human resources, improving urban health infrastructure, and enhancing 
coordination with Urban Local Bodies to effectively address the healthcare needs of 

162 
 
vulnerable populations, including urban poor and migrant workers. The Committee also 
suggests expanding community participation through Mahila Arogya Samitis and 
ASHAs, and strengtheni ng initiatives such as free diagnostics, free drug services, and 
mobile medical units to ensure equitable and accessible healthcare services in urban 
areas. 
 
4.1.9 Budgetary Allocation 
 
 
 
4.1.10  The Committee observes that under the National Health Missi on (NHM), a 
Budget Estimate (BE) of ₹36,000.00 crore was allocated for FY 2024 –25, which remained 
unchanged at the Revised Estimate (RE) stage. Against this, the actual expenditure was 
₹38,889.34 crore, indicating utilisation of about 108.03% of the RE all ocation. In FY 2025 –
26, the allocation was ₹37,226.92 crore at the BE stage, which was marginally reduced to 
₹37,100.07 crore at the RE stage. Out of this, ₹26,862.91 crore has been utilised up to 
31.01.2026, representing about 72.41% utilisation of the RE  allocation which is low as only 
two months of the financial year has been remaining. For FY 2026 –27, the Budget Estimate 
has been increased to ₹39,390.00 crore, indicating a continued upward trend in allocation 
under the Mission.

that NRHM was launched in 2005 with the objective of building public health systems to 
provide accessible, affordable and quality health care to the rural population, especially the 
vulnerable g roups, up to the District Hospitals (DH) level. NUHM  was launched in 2013,

4.1.12 A key pillar of NHM  is Health System Strengthening (HSS ). This includes 
infrastructure expansion, recruitment of doctors, nurses and paramedics on regular and 
contractual basis, skill development, quality assurance initiatives (including National Quality 
Assurance Standards), Kayakalp  for facility cleanliness, biomedical equipment maintenance, 
and IT -enabled systems such as the Health Management Info rmation System (HMIS).  
Through around 1.82 lakh  Ayushman Arogya Mandirs (AAMs), 12 comprehensive primary 
healthcare packages are being provided by strengthening Sub Health Centres (SHCs) and 
Primary Health Centres (PHCs). These AAMs  provide preventive, pro motive, rehabilitative 
and curative care for an expanded range of services encompassing reproductive and child 
healthcare services, Communicable diseases, Non -communicable diseases and other health 
issues. The Pradhan  Mantri Ayushman Bharat Health Infrastr ucture Mission (PM -ABHIM) 
with an outlay of Rs.64,180 crore  aims to support for infrastructure development for Sub -
Health Centres, Urban Health and Wellness Centres, Support for Block Public Health Units, 
Integrated District Public Health Laboratories and Critical Care Hospital Blocks.

4.1.14 On bei ng asked whether the Budgetary allocation for different components under 
NHM for 2026-27 is as per the projected requirements, the Department submitted that Public 
Health‘ & ‗Hospitals‘ are State subjects, hence, the primary responsibility of providing bas ic 
healthcare services to all the citizens of the country lies with respective State/UT 
Government. The National Health Mission (NHM) provides support for improvement in 
health infrastructure, availability of adequate human resources in health facilities, to improve 
availability and accessibility to quality health services for all districts in the country. The 
Ministry of Health and Family Welfare provides technical and financial support to the 
States/UTs to strengthen the public healthcare system, based on  the proposals received in the 
form of Programme Implementation Plans (PIPs) under NHM. Government of India provides 
approval for the proposals in the form of Record of Proceedings (RoPs) as per norms & 
available resources.  
 

164 
 
4.1.15 The Committee notes tha t the expenditure under the National Health Mission 
(NHM) for FY 2024 –25 exceeded the Revised Estimates, indicating strong demand for 
financial resources and the critical role of the Mission in supporting public health 
systems across the country. However, the Committee also observes that in FY 2025 –26, 
the utilisation of funds up to 31.01.2026 stands at about 72.41 per cent of the Revised 
Estimates, which appears relatively low considering that only two months remain in the 
financial year. The Committee is of the view that such variations between allocation, 
utilisation, and actual expenditure point towards issues relating to financial planning, 
timely release of funds, and absorption capacity at the State/UT level. The Committee, 
therefore, recommends that the Department must strengthen monitoring and 
coordination mechanisms with States/UTs to ensure timely submission of Programme 
Implementation Plans (PIPs), faster approval of Record of Proceedings (RoPs), and 
more efficient utilisation of allocated funds. The Committee also desires that the 
Department put in place a robust system for periodic review of expenditure trends so 
that mid -year corrections can be undertaken to avoid both underutilisation and 
overshooting of allocations. 
 
4.1.16 The Committee observes that a substantial portion of the allocation under NHM 
for FY 2026–27 is directed towards the Flexible Pool for Reproductive and Child Health 
(RCH), Health System Strengthening, National Health Programmes, and the National 
Urban Health Mission, which t ogether account for the major share of the total 
allocation. Given the expanding scope of services under the Ayushman Arogya Mandirs 
and the ongoing infrastructure development under the Pradhan Mantri Ayushman 
Bharat Health Infrastructure Mission, the Committee emphasises the need for sustained 
investments in strengthening primary healthcare infrastructure, human resources, and 
diagnostic capacities at the grassroots level. The Committee recommends that the 
Department prioritise effective implementation of these components by ensuring 
adequate staffing of health facilities, timely operationalisation of newly created 
infrastructure, and improved integration of digital health systems such as HMIS for 
real-time monitoring of service delivery. The Committee also  urges the Department to 
work closely with States/UTs to address regional disparities in health infrastructure and 
service delivery so that the benefits of NHM interventions reach vulnerable and 
underserved populations in both rural and urban areas. 
 
4.1.17 As per GFR, up to 75% of central allocation of funds are released to the States/UTs on 
the basis of provisional UCs of the previous financial year and the remaining 25% is subject 
to submission of audited utilization certificate. On a specific query, the  Committee has been 
apprised of that no utilization certificate is pending under NHM.  On a query about the key 
concern areas identified during implementation of various programmes/components of 
NRHM and NUHM during last three years, the Committee was info rmed that the key 
challenges are being addressed through the set objectives under NHM (NRHM and NUHM) 
as given below: 
  
a) National Quality Assurance Standard (NQAS) lays down a set of quality parameters 
for public health facilities. These standards are linke d with well -defined assessment 

165 
 
tools and identified gaps are traversed through continual quality improvement methods 
and tools. NQAS are currently available for District Hospitals, CHCs, PHCs and 
Urban PHCs. Standards are primarily meant for providers to a ssess their own quality 
for improvement through pre -defined standards and to bring up their facilities for 
certification. The NQAS program aims to improve the credibility of public hospitals 
and recognize facilities that perform well. 
b) Pradhan Mantri Ayushm an Bharat Health Infrastructure Mission (PM -ABHIM) with 
an outlay of Rs. 64,180 Crores aims to support for infrastructure development for Sub-
Health Centres, Urban Health and Wellness Centres, Support for Block Public Health 
Units, Integrated District Publ ic Health Laboratories and Critical Care Hospital 
Blocks. The PM -ABHIM scheme aims to fill critical gaps in health infrastructure, 
surveillance and health research – spanning both the urban and rural areas. The 
measures under the scheme focus on developing  capacities of health systems and 
institutions across the continuum of care at all levels viz. primary, secondary and 
tertiary and on preparing health systems in responding effectively to the current and 
future pandemics/ disasters.  
c) 15th Finance Commission has recommended grants aggregating to Rs. 70,051 Crores 
over the period of five years (2021 -2026) through local government to strengthen the 
healthcare system in states. 
d) Financial support is provided to States under National Health Mission (NHM) to 
strengthen the health system including engagement of Nurses, doctors and specialist 
on contractual basis based on the appraisal of requirements proposed by the States in 
their annual Programme Implementation Plans. To increase the availability of doctors, 
several initiatives have been taken to rationalize the norms in medical education, such 
as, relaxation in land requirements, bed strength, increase in ceiling for maximum 
intake for undergraduates, enhancement of teacher -student ratio in PG etc which has 
resulted in substantial increase in number of undergraduate and post graduate seats. 
Government has also approved setting up of ANM/GNM Schools in different States 
besides setting up of Institutes of Paramedical Sciences at National and regional 
levels. In order  to encourage the States to fill up existing vacancies in remote rural 
areas, the states are being incentivized to ensure rational deployment of health human 
resource. 
  
4.1.18 The Department further stated that the Government of India has taken number of 
initiatives in the form of incentives and honorarium to the medical professionals for 
encouraging better service delivery in rural and remote areas in the country, which include: 
 
a) Hard Area Allowance to specialist doctors for serving in rural and remote ar eas so 
that they find it attractive to serve in public health facilities in such areas. 
b) Attractive Honorarium to Gynecologists/ Emergency Obstetric Care (EmoC) trained, 
Pediatricians & Anesthetist/ Life Saving Anaesthesia Skills (LSAS) trained doctors to 
increase availability of specialists for conducting Cesarean Sections in rural & remote 
area. 

166 
 
c) Special incentives for doctors, incentive for ANM for ensuring timely ANC checkup 
and recording, incentives for conducting Adolescent Reproductive and Sexual Healt h 
activities. 
d) States are also allowed to offer negotiable salary to attract specialist including 
flexibility in strategies such as ―You Quote We Pay‖. 
e) Non-monetary incentives such as preferential admission in postgraduate courses for 
staff serving in diffi cult areas and improving accommodation arrangement in rural 
areas have also been introduced under NHM. 
f) Multi-skilling of doctors is supported under NHM to overcome the shortage of 
specialists. Skill upgradation of existing HR is another major strategy unde r the 
National Rural Health Mission (NRHM) for achieving improvement in health 
outcomes. 
 
4.1.19 To ensure availability of essential drugs and reduce the Out of Pocket Expenditure 
(OOPE) of the patients visiting the public health facilities, Government has rolled out the Free 
Drugs Service Initiative under NHM. This includes financial support to States/UTs for 106 
drugs at Sub -Health Centre (SHC) level, 172 at Primary Health Centre (PHC) level, 300 at 
Community Health Centre (CHC) level, 318 at Sub -District Hospitals (SDH) level and 381 
drugs at district Hospitals. This Ministry also supports ‗Free Diagnostics Service Initiative‘ 
programme under NHM with the aim to provide accessible and affordable pathological and 
radiological diagnostics services closer to  the community, which in turn reduces the OOPE. 
Diagnostics services are provided free of cost at all levels of public health facilities [14 tests 
at SHCs, 63 at PHCs, 97 at CHCs, 111 test at SDH and 134 tests at District Hospitals].  
 
4.1.20 Teleconsultation is expanding the reach to specialist care and enabling patients to 
connect with medical officers and specialists from remote locations. Teleconsultation 
platforms such as eSanjeevani and Tele MANAS facilitate real -time communication between 
patients and healthcare providers, allowing accurate and timely diagnosis, treatment planning, 
and follow -up care. This approach enhances the reach and quality of healthcare services, 
reduces Out-Of-Pocket Expenditure (OOPE) for patients with the need of long -distance travel 
in remote areas, and ensures timely consultations. Additionally, teleconsultation supports 
capacity building among primary care providers particularly the Community Health Officers 
(CHOs) and Auxiliary Nurse and Midwife (ANM) at the AAM-SHC by enabling them to seek 
guidance from specialists for handling the patients independently in future.  Ayushman Bharat 
Digital Mission (ABDM) launched in September 2021, aims to create the world‘s largest 
digital health ecosystem, supporting universal health cove rage and developing the backbone 
necessary to support the integrated digital health infrastructure of the country. ABDM 
envisages to create a secure online platform based on open, interoperable digital standards to 
bridge the existing gap amongst different stakeholders of healthcare ecosystem through digital 
pathways. 
 
4.1.21 The Committee notes with satisfaction that no Utilisation Certificate (UC) is 
pending under the National Health Mission (NHM) and that the release of funds to 
States/UTs is being carri ed out in accordance with the provisions of the General 
Financial Rules, whereby up to 75 per cent of the Central allocation is released on the 

167 
 
basis of provisional UCs and the remaining 25 per cent upon submission of audited UCs. 
While this reflects an ov erall sound financial compliance framework, the Committee is 
of the view that the effective utilisation of funds must be complemented by tangible 
improvements in health infrastructure, quality of services and accessibility of healthcare 
facilities, particu larly in underserved regions. In this regard, the Committee 
recommends that the Department further strengthen its monitoring and evaluation 
mechanisms to ensure that the financial resources released under NHM translate into 
measurable improvements in servi ce delivery, especially through initiatives such as the 
National Quality Assurance Standards (NQAS), the Free Drugs and Free Diagnostics 
Service Initiatives, and teleconsultation platforms. The Committee also desires that the 
Department develop a comprehen sive outcome -based monitoring framework, linking 
financial releases with performance indicators such as quality certification of health 
facilities, availability of essential drugs and diagnostics, and reduction in Out -of-Pocket 
Expenditure (OOPE) for patients. 
 
4.1.22 The Committee further notes that despite the various initiatives undertaken by 
the Government under NHM, including incentives for medical professionals, multi -
skilling of doctors, expansion of medical education capacity and digital health init iatives 
such as teleconsultation and the Ayushman Bharat Digital Mission (ABDM), challenges 
related to shortage and uneven distribution of human resources, particularly in rural 
and remote areas, continue to persist. The Committee is of the considered view  that 
strengthening the health workforce is critical for ensuring the effective functioning of 
primary, secondary and tertiary healthcare systems. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends that the Department, in close coordination with States/UTs, intensify 
efforts to address the gaps in availability of doctors, specialists, nurses and paramedical 
staff by ensuring timely recruitment, rational deployment and adequate capacity 
building of healthcare personnel. The Committee also urges the Department to further 
leverage digital health platforms, telemedicine services and the expanding network of 
Ayushman Arogya Mandirs to enhance access to specialist care and improve the quality 
of healthcare delivery, particularly in aspirational districts, remote rural areas and  
urban slums, thereby ensuring equitable access to affordable healthcare across the 
country. 
 
4.1.23 On being asked about the measures taken to ensure that the Ayushman Arogya 
Mandirs (AAMs) under Ayushman Bharat Scheme become fully equipped to deliver 
healthcare services under NHM, the Department submitted that the 
Ayushman Arogya Mandirs (AAMs) incrementally provide 12 package of primary health care 
services across urban, rural and tribal dominated areas, encompassing Reproductive and child 
care services, Communicable diseases, Non Communicable diseases (NCD), Palliative Care 
and elderly Care, Care for common mental disorders, neurological conditions (epilepsy, 
dementia) and substance use disorders management (tobacco, alcohol, drugs), Oral health, 
ENT care, and Basic emergency care. To complement the expanded services, the essential list 
of drugs & diagnostics has been expanded to make available 172 drugs & 63 diagnostics at 
PHC-AAMs and 106 drugs & 14 diagnostics at SHC-AAMs. 
  

168 
 
4.1.24 The teleconsultation  services, available at all operational AAMs  across the country, 
enables people to access the specialist services closer to their homes addressing concerns of 
physical accessibility, shortage of service providers and to facilitate continuum of care. Total 
teleconsultations conducted at AAMs  are 41.93 crore, as on 30.11.2025. Beyond the 
management of illnesses, promotive and preventive health is an integral part of 
comprehensive primary health care. Wellness -related activities like Yoga, cycling, and 
meditation are conducted in AAMs. As on 30.11.2025, a total of 6.54 crore wellness sessions 
have been conducted at AAMs. AAMs are equipped with the required resources including 
upgraded infrastructure, additional human resource, essential drugs & diagnostics, 
Information Technology (IT) systems, etc. to provide twelve packages of primary health care 
services across the country including tribal and remote areas.  On a query on the expenditure 
profile under the Flexible Pool for RCH and Health System Strengthening, N ational Health 
Programme and National Urban Health Mission, the Committee was informed that within the 
Flexible Pool for Health System Strengthening, approvals have been accorded to States/UTs 
for NUHM activities related to Comprehensive Primary Healthcare  (CPHC), Community 
Engagement, Public Health Institutions as per IPHS norms, Quality Assurance, Human 
Resource for Health, Technical Assistance, Planning and Program Management, Innovation, 
Untied Grants, etc. running between FMR code 127 to 149. 
 
4.1.25 On being asked about appropriation of funds to the tune of ₹7350.00 crore in BE 
2026-27 under Infrastructure Maintenance, As per the new procedure based on the DO letter 
of Ministry dated 12.10.2021 funds under Infrastructure Maintenance (IM) are provided to the 
States/UTs only on reimbursement basis i.e. the States/UT are first incurring the expenditure 
towards the salary expenditure of the regular staff and later claim the expenditure from 
Ministry which is reimbursed to the States/UTs in the following man ner: up to 75% of the 
total outstanding amount i.e. arrears of previous year(s) are reimbursed on furnishing of 
provisional Utilization Certificate (UC) and annual expenditure of a particular year ii. Balance 
amount are reimbursed on submission of Accounta nt General (AG) Audited Statement of 
expenditure (ASE) and Utilization Certificate (UC). Regular reviews & monitoring is done to 
maintain the pace of expenditure.  
 
4.1.26 On being queried about any new policies or programs in place to improve maternal 
and child healthcare in 2026 -27, the Department submitted that The MoHFW has developed 
tailored strategies for addressing challenges in various regions to reduce Maternal Mortality 
Ratio (MMR). The Sustainable Development Goal (SDG) target 3.1 related to the maternal 
health is to reduce Maternal Mortality Ratio (MMR) to less than 70 per 100,000 live births by 
2030. India has shown a steady progress in reducing maternal mortality, with MMR declining 
from 97 per 100,000 live births in SRS 2018 -20 to 93 in SRS 2019-21, and currently standing 
at 88 in SRS 2021 -23.  Progress in reducing Maternal Mortality is monitored through the 
Sample Registration System (SRS) released by the Registrar General of India (RGI), which 
provides Maternal Mortality Ratio (MMR) at the Na tional and State level. These statistics are 
published periodically and used to monitor Maternal Mortality Ratio (MMR). Eight States 
namely Kerala (30), Maharashtra (36), Tamil Nadu (35), Andhra Pradesh (30), Telangana 
(59), Jharkhand (54), Gujarat (51), a nd Karnataka (68) have already achieved the SDG target 
for MMR.  

169 
 
 
4.1.27 The MoHFW has developed tailored strategies for addressing challenges in various 
regions to reduce Infant Mortality Rate (IMR). These strategies focus on customized and 
targeted interventions for high-risk mothers and infants to bridge critical gaps in maternal and 
newborn & child healthcare services including nutrition and counselling in high -burden 
regions. Facility-based and community -based newborn care is being strengthened to ensu re 
continuity of care and early identification of high -risk newborns and infants. For improving 
child survival, Ministry is implementing various interventions namely establishment of 
dedicated Mother Newborn Care Units (MNCUs), Special Newborn Care Units ( SNCUs) and 
Newborn Stabilization Units (NBSUs) at District and sub -district level under Facility Based 
Newborn Care (FBNC) with special focus in high burden districts with high NMR and 
U5MR.  A strategy is being developed wherein ASHAs perform home visits with special 
focus on management and follow -up of at -risk newborns and children in the community. 
STOP Diarrhoea and Social Awareness and Actions to Neutralize Pneumonia Successfully 
(SAANS) Campaign are being implemented with intersectoral convergence to reduce 
morbidity and mortality from childhood diarrhoea and pneumonia. Also, a digital tracking 
system is being developed for capturing longitudinal health records of children under 
Rashtriya Bal Swasthya Karyakram (RBSK). 
 
4.1.28 The Committee finds the A yushman Arogya Mandirs (AAMs) are envisaged as 
the cornerstone of comprehensive primary healthcare under the National Health 
Mission, delivering twelve packages of services including reproductive and child health, 
management of communicable and non -communicable diseases, palliative and elderly 
care, mental health services, and basic emergency care. The Committee also takes note 
of the expansion of essential drugs and diagnostics at PHC -AAMs and SHC -AAMs, as 
well as the extensive use of teleconsultation serv ices which have facilitated more than 41 
crore consultations as on 30.11.2025. While appreciating these efforts, the Committee is 
of the view that the effectiveness of AAMs depends largely on the consistent availability 
of trained human resources, uninterr upted supply of medicines and diagnostics, and 
robust digital connectivity, particularly in remote, tribal and aspirational districts. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department strengthen the operational 
capacity of AAMs by ensuring regular monitoring of service delivery standards, timely 
replenishment of drugs and diagnostics, and adequate training and retention of 
Community Health Officers and frontline health workers. The Committee also desires 
that the Department expand the integration of  telemedicine and digital health platforms 
so that specialist consultations and referral services are seamlessly available at the 
primary healthcare level, thereby improving continuity of care and reducing the burden 
on secondary and tertiary healthcare facilities. 
 
4.1.29 The Committee takes note of the progress made in improving maternal and child 
health indicators in the country, particularly the steady decline in the Maternal 
Mortality Ratio and the targeted interventions aimed at reducing Infant Mortal ity Rate 
through initiatives such as Facility Based Newborn Care, Mother Newborn Care Units, 
Special Newborn Care Units and community -based follow-up through ASHA workers. 
While acknowledging that several States have already achieved the Sustainable 

170 
 
Development Goal target for Maternal Mortality Ratio, the Committee observes that 
disparities persist across regions and that high -burden districts continue to require 
focused attention. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department intensify 
targeted interventions in States and districts with relatively higher maternal and child 
mortality indicators by strengthening institutional delivery systems, improving referral 
transport and emergency obstetric care services, and enhancing community -based 
monitoring through frontline health workers. The Committee also urges the Department 
to leverage digital initiatives such as the proposed longitudinal health tracking system 
under Rashtriya Bal Swasthya Karyakram to ensure early identification of high -risk 
mothers and children, better follow -up care, and improved data -driven planning for 
maternal and child healthcare programmes across the country. 
 
4.2 STRENGTHENING OF STATE DRUG REGULATORY SYSTEM 
 
4.2.1 Under the program of Strengthening of State Drug Regulatory  System (SSDRS), the 
Government provides financial assistance to states and UTs for strengthening drug regulatory 
system in the country.  This program is of great importance to address the concerns related to 
quality of generic medicines and drugs manufact ured in the country, and to achieve the 
affordability and quality healthcare across the country, particularly for poor section of the 
population and in the under-served and remote areas.  
4.2.2 The Committee notes that under the program, a Budget Estimate of ₹75.00 crore was 
allocated in 2024 –25, which was reduced to ₹50.00 crore at the Revised Estimates stage. 
Against this, the actual expenditure during the year amounted to ₹48.32 crore, indicating near-
full utilisation of the revised allocation. For the y ear 2025–26, an amount of ₹50.00 crore was 
provided at the Budget Estimates stage; however, the allocation was reduced to nil at the

Revised Estimates stage and no expenditure had been reported. Further, no allocation has 
been proposed under this Head in the Budget Estimates for 2026–27. 
4.2.3 On being asked the reason for non -allocation of any budgetary allocation to the 
scheme in 2026-27, the Department submitted that based on the ‗No Objection‘ received from 
the Department of Expenditure in January, 2026 , extension of the scheme for a further period 
of one year, i.e., up to 31.03.2026, without any additional financial implication, was approved 
in the first week of February, 2026. However, by that time, the Revised Estimates (R.E.) for 
FY 2025 –26 had alrea dy been reduced to zero. As regards FY 2026 –27, no budgetary 
allocation has been made under the existing SSDRS as the scheme period has ended and a 
revamped scheme, namely SSDRS 2.0, has been proposed with revised objectives and an 
outlay of ₹496.16 crore for a period of five years (2026 –27 to 2030 –31). The proposal for 
SSDRS 2.0 is presently under consideration of the Department of Expenditure. Budgetary 
allocation for 2026–27 will be made subject to approval of the new scheme. 
4.2.4 On a query on the steps that can be taken to further strengthen the State Drug 
Regulation system, the Department submitted that s o far under the existing centrally 
sponsored scheme i.e Strengthening of State Drug Regulatory System (SSDRS) Scheme (1.0), 
a total of 19 new State drug testing laboratories have been established, while 28 existing State 
drug testing laboratories have been upgraded. Further, 38 existing State Drug Control offices 
have been upgraded and regulatory manpower has been upskilled and/or engaged. Through 
the initiative, several States have created a drugs testing infrastructure. Several states also 
moved on from shared/rented spaces into own offices. Regional and district level offices were 
also created in various states, ensuring better enforcement.  
4.2.5 The functional testing capacity has increased to 1,66,830 samples per annum from 
57,000 samples in FY 2012 -13 (before inception of the Scheme), with further improvement 
expected once State -level manpower gaps are addressed. In the F.Y 2024 -25 (till sep 2025)  
1,25,020 drug samples have been tested. Risk -based inspections and surveillance; The Risk -
Based Inspection (RBI) initiative involves inspecting drug manufacturing units and labs that 
are identified based on specific risk criteria, including product type, compliance history, 
market feedback, and past violations. As of now, 1230 units have been inspected, resulting in 
1140 actions being taken. These actions include Stop Production Orders (SPO), Stop Testing 
Orders (STO), Suspension of testing, withdrawal of testing, license suspensions/cancellations, 
warning letters and showcase notices, depending on the severity of non -compliance. Th e 
initiative has provided valuable insights into the ground reality of manufacturing practices and 
has led to relevant correcti ve actions, resulting in noticeable improvements in the regulatory 
framework. 
4.2.6 The Committee expresses serious concern that t he budgetary allocation for the 
Strengthening of State Drug Regulatory System (SSDRS) was reduced from ₹56.00 
crore in BE 2025 -26  to nil at the Revised Estimates stage for FY 2025 –26, and that no 
provision exists in the Budget Estimates for 2026 –27, there by meaning that no 
budgetary allocation was made for  strengthening o f States Drug Regulatory System. 
The Committee is concerned to find such abrupt financial hiatus, resulting from 
procedural delays in approving the scheme's extension and the subsequent S SDRS 2.0 

172 
 
proposal, threatens to derail the hard -won infrastructural gains achieved since the 
scheme's inception. Allowing newly established state drug testing laboratories and 
upgraded drug control offices to face a funding vacuum undermines the core objec tive 
of ensuring affordable, high -quality generic medicines. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends that the Department pursue the immediate approval of the ₹496.16 crore 
SSDRS 2.0 scheme with the Department of Expenditure. Furthermore, the Committee is 
of t he view that the Department must explore interim financial mechanisms or 
supplementary grants to ensure that the 19 new and 28 upgraded State drug testing 
laboratories do not face operational paralysis while awaiting the formal rollout of the 
revamped scheme. 
4.2.7 The Committee observes that while SSDRS 1.0 successfully expanded the 
national functional testing capacity to 1,66,830 samples per annum, the actual 
realization of this capacity remains critically constrained by persistent State -level 
manpower gaps. Conversely, the Committee commends the remarkable efficacy of the 
Risk-Based Inspection (RBI) initiative, noting that out of 1,230 inspected units, a 
staggering 1,140 required stringent regulatory actions such as Stop Production Orders 
(SPOs) and licen se suspensions. The Committee believes that state -of-the-art testing 
infrastructure is rendered futile without an adequate cadre of trained analysts and 
uncompromising enforcement mechanisms. The Committee, therefore, recommends that 
the Department, under the ambit of the upcoming SSDRS 2.0, mandate specific, time -
bound targets for States to recruit and upskill regulatory and technical personnel as a 
precondition for future fund disbursements. The Committee is of the view that the Risk - 
Based Inspection fra mework must be permanently institutionalized, standardized, and 
fully digitized across all State Drug Control offices to ensure that crackdowns on non -
compliant manufacturing practices remain consistent, transparent, and data -driven 
nationwide to avoid pub lic health crisis occurred in Madhya Pradesh due to  
consumption of contaminated cough syrup. 
4.2.8 On being asked about the response of the Department to the proposal of centralized 
system of drug regulatory system to prevent the manufacturing of spurious medicine and 
ensure production of quality drugs for treatment of various diseases, the Department stated 
that t he subject 'drugs' falls under the Concurrent List of the Constitution and under the 
present legislation, the powers of the Centre and states are  clearly demarcated. State Drugs 
Authorities regulate sale, distribution, and manufacture of drugs and Class A and B Medical 
Devices. CDSCO governs the import of all drugs, cosmetics and medical devices, approves 
new drugs in the country and high-risk medical devices. Further, Central regulator coordinates 
activities of State Drug Control Organisations and provides expert advice through the Drugs 
Consultative Committee (DCC) meetings held with State Drugs Controllers for uniformity in 
administration of the Drugs and Cosmetics Act. This collaborative approach enables effective 
regulation and enforcement, leveraging both central and state expertise to ensure drug safety 
and quality in India. 
4.2.9 On a query related to the steps that are being taken to de -centralise CDSCO approvals 
to State Government regulators to serve in time and speed up the setting up of Medical 
Devices Industry, the Department stated that t he Medical Device Rules, 2017, categorize 

173 
 
medical devices into four classes (A, B, C, and D) based o n risk, with 'A' being low -risk and 
'D' being high-risk. The regulatory framework is as follows: 
4.2.10 Class A & B devices: Regulated by State Drugs Authorities while Class C & D 
devices are regulated by Central Drugs Authorities (CDSCO), while import of all medical 
devices is regulated by CDSCO.  
4.2.11 This risk -based approach ensures that CDSCO focuses on medium - to high -risk 
devices (C & D), leveraging its expertise and resources. The online portal (MD Online) 
facilitates transparent, predictable, and timely processing of applications, promoting 
uniformity in regulatory processes. 
4.2.12 On being asked about the details of total amount of revenue collected by CDSCO for 
approval/licensing of medical devices during the last three financial years, the foll owing 
submission made: 
Table – 4.2.1 
Financial Year Amount of revenue collected by CDSCO for approval/licensing of medical devices

2023-24 ₹1,18,69,35,925.00 
2024-25 ₹93,95,52,278.00 
 
4.2.13 On a specific query related to functioning of Central Drugs Standard Control 
Organisation (CDSCO), the Department stated that a s per information obtained from CDAC, 
during the last financial year, total number of manufacturing and import applications 
processed for Class C and Class D Medical Device are 2999 and out of these in approx. 62 
percent of applications queries were raised more than twice. Further, out of 1677 import 
applications, 1060 applications belong to Class -C and Class D Medical Devices having 
USFDA/CE certificate. In this, 253 such applications are under process for more than 90 
days. Most of these applications are pending at the applicants end for submission of r equisite 
documents as per MDR-2017.  
 
4.2.14 The Committee notes with concern the significant administrative bottlenecks 
hindering the swift clearance of medical devices, particularly the Class C and Class D 
categories regulated by the Central Drugs Standa rd Control Organisation (CDSCO). 
The Committee observes that out of 2,999 manufacturing and import applications 
processed, approximately 62% were subjected to queries more than twice, and 253 
import applications, even those possessing USFDA/CE certificatio ns, have been pending 
for over 90 days. The Committee believes that such repetitive query cycles indicate a 
systemic lack of clarity in the initial submission guidelines and point toward an 
inefficient, iterative assessment process. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the 
Department immediately overhaul the 'MD Online' portal to include a mandatory, AI -
assisted pre-submission validation checklist to ensure applications are complete before 
formal submission. The Committee is of the view that CDSCO must  adopt a "single 
comprehensive query" policy, wherein all deficiencies in an application are identified 
and communicated to the applicant in a single instance, rather than in piece -meal 
fashion. The Committee, furthermore, recommends the establishment of a  dedicated 

174 
 
facilitation cell within CDSCO to assist domestic manufacturers in navigating the 
Medical Device Rules (MDR) 2017, thereby reducing the delays currently attributed to 
pending applicant responses. 
 
4.2.15 The Committee acknowledges the constituti onally mandated decentralized 
framework where State Authorities regulate drugs and Class A/B devices, while the 
Centre oversees imports and Class C/D devices. The Committee recommends for a 
decentralized framework for imports and Class  C/D devices . The Com mittee strongly 
recommends that CDSCO approval for imports be completely decentralized to the State 
Authorities. Centralization with the DGCI  at the Central level has created major 
bottlenecks and is the biggest hurdle in India being at the forefront of Me dical devices 
manufacturing. The Committee recommends for decentralization of approval for all 
Medical Devices with the State Drug Authorities to reduce pendency. In this current 
phase when we are competing with China, Vietnam, Malaysia giving CDSCO approv als 
for even already approved devices/ technologies beyond a period of 45 days in resulting 
in India losing its competitive edge.  
 
4.2.16 The Committee further observes that CDSCO has generated substantial revenue 
from medical device licensing, collecting  between ₹87.18 crore and ₹118.69 crore 
annually over the last three financial years. The Committee, therefore, recommends that 
the revenue earned should be used for self -financing of the Institution. A designated 
percentage of the revenue collected by CDS CO be ring -fenced and directly reinvested 
into upgrading the digital and technical infrastructure of both the Central and State 
regulatory bodies. The Committee is of the view that the Department must utilize these 
funds to develop a unified, real -time Nat ional Regulatory Dashboard that seamlessly 
integrates CDSCO‘s 'MD Online' portal with State -level licensing databases. This will 
ensure that intelligence regarding non -compliant manufacturers, suspended licenses, 
and spurious drug trails is instantly share d across jurisdictions, leveraging both central 
and state expertise to guarantee uniform enforcement of the Drugs and Cosmetics Act 
nationwide. 
 
4.2.17 In respect of Drugs Inspector (Regulatory), the Committee has been informed that due 
to a court case reg arding the experience, the post of Drugs Inspector could not be filled. The 
Recruitment Rules (RRs) have been revised and notified on 11.11.2025. Subsequently, a 
proposal for filling up 186 vacant posts of Drugs Inspector (Regulatory) has been forwarded 
to UPSC for initiating recruitment. Further, in respect of Drugs Inspector (Medical Devices), 
the Committee has been informed that RRs of the post of DI (Medical Devices) was notified 
on 06.11.2024. Subsequently, a proposal for recruitment of 20 posts of Dru gs Inspector 
(Medical Devices) was also sent to UPSC. The UPSC conducted the examination on 
02.11.2025, however, final list of selected candidates is still awaited from UPSC. 
Furthermore, in respect of Addl. DC (I), JDC (I) and DDC (I) in Medical Devices V ertical, an 
advertisement was published to fill up these posts through Deputation basis. Some, applicants 
were received and the same are under examination. Moreover, a requisition for filling up the 
22 posts of ADC (I), Medical Devices has already been sub mitted to UPSC. Recognizing the 
complexity of high -tech medical devices, the Ministry has strategically recruited Drug 
Inspectors (Medical Devices) with dedicated Engineering backgrounds (such as Biomedical, 

175 
 
Mechanical, and Electronics). Further to bridge the knowledge gap, the department deputes 
the officers from time to time for specialized training programs tailored for technical devices. 
4.2.18 The Committee finds significant vacancy position in the regulatory workforce 
under the Drugs and Medical Devic es verticals, particularly at the level of Drugs 
Inspectors and Assistant Drugs Inspectors. While the sanctioned strength under the 
Drugs Vertical stands at 613 posts, as many as 257 posts remain vacant, including 186 
vacancies of Drugs Inspectors. In the Medical Devices Vertical, only 24 personnel are in 
position against a sanctioned strength of 249 posts. The Committee notes that delays in 
recruitment were partly due to litigation and pending revision of Recruitment Rules, 
and that proposals for filling a  number of posts have now been forwarded to the Union 
Public Service Commission. The Committee is of the view that such a large number of 
vacancies could adversely affect regulatory efficiency, quality surveillance and 
enforcement activities. The Committee , therefore, recommends that the Department 
take urgent and time -bound measures, in coordination with the Union Public Service 
Commission, to expedite the recruitment process and fill the vacant posts. The 
Committee also urges the Department to review the overall human resource framework 
of the regulatory system and consider strengthening technical capacity through 
specialised recruitment, structured training programmes and career progression 
mechanisms so that the regulatory apparatus keeps pace with the r apid growth and 
technological advancement of the pharmaceutical and medical devices sectors in the 
country.

4.3.2  The Committee highlig hts that under TCPs, a Budget Estimate of ₹369.26 
crore was provided in 2024–25, which was reduced to ₹322.90 crore at the Revised Estimates 
stage. Against this, the actual expenditure  during the year amounted to ₹287.72 crore, 
indicating partial utilisation of the revised allocation. For the year 2025 –26, an amount of 
₹483.54 crore was provided at the Budget Estimates stage; however, it was reduced to 
₹295.00 crore at the Revised Estim ates stage, against which an expenditure of ₹167.10 crore 
(about 57% of the already reduced RE) had been incurred up to 17.02.2026. For 2026 –27, an 
allocation of ₹490.00 crore has been proposed, comprising ₹481.00 crore under the Revenue 
head and ₹9.00 crore under the Capital head.  
4.3.3 It is submitted that the consistent reduction in capital outlay at the RE stage under the 
National Mental Health Programme (NMHP) is primarily attributable to procedural and 
compliance-related constraints at the State leve l. In several instances, States have not utilised 
at least 75% of the last instalment released, thereby rendering them ineligible for further 
releases. Delays in creation of Single Nodal Accounts (SNA) and State Linked Schemes 
(SLS) for the Tertiary Care c omponent have also impeded fund flow. Additionally, non -
deposit of accrued interest in Bharat Kosh and non -submission of details regarding release 
and utilisation of the corresponding State share have affected subsequent instalments in 
pervious FYs. In FY 2025–26, the reduction from ₹32 crore at BE to ₹5 crore at RE due to 
transition from SNA to SNA SPARSH (effective July 2025), with onboarding of a large 
number of Centres of Excellence still pending, thereby delaying demand generation and fund 
release. 
4.3.4 Under the  Centrally Sponsored Component of Tertiary Cancer Care Program of 
Tertiary Care Scheme, financial support is given for civil work and procurement of equipment 
by SCI/TCCC.   The capital outlay was reduced mainly due to, delay in submission of 
procurement proposals by States/UTs and Pending utilization certificates and compliance 
requirements from States/UTs. 
4.3.5 During 2024 -25, ₹ 5.00 crores was to be released to CMSS for procurement of 
machinery and equipment for National Tobacco Testing Laboratories (NTTLs), however, the 
demand for machinery/equipment was revised by NTTLs. Accordingly, the requirements and 
specificaitions were revised and the revised procurement proposal for instruments was 
forwarded to CMSS. CMSS has initiated the purchase process for equipment for NTTLs.

4.3.7 The fund of ₹ 490 crore has been allocated under Component of Tertiary Care Scheme 
in the 2026-27 on the basis of past tre nds of expenditure, SNA balances available. However, 
in case of any shortfall during 2026 -27, the same shall be projected in the supplementary 
demands for the grants or at the time of the finalization of the RE 2026-27. 
4.3.8 The Committee notes that the T ertiary Care Programmes under the National 
Health framework support a number of critical public health initiatives such as the 
National Mental Health Programme, National Programme for Prevention and Control 
of Cancer, Diabetes, Cardiovascular Diseases and Stroke, Trauma Care, Health Care 
for the Elderly, Telemedicine and Tobacco Control. The Committee , however, observes 
a recurring pattern of reduction in allocations at the Revised Estimates stage however 
partial utilisation of funds across several componen ts. In 2024 –25, the actual 
expenditure of ₹287.72 crore fell short of the Revised Estimates of ₹322.90 crore, and in 
2025–26, only ₹167.10 crore (about 57 per cent of the reduced RE) had been utilised up 
to 17.02.2026. The Committee observes with serious c oncern that the shortfall is largely 
attributed to delays at the State level in fulfilling procedural requirements such as 
utilisation of earlier instalments, creation of Single Nodal Accounts, submission of 
utilisation certificates, and compliance with fi nancial reporting norms. The Committee 
is of the view that these procedural and administrative constraints are adversely 
affecting the timely implementation of important tertiary healthcare programmes. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends that the Departme nt strengthen coordination and 
capacity-building mechanisms with States/UTs to ensure timely compliance with 
financial procedures, faster on boarding of institutions under the SNA -SPARSH system, 
and prompt submission of utilisation certificates and procure ment proposals so that the 
allocated funds are effectively utilised for strengthening tertiary healthcare services. 
 
4.3.9 The Committee expresses dissatisfaction to find that several critical components 
such as the National Mental Health Programme and the  National Programme for 
Prevention and Control of Cancer, Diabetes, Cardiovascular Diseases and Stroke have 
witnessed substantial reductions in allocations at the Revised Estimates stage due to 
delays in project proposals, procurement processes and institu tional readiness. 
Considering the rising burden of non -communicable diseases, mental health issues and 
trauma-related cases in the country, the Committee is of the view that sustained and 
predictable funding for these programmes is essential for strengthen ing specialised 
healthcare services. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department should 
adopt a more proactive planning and monitoring framework to ensure timely 
submission of proposals by States/UTs, expeditious approval of projects, and impr oved 

178 
 
utilisation of funds under these programmes. The Committee also desires that special 
attention be given to expanding telemedicine infrastructure, strengthening trauma and 
emergency care facilities, and enhancing institutional capacities for cancer and  mental 
health care so that tertiary healthcare services become more accessible and responsive to 
the growing healthcare needs of the population. 
 
National Mental Health Programme 
 
4.3.10 Under the Tertiary Care Program, Manpower Development Scheme (MDS) w as 
initiated in the year 2009, during the 11 th Five Year Plan (FYP) which was continued in the 
12th FYP and beyond to increase the availability of trained manpower in the field of mental 
health, to enhance the capacity of tertiary care mental health facilities and generate knowledge 
and evidence through research on issues related with mental health. Under this Scheme, two 
separate sub-schemes were operationalized, namely,  
 
a) Manpower Development Scheme A - Establishment of Centres of Excellence 
(CoE) in the field of mental health  
b) Manpower Development Scheme B - Strengthening Post Graduate Departments of 
medical colleges/general hospitals/state run mental health institutes 
Manpower Development Scheme -A: 25 existing mental health institutions/medical 
colleges/hospitals have been selected to upgrade them as Centres of Excellence(CoE) in the 
field of mental health.  
Manpower Development Scheme -B: To provide an impetus for development of Manpower 
in Mental Health, other training centres like Government Medical Col leges, General  
Hospitals and State -run Mental Health Institutes are supported for starting PG courses in 
Mental Health or increasing the existing intake capacity for PG training in Mental Health. A 
total of 19 Institutes are supported for establishment/ st rengthening of 47 departments in 
mental health specialties.

4.3.13 To strengthen the Tertiary Care Services for effective implementation of National 
Mental Health Program (Tertiary Care), Program may be restructured and continued through 
the fresh identification and selection of Centres of Excellence, aligning with current sectoral 
priorities and emerging needs. To sustain the increase in trained mental health  manpower, to 
develop state -of-the-art mental healthcare facilities, and to promote the generation of 
knowledge and evidence through research on mental health issues, it is imperative to 
restructure the National Mental Health Program (Tertiary Care). In th e current healthcare 
scenario, there is a pressing need for integrated neuropsychiatric care encompassing 
Psychiatry, Neurology, and Neurosurgery under a single institutional framework. The 
convergence of these disciplines is essential for addressing compl ex mental and neurological 
disorders comprehensively and efficiently. The National Mental Health Program needs to be 
restructured to create state -of-the art infrastructure, advanced diagnostic and therapeutic 
facilities, interdisciplinary collaboration, ac ademic excellence, digital health integration and 
strong research and policy development capabilities by providing financial aid to Mental 
Health Institutes and Hospitals in the country.  The Manpower Development Scheme A & B 
may be modified to incorporate Mental Health Institutes, Medical Colleges, or Hospitals by 
new identification and selection including the opportunity for the Institutes that were 
previously approved under Manpower Development Scheme A & B, during earlier plan 
periods.  
 
4.3.14 The Commi ttee notes that the Manpower Development Scheme under the 
National Mental Health Programme has contributed significantly to strengthening the 
availability of trained mental health professionals in the country through the 
establishment of Centres of Excelle nce and the strengthening of postgraduate 
departments in medical institutions. The Committee notes that the Scheme has 
facilitated the creation of 594 training seats across key mental health disciplines and has 
produced 2345 trained professionals over the years.  
4.3.15 While acknowledging these achievements, the Committee, however, is of the view 
that the growing burden of mental health disorders and the shortage of specialised 
professionals across many States require further expansion and modernisation of  
tertiary mental health infrastructure. The Committee, therefore, recommends the 
Department to strengthen the Tertiary Care Services for effective implementation of 
National Mental Health Program (Tertiary Care), Program may be restructured and 
continued t hrough the fresh identification and selection of Centres of Excellence, 
aligning with current sectoral priorities and emerging needs. To sustain the increase in

4.3.16 The Committee expresses deep concern over the increasing incidence of 
depression and other psychiatric disorders in recent years, including within the medical 
and healthcare community. The Committee is of the view that the rising burden of 
mental health conditions calls for strengthening the availability of trained psychiatrists 
and promot ing greater focus on mental health within the healthcare system. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department take steps to expand 
psychiatric education and training by strengthening mental health departments in 
medical institutions, increasing  postgraduate opportunities in psychiatry and 
encouraging medical graduates to pursue the specialization. At the same time, the 
Committee emphasises the importance of preventive and holistic approaches to mental 
wellbeing and suggests that practices such a s Yoga and other elements of the Indian 
Knowledge System be promoted.  
National Programme for Prevention & Control of Non -Communicable Diseases (NP -
NCD)- Tertiary care 
4.3.17 The Centrally Sponsored component of the programme focuses on strengthening the 
capacity and infrastructure of State Cancer Institute‘s (SCIs) and Tertiary Care Cancer 
Centres (TCCCs) across the country. This component aims to enhance the availability of 
comprehensive cancer care services, including prevention, early detection, diagnos is, 
treatment, and palliative care, thereby ensuring equitable access to quality oncology services 
within each state. The Central component is implemented through the National Cancer 
Registry Programme (NCRP) of the Indian Council of Medical Research – National Centre 
for Disease Informatics and Research (ICMR -NCDIR). Under this component, network of 52 
Hospital-Based Cancer Registries (HBCRs) located in SCIs, TCCCs, and Regional Cancer 
Centres (RCCs) has been established. This network facilitates systemati c collection, Hospital-
Based Cancer Registries (HBCRs) located in SCIs, TCCCs, and Regional Cancer Centres 
(RCCs) has been established. This network facilitates systematic collection, analysis, and 
dissemination of cancer incidence and outcome data to guid e evidence -based policy 

181 
 
formulation, resource allocation, and strengthening of the national cancer control response. 19 
State Cancer Institutes and 20 Tertiary Cancer Care Centre are supported under the Tertiary 
Cancer Care Scheme. Out of them, 17 SCIs and  19 TCCCs are fully functional till date 
providing radiotherapy, chemotherapy and surgical oncology services. 
4.3.18 The Committee also notes that under the National Programme for Prevention 
and Control of Non -Communicable Diseases (NP -NCD), support is bei ng provided to 
State Cancer Institutes and Tertiary Care Cancer Centres to expand specialised 
oncology services across the country. The establishment of Hospital -Based Cancer 
Registries under the National Cancer Registry Programme has enabled systematic da ta 
collection and evidence-based planning for cancer control. The Committee  observes that 
several institutions have been supported under the scheme, however , the Committee 
desires that remaining all approved institutes become fully functional given the ris ing 
burden of cancer and other non -communicable diseases in the country. The Committee, 
in this regard, recommends that the Department intensify efforts to ensure early 
operationalisation of all sanctioned State Cancer Institutes and Tertiary Cancer Care 
Centres by addressing delays in infrastructure development, procurement of equipment 
and staffing. The Committee desires the Department to strengthen the cancer registry 
network and ensure effective utilisation of the generated data for policy formulation, 
resource allocation and targeted interventions aimed at improving early detection, 
treatment outcomes and equitable access to tertiary cancer care services across the 
country.

4.4.1 The Committee note s that under the Head ― Human Resource for Health and Medical

which was reduced to ₹579.12 crore at the Revised Estimates stage (indicating substantial 
reduction at RE stage). Against this, the actual expenditure during the year amounted to 
₹549.33 crore . For the year 2025 –26, an amount of ₹1,675.00 crore was provided at the

4.4.2 Components of HRH & ME scheme: - 
i. Establishment of new medical colleges and increase of UG & PG seats in existing 
medical colleges- 
a. Establishment of new medical colleges attached with district/referral hospitals, 
b. CSS for Upgradation of existing State Government/Central Government 
medical colleges to increase MBBS seats in the country; and, 
c. Strengthening and upgradation of State Government medical colleges f or 
starting new PG disciplines and increasing PG seats. 
 Establishment of New Colleges of Nursing (CoN) in co-location with Medical Colleges  
4.4.3 The components of medical education under the umbrella scheme Human Resource 
for Health & Medical Education are as under: 
(i) Establishing new medical colleges attached with existing District Hospitals:  To reduce 
the urban/rural divide, as also regional imbalance in spread of medical Institutions and with 
an aim to bring affordable health care to underserved are as, 157 new medical colleges have 
been approved through Centrally Sponsored Scheme for ―Establishment of New Medical 
Colleges attached with existing District/Referral hospitals‖ in those Parliamentary 
constituencies/ Districts which do not have a medical c ollege either in Government or private 
sector. As per the scheme, fund sharing between the Central Government and States is in the 
ratio of 90:10 for NE/special category states and 60:40 for other States. Out of the 157 
envisaged medical colleges, a total of 137 medical colleges are functional as of now. A total 
of 43 medical colleges became operational in the years 2023 -24 (14), 2024 -25 (23) & 2025 -
26 (6). 
(ii) CSS for Upgradation of existing State Government/Central Government medical 
colleges to increase  MBBS seats in the country:  Under the Scheme, the upper ceiling cost 
was pegged at ₹1.20 crore per seat with the funding pattern in the ratio of 90:10 by Central 
and State Governments respectively for North Eastern States & Special category States and 
60:40 for other States. Under the scheme, 83 Government Medical Colleges in 17 States have 
been approved to increase 4977 MBBS seats with total cost of 5972.20Cr. Out of central 
share of ₹ 3684.10 Cr, an amount to the tune of ₹2756.43 Crore have been released to the 
State Governments so far. The said scheme has been extended recently by the cabinet with 

183 
 
cost augmentation from ₹ 1.20 Cr to ₹ 1.50 cr for the remaining 5023 MBBS seats from the 
period of 2025 -26 to 2028 -29. The States have been requested to submit DPRs with proper 
gap analysis for further consideration by the Ministry. 
 
(iii) Strengthening and upgradation of State Government medical colleges for starting new 
PG disciplines and increasing PG seats:  Under Centrally Sponsored Scheme (CSS) for 
‗Strengthening and upgradation of State Government medical colleges for starting new PG 
disciplines and increasing PG seats‘, 8048 seats have been approved in two Phases. Target of 
the Scheme has been achieved.  
Phase –I   The scheme was launched in the XI Plan per iod with the objective to strengthen 
and upgrade State/Central Government Medical Colleges to create new PG seats. A total of 
72 Government Medical colleges in 21 States/ UTs are covered under the scheme for 
increasing 4058 PG seats with total cost of 1498 .32Cr. Entire central share of ₹1063.01 Cr. 
 have been released under the scheme. 
   
Phase –II   With the objective of creating 4000 PG seats in Government Colleges in the 
country, the Ministry of Health & Family Welfare is implementing Phase -II of the Cen trally 
Sponsored Scheme for Up -gradation of existing State Government/Central Government 
medical colleges to increase PG seats. The funding pattern is 90:10 by Central and State 
Governments respectively for North Eastern States and Special Category States and 60:40 for 
other States with the upper ceiling cost pegged at ₹1.20 crore per seat. A total of 65 
Government Medical Colleges have been approved under the scheme in 10 States for 
increasing 4000 PG seats with total cost of 4475.55 Cr. Out of central sha re of ₹ 2711.41 Cr, 
₹ 1285.56 Cr have been released under the scheme till date. 
  
Phase –III The cabinet has recently approved the phase -III of the scheme with a target of 
increasing 5000 PG seats by 2028 -29 at a cost of ₹ 1.50 cr per seat. The States have  been 
requested to submit DPRs with proper gap analysis for further consideration by the Ministry.
