4.4.4 In its submission the Department stated that under the Centrally Sponsored 
Scheme “Augmenting Nursing Education – Establishment of New Colleges of Nursing in Co -
location with Medi cal Colleges” , construction of six (06) Colleges of Nursing has been 
completed in the State of  Rajasthan. However, none of these Colleges has commenced 
operations during the Academic Year 2025–26. 
 
4.4.5 Funds for establishment of new Medical Colleges, aug mentation of nursing education 
and establishment of new Colleges of Nursing in co -location with Medical Colleges are 
released in accordance with the SNA -SPARSH guidelines issued by the Ministry of Finance. 
Under the revised SNA -SPARSH system, the Central s hare is released directly to the State 
Government‘s SNA account maintained in the Reserve Bank of India (RBI). Conditions for 
release of funds: 
a) State/UT must sign MoU with Ministry of Health & Family Welfare committing land 
provision, faculty appointment, recurring expenses and state share for infrastructure 
and operations.  
b) Central assistance shared with States 90:10 for North Eastern and Special Category 
States and 60:40 for other states. 
c) Funds are released in instalments tied to release of state share, p rogress of 
construction, equipment, and submission of Utilization Certificates. 
4.4.6 The Committee understand s that the Human Resource for Health and Medical 
Education (HRH & ME) scheme plays a crucial role in addressing the shortage of 
medical professionals and reducing regional imbalances in access to medical education 
and healthcare services. The Committee points out that although substantial allocations 
have been made under the Head, there has been a significant reduction at the Revised 
Estimates stage. A Budget Estimate of ₹1,274.79 crore was provided in 2024 –25, which 
was reduced to ₹579.12 crore at the Revised Estimates stage (indicating substantial 
reduction at RE stage). Against this, the actual expenditure during the year amounted to 
₹549.33 crore. For the year 2025 –26, an amount of ₹1,675.00 crore was provided at the 
Budget Estimates stage and revised to ₹1,630.00 crore at the Revised Estimates stage, 
against which an expenditure of ₹779.48 crore had been incurred up to 31.01.2026 
(which is about 48% of reduced RE allocation). 
 
4.4.7 The Committee finds that under the scheme, a total of 157 new medical colleges 
have been approved but out of that 137 Medical College s are functional, with expansion 
of MBBS seats in existing institutions, and the creatio n of postgraduate medical seats 
across the country. While appreciating the initiatives, the Committee is of the view that 
timely completion and operationalisation of approved institutions are essential for 
translating financial allocations into tangible im provements in healthcare manpower. 
The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department strengthen monitoring and 
coordination with States/UTs to ensure timely submission of DPRs, completion of 
infrastructure projects, recruitment of faculty and adhere nce to funding conditions 
under the SNA -SPARSH framework so that the approved medical colleges and 
expanded seats become fully functional within the stipulated timelines.  There is need to 

185 
 
strengthen infrastructure in medical college and maintain quality of  Medical education 
to produce professionals of global quality instead of below -average mass production of 
bunch of incompetent medical professionals who will definitely prove threat to human 
life and safety due to lack of clinical exposure and quality education.  
 
4.4.8 The Committee further notes that under the scheme for augmenting nursing 
education, the construction of six Colleges of Nursing in Rajasthan has been completed 
but none of them had commenced operations during the academic year 2025 –26. 
Considering the critical shortage of trained nursing professionals in the healthcare 
system, the Committee is of the view that such delays undermine the objective of 
strengthening the health workforce. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the 
Department wo rk closely with the concerned State Government to ensure early 
operationalisation of the completed Colleges of Nursing and to expedite similar projects 
across other States. The Committee also urges the Department to adopt a 
comprehensive approach towards s trengthening the entire healthcare workforce by 
simultaneously expanding medical, nursing and allied health education capacities, 
particularly in underserved regions, so that the growing demand for skilled health 
professionals in the country can be effectively met. 
 
4.4.9 The Committee strongly recommends for adoption of nearby Primary Health 
Care (PHC), CHC and sub centers hospitals by medical colleges both government and 
private can significantly strengthen healthcare delivery at the grassroots level. It can 
improve the availability of doctors and ensure a steady presence of interns, postgraduate 
trainees, and other healthcare personnel. This additional manpower can enhance service 
delivery, support preventive and promotive health activities, and improve c ontinuity of 
care. Such collaboration would particularly benefit maternal and child health services 
by ensuring better antenatal care, safe deliveries, immunization coverage, and early 
identification and management of high -risk cases, while also providing valuable 
community-based training opportunities for medical students. 
 
4.5 PRADHAN MANTRI AYUSHMAN BHARAT HEALTH INFRASTRUCTURE 
MISSION (PM-ABHIM) 
4.5.1 The PM-Ayushman Bharat Health Infrastructure Mission (PM -ABHIM) was launched 
on 25th October, 2021 with an outlay of Rs.64,180 Cr to be implemented over five years from 
2021-22 to 2025-26, with the aim to strengthen the public health infrastructure to fill critical 
gaps in health infrastructure, surveillance and health research - spanning both the urban and  
rural areas. The objectives of the scheme are as follows: 
i. To develop capacities of health systems and institutions across the continuum of 
care at all levels viz. primary, secondary and tertiary. 
ii. To strengthen grass root public health institutions to deli ver universal 
comprehensive primary health care and governance capacities for comprehensive 
diagnostic and treatment including for critical care services.  

186 
 
iii. To expand and build an IT enabled disease surveillance system by developing a 
network of surveillanc e laboratories for effectively detecting, investigating, 
preventing and combating Public Health Emergencies and Disease Outbreaks. 
 
4.5.2 PM-ABHIM is a Centrally Sponsored Scheme with Central Sector Components. The 
envisaged targets under the CSS component s (including XV -FC share) of the scheme 
includes: 
i. Infrastructure Support for 17,788 Rural building -less Sub Health Centers -Health 
and Wellness Centers, now renamed as Ayushman Arogya Mandir (AAM). 
ii. Establishment of 11,024 Urban Health and Wellness Centers ( Ayushman Arogya 
Mandir) in all the States with a focus on slum and slum like areas. 
iii. Establishment of 3382 Block Public Health Units at block levels. 
iv. Setting up of 730 Integrated Public Health Labs in all districts across the Country. 
v. Establishment of 602 Critical Care Blocks in all the districts with population more 
than 5 lakhs and referral linkages in other districts.  
 
4.5.3 The Committee notes that the Pradhan Mantri Ayushman Bharat Health 
Infrastructure Mission (PM-ABHIM) was launched with a substantia l outlay of ₹64,180 
crore for the period 2021 –22 to 2025 –26 with the objective of strengthening public 
health infrastructure across primary, secondary and tertiary levels of care. The Mission 
envisages a wide range of infrastructure interventions including  construction of 
building-less Sub -Health Centres as Ayushman Arogya Mandirs, establishment of 
Urban Health and Wellness Centres, Block Public Health Units, Integrated Public 
Health Laboratories and Critical Care Hospital Blocks across districts. While 
appreciating the comprehensive design of the Mission aimed at strengthening health 
systems, surveillance networks and emergency preparedness, the Committee is of the 
view that the success of such a large -scale initiative depends on timely completion of 
infrastructure, operationalisation of facilities and adequate human resources and 
equipment support. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department 
strengthen coordination and monitoring mechanisms with States/UTs to ensure timely 
completion of sanctio ned infrastructure, adherence to project timelines and optimal 
utilisation of funds so that the envisaged public health facilities become fully functional 
at the earliest to facilitate healthcare of the global standard. 

187 
 
 
 
4.5.4  The Committee expresses de ep concern that under PM -ABHIM, a Budget 
Estimate of ₹3,200.00 crore was provided in 2024 –25, which was revised to ₹3,000.00 crore 
at the Revised Estimates stage. Against this, the actual expenditure during the year amounted 
to ₹2,086.34 crore (just about 70% of the already reduced RE). For the year 2025 –26, an 
amount of ₹4,200.00 crore was provided at the Budget Estimates stage and revised to 
₹2,442.50 crore (reduced to about 58% of the allocated BE) at the Revised Estimates stage, 
against which an expendi ture of ₹1,444.52 crore (about 59% of the reduced RE) had been 
incurred up to 31.01.2026. For 2026–27, an allocation of ₹4,200.00 crore has been proposed. 
4.5.5 On being asked for consistent under -utilisation of funds seen in FYs 2023 -24 and 
2024-25, the Department stated the reasons that: 
 
a) PM-ABHIM is a capital -intensive infrastructure initiative that involves the 
construction of new facilities, upgradation of existing institutions, procurement of 
equipment, and establishment of public health units across States/UTs. Such activities 
inherently entail gestation periods, including preparation of Detailed Project Reports 
(DPRs), obtaining technical approvals, ensuring land availability, completing 
tendering processes, and complying with State -specific procedur al requirements. 
These factors collectively influence the pace of expenditure. 
 
b) The scheme experienced a relatively slow pace of expenditure during the initial years 
of implementation due to systemic and operational challenges. The SNA -SPARSH

188 
 
mechanism for  PM-ABHIM was introduced on a pilot basis in FY 2023 –24 and 
remained in a transition phase during FY 2024 –25. This transition temporarily 
affected the pace of expenditure as States/UTs aligned their financial and treasury 
systems with the new platform. Add itionally, delays in procurement, civil works, and 
recruitment of specialized human resources contributed to slower physical and 
financial progress. 
 
c) To address under -utilisation and enhance absorptive capacity, capacity -building 
initiatives and hands -on t raining have been provided to States/UTs under the SNA -
SPARSH framework to ensure smooth adoption. The Ministry continues to closely 
monitor physical progress through the NHM Programme Management System (PMS) 
portal, enabling regular milestone tracking and  early identification of implementation 
issues. Furthermore, technical and regional support through dedicated State 
Programme Management Units (PMUs) is being extended to strengthen planning, 
approvals, and execution. 
 
d) With the stabilization of the SNA -SPARSH system and continued technical support, 
an accelerated pace of expenditure and improved overall performance under PM -
ABHIM are anticipated. 
 
4.5.6 On being asked for the reasons for reduction of budget from ₹ 4,200 crore (BE) to ₹ 
2,442.50 crore (RE) in 2025-26, the Department stated that:  
a) PM-ABHIM is a capital -intensive infrastructure scheme involving construction, 
renovation, procurement of equipment, and establishment of facilities across 
States/UTs. Such activities are subject to procedural requirements, tendering timelines, 
site readiness, and State -specific implementation capacities, which can lead to 
variations between projected and actual expenditure within a financial year.  
b) In addition, during the year, all 31 States/UTs were onboarded onto the SNA SPARSH 
platform under PM -ABHIM. The transition to the SNA SPARSH system —including 
opening of Single Nodal Accounts, mapping of implementing agencies, and alignment 
of financial reporting processes initiall y moderated the pace of fund releases and 
expenditure.  
c) Further, funds under PM -ABHIM are released to States/UTs on a lump -sum basis, 
based on approved proposals and fulfilment of requisite conditions. Consequently, 
actual expenditure in a given year depen ds on the pace at which States/UTs utilize 
previously released funds and meet conditions for subsequent releases, which may not 
always align evenly with annual budget estimates. 
d) The adjustment was not attributable to any single component alone. Variations were 
observed across multiple infrastructure components including Urban Health & 
Wellness Centres, Block Public Health Units, Integrated Public Health Laboratories, 
and Critical Care Hospital Blocks, depending on the stage of tendering, construction 
progress, and procurement processes in different States/UTs. The reduction reflects 
timing and implementation related factors rather than any curtailment of the approved 
scope or objectives of the scheme. 

189 
 
4.5.7 The Committee is displeased to observe the persiste nt and drastic under -
utilization of funds under the PM -ABHIM scheme, evidenced by the severe reduction of 
the 2025–26 Budget Estimate from ₹4,200.00 crore to a Revised Estimate of ₹2,442.50 
crore, of which only 59% had been utilized by January 2026. The Co mmittee believes 
that while the onboarding of all 31 States and UTs onto the SNA -SPARSH platform is a 
necessary financial reform for transparency, but such transition should not become a 
perennial bottleneck that stalls the rollout of critical health infra structure. The 
Committee, therefore, desires that the Department should immediately deploy dedicated 
Task Forces. These task forces must move beyond basic capacity building and actively 
assist state treasuries in mapping implementing agencies, reconciling unspent balances, 
and expediting the fulfillment of conditions required for subsequent lump -sum releases. 
The Committee is of the view that until the SNA -SPARSH system is fully stabilized, the 
Department should conditionally ease procedural rigidities to e nsure that fund flows for 
life-saving infrastructure are not artificially constricted. 
4.5.8 The Committee observes that the fundamental delays in executing PM -ABHIM 
are consistently attributed to the gestation periods inherent in capital -intensive projects, 
including the preparation of Detailed Project Reports (DPRs), land acquisition, and 
state-specific tendering processes across various components like Integrated Public 
Health Laboratories (IPHLs) and Critical Care Hospital Blocks (CCHBs). The 
Committee believes that leaving States entirely to their own administrative capacities to 
navigate these complex pre -construction phases inevitably leads to the highly uneven 
physical and financial progress currently observed. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends th at the Department establish a centralized 'Infrastructure Project 
Management Unit' (IPMU) strictly dedicated to PM -ABHIM. This unit should provide 
States with pre -approved, standardized DPR templates, centralized architectural 
blueprints, and model tenderi ng documents to bypass local bureaucratic inertia. 
Furthermore, the Committee is of the view that future fund releases must be linked to 
centrally monitored timelines for land allotment and human resource recruitment, 
ensuring that once physical infrastruc ture is erected, it does not lie dormant due to a 
lack of specialized medical personnel. 
4.5.9 On a query regarding the allocation of ₹4,200 crore at the BE stage for 2026 –27 under 
the scheme, despite the PM -ABHIM being originally envisaged for implementation over a 
five-year period from 2021–22 to 2025–26, the Department stated that: 
a) PM-ABHIM is  a capital -intensive infrastructure initiative aimed at strengthening 
public health facilities up to the block level. Given the nature of construction, 
procurement, and upgradation activities, the scheme entails inherent gestation periods, 
which resulted i n relatively lower expenditure during the initial years of 
implementation. As the scheme has now reached at an advanced stage, with a 
substantial number of projects under execution and nearing completion, expenditure is 
expected to accelerate in line with implementation progress.  
b) The allocation of Rs. 4,200 crore in BE 2026 -27 has been made keeping in view the 
scheme‘s committed liabilities, and the anticipated scale -up and completion of 

190 
 
ongoing projects, rather than past expenditure trends alone. The prov ision is intended 
to ensure continuity of works already approved and to meet financial requirements 
arising from physical progress on the ground. Further, EFC document for extension of 
the scheme has been circulated & proposal is under consideration. 
c) The Ministry continuously reviews State/UT-wise physical and financial progress, the 
pipeline of approved projects, utilization patterns, and implementation readiness 
through regular review meetings and Common Review Missions. Budgetary 
projections are aligned with realistic absorptive capacity and the requirement to 
complete approved deliverables within the approved framework of the scheme. 
 
4.5.10 Status of Implementation of CSS component of the PM -ABHIM scheme (excluding 
FC XV components) is as under:

a) PM-ABHIM performance was de layed by multiple bottlenecks such as delayed 
availability of adequate land, prolonged tendering and procurement processes, 
coordination with project executing agencies, and inadequate pre -implementation 
readiness such as incomplete DPRs and pending approvals.  
b) In order to address the issues, the States adopted several measures such as strengthening 
Program Management Units, establishment of Project Management Consultancies, 
expediting approvals of DPRs and necessary procurement clearances. Further review 
meetings by senior government officials, directing States to ensure land and site readiness 
for the approved projects, enhancing inter -departmental coordination through senior -level 
reviews, and improving monitoring through IT -based system such as NHM -PMS Portal. 
MoHFW is also monitoring district wise progress, particularly in aspirational districts, to 
help address issues and improve implementation. These interventions, and oversight, 
helped accelerate to achieve the progress of various components of PM-ABHIM.  
c) As of January 2026, substantial progress has been made in the establishment of key 
infrastructure components under the PM -Ayushman Bharat Health Infrastructure Mission 
(PM-ABHIM), including Building -less Ayushman Arogya Mandirs (Sub Centre –AAM), 
Urban AAMs, Block Public Health Units (BPHUs), Integrated Public Health Laboratories 
(IPHLs), and Critical Care Blocks (CCBs). Out of 9,519 approved Building -less AAMs, 
6,860 (72%) have commenced work and 4,856 have been completed. Of the 5,456 

191 
 
approved Urban-AAMs, 3,695 (68%) have been operationalized. Among 2,151 approved 
BPHUs, 1,904 (89%) have commenced work and 1,052 (49%) have been completed. 
Similarly, of the 744 approved IPHLs, 664 (89%) have commenced work and 351 (47%) 
have been completed. Under the CC B component, 621 units have been approved, with 
530 (85%) having commenced work and 74 (12%) completed. The remaining works are 
progressing as per approved timelines. 
 
4.5.11 The Committee is displeased to find the stark disparity between the initiation of  
infrastructure projects and their actual operationalization under PM -ABHIM. While 
85% of the 621 approved Critical Care Blocks (CCBs) have commenced construction, a 
critically low 4% are currently functional. Similarly, functional rates for Integrated 
Public Health Laboratories (IPHLs) and Block Public Health Units (BPHUs) languish at 
35% and 39%, respectively. This is a matter of concern to the Committee. The 
Committee believes that the ultimate metric of a healthcare scheme's success is not 
merely the progression of civil works, but the actual delivery of accessible services to the 
public. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department immediately pivot 
its primary monitoring focus from 'work started' milestones to 'functional status' 
outcomes. This must be achieved by strictly synchronizing the procurement of essential 
medical equipment and the recruitment of specialized human resources with the 
ongoing civil construction timelines. The Committee is of the view that the national 
Program Management Unit must mandate that states initiate hiring and procurement 
processes well before the physical completion of the buildings, ensuring that newly 
constructed facilities do not sit idle due to administrative delays. 
 
4.5.12 In view of recurring and conce rning trend of substantial budgetary reduction at 
RE stage and further under -utilization, of allocated fund , the Committee wish es to 
highlighted that despite such grave inabil ity to absorb funds, the allocation of ₹4,200.00 
crore has been proposed for FY 2026 -27. The Committee believes that while capital -
intensive schemes involve inherent gestation periods, however projecting estimates 
should calculate the ground -level executio n capacity , otherwise vagabo nd budgeting 
with distorts broader financial planning. The Committee, therefore, recommends that 
the Department formulate realistic, milestone -based budgetary demand intricately tied 
to the actual absorptive capacity of implementing agencies of States and UTs.  
 
4.6 AYUSHMAN BHARAT -PRADHAN MANTRI JAN AROGYA YOJANA (AB -
PMJAY) 
 
4.6.1 Ayushman Bharat Pradhan Mantri – Jan Arogya Yojana (AB PM -JAY) is the world‘s 
largest health assurance scheme that intends to reduce the out -of-pocket expenditure arising 
due to catastrophic expenditure on healthcare. AB PM-JAY was launched on 23rd September, 
2018 as recommended by the National Health Policy 2017, to achieve the SDGs vision of 
Universal Health Coverage (UHC), subsuming the erstwhile Ras htriya Swasthya Bima 
Yojana (RSBY). The scheme provides health cover of ₹ 5 lakhs per family per year for 
secondary and tertiary care hospitalization to bottom 40% of the India‘s poor and vulnerable 
population.  

192 
 
 
4.6.2 Initially, 10.74 crore eligible households were identified under the scheme from SECC 
2011 based on certain deprivation and occupational criteria in rural and urban areas 
respectively. Further, in January 2022, the Cabinet to the Government of India revised the 
beneficiary based to 12 crore f amilies, considering the decadal population growth rate of 
11.7% and gave the flexibility to States/UTs to use other State scheme databases.  
 
4.6.3 In March 2024, 37 lakh families of Accredited Social Health Activists (ASHAs), 
Anganwadi Workers (AWWs) and  Anganwadi Helpers (AWHs) were also included in the 
scheme. Further, on 29th October 2024, the Government has expanded AB PM -JAY to 
provide free treatment benefits of up to ₹5 lakh per year to all senior citizens aged 70 years 
and above along with their families, irrespective of their socio-economic status. 
 
Salient Features of AB PM-JAY  
a) Health Cover of ₹5 Lakh per family per year for secondary and tertiary care 
b) Completely paperless and cashless services  
c) No cap on family size, age or gender to ensure that  all members of the beneficiary 
families are covered.  
d) Provides healthcare services across 27 specialties covering 1961 procedures.   
e) Includes 3 days of pre-hospitalisation and 15 days of post-hospitalisation expenses.  
f) All pre–existing conditions are covered from day one. 
g) The benefits under the scheme are portable across the country i.e., benefits can be 
availed at any Empanelled Healthcare Providers across the Country 
h) 35 States/UTs are currently implementing Ayushman Bharat PM -JAY except West 
Bengal.

4.6.4 The Committee notes that under the scheme, a Budget Estimate of ₹7,300.00 crore 
was provided in 2024 –25, which was increased to ₹7,605.54 crore at the Revised Estimates 
stage. Against this, the actual expenditure during the year amounted to ₹7,178.64 cr ore. For 
the year 2025–26, an amount of ₹9,406.00 crore was provided at the Budget Estimates stage 
and reduced to ₹9,000.00 crore at the Revised Estimates stage, against which an expenditure 
of ₹6,868.22 crore (which is about 73% of the BE allocated) had b een incurred up to 
31.01.2026. For 2026–27, an allocation of ₹9,500.00 crore has been allocated. 
4.6.5 The Committee notes that the budgetary allocation for AB -PMJAY has seen a 
progressive increase, with ₹9,500.00 crore allocated for FY 2026 -27, however, t he 
Committee observes that fund utilization in FY 2025 -26 appeared sluggish, with only 
₹6,868.22 crore (approximately 73% of the BE) utilized as of January 2026. The 
Committee believes that such slow pace of expenditure is inconsistent with the recent, 
massive expansion of the scheme‘s ma ndate, keeping in view inclusion  of 37 lakh 
families of ASHAs and Anganwadi workers, as well as the universal coverage of all 
senior citizens aged 70 and above. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the 
Department conduct a mid -year actuarial review of the claims ratio to ensure that the 
BE for 2026-27 is sufficient to cover the anticipated surge in secondary and tertiary care 
demand from these high -utilization groups. The Committee is of the view that the 
Department mus t streamline the settlement process between the National Health 
Authority (NHA) and State Health Agencies to accelerate fund absorption, ensuring that 
the world's largest health assurance scheme is backed by a financial pipeline that is as 
responsive as its expanded mandate.

4.6.6 The Committee observes that as of January 2026, 33,271 hospitals have been 
empanelled, with a split between public (17,508) and private (15,763) institutions. While 
the creation of 43.17 crore Ayushman Cards is commendable, the Co mmittee believes 
that the true efficacy of the scheme lies in the "portability" and "cashless" nature of the 
services delivered. The Committee is concerned that with 11.46 crore authorized 
hospital admissions, the pressure on the existing network of 33,271  hospitals will 
intensify considering the inclusion of senior citizens. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends that the Department launch a targeted drive to empanel more private 
tertiary care specialty hospitals, particularly in underserved and remote areas, to reduce 
the travel burden for vulnerable populations. Furthermore, the Committee is of the view 
that the Department must institutionalize a robust, real -time "Patient Satisfaction and 
Outcome Audit" to ensure that the "completely paperless and cashless " promise is 
strictly adhered to on the ground, preventing any instances of unauthorized out -of-
pocket charges at the point of care. 
4.6.7 On being asked for the reasons of increasing allocation ₹ 9,500 crore in BE 2026 -27, 
the Department stated that u nder AB PMJA Y, the budget requirement is estimated based on 
the number of eligible beneficiary families and fixed annual premium of ₹1,052 per family 
per year. As the AB PMJAY is a Centrally Sponsore d Scheme, the cost is shared between the 
Centre and the States. Accordingly, the budget for the core AB PMJAY beneficiary family 
base for 12 crore families remains a committed and largely fixed liability. Further, in FY 
2024-25, coverage under AB PM -JAY wa s expanded to include approximately 37 lakh 
families of ASHA, AWW and AWH workers, as well as about 4.5 crore senior citizen 
families aged 70 years and above.  
4.6.8 During FY 2025–26, a total central share of ₹ 7,093.51 crore has been released so far 
to t he States/UTs based on demands raised. Of this, ₹ 307.87 crore has been released for 
Ayushman Vay Vandana category, and ₹ 47.89 crore under the ASHA/AWW/AWH category 
during FY 2025-26. However, several States/UTs have not yet raised demands for the central  
share under Ayushman Vay Vandana and ASHA/AWW/AWH category due to various 
implementation related challenges.  It is expected that the pending demands for FY 2025 –26, 
along with the regular demands for FY 2026 –27, will be raised in the subsequent financial 
year. Further, the expansion of beneficiary groups under Ayushman Vay Vandana and 
ASHA/AWW/AWH has led to an increase in Ayushman Card creation, hospital admissions, 
and claims submission. In view of this rising utilisation and expanded beneficiary base, 
demands from States/UTs are expected to increase in FY 2026 –27, and th erefore the 
enhanced Budget Estimate of ₹ 9,500 crore is expected to be fully utilised.  
4.6.9 On being asked the percentage of funds have been utilised for rural and tribal areas, it 
is submitted that an amount of Rs. 320.00 crore has been surrendered und er the North Eastern 
Region (NER) component out of the allocated Rs. 940.00 crore (constituting 10% of the total 
BE). This surrender is primarily due to the fact that, under AB PM -JAY the total estimated 
beneficiaries for the NER, based on SECC data, accou nt for only 4% of the total beneficiaries 
of PMJAY, whereas the total fund allocation kept for the NER is 10% of the BE. Therefore, 
the budget under the NER component remains unutilised. This was also the reason for 97% 
utilization in FY 2024-25.  

195 
 
4.6.10 On being asked whether the allocation of ₹ 9,500 crore is sufficient on account of t he 
expansion of AB PMJAY to cover senior citizens aged 70 years and above, it is submitted 
that as on 13.02.2026, over 1.11 crore Ayushman Vay Vandana Cards have been create d and 
a total of 11.68 lakh hospital admissions have been authorised under this category. Since the 
enrolment of senior citizens is ongoing, the budget estimate have been done based on actual 
utilisation patterns, claim trends, and the number of cards crea ted. Based on these utilisation 
trends, it is assessed that the Budget Estimate of ₹ 9,500 crores for FY 2026 –27 is adequate to 
meet the financial requirements arising from the expanded senior citizen coverage.

4.6.12 On being asked about the details of Gig workers under the scheme, it is submitted that 
the scheme has not yet been implemented, as the decision is still pending with the Ministry of 
Labour and Employment. 
4.6.13 On query related to major problems being faced in im plementation of AB PMJAY, it 
is submitted that as AB PMJAY continues to mature, both the scale and quality of healthcare 
services delivered under the Scheme have shown steady improvement. However, key 
implementation challenges include reaching eligible ben eficiaries in remote, tribal, semi -
urban and low-connectivity areas, ensuring timely service delivery, and preventing misuse of 
benefits. 
a) To address these challenges and strengthen outreach, NHA is undertaking extensive 
IEC activities and Ayushman Card cre ation drives across the country. These efforts 
include outreach through newspapers, community radio, street plays, digital displays, 
radio campaigns, mass messaging through social media platforms, and telecast of 
beneficiary testimonials.  
b) To facilitate en rolment in areas with poor or no internet connectivity, an offline 
module of the Ayushman App has been developed, enabling beneficiary enrolment 
and card generation in remote locations. 
c) To improve efficiency and prevent fraud, the National Anti -Fraud Unit (NAFU) uses 
AI/ML tools to identify unusual claims such as duplicate claims, inflated procedures, 
or misuse of patient identity. NAFU has also strengthened oversight through an ―audit 
of audit‖ mechanism, which helps detect gaps in claim reviews and provid es feedback 
to States for corrective action. 
d) Further, Ayushman mobile application has been upgraded with enhanced features, 
allowing beneficiaries to check eligibility, view wallet balance, track treatment 
history, and provide feedback, thereby improving transparency and user experience. 

196 
 
e) In addition, a Centralized Grievance Redressal Management System (CGRMS) and a 
24×7 toll -free helpline (14555) have been established. Grievances are addressed 
through a three -tier mechanism at the District, State and Nation al levels, with 
designated nodal officers and grievance committees ensuring timely resolution and 
accountability. 
Collectively, these measures are strengthening the implementation of PM -JAY, expanding its 
outreach in underserved areas, and enhancing beneficiary trust and system accountability. 
4.6.14 On a query related to the steps taken to expand the scheme to include missing middle 
class (as highlighted in NITI Aayog's Health Insurance for India's Missing Middle report) that 
is outside any health insuranc e coverage a nd bring them under the financial risk protection, 
the Department stated that AB PMJAY provides health cover of ₹ 5 lakh per family per year 
for secondary and tertiary care hospitalisation to 12.37 crore families, covering the bottom 
40% of the population.  Further, in October 2024, the scheme was expanded to cover 6 crore 
senior citizens of age 70 years and above belonging to 4.5 crore families irrespective of their 
socio-economic status.  This universal coverage for senior citizens aged 70 years and above 
directly addresses a key segment of the ―missing middle‖ that is vulnerable to high out -of-
pocket health expenditure. Additionally, several States/UTs are using the NHA‘s IT platform 
to extend coverage to additional families under their state‘s schemes, the reby progressively 
expanding health insurance coverage beyond the AB PM-JAY.  In addition, several ministries 
are utilising the NHA‘s IT platform to implement Government health insurance schemes such 
as Ayushman CAPF, CGHS, NAMASTE, SMILE, and PM -CARES etc. These schemes also 
extend health coverage to segments of the missing middle, thereby expanding access to 
Government-funded health insurance beyond the core AB PM-JAY beneficiaries. 
4.6.15 On a specific query, it is submitted that AB PM -JAY has made a sig nificant 
contribution towards reducing the out -of-pocket expenditure (OOPE) by providing cashless 
secondary and tertiary care hospitalisation up to ₹ 5 lakh per family per year. As on 31 st 
January 2026, a cumulative 11.46 crore hospital admissions amountin g to approximately 1.69 
lakh crore have been authorised under AB PM -JAY. Over the last three years, the scheme has 
consistently enabled a large volume of cashless treatments. The detail of hospital admissions 
for the last three years is as under:

197 
 
4.6.16 On a specific query related to fraud cases and irregularities under the scheme, it is 
submitted that NHA follows a zero -tolerance approach towards fraud under AB PM -JAY. 
The National Anti-Fraud Unit (NAFU), uses AI/ML -based automated triggers to flag unusual 
claim patterns such as duplicate entries, inflated procedures, or misuse of  patient identity, etc. 
All submitted claims are subjected to fraud and abuse analytics within 24 hours of c laim 
submission by hospitals. Claims that are identified suspicious through system -based analytics 
and triggers are flagged and forwarded to the respective State Anti -Fraud Units (SAFUs) for 
further verification and detailed investigation. 
4.6.17 Some of t he major categories of abuse/fraud identified through these system -based 
checks include Medical Identity Theft (such as use of duplicate or forged patient images), 
billing for services not rendered (e.g. Duplicate claim raised for a patient on same day), 
upcoding or unbundling of services (where higher -value packages are claimed or bundled 
services are split to seek excess reimbursement), deviation from Standard Treatment 
Guidelines (STGs), etc. As on 21 January 2026, cases involving fraud and abuse amounting to 
₹644.04 crore have been prevented through rejection of the associated claims prior to 
payment, thereby safeguarding public funds. Further, a near real -time dedicated anti -fraud 
dashboard has been developed to enhance monitoring and oversight at the S tate/UT level. In 
addition, regular capacity -building workshops and field -level training programmes are 
conducted for States/UTs to strengthen the anti -fraud framework and reinforce compliance 
among all stakeholders. The year -wise status of total claims su bmitted and claims identified 
as fraud under the anti-fraud framework for the last five years is as under: 
Table 4.6.4

(₹ in Lakh)

This data excludes data of Karnataka, Andhra Pradesh, Maharashtra, Ra jasthan, Telangana 
and Tamil Nadu as in these states AB-PMJAY is run on their own IT platforms. 
 
4.6.18 On being asked whether the Government is considering any study to increase the cap 
under PMJAY taking into account the inflation and costly medical inte rventions, particularly 
for people aged 70 years and more, it is submitted that  NHA has circulated EFC proposal for 
continuation of AB PM -JAY for next five years, from 2026 -27 to 2030 -31  with upward 
revision of the premium per -family per year to accommoda te an increased utilization and 
rising healthcare costs which are attributable to enhanced beneficiary awareness, increased 
enrolment, periodic expansion and revision of health benefit packages, and sustained health 
inflation. Further, NHA is continuously reviewing the performance of the scheme to ensure its 
responsiveness to emerging healthcare needs, including the impact of medical inflation and 
the rising cost of advanced medical interventions. In parallel, several initiatives are being 

198 
 
undertaken to make AB PM-JAY more patient-centric and hospital-friendly and to strengthen 
scheme implementation.  
 
a) To expand the network of empanelled hospitals, consultations have been held with 
private healthcare providers to encourage establishment of healthcare infrast ructure in 
remote and semi -urban areas. Additionally, NHA is in the process of onboarding 
hospitals under the National Cancer Grid to enhance access to comprehensive cancer 
care for eligible AB PM-JAY beneficiaries.  
b) Further, rationalisation of Health Bene fit Packages (HBPs) is also an ongoing process 
to ensure that packages are clinically relevant and financial viable for hospitals.  
c) In addition, upgradation of the IT systems is a continuous process to facilitate both 
beneficiaries and hospitals. Through t he recent upgrade of the Ayushman mobile 
application, beneficiaries can now check their available wallet balance, search the 
empanelled hospital network, view their treatment history and provide feedback on 
services received, thereby enhancing transparency, accessibility and patient experience 
under the scheme. 
4.6.19 On being asked about the data that supports maintaining the current ₹5 lakh cap for 
elderly patients with comorbidities, it is submitted that t he current coverage limit of ₹ 5 lakh 
per family per year has been designed based on actuarial estimates of expected utilisation and 
expenditure patterns. As per current utilisation trends under PM -JAY, approximately 99% of 
beneficiary families utilise less than ₹ 2 lakhs from their available annual cove r, indicating 
that the existing ₹ 5 lakh limit is adequate to meet the hospitalisation needs of the vast 
majority of beneficiaries, including elderly patients. Further, in cases involving patients with 
comorbidities, the scheme allows to avail multiple pro cedures within a single pre -
authorisation, during the same episode of care, without compromising access or quality of 
treatment. 
4.6.20 It is further submitted that the senior citizen category under AB PM -JAY was 
launched in October 2024. NHA has analysed wallet utilisation patterns of senior citizen 
beneficiary families who availed treatment during FY 2025 –26 to assess adequacy of the 
existing coverage. As per the current utilization pattern based on the data available with 
NHA, for senior citizen benefici ary families, for the FY 2025 -26, more than 91% of families 
have utilized less than ₹1 lakh of wallet. In view of this, it appears that  existing ₹ 5 lakh per 
family per year coverage is adequate to meet the hospitalisation needs of senior citizens, 
including those with comorbidities. Further, the scheme design allows beneficiaries with 
comorbid conditions to avail multiple procedures within a single pre -authorisation. In view of 
the above, no separate estimation of additional budgetary requirement for intro duction of a 
higher or differential cover of over and above Rs 5 lakh for senior citizens has been 
undertaken at this stage.   
4.6.21 The Committee notes the enhanced allocation of ₹9,500 crore for FY 2026 –27 under 
Ayushman Bharat –Pradhan Mantri Jan Arogya Yojana (AB PM -JAY) in view of the 
expansion of beneficiary categories, including senior citizens aged 70 years and above and 
frontline workers such as ASHA, AWW and AWH. The Committee is of the view that while 

199 
 
the scheme has contributed to reducing out -of-pocket expenditure and has enabled a large 
number of cashless hospitalisations across the country, the Committee observes that 
challenges remain in terms of outreach, timely claim settlement, and adequacy of coverage 
for critical and high-cost treatments.  
 
4.6.22 The Committee reiterates its recommend ation that the Department undertake a 
comprehensive review of the scheme with a view t o further widening coverage, including 
rationalisation of the eligibility criteria for senior citizens by considering lowering the age 
threshold from 70 years to 60 years irrespective of socio -economic status. The Committee 
also recommends that the Departm ent evaluate the feasibility of enhancing the existing 
insurance cover from ₹5 lakh to ₹10 lakh limit in view of rising medical costs and expensive 
tertiary care interventions. Further, the Department may review and expand the list of 
procedures and diagno stics covered under the scheme, particularly high -end investigations 
such as CT scans, MRI and nuclear imaging, as well as advanced treatments for critical 
illnesses, so as to ensure that beneficiaries receive comprehensive and financially protected 
healthcare services without being compelled to incur additional out-of-pocket expenditure. 
 
4.6.23 The Committee notes that the Government had announced the inclusion of gig and 
platform workers under the scheme with the objective of extending health protection to this 
emerging segment of the workforce,  however, the Committee is constrained to observe that 
the proposal has not yet been operationalised as the matter is stated to be under 
consideration with the Ministry of Labour and Employment. The Committee empha sises 
that gig and platform workers constitute a rapidly growing section of the labour force that 
often lack access to formal social security and health coverage. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends that the Department take up the matter with the Ministry  of Labour and 
Employment on priority so that the modalities for identification, enrolment and coverage of 
gig and platform workers are finalised expeditiously and the scheme is implemented without 
further delay. Early operationalisation of this initiative  would help extend the benefits of 
financial protection in healthcare to a vulnerable and expanding workforce segment and 
strengthen the overall objective of inclusive health coverage. 
 
4.6.24 The Committee further notes that despite the growing utilisatio n of the scheme, 
several implementation -related challenges persist, including delays in raising demand by 
some States/UTs, limited awareness among eligible beneficiaries, and occasional delays in 
settlement of claims of empanelled hospitals. The Committee , therefore, recommends the 
Department to intensify awareness and outreach campaigns across rural, tribal and remote 
areas so that eligible families are fully informed about the benefits of the scheme and are not 
left out from enrolment.  
 
4.6.25 The Commit tee further recommends that the Department should strengthen 
coordination with States/UTs to ensure timely raising of claims and efficient utilisation of 
allocated funds, particularly in regions where utilisation remains low. The Committee 
further recommen ds that the Department to streamline and strictly monitor the claim 
settlement mechanism to ensure that payments to empanelled hospitals are processed within 
the stipulated timelines. In addition, the package rates under the scheme may be periodically 

200 
 
reviewed in consultation with States/UTs and healthcare providers so as to maintain 
financial viability for hospitals and prevent denial of treatment to beneficiaries. Such 
measures would enhance the effectiveness, transparency and sustainability of the scheme  
while ensuring equitable access to quality healthcare for all eligible citizens. 
 
4.6.26 The Committee observes that the effectiveness of the scheme is closely linked to the 
availability and equitable distribution of empanelled hospitals and quality healt hcare 
infrastructure across States/UTs. The Committee wish es to highlight that although a large 
number of hospitals have been empanelled under the scheme, but there continue to be 
regional disparities, particularly in rural, aspirational and remote areas w here access to 
empanelled facilities remains limited. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the 
Department, in coordination with States/UTs, take proactive measures to expand the 
network of empanelled public and private hospitals in underserved regions  so that 
beneficiaries do not have to travel long distances to avail treatment. The Committee also 
emphasises the need to strengthen public health institutions and encourage their greater 
participation under the scheme, along with periodic quality audits o f empanelled facilities to 
ensure adherence to prescribed standards of care. Such steps would enhance accessibility, 
improve service delivery and ensure that the benefits of the scheme reach beneficiaries 
across all regions in an equitable manner. 
 
4.6.27 The Committee notes that the success of the scheme also depends on robust 
monitoring, transparency and effective use of digital platforms for beneficiary identification, 
hospital empanelment and claim processing. While the Department has developed IT -based 
systems for implementation and monitoring, the Committee is of the view that there is scope 
for further strengthening data analytics and real -time monitoring mechanisms to detect 
irregularities, prevent fraudulent claims and improve overall efficiency of the scheme. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department to  strengthen the digital ecosystem 
of the scheme by integrating advanced analytics, periodic audits and grievance redressal 
mechanisms so as to ensure transparency and accountability in  its implementation. The 
Committee desires that feedback received from beneficiaries and empanelled hospitals be 
systematically collected and analysed to identify operational issues and improve service 
delivery under the scheme. Such measures would help in  ensuring that the scheme functions 
in a transparent, efficient and beneficiary -centric manner while safeguarding public 
resources.

CHAPTER V 
 
ISSUES RAISED AND ROAD AHEAD 
 
5.1 PROMOTION OF INDIGENOUS MEDICAL TECHNOLOGY AND EASING 
REGULATIONS 
 
5.1.1 On being asked what measures are proposed in 2026 –27 for promoting indigenous 
medical technology, rationalising duties/taxes, easing regulations, and improving affordability 
and accessibility of healthcare, the Department submitted that the Government  is making 
efforts to reduce the OOPE as a percentage of Total Health Expenditure (THE). There has 
been a significant increase in Government Health Expenditure (GHE) as percentage of THE, 
which has increased from 29.0% in 2014-15 to 48.0% in 2021-22.  
5.1.2 Ministry of Health and Family Welfare has rolled out and implemented various 
schemes and programs to provide accessible, affordable and quality healthcare services to all, 
including those in rural areas. These key schemes/programs have contributed in red ucing out-
of-pocket expenditures in availing health services, some of these initiatives are National 
Health Mission (NHM): Ayushman Arogya Mandir (AAM) : Pradhan Mantri Ayushman 
Bharat Health Infrastructure Mission ( PM-ABHIM) Ayushman Bharat Pradhan Mantri  Jan 
Arogya Yojana (AB-PMJAY).  
5.1.3 Besides, quality generic medicines are made available at affordable prices to all under 
Pradhan Mantri Bhartiya Janaushadhi Pariyojana (PMBJP) in collaboration with the State 
Governments. Affordable Medicines and Relia ble Implants for Treatment (AMRIT) 
Pharmacy stores have been set up in some hospitals/Institutions.―Make in India‖ guidelines 
are followed for procurement and indigenous technologies are introduced in public health 
system from time to time depending on req uirement, regulatory approval, recommendation of 
Health Technology Assessment Board under DHR and feasibility of implementation.  
5.1.4 The Committee consider s that the Department should adopt a coordinated 
strategy in 2026 –27 to promote indigenous medical  technology while simultaneously 
improving affordability and accessibility of healthcare. This should include prioritising 
procurement under ―Make in India‖ guidelines, rationalising duties and taxes on 
essential medical devices and drugs, and streamlining  regulatory approvals through a 
single-window mechanism to encourage innovation and faster adoption.  
5.2 AYUSHMAN BHARAT SCHEME 
5.2.1 On a query on how does Government assess the significant rise in beneficiaries and 
expenditure under the Ayushman Bharat Scheme from 2018 –19 to 2025 –26 The rise in 
beneficiaries base and expenditure under AB  PM-JAY reflects the scheme's gradual 
expansion and effectiveness of the steps taken to make it more accessible The following 
measures have been undertaken to enhance ben eficiary access, improve awareness of 
entitlements, and facilitate increased utilisation of services under AB PMJAY: 

202 
 
a. Initially, in 2018 the scheme covered 10.74 crore families, which was expanded to 12 
crore families in January 2022. As on 28 th February 2026, over 43.52 crore Ayushman 
Cards are issued under the scheme. 
b. As on 31st January 2026, more than 11.46 crore hospital admissions amounting to Rs. 
1.69 lakh crore have been authorised under the scheme. 
c. As on 28 th February 2026, a total of 36,229 hospital s has been empanelled under AB 
PM-JAY, of which 16,745 are private hospitals. 
d. The Health Benefit Packages (HBP -2022), covering 1,961 procedures across 27 
specialties. 
5.2.2 The Committee recommends  that the Department should further build on the 
demonstrated expansion of Ayushman Bharat –PMJAY by instituting a comprehensive 
monitoring and sustainability framework  to manage the significant rise in beneficiaries 
and expenditure. While the increase reflects improved access, awareness, and hospital 
empanelment, the Committee urges the Department to regularly review Health Benefit 
Packages to align with clinical and cost trends, strengthen fraud detection through 
AI/ML analytics, and ensure equitable distribution of empanelled hospitals across 
underserved regions.  
5.2.3 On a specific query, it is submitted that urban -Ayushman Arogya Mandirs (U-AAMs) 
are being operationalised in Delhi to strengthen comprehensive primary healthcare 
infrastructure. The Cabinet of Ministers, GNCTD approved implementation of U -AAMs in 
Delhi vide Cabinet Decision dated 20th February 2025. As on date, 370 Urban Ayushman  
Arogya Mandirs are operational in Delhi, against the proposed expansion to 1,139 centres. 
5.2.4 The Committee recommends that the Department must expedite the planned 
expansion of Ayushman Arogya Mandirs (AAMs) in Delhi from the current 370 to the 
proposed 1,139 centres through a phased but time‑bound roadmap. While approvals 
have been secured and implementation activities are underway, the Committee urges the 
Department to ensure that infrastructure strengthening, staffing, and service readiness 
requirements are completed within defined milestones.  
5.2.5 On being asked about any proposal to extend the benefits of the Ayushman Bharat 
scheme to AYUSH hospitals and to ensure its implementation across major cancer institutions 
such as Tata Memorial Hospital, it is submitted that NHA is in the process of onboarding of 
all hospitals under the National Cancer Grid to enhance access to comprehensive cancer care 
for eligible AB PM -JAY beneficiaries. As on 28th February 2026, a total of 36,229 hospitals  
has been empanelled under the PM -JAY network, out of which 255 hospitals are affiliated 
with the National Cancer Grid. Ayushman Bharat facility is already available at Chittaranjan 
National Cancer Institute, Kolkata. It may also be noted that CNCI has been providing cancer 
treatment to Ayushman Bharat beneficiaries since 2020 
5.2.6 The Committee recommends that the Department  must expedite the extension of 
Ayushman Bharat –PMJAY benefit s to AYUSH hospitals and ensure full 
implementation across all major cancer institutions, including Tata Memorial Hospital 

203 
 
and those under the National Cancer Grid. While 255 cancer hospitals including 
Chittaranjan National Cancer Institute are already aff iliated and providing services 
under the scheme, the Committee urges the Department to establish a time-bound 
roadmap for universal empanelment of recognised AYUSH and cancer care centres , 
backed by streamlined regulatory approvals and adequate reimbursement packages.  
5.2.7 On inclusion of 17 immunotherapy cancer drugs and steps taken to make these high -
cost treatments (₹30 –60 lakh) more accessible under public schemes like Ayushman Bharat, 
the Department made submission that u nder the latest national mast er of the Health Benefit 
Package (HBP) of AB PM -JAY Cancer related treatment is provided under more than 500 
procedures of Medical Oncology, Surgical Oncology, Radiation Oncology and Palliative 
Medicine in the national Health Benefit Package (HBP) master.  Under the Tertiary Care 
Cancer Centre Scheme, financial assistance is provided to State Governments for 
establishment and strengthening of oncology facilities in Government Medical Colleges and 
tertiary care institutions. The scheme supports procurement of  advanced equipment such as 
linear accelerators, CT simulators, brachytherapy units and chemotherapy infrastructure, 
thereby enhancing capacity for radiotherapy, medical oncology and surgical oncology 
services in the public sector. Treatment in these Gover nment facilities is provided either free 
of cost or at highly subsidized rates as per State Government norms, and eligible beneficiaries 
are also covered under public health assurance schemes, thereby reducing out -of-pocket 
expenditure and regional disparities in access to advanced cancer care. 
5.2.8 The Committee recommends that the Department take proactive measures to 
make high -cost immunotherapy cancer treatments, now exempted from customs duty, 
more accessible under public schemes like Ayushman Bharat –PMJAY. While PMJAY 
currently provides coverage of ₹5 lakh per family and supports over 500 oncology 
procedures, the Committee urges the Department to develop special high -cost treatment 
packages for immunotherapy and advanced cancer care, supported by pool ed financing 
and negotiated pricing with manufacturers.  
5.2.9 The Committee was informed that the health benefits packages have been revised five 
times since launch of the scheme. Initially, the scheme included 1,393 packages, now the 
latest HBP- 2022 comprises 1,961 packages across 27 medical specialties. As on 28 February 
2026, a total of 26 States/UTs have adopted the latest HBP 2022. The empanelment of private 
hospitals under the scheme is entirely voluntary. As on 28 th February 2026, a total of 36,229  
hospitals has been empaneled under the scheme, of which 16,745 are private hospitals. 
5.2.10 The Committee is of the considered view that the Department must strengthen 
affordability and accessibility of high-cost treatments under Ayushman Bharat–PMJAY 
by ensuring that revisions to Health Benefit Package (HBP) rates are undertaken in a 
systematic and time -bound manner , with transparent alignment to market costs and 
utilisation trends. The Committee further urges the Department to incentivise private 
hospital empanelment by addressing reimbursement concerns, simplifying regulatory 
processes, and enhancing grievance redressal mechanisms.  
 

204 
 
5.2.11 On a query on enhancing the ₹5 lakh coverage limit under Ayushman Bharat to ₹10 
lakh to support high -cost procedur es such as kidney transplants and cardiac surgeries, the 
Department provided that at present, there is no proposal under consideration to enhance the 
coverage limit under the scheme. As per the utilisation trends of previous year (FY 2024 -25), 
nearly 99% of beneficiary families avail benefits amounting to Rs. 2 lakh or less in a year. In 
view of this utilisation pattern, the existing coverage of Rs.5 lakh per family per year for 
secondary and tertiary care hospitalisation is considered adequate to meet the healthcare needs 
of the vast majority of beneficiaries. Under AB PM -JAY, kidney transplants is already 
covered, under which cost of surgery, medicines, diagnostics, hospital stay, and follow -up 
care are included within the prescribed package rates. 
5.2.12 The Committee recommends that the Department consider revising the 
Ayushman Bharat–PMJAY coverage framework by introducing a differential treatment 
cost model for high -cost and complex procedures. The current ₹5 lakh coverage limit 
does not adequately meet s the needs of most beneficiaries, as certain advanced 
interventions such as organ transplants like liver transplant, cardiac surgeries, and 
immunotherapy treatments far exceed the threshold. The Committee, therefore 
reiterates its recommendation to enhanc e the ₹5 lakh coverage limit to ₹10 lakh under 
Ayushman Bharat, and create special high -cost packages within PMJAY, supported by 
pooled financing, negotiated pricing with hospitals and manufacturers, and co -funding 
mechanisms with States. Such targeted enh ancement will ensure that beneficiaries 
requiring expensive, life -saving procedures are not financially excluded, while 
maintaining fiscal sustainability for the scheme overall. 
5.2.13 On a query, the Department submitted that pre-authorization is the first step to provide 
cashless treatment to eligible beneficiaries. To avoid delays in treatment, NHA has defined a 
Turnaround Time (TAT) of up to 6 hours for processing pre -authorization requests. Aadhaar-
based biometric authentication is mandatory during reg istration. NHA has provided real -time 
dashboard for monitoring at the hospital‘s logins, enabling users to track, response, and 
process pre -authorization requests through dedicated workflow buckets, thereby ensuring 
timely action, better visibility, and effective management of pre-authorization cases. 
5.2.14 The Committee desires that the Department should further should strengthen 
mechanisms to ensure time-bound approval of cashless treatment cases under 
Ayushman Bharat–PMJAY, given persistent delays despi te defined turnaround norms. 
While measures such as Aadhaar -based authentication, retrospective pre -authorization, 
auto-approval for high -utilisation packages, and real -time dashboards have been 
introduced, the Committee urges the Department to institute strict monitoring of 
adherence to the six -hour Turn-Around Time (TAT) , backed by penalty provisions for 
hospitals or agencies causing undue delays. Additionally, periodic audits of 
pre-authorization workflows, expansion of auto -approval to more low -risk procedures, 
and deployment of AI-enabled case tracking should be prioritised.  
5.2.15 On being asked about any proposal to mandate clear diagnosis and prescription 
documentation by doctors under Ayushman Bharat, the Department submitted that AB PM -
JAY follows  a bundled package -based payment framework, wherein  each Health Benefit 

205 
 
Package (HBP) is designed to comprehensively cover the cost of treatment from admission to 
discharge. Standard Treatment Guidelines (STGs) have been adopted to promote evidence -
based, uniform, and cost -effective clinical care across empaneled hospitals. These guidelines 
help ensure standardisation of treatment protocols, rational use of resources, and improved 
quality of care while reducing unwarranted variations in clinical practices. 
5.2.16 The Committee is of the firm view that the Department must issue guidelines for 
clear and standardised documentation of diagnosis and prescriptions by doctors under 
Ayushman Bharat–PMJAY to strengthen transparency in treatment and billing. While 
bundled package payments and Standard Treatment Guidelines already promote 
uniformity, the Committee urges the Department to integrate mandatory digital 
prescription and diagnosis records into the PMJAY IT platform, linked with 
Aadhaar-based beneficiary authe ntication to ensure traceability of clinical decisions, 
reduce unwarranted variations in practice, and enhance accountability of empanelled 
hospitals.  
5.2.17 On a query for issuing the Ayushman Cards to the remaining eligible beneficiaries, the 
Department submitted that National Health Authority, in coordination with State Health 
Agencies (SHAs), has undertaken several initiatives to achieve 100% Ayushman Card 
saturation across the country. In this regard, NHA implemented the ―AapkeDwarAyushman‖ 
mission-mode campaign, conducting door -to-door outreach to mobilise eligible beneficiaries 
and assist them in the creation of Ayushman Cards. Further, the Beneficiary Identification 
System (BIS -2.0) enables self -verification, thereby reducing dependency on intermedi aries 
and streamlining the card creation process. To facilitate enrolment in areas with poor or no 
internet connectivity, an offline module of the Ayushman App has been developed, enabling 
beneficiary enrolment and card generation in remote locations. As a  result of these initiatives, 
as on 28 th February 2026, a total of 43.52 crore Ayushman Cards  have been created across 
States and Union Territories. 
5.2.18 The Committee recommends the Department to intensify efforts to achieve 100% 
Ayushman Card saturatio n within a defined timeline, building on the success of 
initiatives such as Aapke Dwar Ayushman  and BIS -2.0 self -verification. While over 43 
crore cards have already been issued, the Committee urges the Department to ensure 
last-mile enrolment through expa nded offline modules in connectivity -poor regions and 
continuous mobilisation of frontline workers. To strengthen acceptance in private 
hospitals, the Department should address reimbursement concerns, streamline 
empanelment processes, and enhance transpare ncy through real -time dashboards and 
grievance redressal mechanisms.  
5.2.19 On asking how many out of the 1.8 lakh Ayushman Arogya Mandir are fully 
functional with availability of essential medicines, diagnostic services, and comprehensive 
primary care including mental, oral, ENT, and eye health services, the Department stated that 
launched in 2018, 1,50,000 Ayushsman Bharat Health and Wellness Centre (AB-HWCs) have 
been established by upgrading the Sub Health Centres (SHCs) and Primary Health Centres 
(PHCs) in rural and urban areas across the country by December 2022. Now renamed 
Ayushman Arogya Mandir (AAM), currently more than 1.83 lakh AAM are functional across 

206 
 
the States/UTs, which also includes AYUSH AAM. Comprehensive primary healthcare 
services for mental health, ENT, ophthalmology, oral health, geriatric and palliative care and 
trauma care are being incrementally added. 
5.2.20 The Committee recommends that the Department publish a clear status report 
on the functionality of Ayushman Arogya Mandirs ( AAMs), specifically highlighting the 
availability of essential medicines, diagnostic services, and expanded primary care 
including mental health, oral, ENT, and eye health services. While over 1.83 lakh AAMs 
are operational, the Committee urges the Departm ent to institute regular audits and 
public disclosure of service readiness, ensuring that each centre meets the prescribed 
standards of comprehensive care. To improve accountability and awareness, the 
Committee desires that besides NHA portal, the updated lists of empanelled hospitals 
should also be prominently displayed at local AAMs.  
 
5.3 AIIMS INSTITUTION 
5.3.1 On queries regarding proposal to establish an additional AIIMS in Delhi with separate 
administration, steps taken to address the daily footfall of 18,000–20,000 patients at AIIMS 
and possibility of establishing AIIMS branch on available 400 acres of vacant land in 
Badarpur and introducing two-shift OPD systems, the Department stated that there is no such 
proposal for establishing a new branch of A IIMS, New Delhi. It is to submit that National 
Cancer Institute, established in Jhajjar  as an extended campus of AIIMS, New Delhi, has a 
sprawling campus of 288 acres. Further, AIIMS New Delhi has an area of 244 acres spreading 
over 06 land parcels includi ng East & West Ansari Nagar, AyurVigyan Nagar, Masjif Moth, 
Trauma Centre Campus and New Hostel Complex. As there are unutilised land parcels in 
AIIMS Delhi main campus and at Jhajjar, there is a plan  to develop such areas in a phased 
manner for additional healthcare facilities etc.  
5.3.2 Under PMSSY a 750 -bedded All India Institute of Medical Science (AIIMS) is being 
set up in Village Majra Mastil Bhalkhi, Tehsil Manethi in District Rewari, Haryana. The 
progress of physical works is 55% and the project i s scheduled to complete by December 
2026. 
5.3.3 The Institute has expanded and optimized digital technology to address increased 
patient load through introduction of Online Registration System (ORS) with real time 
dashboard for scheduling OPD visits, IPD a dmission, diagnostics, discharging and billing. In 
line with commitment to innovation and excellence in healthcare delivery AIIMS has 
operationalized ‗TRED‘ (Triage Register for Emergency Department), a web based 
application designed to manage patient mana gement and operational efficiency in Emergency 
Department and ‗AIIMS Inter Referral System‘ a centralized platform that can enable 
seamless patient referrals across the entire network of AIIMS all over India, ensuring 
coordinated and efficient inter -institutional care. AIIMS, New Delhi has also started a 
comprehensive two -shift system morning OPD services and afternoon specialty clinics to 
manage high patient load.  

207 
 
5.3.4 The Committee recommends that the Department adopt a dual strategy of digital 
optimisation and infrastructure expansion  to address the daily footfall of 18,000 –20,000 
patients at AIIMS Delhi. While initiatives such as ORS, TRED, inter -AIIMS referral 
systems, two -shift OPDs, and ABDM integration have been acknowledged by the 
Committee, the Department should prioritise capacity augmentation and early 
completion of ongoing projects at AIIMS Delhi and its extended Jhajjar campus.  
5.3.5 The Committee recommends that to ensure optimal utilization of tertiary care 
institutions like AIIMS and preve nt excessive patient load, it may be considered to 
strengthen a structured referral -based healthcare delivery system. Under such a 
framework, tertiary care hospitals like AIIMS would primarily manage patients 
requiring advanced diagnostics, specialized int erventions, and complex 
multidisciplinary care. Common and uncomplicated conditions should ideally be 
managed at primary and secondary healthcare facilities, supported by clear referral 
guidelines and effective triage mechanisms. Strengthening the infrastr ucture, specialist 
availability, and diagnostic capabilities at district and sub -district hospitals, along with 
implementing robust referral and reverse -referral pathways, would help ensure 
appropriate patient distribution across levels of care. Such measu res would enable 
tertiary institutions to focus on complex clinical care, research, and high -quality 
postgraduate medical training while improving efficiency and accessibility across the 
healthcare system. 
5.3.6 In parallel, the Department should undertake a review study of adjoining regions 
to assess patient load and healthcare gaps , and consider establishing satellite centres of 
AIIMS in Delhi‘s periphery and nearby regions to decentralise  routine and moderately 
complex care, reserving the Delhi main AIIM S campus for high -end tertiary 
procedures. This dual approach will reduce overcrowding, improve access, and 
strengthen equitable healthcare delivery across the National Capital Region. In this 
context, the feasibility of utilising the 400 acres of centrall y acquired land at Badarpur 
may also be examined for setting up of another AIIMS with separate administrative 
structure, particularly to serve patients from Delhi, Haryana, and Uttar Pradesh. 
5.3.7 Given the availability of unutilised land parcels and ongo ing projects such as the 
RAK OPD Block, Mother and Child Block, Super -Specialty OPD at Masjid Moth, and 
the 150 -bedded Critical Care Block under PM -ABHIM, the Committee urges the 
Department to adopt a phased expansion plan with dedicated faculty recruitmen t and 
infrastructure strengthening . Additionally, the upcoming AIIMS at Rewari, Haryana, 
scheduled for completion in December 2026, should be leveraged to reduce referral 
pressure on AIIMS Delhi. These measures will enhance patient access, reduce 
overcrowding, and ensure equitable distribution of advanced healthcare services in the 
National Capital Region. 
5.3.8 On a specific query on steps taken to address infrastructure gaps and staff shortages at 
AIIMS Guwahati, the Department stated that the AIIMS, Guwa hati is a 750 -bedded hospital 
functional with teaching learning  & research, IPD/OPD facilities, emergency & trauma, 

208 
 
diagnostic facilities, various Specialty and Super - Specialty Departments, AMRIT pharmacy, 
Jan Aushadhi Kendra etc.

Orthopedics, Neurology & Neurosurgery, Pediatrics & Pediatric Surgery, Psychiatry, Burn &

Department to adopt a phased expansion plan under PMSSY, ensuring early completion

Plan to strengthen tertiary healthcare capacity in Tripura by bui lding upon the
