improving equitable access to advanced healthcare for patients across Tripura and 
neighbouring states. 
5.3.14 On a concern raised about steps taken to improve patient experience and staff 
behaviour at AIIMS Delhi, the Department stated that as far as AIIMS Hospital, New Delhi is 
concerned, to improve the first point of contact staff -patient interactions and make it a 
pleasant experience for them, the Department of Hospital Administration has undertaken this 
initiative where the Front -Line Workers of the New RAK OPD undergo focused training 
program on select soft skills essential for their r oles as Patient Care Managers (PCM), Patient 
Care Coordinators (PCC), and Data Entry Operators (DEO). The training curriculum covers 
four skill areas Self -management, Communication, Empathy and Dealing with Angry 
Individuals designed to enhance the partici pant‘s ability to build emotional resilience and 
deliver patient -centred care. Further, the entire program anchored on the core principles of 
healthcare encapsulated in 3S framework Sparsh (compassionate touch), Sanchar (clear and 
respectful communication) and Samanubhuti (empathy). 
5.3.15 The Committee expects the Department to intensify measures to improve patient 
experience and staff behaviour at AIIMS Delhi, particularly during night -time 
emergency services when patient vulnerability is highest. The Com mittee urges the 
Department to institutionalise regular, mandatory training modules for all categories of 
staff, backed by periodic performance audits and patient feedback mechanisms. 
 
5.3.16 The Committee has been apprised that one of the reasons for r esignation of faculty is 
prefering to hometown and exploring opportunities in private sector.  
 
5.3.17 The Committee, keeping in view the high attrition rate in AIIMS , recommends 
the Department to introduce a structured inter -AIIMS transfer policy to allow do ctors 
to relocate closer to their hometowns, thereby reducing attrition and improving 
retention. While existing measures such as academic allowances, research funding, and 
housing facilities are valuable, enabling faculty mobility across AIIMS institutions  will 
directly address relocation concerns and provide flexibility without loss of seniority or 

210 
 
service benefits. This approach, combined with accelerated recruitment and 
strengthened career progression pathways, will help retain talent within the AIIMS 
system while ensuring equitable distribution of qualified doctors across regions. 
 
5.3.18 On a query regarding adequate infrastructure and practical training facilities, such as 
postmortem units, in newly established AIIMS institutions including AIIMS Rishik esh, the 
Department provided that new AIIMS are equipped with the necessary infrastructure and 
well-established practical training facilities including Simulation Labs to support both 
medical and non-medical academic programs through a robust, practice -oriented pedagogical 
approach. All AIIMS are mandated  to have Department of Forensics along -with a mortuary. 
The details of Hospital Departments, Medical Support Services and other facilities are 
included in the Master Plan and Detailed Project Report for eac h AIIMS which is 
standardised . 
 
5.3.19 The Committee notes the submission of the Department that all newly established 
All India Institute of Medical Sciences institutions, including AIIMS Rishikesh, are 
planned with standardized infrastructure under the approved Master Plan and Detailed 
Project Report, including departments of forensic medicine, mortuary facilities and 
simulation laboratories for practical training. While acknowledging the existence of 
such provisions on paper, the Committee recommends th at the Department put in place 
a periodic monitoring and evaluation mechanism to ensure that these facilities are fully 
functional, adequately equipped and effectively utilized for practical training, 
particularly in critical areas such as forensic medicin e and postmortem services, so as to 
maintain uniform standards of medical education and training across all AIIMS 
institutions. 
 
5.4 FOOD SAFETY AND STANDARDS AUTHORITY OF INDIA 
5.4.1 On a query on the issue that ch ronic kidney diseases are increasing due to misuse of 
preservatives in packaged food and action taken by FSSAI in this regard, the Department 
stated that FSSAI is taking the following measures to address the issues raised: 
 
a) FSSAI has limited the presence of additives (including preservatives) in food 
products by notifying the Food Safety and Standards (Food Products Standards and 
Food Additives) Regulations, 2011 which are in harmonization with global 
standards like Codex.  
b) Additives permitted in various food categories along with their usage limi ts are 
prescribed in the Appendix A of the above -mentioned regulations. Risk assessment 
is carried out at international level by bodies like Joint FAO Expert Committee on 
Food Additives (JECFA) and European Food Safety Authority (EFSA).  Additives 
are permitted in food by FSSAI only when risk assessments establish the safety of 
the additives.  
c) FSSAI through State/ UTs and its regional offices has stepped up enforcement that is 
primarily done through the state government and conduct regular surveillance, 

211 
 
monitoring, inspection and random sampling of food products to check compliance of 
the standards laid down under Food Safety and Standards Act, 2006, Rules and 
Regulations made thereunder. 
d) Annual mandatory inspections and Third -Party Audits are mandatory for FBOs 
dealing in high-risk product categories. 
5.4.2 The Committee recommends that the Department, through FSSAI, intensify 
efforts to curb the misuse of preservatives in packaged food, given the rising incidence of 
chronic kidney diseases. While regulatory  limits on additives are already harmonised 
with Codex standards and enforcement mechanisms such as surveillance, random 
sampling, and third -party audits are in place, the Committee urges the Department to 
strengthen risk -based monitoring and targeted enfo rcement drives for high -risk food 
categories. Additionally, the Committee recommends enhancing public awareness 
campaigns on the health risks of excessive preservative consumption, coupled with 
stricter penalties for non-compliance by Food Business Operators. 
5.5 REGULATION OF QUACKS PRACTICING MEDICINE 
 
5.5.1 On a query to control the rising number of quacks practicing medicine, particularly in 
North India,  the Department submitted that t he Clinical Establishments (Registration and 
Regulation) Act, 2010 (C EA, 2010) provides for mandatory registration and regulation of all 
clinical establishments, including private hospitals, clinics and diagnostic centres, in the 
States and Union Territories which have adopted the Act. Under the Act, no person can run a 
clinical establishment without valid registration, and only qualified and duly registered 
medical practitioners are permitted to practice. The Act empowers the State/UT Governments 
to enforce compliance through designated authorities. At the district level, t he District 
Collector/District Magistrate, who functions as the District Registering Authority (DRA) 
under the Act in many States/UTs, is responsible for monitoring implementation, ensuring 
registration of establishments, conducting inspections, and initia ting action against 
unregistered or illegally operating entities including quacks.  
 
5.5.2 The Committee is of considered view that the Department must strengthen 
enforcement against quack practitioners by ensuring strict implementation of the 
Clinical Establishments Act, 2010 in States and Union Territories, particularly in North 
India where the problem is acute. The Committee recommends the Department to 
institute regular inspections, coordinated drives, and public reporting mechanisms to 
identify and pen alize unauthorized practitioners. Further, the Committee recommends 
enhancing coordination between State Medical Councils, District Authorities, and law 
enforcement agencies to ensure that only duly qualified and registered medical 
professionals are permitted to practice.  
 
5.6 STRENGTHENING PREVENTIVE CARE 
 
5.6.1 On the issue of strengthening preventive public health systems to manage seasonal 
outbreaks like malaria, especially in high -risk urban areas, the Department submitted that in 
2015, a total of 155 districts were High Burden Districts with Annual Parasite Incidence (API: 

212 
 
cases per 1000 population per year) of more than 1, which declined to 32 in 2025. National 
Malaria Elimination Programme under National Center for Vector Borne Diseases Control 
focuses on  strengthening of surveillance, early diagnosis & complete treatment, Integrated 
Vector Management and IEC/BCC activities, across the country including the high burden 
districts.  In urban areas, inter -sectoral convergence and involvement of Municipa lity is 
ensured for effective control of Malaria.  Malaria transmission intensifies during the monsoon 
season, which creates favourable conditions for vector breeding. Awareness campaigns are 
organized on World Malaria Day (25 th April) to strengthen IEC/BC C activities and review 
preparedness for the upcoming transmission season. Additionally, Anti -Malaria Month is 
observed in June to sustain the momentum of awareness campaigns and reinforce preventive 
and control measures during the peak transmission period. 
 
5.6.2 The Committee is of the firm view that the Department must strengthen 
preventive public health systems to proactively manage seasonal outbreaks of diseases 
like malaria, particularly in high -risk urban areas and undertake immediate measure s 
to contain and eliminate the outbreak then and there. The Committee, therefore , urges 
the Department to institutionalise year -round surveillance, rapid diagnostic capacity, 
and integrated vector management with special focus on urban slums and densely 
populated areas. Further, the Committee recommends scaling up community awareness 
drives beyond seasonal observances, ensuring sustained behavioural change in 
sanitation and mosquito-control practices.  
5.6.3 On a query regarding efforts taken for immunization for m igrants, the Department 
stated that the MoHFW has taken several steps for nationwide scaling up of the Universal 
Immunization Programme for beneficiaries including the migrant community. The Universal 
Immunization Programme is an equitable vaccination prog ram for all categories of the 
population irrespective of socio -economic status, providing 11 vaccines for 12 vaccine 
preventable diseases, free of cost to all eligible beneficiaries. The sessions sites are held in 
fixed health facilities like in the primary health centre, sub health centre and outreach sessions 
in areas such as urban slums, remote areas, migrant settlements etc. Various outreach 
programs are held at the migrant habitations like brick kilns, agricultural farms, construction 
sites and VHSND in villages for providing routine immunization to migrant beneficiaries.  
5.6.4 The Universal Immunization Program is supported by a single digital platform UWIN, 
which is a national repository of immunization services on a single digital platform, replacin g 
paper formats with digitized records, recording all vaccination events of eligible beneficiaries, 
including migrants and can be accessed anywhere in India, ensuring continuity of the 
vaccination schedule (due doses) where the migrant families have relocated.  
5.6.5 The Committee recommends that the Department intensify efforts to ensure 
equitable immunization coverage for migrant populations, who often face 
mobility-related barriers in accessing routine services. While the Universal 
Immunization Programme  already provides free vaccines nationwide, the Committee 
urges the Department to strengthen micro -planning, targeted outreach at high -density 
migrant habitations, and real -time tracking through the U -WIN digital platform. The 

213 
 
Committee also recommends enh ancing inter -state coordination and portability of 
immunization records, so that migrant families can seamlessly continue vaccination 
schedules after relocation.  
5.6.6 On being asked about the STEMI -based Heart Attack Management Programme under 
NHM and an y plan to introduce a similar structured programme for stroke management, the 
Department stated that STEMI -based heart attack management is implemented under  the 
CVD component of NP -NCD through a hub -and-spoke model to ensure timely diagnosis, 
stabilization and referral of acute cardiac emergencies. The programme supports 
strengthening of infrastructure including operationalisation of Cardiac Care Units (CCUs) at 
District Hospitals and strengthening of referral linkages with higher centres. Peripheral 
facilities such as PHCs, CHCs and Sub -District Hospitals are equipped for early recognition 
through ECG, initial stabilization and thrombolysis (where indicated), followed by referral to 
designated hubs such as District Hospitals and Medical Colleges with advan ced cardiac care 
facilities.  
5.6.7 Stroke is covered under the CVD component of NP -NCD through a comprehensive 
approach encompassing prevention, early detection, acute management and long -term care. 
The programme provides population -based screening of ind ividuals aged 30 years and above 
for hypertension and diabetes at Ayushman Bharat – Health and Wellness Centres to reduce 
stroke risk, along with IEC activities promoting tobacco cessation, salt reduction, healthy diet, 
physical activity and control of blo od pressure and blood sugar. Stroke care is delivered 
through a hub -and-spoke model wherein PHCs, CHCs and District Hospitals ensure early 
recognition, stabilization, CT-based diagnosis and thrombolysis (where available), and timely 
referral, while Medical  Colleges and tertiary centres provide advanced diagnostics including 
24×7 CT/MRI facilities, mechanical thrombectomy (where available), dedicated stroke units, 
multidisciplinary management and comprehensive rehabilitation services. Follow-up of stroke 
patients for secondary prevention and long-term risk factor control is undertaken through NP -
NCD clinics.  
5.6.8 The Committee emphasizes that the Department must strengthen nationwide 
implementation of the STEMI -based Heart Attack Management Programme under 
NHM while ensuring uniform standards of care across States through the 
hub-and-spoke model. The Committee further urges the Department to develop a 
structured, nationwide stroke management programme on similar lines, with dedicated 
stroke units, 24×7 imagi ng facilities, and referral linkages for advanced interventions, 
and the setting up of Mobile Stroke Units at District Hospitals to ensure timely diagnosis 
and treatment in underserved areas. This dual approach, consolidating STEMI care and 
institutionalising structured stroke management, will enhance early diagnosis, timely 
stabilization, and equitable access to advanced emergency care, thereby reducing 
mortality and long-term disability from acute cardiac and cerebrovascular events. 
5.6.9 On being asked t hat given rising longevity and pneumonia burden among elderly 
populations, whether the Government considering introducing an Adult Immunization 
Programme e.g. pneumococcal Vaccine for citizens aged 70+ with comorbidities, the 

214 
 
Department replied that respiratory diseases remain significant contributors for morbidity and 
mortality among older persons in India. Findings from the Longitudinal Ageing Study in India 
(LASI)7, which is first nationwide population -based survey in India to assess lung function, 
highlight the declining respiratory health associated with ageing. The survey indicates that 
nearly 49% of individuals aged 45 years and above have either restrictive or obstructive lung 
disease. Approximately 40% have restrictive lung disease, while about 9% h ave obstructive 
lung disease (including mild and moderate -severe forms). The findings underscore the high 
prevalence of compromised lung function among older adults, which increases susceptibility 
to respiratory infections, including pneumonia, particularl y in those with comorbid 
conditions. 
5.6.10 At present, a universal Adult Immunization Programme for elderly persons has not 
been introduced under the public health system. Inclusion  of any vaccine in the national 
immunization schedule requires comprehensive evaluation of epidemiological evidence, cost -
effectiveness, financial sustainability, operational feasibility, and recommendations/approval 
by the Central Drugs Standard Control Organization (CDSCO) under the regulatory 
framework of the Drugs and Cosmet ics Act and the National Technical Advisory Group on 
Immunization (NTAGI). As of now, adequate policy approvals for nationwide public rollout 
of routine pneumococcal vaccination for all persons aged 70 years and above have not been 
accorded. The Government  of India continues to address pneumonia prevention and 
respiratory health among the elderly through existing public health programmes. The National 
Programme for Health Care of the Elderly (NPHCE) provides preventive, promotive, curative 
and rehabilitativ e services for older persons at various levels of healthcare delivery. 
Additionally, under Ayushman Bharat Comprehensive Primary Health Care (CPHC) through 
Health & Wellness Centres, a continuum of care is provided for non-communicable diseases. 
5.6.11 The Committee recommends  that the Department evaluate the feasibility of 
introducing a structured Adult Immunization Programme targeting elderly populations, 
particularly citizens aged 60+ with comorbidities, in view of the rising pneumonia 
burden and declini ng respiratory health highlighted by Longitudinal Ageing Study in 
India (LASI) findings. While elderly care is currently addressed through NPHCE and 
Ayushman Bharat CPHC, the Committee urges the Department to initiate expert 
consultations with National Technical Advisory Group on Immunization (NTAGI)  and 
CDSCO to assess epidemiological evidence, cost-effectiveness, and operational feasibility 
of pneumococcal vaccination for high-risk groups. 
 
5.6.12 On being asked about the reason for the delay in approval of indigenous cervical 
cancer vaccines by Drug Controller General of India despite their potential affordability, the 
Department submitted that the Central Drugs Standard Control Organisation (CDSCO) has 
granted permission for manufacturing and marketing of Quadrivalent Human Papilloma Virus 
(Serotypes 6, 11, 16 & 18) Vaccine (Recombinant) (qHPV), a double dose vaccine to M/s 
Serum Institute of India Pvt. Ltd., Pune for prevention of the disease caused by Human 
Papilloma Virus types 6, 11, 16 and 18. The New Drugs and Clinical Trials Rules, 2019 under 
                                                           
 

215 
 
which vaccines are regulated, contains provisions for accelerated/ expedited approval process 
in certain situations.  
 
5.6.13 The Committee recommends that the Department  to expedite the rollout and 
public ava ilability of indigenous cervical cancer vaccines, given their potential 
affordability and impact on reducing disease burden. While CDSCO has already 
granted approval for manufacturing and marketing of the quadrivalent HPV vaccine 
under the New Drugs and Cl inical Trials Rules, 2019, the Committee urges the 
Department to ensure timely procurement, inclusion in the national immunization 
schedule, and awareness campaigns to maximize uptake. 
 
5.7 MEDICAL EDUCATION 
5.7.1 On a query related to address the demand -supply gap in medical education, 
considering the large number of NEET aspirants each year, the Department stated that in 
order to address shortage of medical professionals in the country, the Government is 
implementing following schemes: 
a) Centrally Sponsored Scheme for establishment of new medical college by upgrading 
district/ referral hospital under which 137 new medical colleges are functional out of 
157 approved medical colleges. 
b) Centrally Sponsored Scheme for strengthening/ upgradation of existing State 
Government/Central Government Medical Colleges to increase MBBS and PG seats. 
c) Upgradation of Government Medical Colleges by construction of Super Specialty 
Blocks/Trauma care centres, etc. 
5.7.2 There are now 22 publicly funded and leading All India Instit ute of Medical Sciences 
institutes. There are 2,086 medical colleges and medical education seats for the MBBS 
(Bachelor of Medicine and Bachelor of Surgery) increased by 151% (from 51,348 to 128976), 
and postgraduate seats grew by 173% (from 31,185 to 8502 0). The Family Adoption 
Programme (FAP) has been incorporated into the MBBS curriculum to provide equitable 
healthcare access to rural population. It also helps in educating families about ongoing 
government health programmes. Further, the District Residen cy Programme (DRP) notified 
by the National Medical Commission (NMC) provides for a compulsory three months posting 
cum training of PG medical students at District Hospitals as a part of the course curriculum. 
DRP benefits the public by strengthening healthcare delivery in rural and underserved areas. 
5.7.3 The Committee recommends that the Department to continue expanding medical 
education capacity while simultaneously addressing the demand -supply gap created by 
the large number of NEET aspirants each year. The Committee urges the Department to 
adopt a multi-pronged strategy . This should include further expansion of government 
medical colleges in underserved regions , including the North -East region like the States 
of Arunachal Pradesh and Nagaland , strengthening faculty availability, and enhancing 
infrastructure for super -specialty training. These measures will ensure that the 
adequate increase in medical seats translates into equitable access and quality training.  

216 
 
5.7.4 The Committee has been apprised of t hat under the present regulations of the 
National Medical Commission, MBBS students are currently permitted a maximum of 
four attempts to clear the First Professional Examination. Considering the academic 
intensity of foundational subjects such as anatomy,  physiology, and biochemistry, this 
restriction can be overly stringent for many students adjusting to the demanding nature 
of medical education. The Committee, therefore, suggested that the permissible limit be 
increased to six attempts, while ensuring that students complete the entire MBBS course 
within a maximum period of ten years from the date of admission. This balanced 
approach will maintain academic standards while providing a more humane and 
student-friendly framework, preventing capable students f rom being forced to 
discontinue medical education due to early academic setbacks. 
5.8 STRENGTHENING OF HEALTH INFRASTRUCTURE AND RESOURCES 
5.8.1 On a query related to the essentiality of profession in Hospital Management, the 
Department submitted that unde r NHM, support is provided for establishment of Health care 
facilities like SHC AAM, PHC AAM, CHC, SDH and DH. As a part of Flexipool, the funds 
are provided to the States/UTs. Further, Pradhan Mantri Ayushman Bharat Health 
Infrastructure Mission (PM -ABHIM) is a Centrally Sponsored Scheme (CSS) with some 
Central Sector Components (CS) which has an outlay of Rs. 64,180 Crores for the scheme 
period (FY2021-22 to FY2025-26). The scheme was introduced to strengthen the rural health 
infrastructure. Health is a S tate Subject and thus HR management and policies falls under the 
purview of State Government. States are encouraged time and again to introduce Public 
Health Management Cadres. Thus, segregating the Public Health, Health Management and 
Clinical roles. 
5.8.2 The Committee desires that the Department should intensify efforts to strengthen 
healthcare infrastructure in Tier -2 and Tier -3 cities, where higher mortality rates 
highlight critical gaps in service delivery. While NHM and PM -ABHIM are already 
supporting the establishment of sub -centres, PHCs, CHCs, sub -district hospitals, and 
critical care blocks, the Committee recommends the Department to ensure timely 
completion and operationalisation of these facilities with adequate staffing and 
equipment. The Commi ttee also recommends that States be encouraged to 
institutionalise Public Health Management Cadres to professionalize hospital 
administration. Segregating clinical, public health, and management roles will improve 
efficiency, accountability, and patient outcomes. 
5.8.3 The expansion of premier institutions such as the All India Institute of Medical 
Sciences (AIIMS) has significantly strengthened tertiary healthcare in India. However, 
establishing AIIMS in every region requires substantial financial investme nt and long 
gestation periods. A pragmatic approach would be to establish Regional Medical 
Institutes (RMIs) in underserved regions as advanced tertiary hospitals with strong 
teaching and research facilities. These institutes can address region -specific healthcare 
needs and significantly improve access to specialized treatment. States such as Tamil 
Nadu demonstrate how institutions like Madras Medical College evolved in to large 

217 
 
tertiary referral centres serving multiple districts. By first developing RMIs and later 
upgrading the most successful institutions into AIIMS -level centres, the government can 
ensure faster healthcare expansion, better regional equity, and sustainable institutional 
growth. 
5.8.4 On being asked about the timeline for establishing tra uma centres in every district, the 
Department stated that t ill now 196 Trauma Care Facilities (TCF) have been sanctioned in 
11th and 12th Five Year Plan, covering all the States, under the scheme National Programme 
for Prevention and Management of Trauma a nd Burn Injuries (NPPMT&BI). 14 Medical 
Colleges/ Hospitals have been established as Centre of Excellence (CoE) under the 
programme and funds have been released for strengthening the emergency, trauma and burn 
care services. CoE provides high quality advan ced comprehensive care to all emergency, 
trauma and burn patients, Hand holding for the other medical colleges and district hospitals 
for clinical care, Quality Rehabilitation services for post injury management, academic & 
research, development of training courses and modules. These institutes provide leadership to 
local and regional Emergency Medical Service (EMS) System in terms of innovation, public 
awareness campaigns and periodic feedback. Additional Medical Colleges/ Hospitals will be 
selected as Cen tre of Excellence in 2026 -2027 to cover more regions in the country. These 
CoEs will also play a major role to upgrade/enhance the capacity and capability of other 
surrounding Trauma and Burn facility to handle critical patients also. Capacity Building is 
done through Trauma & Burn care management trainings in CoEs, and Dr. R.M.L. Hospital. 
Standard Treatment Guidelines for Trauma care management and Standard Treatment 
Guidelines for Management of Burns have been developed. Emergency and trauma care 
services is under expansion to other district hospitals as per the budget announcement para 88 
of Budget 2026 -27: Emergencies expose families, particularly the poor and vulnerable, to 
unexpected expenditure. We will strengthen and increase these capacities by 50%  in District 
capacities by establishing Emergency and Trauma Care Centres. 
5.8.5 The Committee recommends that the Department accelerate the establishment of 
trauma centres in every district to reduce the burden on major city hospitals. While 196 
Trauma Care Facilities and 14 Centres of Excellence have already been sanctioned, and 
expansion is planned under Budget 2026 -27 to increase district capacities by 50%, the 
Committee urges the Department to lay down a clear phased implementation timeline 
with annual  targets to ensure timely coverage across all districts. The Committee 
recommends leveraging Centres of Excellence for capacity building, standardized 
treatment protocols, and referral linkages so that district -level trauma centres are fully 
equipped to ha ndle emergencies. Th e structured expansion, backed by monitoring and 
accountability mechanisms, will strengthen emergency care nationwide and reduce 
preventable mortality from trauma and burn injuries. 
5.8.6 On a specific query related to address service g aps at Community Health Centres, 
particularly the shifting of gynaecologists and paediatricians, the Department relied that under 
NHM, support is provided for engagement of contractual workers. More than 5 lakhs health 
workers like MPW, Staff Nurse, Lab Te chnicians, Medical Officers, Specialists and CHOs 
are supported under NHM. The supply of Human Resources for Health (HRH) has shown 

218 
 
significant improvement over the past  decade. This expansion in pre -service education 
capacity has gradually translated into  better availability of health workforce in public health 
facilities. With the expansion of infrastructure across States/ UTs, NHM has continued to 
support filling up of HR gaps against IPHS norms. Overall availability of these key HRH has 
increased over the years, especially in the regular cadre. The number of UG and PG seats also 
has increased in the past few years. Also, under NHM, support is also provided for creation of 
DNB seats, to increase the availability of doctors. Specilaized training programs l ike LSAS 
training, BEmONC (Basic Emergency Obstetric and New Born Care) and CEmONC 
(Comprehensive Emergency Obstetric and Newborn Care including C Sections) are provided 
through NHM to address specialist shortages. Further, some States are providing reserv ation 
to in -service candidates who have mandatorily served for defined number of years in rural 
areas under the State Quota Seats in NEET PG.   
5.8.7 On an another query related to strengthening ambulance services (102/108) and 
establishing blood storage u nits at Rural Hospitals, the Department replied that u nder the 
National Health Mission (NHM), the Government of India has undertaken various initiative to 
strengthen health facilities and service delivery to reduce the maternal mortality across States 
and Union Territories, including in rural and tribal areas which are as follows: 
a) Janani Suraksha Yojana (JSY) is a demand  promotion and conditional cash transfer 
scheme for promoting institutional delivery. 
 
b) Janani Shishu Suraksha Karyakram (JSSK)  entitles all pregnant woman delivering in 
public health institutions to have absolutely free and no expense delivery, including 
caesarean section. The entitlements include free drugs, consumables, free diet during 
stay, free diagnostics, free transportation (to and fr o) (102/108) and free blood 
transfusion, if required. Similar entitlements are also in place for sick infants up to one 
year of age.  
 
c) The Government of India also facilitates alternative modes of ambulance 
transportation in hard -to-reach tribal areas, inc luding boat ambulances, bike 
ambulances, air ambulances, palkis and other locally appropriate transport 
mechanisms to ensure timely access to care. 
 
d) Further, under the National Ambulance Services (NAS) the Government of India 
provides free, 24×7 emergency and patient transport services across States and Union 
Territories. Operated primarily through the toll -free numbers 102 (for pregnant 
women, mothers and sick infants) and 108 (for medical, trauma and other 
emergencies), the service ensures timely access t o public health facilities, particularly 
in rural, remote and tribal areas. These ambulances are equipped with essential life -
saving equipment and trained personnel to provide basic emergency care during 
transit, thereby reducing delays in accessing critic al maternal and emergency health 
services. 

219 
 
5.8.8 The Committee views that the Department must address service gaps , especially 
strengthening women and child healthcare,  at Community Health Centres by ensuring 
rational deployment of specialists  such as gyna ecologists and paediatricians  with 
establishing facilities like ambulance services and blood storage units , even in facilities 
with lower delivery loads, particularly in rural areas, so that broader care availability is 
not compromised. The Committee urges  the Department to guide States to create 
regular posts under IPHS norms  and strengthen retention policies to stabilize specialist 
availability at CHCs. Further, the Committee recommends expanding reservation and 
incentives for in -service candidates servin g in rural areas , alongside scaling up DNB 
seats and targeted training, to build a sustainable pipeline of specialists. The measures 
so undertaken will ensure that CHCs remain adequately staffed, provide comprehensive 
maternal and child health services, an d reduce dependence on district hospitals for 
routine care.  
5.8.9 On being asked about the current status of rollout of Day Care Cancer Centres 
(DCCCs), the Department stated that under the National Programme for Prevention and 
Control of Non -Communicable Diseases (NP-NCD), the establishment of District Day Care 
Cancer Centres (DCCCs) is being implemented  in a phased manner. As per budget 
announcement, 200 DCCs needed to be made functional. In FY 2025 -26, a total of 297 
centres has been approved through t he NHM PIP mechanism. As of date, 479 Day Care 
Cancer Centres are functional across the country. The Committee observes from the 
submission that a total of 479 District Critical Care Centres (DCCCs) are reported to be 
functional across various States/UTs. The highest numbers are in Madhya Pradesh (52), 
Rajasthan (41), Tamil Nadu (38) and West Bengal (38), followed by Gujarat (36), Telangana 
(34) and Odisha (32). Moderate numbers are reported from Bihar (31), Kerala (28) and 
Chhattisgarh (26), while several States/UTs such as Arunachal Pradesh, Chandigarh, 
Jharkhand, Puducherry, Sikkim and Uttar Pradesh have reported no functional DCCCs. Other 
States/UTs have relatively smaller numbers ranging between 1 and 16 centres.  
5.8.10 On a query raised on the basis of a article titled ―Day Care Cancer Centers yet to come 
up in Delhi even a year after announcement‖ published in print media that none of the four 
approved centres in Delhi have been operationalised despite allocation of funds and the 
reasons for non-deployment of the trained staff, reportedly available since July 2025, not yet 
been deployed, the Department stated that Health is a State subject. Operationalisation of 
DCCCs depends on availability of space, completion of civil works, procurement processes 
and staff deployment by the concerned State Government. Delays reported in certain locations 
are attributable to State -level administrative and infrastructural processes. The matter has 
been taken up with the concerned State Government, and progress is revie wed periodically 
based on reports received. Deployment of trained staff is within the purview of the State 
Government. On being asked about the strategy to integrate cancer care with broader Non -
Communicable Disease (NCD) strategies and partnerships (publi c–private, NGOs, academic 
institutions, etc.) being explored to strengthen cancer care delivery, the Department stated that 
based on gap analysis and the stated proposal, the DCCS will be scaled up through NHM PIP 
mechanism subsequent years. Over the next few years as per budget announcement DCCCs 
will be established in all district hospitals. Expansion of DCCCs is considered based on 

220 
 
disease burden, service gaps and proposals received from States/UTs under the PIP process. 
Cancer services under DCCCs are i mplemented within the NP -NCD framework, ensuring 
linkage with population-based screening, District NCD Clinics, treatment services and follow-
up including palliative care. Digital platforms support reporting and monitoring. States/UTs 
may engage with publi c institutions, medical colleges, accredited private facilities and civil 
society organisations as per extant guidelines to augment service delivery.  
5.8.11 The Committee recommends  that the Department ensure the timely 
operationalisation of approved Day Care Cancer Centres (DCCCs)  across all 
States/UTs, with particular attention to regions where centres remain non -functional 
despite approvals and fund allocation, such as Delhi. While 479 centres are reported 
functional against the target of 200, uneven di stribution and delays in staff deployment 
highlight the need for stronger State-level accountability and monitoring mechanisms . 
The Committee recommends integrating DCCCs more closely with the broader 
NP-NCD framework through population-based screening, re ferral linkages, and 
partnerships with medical colleges, accredited private facilities, and NGOs . Establishing 
a clear phased timeline for expansion, coupled with transparent reporting on manpower 
and equipment availability, will ensure equitable access to  cancer care and reduce the 
burden on tertiary hospitals. 
5.9 DISEASE CONTROL 
5.9.1 On certain queries related to malaria elimination programme to strengthen targeted 
interventions in high -burden districts of Tripura, strengthen critical areas such as deng ue 
control, rare disease research, the Department stated that Tripura is a Category -3 State in 
respect of Malaria disease burden, with Annual Parasite Incidence (API: cases per 1000 
population per year) of 1.54 in 2025 which is 37.65% decline as compared to 2024 where API 
was 2.47. The state maintains adequate surveillance in terms of ABER (Annual Blood 
Examination Rate) of >10% (21.65%). Malaria cases have decreased in the state by 37.45% in 
2025 (6091 cases) as compared to 2024 (9738 cases) due to strengt hening of surveillance, 
early diagnosis & complete treatment, Integrated Vector Management and IEC/BCC 
activities. High burden districts in the State of Tripura have decreased to 1 (Dhalai) in 2025 as 
compared to 4 in 2024 (Dhalai, South Tripura, Khowai and Gomati). 
5.9.2 Dengue is a mosquito borne viral disease , mostly self-limiting, but in its severe form 
due to vascular permeability results in hemorrhagic manifestation leads to shock and causes 
deaths. Dengue virus has four serotypes ( DENV 1, 2, 3, 4). All can cause the disease and 
infection by one serotype do not confer protection to the other. Individuals who are infected 
for the second time or more are at greater risk of severe dengue.  There is no specific 
treatment/drug available for Dengue, cases ar e managed symptomatically. Trainings of 
Trainers were conducted for States/UTs to strengthen clinical management and reduce 
deaths. As a result of the cascading trainings, the CFR for Dengue is sustained below 1% 
(National target), which was 0.09% in 2025.  

221 
 
5.9.3 Health is a State subject and the Centre provides support based on the proposals 
submitted by States/UTs in their annual Programme Implementation Plans (PIPs), and funds 
are released in accordance with approved plans. States are encouraged to raise  appropriate 
demands in their PIPs to further expand dialysis services as per needs. T he Government of 
India, through the Integrated Disease Surveillance Programme (IDSP), provides structured 
technical leadership, national surveillance standards, and conti nuous monitoring support to 
States and Union Territories for disease surveillance, outbreak preparedness, and rapid 
response.  
5.9.4 The Committee believes that while the Department has achieved significant 
epidemiological milestones, evidenced by the stee p decline in malaria cases in Tripura and 
the sustained dengue case fatality rate of 0.09% nationally, the existing framework's 
reliance on state -initiated Programme Implementation Plans (PIPs) for critical 
infrastructure risks creating regional disparitie s in healthcare access. The Committee is of 
the view that passive central support is insufficient for addressing acute rural gaps, even 
with the commendable expansions under the Integrated Health Information Platform 
(IHIP). The Committee, therefore, recom mends that the Department immediately 
transition from a responsive to a proactive national mandate, directly standardizing the 
deployment of advanced diagnostic laboratories, Rapid Response Teams (RRTs),  critical 
care units , ambulance services and blood st orage units  across all high -burden and rural 
districts, ensuring that inter -sectoral coordination under the One Health approach is 
uniformly operationalized rather than contingent upon individual state proposals. 
5.9.5 On being asked about the targeted strategies being adopted to address drug -resistant 
TB, the Department stated that u nder TB Mukt Bharat Abhiyan, the Government , to address 
TB, including drug -resistant TB among the vulnerable population across the country, 
implements key strategies like mappi ng of congregate settings & vulnerable population 
including asymptomatic, pro -active screening of vulnerable population by chest x -ray for 
early detection, upfront nucleic acid amplification test (NAAT) for all presumptive TB cases, 
timely treatment initia tion & completion, differentiated TB care for managing high -risk TB 
cases, nutrition support and preventive treatment to eligible vulnerable population. Further, 
the Government, has provided 1,191 portable AI -enabled handheld X-ray machines covering 
all districts of the country & an additional 2,247 more handheld X -ray machines are under 
supply. 9,826 molecular laboratories for conducting NAAT tests across the country have been 
established. These portable equipment‘s have contributed to the scaling up of sc reening of 
vulnerable population for early detection of TB & drug -resistant TB across the country. As 
per the World Health Organization‘s Global TB Report 2025, the incidence rate of TB in 
India has shown a 21% decline from 2015 to 2024, while the mortalit y rate has reduced by 
25% during the same period.  
 
5.9.6 The Committee appreciates the targeted strategies under the TB Mukt Bharat 
Abhiyan to address drug -resistant TB, particularly at the rural hospital level. While 
significant progress has been made th rough universal drug susceptibility testing, 
availability of newer drugs, and decentralized DR -TB centres, the Committee urges the 
Department to ensure full deployment and optimal utilization of portable AI -enabled 

222 
 
diagnostic technologies, such as handheld  X-ray machines and NAAT facilities, across 
all districts. The Committee also recommends greater involvement of private healthcare 
partners in TB detection, diagnosis, notification, and treatment under a strengthened 
public–private partnership framework. Further, integration of portable diagnostics with 
Nikshay tracking, nutrition support, curbing illicit liquor consumption and treatment 
adherence mechanisms should be strengthened, alongside capacity building and training 
of frontline health workers for early detection, diagnosis, and timely referral. 
5.9.7 The Committee notes that portable X -ray systems are being used for rapid and 
accessible chest imaging for early detection of Tuberculosis. The Committee is, however, 
concerned about the influx of cheap de vices, reportedly sourced from countries 
bordering India and marketed as ―Make in India‖, emitting high radiation exposure. 
Considering the high patient load in Government healthcare facilities, the Committee 
recommends that all deployed portable X -Ray sys tems adhere to the highest available  
global safety and precision standards of 0.4mm or smaller focal spot of X- Ray tube with 
adjustable tube current of minimum 5 mA to a maximum of 8 mA so as to ensure 
responsible use of diagnostic radiation for improved patient safety. 
5.10 CANCER PREVENTION 
 
5.10.1 On a query about the steps taken to strengthen national cancer screening programmes, 
particularly for breast, cervical, and ovarian cancers, in high -burden states like Madhya 
Pradesh, the Department replied t hat under the National Programme for Prevention and 
Control of Non Communicable Diseases (NP -NCD) as part of the National Health Mission 
(NHM), 770 District NCD Clinics, 364 District Day Care Cancer Centres (DCCC) and 6,410 
NCD clinics at Community Health Centres have been set up across the country. As per the 
announcement of Union Budget 2025 -26, for financial year 2025 -26, 297 Day Care Cancer 
Centres (DCCCs) have been approved for establishment across the country to strengthen 
decentralized cancer care by  aligning infrastructure development with patient needs. Under 
Strengthening of Tertiary Care Cancer Facilities Scheme, 19 State Cancer Institutes (SCI) and 
20 Tertiary Care Cancer Centres (TCCC) have been established across the country including 
1 SCI and 1 TCCC in State of Madhya Pradesh. 
5.10.2 A population-based initiative for prevention, control and screening for common NCDs 
i.e., diabetes, hypertension including three common cancers (oral cancer, breast cancer and 
cervical cancer) has been rolled out in the country under NHM and also as a part of 
Comprehensive Primary Health Care. As per the data available on the NCD Portal, the 
following progress has been recorded in the state of Madhya Pradesh, up to 21 December 
2025:-

5.10.3 The Committee is of the strong view that the Department must intensify efforts to 
strengthen national cancer screening programmes, particularly for breast, cervical, and 
oral cancers, in high -burden states such as Madhya Pradesh. The Committee, in this 
regard, recommends prioritizing capacity building of frontline health workers, 
expansion of screening infrastructure at Ayushman Arogya Mandirs, and integration of 
tertiary care facilities with district -level programmes. A clear monitoring framework 
with state-wise reporting on screened, diagnosed, and treated cases will abridge service 
gaps, uniform coverage, reduce late -stage detection, and strengthen cancer control in 
high-burden regions.  
5.10.4 On a suggestion of considering launching a structured ―Mother and Daughter Cancer 
Prevention Programme‖ combining mammography screening for wo men above 40 and 
cervical cancer vaccination for adolescent girls, the Department stated that HPV vaccine has 
been launched nationwide by the Hon'ble Prime Minister, on 28th February 2026 for girls 
aged 14yrs of age for the prevention of cervical cancer. T his vaccine will be provided free of 
cost in all public health facilities across the country. 
5.10.5 The Committee appreciates that the Department build upon the nationwide 
rollout of the HPV vaccine for adolescent girls by considering to the suggestion fo r the 
launch of a structured ―Mother and Daughter Cancer Prevention Programme 
(MDCPP).‖  The Committee recommends that MDCPP should combine mammography 
screening for women above 40 years with the ongoing cervical cancer vaccination drive 
for adolescent gir ls, thereby creating a comprehensive, life‑course approach to cancer 
prevention. Such an integrated initiative would strengthen early detection and 
prevention strategies, reduce the burden of breast and cervical cancers in high‑risk 
populations, and promot e awareness through family‑based outreach. By aligning 
vaccination and screening under a unified framework, the Department can ensure 
greater coverage, continuity of care, and long‑term impact on women‘s health outcomes 
nationwide. 
5.10.6 On a specific que ry about the status of Cancer Care Hospitals approved in states like 
Assam and Maharashtra and reasons for shifting the approved facility from Dhule to Jalgaon, 
the Department stated that health is a State Subject.  As far as MoH&FW is concerned, this 
Ministry supports States under the Tertiary Care Cancer Schemeto strengthen advanced 
cancer treatment facilities across the country. At the time of its inception, the scheme aimed 
to establish and support 19 State Cancer Institutes (SCIs) and 20 Tertiary Care Cancer Centres 
(TCCCs) to provide comprehensive cancer care services, including radiotherapy, 
chemotherapy, and surgical oncology. Under this scheme, one State Cancer Institute has been 
recommended in Assam at Guwahati Medical College & Hospital, which is providing 
advanced cancer treatment services such as radiotherapy, chemotherapy, and surgical 
procedures. Under this scheme, three institutions in Maharashtra were recommended: 
1. RashtrasantTukdoji Regional Cancer Hospital & Research Centre (TCCC) – Nagpur 
2. Vivekanand Foundation & Research Centre (TCCC) – Latur 
3. Government Medical College, Aurangabad (SCI) 

224 
 
 
5.10.7 The Committee desires that the Department must ensure effective 
operationalisation of cancer care facilities approved under the Tertiary Care Cancer 
Scheme, particularly the State Cancer Institute at Guwahati Medical College in Assam 
and the three institutions in Maharashtra. The Committee urges the Department to 
establish a robust monitoring framework to track progress on infrastructure, manpower 
deployment, and equipment installation. The Committee recommends that reasons for

coordination with  State Governments and ensuring timely completi on of sanctioned

5.11 NATIONAL MEDICAL COMMISSION  AND OTHER REGULATORY 
INSTITUTIONS 
 
5.11.1 On a specific query on strategy of the Government to ensure quality training and avoid 
future unemployment or under -skilled surgical specialists, as India rapidly increases MBBS 
and PG seats under the oversight of the National Medical Commission, the Department stated 
that the Govern ment of India is systematically expanding medical education to keep pace. 
Over the  past 11 years, the Government of India made gains in making quality medical 
education more accessible. There are now 23 publicly funded and leading All India Institute 
of Me dical Sciences institutes, including the first -ever AIIMS in the northeastern area in 
Assam. There are 2,086 medical colleges, including 818 allopathy, 323 dental and 942 
AYUSH institutions. Medical education seats for the MBBS (Bachelor of Medicine and 
Bachelor of Surgery) increased by 151% (from 51,348 to 128976), and postgraduate seats 
grew by 173% (from 31,185 to 85020). DNB qualification has been recognized for 
appointment as teaching faculty to take care of shortage of faculty. 
5.11.2 The Committee st rongly recommends that the Department must ensure that the 
rapid expansion of MBBS and PG seats under the oversight of the National Medical 
Commission is matched with robust quality assurance mechanisms to safeguard training 
standards and prevent future un employment or under -skilled specialists. In parallel to 
the establishment of new medical colleges, seat expansion, and recognition of DNB 
qualifications, the Committee urges the Department to strengthen faculty development, 
clinical exposure, and skill -based training modules  across institutions. Further, the 
Committee recommends instituting periodic accreditation, competency -based 
assessments, and structured career planning  to align medical education output with 
national health system needs. By balancing ex pansion with quality and workforce 
planning, the Department can ensure that the growing pool of medical graduates and 
specialists are well -trained, employable, and capable of delivering high -quality 
healthcare services nationwide. 
5.11.3 On being asked wha t incentives are being considered to encourage MBBS doctors to 
serve in rural areassuch as salary enhancement under NHM or additional marks in NEET -PG 
for rural service, the Department stated that the Government of India has taken number of 

225 
 
initiatives in the form of incentives and honorarium to the medical professionals for 
encouraging better service delivery in rural and remote areas in the country, which include: 
a) Hard Area Allowance to specialist doctors for serving in rural and remote areas so that 
they find it attractive to serve in public health facilities in such areas. 
b) Honorarium to Gynecologists/ Emergency Obstetric Care (EmoC) trained, 
Pediatricians & Anesthetist/ Life Saving Anaesthesia Skills (LSAS) trained doctors to 
increase availability of specialists for conducting Cesarean Sections in rural & remote 
area. 
c) Special incentives for doctors, incentive for ANM for ensuring timely ANC checkup 
and recording, incentives for conducting Adolescent Reproductive and Sexual Health 
activities. 
d) States are al so allowed to offer negotiable salary to attract specialist including 
flexibility in strategies such as ―You Quote We Pay‖. 
e) Non-monetary incentives such as preferential admission in postgraduate courses for 
staff serving in difficult areas and improving ac commodation arrangement in rural 
areas have also been introduced under NHM. 
f) Multi-skilling of doctors is supported under NHM to overcome the shortage of 
specialists. Skill upgradation of existing HR is another major strategy under the 
National Rural Health  Mission (NRHM) for achieving improvement in health 
outcomes. 
Further, some States are providing reservation to in -service candidates who have mandatorily 
served for defined number of years in rural areas under the State Quota Seats in NEET PG. 
 
5.11.4 The Committee is of the view that the De partment should offer competitive pay 
other incentives under NHM to encourage MBBS doctors to serve in rural and remote 
areas. While monetary measures such as hard area allowances, honoraria, and 
negotiable salaries have been introduced, however, the Committee urges the Department 
to expand non -monetary incentives including preferential admission in postgraduate 
courses, additional marks or reservation in NEET -PG for rural service, and improved 
living and accommodation f acilities. Further, the Committee recommends scaling up 
multi-skilling and continuous professional development programmes to enhance career 
prospects for doctors serving in difficult areas. A balanced package of financial, 
academic, and professional incent ives will ensure sustained availability of qualified 
medical professionals in rural hospitals. 
 
5.11.5 The Committee came across from a print media article in December 2025 that 
applications for new medical colleges was invited between Dec 29 and Jan 28, a s announced 
by the National Medical Commission (NMC) for the academic year 2026 -27. Under the 
revised norms, the NMC significantly increased the application and processing fees for both 
new colleges and the addition of seats. The notification says that private institutions proposing 
to start a 50 -seat medical college will now have to pay an application fee of Rs 7.5 lakh plus 
GST. For a 100-seat college, the fee has been fixed at Rs 15 lakh plus GST, while a 150 -seat 
medical college will have to pay Rs 22.5  lakh plus GST. For govt medical colleges, the 

226 
 
revised fee has been set at Rs 6.2 lakh plus GST for 50 seats, Rs 12.5 lakh plus GST for 100 
seats, and Rs 18.7 lakh plus GST for 150 seats. A non -refundable application fee of Rs 2 lakh 
has also been made mandatory. Further, the NMC has prescribed substantial security deposits, 
to be paid for six years: Rs 15 crore for 50 -seat colleges, Rs 20 crore for 100 -seat colleges, 
and Rs 25 crore for 150-seat colleges.

and expenditure for the year 2025-26 are mentioned in the Councils Budget Estimate of 2025-

(5) and (6) that the grantee organization has to lay their Annual Reports and Annual Accounts 
if the grant received is in excess of Rs. 50 Lakh. 
5.11.11 The following is submitted by NMC regarding the  revenue generated by the 
commission that the National Medical Commission (NMC) derives the majority of its revenue 
from fees collected from medical colleges and indi vidual applicants. These include charges 
levied on medical colleges for applications, inspections, permissions, and related regulatory 
processes, as well as fees for registration and other statutory services. The funds so generated 
are utilized strictly for the discharge of statutory and regulatory functions of the Commission. 
To maintain fiscal discipline and financial propriety, expenditure is regulated through a 
defined hierarchy of authority: 
a) Primary Authority: Most expenditure proposals require formal approval of the 
Chairman/Secretary, NMC, in accordance with the applicable financial delegation 
framework. 
b) Delegated Powers: Specified financial powers are delegated to subordinate officers as 
per the Commission‘s internal delegation of financial powers, e nsuring operational 
efficiency while maintaining accountability and control. 
5.11.12 Response from CDSCO:  CDSCO submitted that the licensing fees pertaining 
to CDSCO are collected through the Bharatkosh portal and submitted to Consolidated Fund of 
India. The fee receipt is generated when the transactions are completed. These funds raised 
from License fee are subsequently reflected in Public Financial Management System (PFMS) 
reports under the PAO of the Ministry of He alth and Family Welfare. CDSCO regulates 
quality, efficacy and safety of medicines in the country while the National Medical 
Commission (NMC) is the regulatory body responsible for regulation of medical education 
and professionals. Both these organizations under MoHFW have different roles.  
5.11.13 The Committee notes a concerning inconsistency in the financial reporting 
and legislative oversight mechanisms among the apex regulatory bodies under the 
Department of Health and Family Welfare. The Committee takes into account the 
substantial increase  in revenue generated by regulatory bodies such as the National 

228 
 
Medical Commission, which rose significantly from ₹125.36 crore in 2022 -23 to ₹406.17 
crore in 2024-25, largely through application, inspection and licensing fees following the 
revision of norms. While the Department has stated that the accounts are audited by the 
Comptroller and Auditor General of India and laid before Parliament, the Committee 
recommends that the Department ensure the preparation and publication of more 
detailed, consolidated  annual financial reports indicating the source -wise revenue 
generation and head-wise utilization of funds by all regulatory bodies under its purview, 
including the Dental Council of India and Pharmacy Council of India, so as to 
strengthen transparency, accountability and parliamentary oversight. 
 
5.11.14 The Committee also observes that significant amounts are collected 
through regulatory and licensing fees, including those processed through the 
Bharatkosh portal by the Central Drugs Standard Control Organ ization. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department evolve a uniform reporting and 
disclosure framework for all health sector regulatory bodies to periodically report 
revenue receipts, security deposits and their utilisation, particularly in the context of 
increased application fees for medical colleges, so that the financial resources generated 
through regulatory functions are transparently monitored and effectively aligned with 
the broader objectives of strengthening medical education and he althcare regulation in 
the country. 
 
5.11.15 The Committee believes that it is financially imprudent and contrary to 
the principles of parliamentary accountability for statutory bodies generating hundreds 
of crores in regulatory charges to evade legislativ e scrutiny. Specifically, the Pharmacy 
Council of India (PCI) generates upwards of ₹300 crore annually through Pharmacy 
Education Regulatory Charges (PERC) but bypasses parliamentary presentation by 
exploiting General Financial Rule (GFR) 238 (5) and (6), citing that its direct 
government grant for salaries is only ₹20 lakh. Similarly, the Dental Council of India 
(DCI) retains its annual revenue of approximately ₹36 crore entirely as internal receipts 
under the provisions of the Dentists Act, 1948. 
 
5.11.16 The Committee is of the view that revenue generated by statutory 
regulatory bodies through mandatory fees, inspections, and licensing is inherently public 
money. Permitting these funds to be retained autonomously as internal receipts or 
parked indefinitely in Fixed Deposit Receipts (FDRs) without mandatory parliamentary 
oversight creates a severe accountability vacuum and contravenes Government‘s 
standard financial propriety. 
 
5.11.17 On being asked about the strategy being developed to encourage medical 
students to opt for critical specializations such as neurosurgery and cardiovascular surgery, 
the Committee has been apprised that postgraduate and super-specialty capacity has expanded 
in parallel with undergraduate growth, with sustained increase in PG an d DM/MCh seats to 
address shortages in high -need disciplines such as Neurosurgery and Cardiothoracic and 
vascular surgery. Seat expansion is aligned with national disease burden and tertiary care 
requirements. The Commission facilitates development of supe r-specialty departments, 

229 
 
particularly in government institutions to widen access to advanced training. PGMSR -2023 
has been rationalized to facilitate this requirement, esp. in underserved regions. 
5.11.18 The Committee firmly believes that the Department s hould complement 
the expansion of PG and super -specialty seats with targeted strategies to encourage 
medical students to opt for critical disciplines such as neurosurgery and cardiovascular 
surgery. While seat growth has been aligned with disease burden an d tertiary care 
requirements, the Committee urges the Department to strengthen mentorship 
programmes, structured career pathways, and financial/academic incentives to attract 
talent into these high-need specializations. 
5.12 CENTRAL GOVERNMENT HEALTH SCHEME 
 
5.12.1 On a specific query on proposal for cashless treatment to be extended to retired Air 
India employees, the Department submitted that CGHS provides OPD treatment, medicines 
and referrals to the Air India retirees. It is pertinent to mention that th e IPD services arising 
from referrals that relates to the associated hospital expenditure, and medical reimbursements 
are the sole responsibilities of Air India Assets Holding Limited (AIAHL). AIAHL has a legal 
agreement with UTIITSL which is their Bill Cl earing Agency (BCA). It is the responsibility 
of the UTIITSL to extend credit facilities to AIAHL beneficiaries. 
5.12.2 The Committee desires that the Department may consider to extend cashless 
treatment facilities for retired Air India employees by ensuri ng seamless linkage 
between CGHS, Air India Assets Holding Limited (AIAHL) , and UTI Infrastructure 
Technology and Services Limited ( UTIITSL), thereby reducing delays in service 
delivery. Further, the Committee recommends that expansion of CGHS wellness cli nics 
across districts be examined with integration into the broader continuum of care. A 
phased approach to extending CGHS coverage to other autonomous bodies such as KVS 
and NVS will promote equity and optimize resources besides extending. 
 
5.13 NATIONAL HEALTH MISSION 
 
5.13.1 On a query related to any proposal to revise and ensure timely payment of honorarium 
for ASHA workers under the National Health Mission, the Department stated that Under 
NHM, support is provided for engagement of contractual workers.  More than 5 lakhs health 
workers like MPW, Staff Nurse, Lab Technicians, Medical Officers, Specialists and CHOs 
are supported under NHM. The rational deployment of HR and optimum utilization of 
resources has been stressed during various meetings and PIPs to the States. Increase in regular 
positions through mission mode recruitment drive is promoted. Further, States are also 
encouraged to provide additional weightage to people having experience in NHM. 
5.13.2 For ASHAs, they  are envisaged to be community he alth volunteer and are entitled to 
task/activity-based incentives. ASHAs receive a fixed incentive of per month for routine and 
recurring activities. ASHA Facilitators receive Rs. 300/ - per visit and undertake about 20 
supervisory visits per month. Additio nally, ASHAs are provided performance -based 
incentives for a varied set of activities under various National Health Programmes. After the 

230 
 
launch of Ayushman Arogya Mandir, ASHAs are eligible additionally for Team Based 
Incentives (TBIs) along with ANMs bas ed on monitored performance indicators (up to ₹1000 
per month). ASHAs are also entitled to non -monetary incentives like – ASHA Uniform, 
Identity Card, Cycle, Mobile, CUG Sim, ASHA Diary, Drug Kit, ASHA rest room etc.  
5.13.3 ASHAs and ASHA Facilitator are covered under Coverage include Pradhan Mantri 
Jeevan JyotiBeemaYojana (PMJJBY) with a benefit Rs. 2.00 Lakh in case of death of the 
insured, coverage of Pradhan Mantri Suraksha BeemaYojana (PMSBY) with a benefit of 
Rs.2.00 lakh for accidental death or perm anent disability and Rs. 1.00 lakh for partial 
disability. Pradhan MantriShram Yogi MaanDhan (PM -SYM) with pension benefit of Rs. 
3,000/- pm after age of 60 years. Also, ASHAs along with their family members are provided 
with health care annual coverage of  Rs 5 Lakhs under Ayushman Bharat Pradhan Mantri Jan 
Arogya Yojana (AB -PMJAY). The Government has also approved a recognition amount of 
₹20,000/- and a citation to ASHAs who leave the programme after working as ASHAs for 
minimum of 10 years, as an acknowle dgement of their contribution. Under SNA SPARSH 
(Just in Time) fund flow mechanism, the bills are envisaged to be cleared on time.  
5.13.4 The Committee recommends that the Department strengthen measures to ensure 
timely and adequate payment of honorarium to ASHA workers under NHM, given their 
expanding workload and responsibilities. In parallel, the Committee urges the 
Department to establish a transparent and streamlined payment mechanism  under the 
SNA SPARSH fund flow system to avoid delays and ensure pr edictability of income. 
Further, the Committee recommends examining the scope for enhanced central 
allocation under NHM Flexipool funds  to support increased remuneration and 
performance-linked incentives for ASHAs and facilitators. A balanced package of 
financial recognition, social security coverage, and career progression opportunities will 
sustain motivation, strengthen community health outreach, and improve service delivery 
at the grassroots level. 
5.13.5 On a query on expansion of dialysis infrastructu re, the Department stated that in 
respect of expanding dialysis infrastructure in districts hospitals, under the Pradhan Mantri 
National Dialysis Programme (PMNDP), implemented under the National Health Mission, the 
Government provides financial and techni cal support to States and Union Territories for 
establishing and expanding dialysis services. The programme facilitates service delivery 
through suitable models including Public –Private Partnership, with the objective of ensuring 
accessible and affordable dialysis care for patients suffering from chronic kidney disease. 
Health is a State subject. The Centre provides support based on the proposals submitted by 
States/UTs in their annual Programme Implementation Plans (PIPs), and funds are released in 
accordance with approved plans. States are encouraged to raise appropriate demands in their 
PIPs to further expand dialysis services as per needs. 
5.13.6 The Committee is of the view that the Department  should chalk out Accelerated  
Action Plan to expand the Pradhan Mantri National Dialysis Programme (PMNDP) in a 
structured manner to meet the growing demand for dialysis services. While financial 
and technical support is being provided to States/UTs under NHM, the Committee urges 

231 
 
the Department to ensure universal  availability of dialysis units at district hospitals, 
with clear annual expansion targets and monitoring of service quality. Further, the 
Committee recommends strengthening public –private partnership models for rapid 
infrastructure scaling, alongside esta blishing a transparent mechanism for donor 
management and coordination with organ transplant registries. The Committee also 
desires that Department ensure ‗Mobile Dialysis Facility ‘ for remote, rural and hilly 
areas. 
5.13.7 On being asked for the incentive s in place for private parties setting up hospitals in 
Tier-II & Tier-III cities, the Department submitted that as health is a state subject, the primary 
responsibility of providing health services including the implementation of primary health 
services lies with the respective State/UT.  However, under National Health Mission (NHM), 
need based technical and financial support is provided to the States/UTs to strengthen their 
healthcare systems, including strengthening of existing or for setting up of hospit als in Tier II 
and Tier III cities with private parties on PPP mode. This is done through a decentralized 
planning process and based on proposals of States/UT Governments submitted in their 
Programme Implementation Plans (PIPs) within their overall resource envelope.  
 
5.13.8 The Committee susggests the Department to extend  incentives for private parties 
to set up hospitals in Tier -II and Tier -III cities under NHM, particularly through 
well-structured Public –Private Partnership (PPP) models. While support is currently 
provided based on State/UT proposals in their Programme Implementation Plans, the 
Committee urges the Department to develop a uniform incentive framework that 
includes financial viability gap funding, concessional land allotment, tax benefits,  and 
streamlined regulatory clearances to attract private investment in underserved regions. 
The Committee further recommends prioritizing district -level hospital expansion in 
underserved regions at Block level and to ensure equitable access to secondary a nd 
tertiary care.  
 
5.13.9 On being asked about Cochlear implant eligibility to be increased to children upto 3 
years of age, the Department submitted that presently, the eligibility criteria for cochlear 
implant is upto 2 years under RBSK. RBSK 2.0 is und er formulation. The proposal would be 
taken up for examination under RBSK 2.0.  
 
5.13.10 The Committee is of the view that the Department may consider revising 
the eligibility criteria for cochlear implants under RBSK 2.0 to include children up to 3 
years of age, in view of clinical evidence that early intervention significantly improves 
language and cognitive outcomes. While the current limit is 2 years, expanding eligibility 
will ensure that children diagnosed slightly later are not excluded from timely 
treatment. 
 
5.14 MEDICINE 
 
5.14.1 On being asked about plans to regulate drug pricing to minimize the gap between 
MRP and actual prices, and to extend duty exemptions to essential post -transplant 

232 
 
immunosuppressant drugs, the Department stated that National P harmaceutical Pricing 
Authority (NPPA) under Department of Pharmaceuticals fixes the ceiling prices of scheduled 
drugs as listed in Schedule -I of DPCO, 2013 which is based on the National List of Essential 
Medicines (NLEM) published by Ministry of Health a nd Family Welfare. No manufacturer 
(whether generic or branded) of scheduled medicines can sell such drug above the ceiling 
price (plus applicable Goods and Service Tax) fixed by the NPPA whether in urban or rural 
area. As per the provisions of DPCO, 2013,  ceiling prices of scheduled formulations are 
revised every year as per annual Wholesale Price Index (WPI) (all commodities) for 
preceding calendar year on or before 1st April of every year, which is notified by the 
Government on the 1st day of April every  year. In case of non -scheduled formulation, 
manufacturers are required not to increase MRP by more than 10% of the MRP during 
preceding 12 months. 
5.14.2 The Committee is of the considered opinion  that the Department, in coordination 
with the Department o f Pharmaceuticals and NPPA, must undertake  measures to 
minimize the gap between MRP and actual sale prices of medicines  by ensuring stricter 
enforcement of ceiling prices under DPCO, 2013 and enhancing transparency in retail 
pricing across States. Uniform monitoring mechanisms should be instituted to prevent 
regional disparities and ensure affordability for patients nationwide. Further, the 
Committee recommends that the Department examine the proposal to extend duty 
exemptions to essential post -transplant i mmunosuppressant drugs , given their critical 
role in patient survival and long-term care.  
5.15 Mental Health 
 
5.15.1 On being asked about the Government considering allocation of funds for suicide 
prevention initiatives and establishment of infertility cl inics in tertiary care hospitals or 
government medical colleges, the Department stated that t he Government has released the 
National Suicide Prevention Strategy in November, 2022. The National Strategy for Suicide 
Prevention provides a framework for multip le stakeholders to implement activities for 
prevention of suicides in India. 
5.15.2 The Government has launched a ―National Tele Mental Health Programme‖ to further 
improve access to quality mental health counselling and care services in the country. As on  
24.02.2026, 36 States/ UTs have set up 53 Tele MANAS Cells. Tele -MANAS services are 
available in 20 languages based on language opted by States. More than 33,92,000 calls have 
been handled on the helpline number. The Government has also launched Tele MANA S 
Mobile Application on the occasion of World Mental Health Day - October 10, 2024. Tele -
MANAS Mobile Application is a comprehensive mobile platform that has been developed to 
provide support for mental health issues ranging from well -being to mental disor ders. The 
helpline number 14416 has also been utilized for counselling in Mental issues like suicidal 
ideation. In addition, a video consultation facility has been introduced under Tele -MANAS, 
as an upgrade to the existing audio calling service. Under the National Tele-Mental Health 
Programme, a financial allocation of ₹45 crore has been provided at Revised Estimates (RE) 
2025–26 and ₹79.60 crore at Budget Estimates (BE) 2026 –27 to support expansion and 
strengthening of these services. 

233 
 
 
5.15.3 The Committee recommends that the Department to seek additional budgetary 
support for suicide prevention initiatives and infertility services in tertiary care 
hospitals and government medical colleges. In parallel, the Committee urges the 
Department to ensure sustained  funding and integration of suicide prevention services 
into district and medical college hospitals, with emphasis on counselling, crisis 
intervention, and follow-up care. 
 
5.15.4 The Committee, further, recommends that the Department examine the 
establishment of infertility clinics within tertiary care institutions, in compliance with 
guidelines and financial provisions, to address rising demand and reduce dependence on 
private facilities. A combined approach of targeted funding, integration into existing 
infrastructure, and structured service delivery will strengthen both mental health and 
reproductive health outcomes nationwide.  
 
5.15.5 The Committee notes with concern that Mental health has emerged as a major 
public health concern requiring urgent polic y attention. Data from the World Health 
Organization indicates that one in seven individuals in India experiences some form of 
mental disorder, yet the availability of trained psychiatrists and mental health 
professionals remains severely limited. Accordin g to the National Crime Records 
Bureau, India reports over 1.7 lakh suicides every year, reflecting the urgent need for 
expanded psychiatric services and early intervention systems. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends strengthening psychology and psychiat ry departments in medical colleges, 
increasing postgraduate seats in psychiatry, and availability of trained counsellors and 
psychologists, particularly at the community and primary healthcare levels.  
 
5.15.6 The Committee recommends that suicide preventi on efforts be strengthened 
through a community-based approach that emphasizes training of teachers, community 
healthcare workers, and other key stakeholders to identify early warning signs of 
emotional distress and suicidal behavior and provide timely supp ort or referral. Such 
capacity building initiatives, along with accessible counselling services and sustained 
public awareness campaigns to reduce stigma around mental health, can help ensure 
early intervention and create a supportive environment for individuals at risk.  
 
5.16 ORAL HEALTH 
 
5.16.1 The committee understand s that Oral diseases represent a significant but often 
neglected component of the national disease burden. According to the World Health 
Organization, oral diseases affect billions globally , and in India dental caries affect 60 –
90% of school children, while periodontal diseases impact nearly 50% of adults. India 
produces more than 35,000 dental graduates annually, yet the potential of this 
workforce remains underutilized due to limited natio nal oral health programmes. 
Establishing a Ministry of Oral Health, on the lines of the Ministry of AYUSH, could 
institutionalize preventive dental care, strengthen research, and integrate oral health 
services in to primary healthcare. International public  health models, particularly in 

234 
 
Brazil and the United Kingdom, shows that structured national oral health programmes 
significantly reduce long-term disease burden and healthcare costs. 
 
5.17 CENTRAL ARMED POLICE FORCES INSTITUTE OF MEDICAL 
SCIENCES 
5.17.1 On being asked about the implementation status of Central Armed Police Forces 
Institute of Medical Sciences (CAPFIMS) hospital, the Department submitted that a 
Memorandum of Association has been signed between Central Armed Police Forces Institute 
of Medical Sciences (CAPFIMS) and AIIMS to run CAPFIMS as a campus  of AIIMS New 
Delhi. CAPFIMS is 970 -bedded hospital with a Medical College, a Nursing College and 
School of Paramedics with 100, 60 and 300 seats respectively. Status of CAPFIMS is as 
under: 
a) Civil works of the Hospital including Hospital Building, Academic Block, College of 
Nursing, School of Paramedical Sciences, residential building, hostels, Auditorium, 
barracks building, multi-level car parking has been completed.  
b) Patient care services have star ted since May 2025, with OPD/diagnostic services 
available in 13 Departments. 
c) 53 contractual faculty along with 75 Senior Residents (Tenure posts) are in place.  
Recruitment of regular faculty is underway.  Further, 82 Senior Residents have joined 
for Janu ary 2026 Session.  In addition, 256 Nursing Officers along with staff of 
paramedics and administrative staff have been engaged.   
d) Various Health education sessions have also been undertaken including Continuing 
Medical Education (CME) in Forensic Odontology. 
 
5.17.2 The Committee notes that the 970 -bedded Central Armed Police Forces Institute 
of Medical Sciences (CAPFIMS) hospital at Maidangarhi has been completed and that 
patient care services have commenced since May 2025 with OPD and diagnostic services 
operational in multiple departments. The Committee also takes note that the institute is 
being run as a campus of All India Institute of Medical Sciences, New Delhi, and that 
recruitment of regular faculty and staff for different departments is presently 
underway. The Committee recommends that the Department take expeditious steps to
