complete the recruitment of regular faculty and operationalize the full range of hospital 
and academic services, including the medical, nursing and paramedical colleges, so tha t 
the institution can function at its intended capacity and effectively serve both CAPF 
personnel and the general public. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 

235 
 
RECOMMENDATIONS/OBSERVATIONS OF THE COMMITTEE-AT A GLANCE 
VISION, MISSION AND OBJECTIVES 
1. The Committee takes note of the Vi sion, Mission, and Objectives of the Ministry 
of Health and Family Welfare, which collectively outline the strategic roadmap for the 
Ministry‘s activities. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department of 
Health and Family Welfare should underta ke all projects and schemes in strict 
consonance with the vision of providing universal access to quality healthcare without 
causing financial hardship to the citizens and execute these initiatives with the clear 
intention of achieving its cherished object ives, particularly the progressive realization of 
Universal Health Coverage and the establishment of robust linkages between primary, 
secondary, and tertiary care. The Committee believes that the Department must move 
forward with 'Management by Objective' (MBO) rather than 'Management by 
Activities' (MBA). This approach is essential to ensure equitable healthcare for 
marginalized communities, optimize human resources, and achieve the broader targets 
set out in the National Health Policy (NHP) 2017. 
(Para 1.3.2) 
DEPARTMENT OF HEALTH AND FAMILY WELFARE 
2. The Committee is of the view that the statutory/regulatory bodies and 
autonomous institutions act as the vital organs and play a pivotal role in the successful 
functioning of the Department. Their efficient a nd effective working is, therefore, 
indispensable for the result -oriented implementation of the core commitments 
undertaken by the Department, such as expanding decentralized public health 
infrastructure, ensuring adequate staffing, and providing a compreh ensive package of 
services to achieve the objectives of universal access to healthcare, prioritizing women 
and children.  
(Para 1.4.5) 
3. The Committee, moreover, feels that a streamlined monitoring mechanism 
utilizing Always Better Control (ABC) would fur ther ensure the desirable Rate of 
Returns (RoR) on the heavy investments made in upgrading health infrastructure and 
mitigating financial hardship for citizens. The Committee, moreover, recommends that 
the Department should keep a constant Strength, Weakne sses, Opportunities and 
Threats (SWOT) Analysis of its organs/institutions for better results and efficient social 
protection. 
(Para 1.4.6) 
ESTABLISHMENT EXPENDITURE 
4. The Committee believes that given the Secretariat‘s pivotal role in driving 
national he althcare policies, the persistent trend of fund underutilisation under head 
―Direction and Administration‖ is a matter of concern. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends that the Department must overhaul its expenditure planning for BE 2026 -
27 to ensure opti mal utilization of allocated fund as per originally conceived plan. The 

236 
 
Committee is of the view that the Department should undertake a comprehensive review 
of the factors leading to persistent under -utilisation of funds under the heads 
―Secretariat‖ and ―Direction and Administration‖ and put in place a robust expenditure 
planning and monitoring mechanism, including quarterly utilisation targets and 
outcome-based review. 
(Para 1.5.5) 
5. The Committee emphasises that a strong and well -equipped Secretariat an d 
administrative framework is essential for effective policy implementation, monitoring 
and optimal utilisation of resources in the health sector. Keeping in view inflationary 
pressure, and the need to strengthen institutional capacity and infrastructure, along 
with the expanding scope of programme implementation and monitoring 
responsibilities, the Committee believes that the allocation of fund under the heads 
―Secretariat‖ and ―Direction and Administration‖ for FY 2026–27 needs to be enhanced 
aligning wit h actual functional requirements. This would strengthen institutional 
capacity (both administrative and technical), improve fund absorption, and enhance the 
overall efficiency and effectiveness of health and family welfare programmes 
implementation and oversight. 
(Para 1.5.6) 
STAFF STRENGTH IN THE DEPARTMENT 
6. The Committee observes with concern that the Department of Health and Family 
Welfare continues to face a significant level of vacancies under its different Divisions, 
with 380 posts remaining unfille d against the sanctioned strength of 1467 for the year 
2025. While the Committee appreciates the efforts made by the Department in reducing 
vacancies from 428 in 2024 to 380 in 2025 through regular follow -up with Cadre 
Controlling Authorities and recruitin g agencies, the persisting shortfall is likely to 
adversely affect the efficiency of policy implementation, programme monitoring and 
administrative functioning.  The Committee, therefore, reiterates its recommendation 
made in the 143rd and 163rd Reports that the Ministry needs to take appropriate steps to 
fill up the vacant positions at various levels and implement better work management 
practices in the Department. The Committee further recommends the Department to 
take up the matter of vacancies with the C adre Controlling Authorities and recruiting 
agencies to expedite the recruitment of officers/Staffs at all levels in the Department.  
(Para 1.6.5) 
7. The Committee further notes with concern that the Department has not 
undertaken a comprehensive workload analysis to assess whether the existing sanctioned 
strength is adequate to align with its expanding responsibilities. Given the increasing 
scope and complexity of health and family welfare programmes, the Committee 
recommends that the Department conduct a s cientific manpower and workload 
assessment at the earliest and rationalise the sanctioned strength accordingly and 
subsequently, undertake special drive to fill up the vacant posts. 
(Para 1.6.6) 
8. The Committee is of the view that while the engagement of contractual technical 

237 
 
staff may provide short -term operational support, the core and long -term functions 
should be supported by regular staff to ensure institutional continuity, accountability 
and capacity building. The Committee, therefore, recommends the  Department for 
filling up vacancies, improving manpower planning and forcasting, and strengthening its 
human resource capacity to ensure effective implementation and monitoring of health 
and family welfare programmes. The Committee also urges the Departme nt to analyse 
the reasons for high attrition and non -joining, particularly at the ASO and MTS levels, 
and take corrective measures to improve retention. 
(Para 1.6.7) 
BUDGETARY PROJECTION VIS-A-VIS ALLOCATION AND EXPENDITURE  
 
9. From the submissions made by the Department, the Committee observed that the 
projected demand has grown steadily, reaching ₹1.22 lakh crore in FY 2026 –27. The 
Budget Estimates have consistently been lower than the proposed/projected demands, 
reflecting partial acceptance of the Depar tment‘s requests by the Ministry of Finance. 
The persistent gap between proposed demand and BE may constrain the expansion of 
health programmes. In FY 2026 –27, the gap between the proposed demand and BE 
exceeds 17%, which is substantial. 
(Para 2.1.5) 
 
10. The Department observes that the BE for 2026 –27 has increased by 
approximately 6% over BE 2025 –26, however, considering the average inflation rate of 
around 5%, population growth, and the National Health Policy target of raising 
government health expenditu re to 2.5% of GDP by 2025, the Committee is of the view 
that the effective increase is marginal. In its 163 rd Report, the Committee had 
recommended higher allocations to significantly enhance healthcare infrastructure and 
services nationwide, particularly in light of the challenges experienced during the 
COVID-19 pandemic, to ensure preparedness for future public health emergencies. 
(Para 2.1.6) 
 
11. The Committee expresses concern over the consistent gap between the 
Department‘s projected demand and the Bu dget Estimates (BE), which has constricted 
the expansion of health programmes. In FY 2026–27, the gap exceeds 17%, a substantial 
shortfall that risks undermining the objectives of the National Health Policy, 
particularly the target of raising government he alth expenditure to 2.5% of GDP. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department should engage more 
proactively with the Ministry of Finance during the pre -budget consultations to ensure 
that allocations are better aligned with projected needs, es pecially for critical schemes 
such as NACO, PMSSY, PM-ABHIM, and tertiary care services. 
(Para 2.1.9) 
 
12. While the BE for FY 2026 –27 shows a nominal 6% increase, this is effectively 
neutralized by inflation averaging around 5% and population growth, and is insufficient 

238 
 
to meet the rising healthcare demands of the nation. Further, the Department submitted 
that the Annual GDP growth rate during the financial year 2025 -26 is 7.3% on constant 
price. In view thereof, keeping in view the factors of inflation, p opulation growth and 
alignment with the National Health Policy‘s mandate of increasing government health 
expenditure to 2.5% of GDP, the year -on-year budget allocation growth should be 
significantly more than the rate of 7.3%, must be around 14 -15%. The Co mmittee, 
therefore, recommends that future allocations be significantly enhanced to strengthen 
healthcare infrastructure, expand service delivery, and ensure preparedness for public 
health emergencies, in line with lessons learned from the COVID -19 pandemic. Further, 
the Committee also believes that by leveraging its IT platforms, third-party reviews, and 
stakeholder consultations, the Department should formulate realistic, evidence -based 
initial budget projections that permit full funding and minimize the recurring cycle of 
mid-year downward revisions. 
(Para 2.1.10) 
13. The Committee further observes that frequent mid -year reductions at the 
Revised Estimate (RE) stage have disrupted the continuity and momentum of long -term 
health initiatives. The Department  must take proactive measures to safeguard core, 
long-term health initiatives, such as NACO, PMSSY, PM -ABHIM, and Tertiary Care 
projects, from being disproportionately derailed by mid -year financial cuts. Reductions 
of ₹6,600 crore, ₹8,500 crore, and ₹3,00 0 crore in FYs 2022 –23, 2023–24, and 2025 –26 
respectively highlight the need for more stable and predictable funding for better result. 
Given the vulnerabilities exposed during the COVID -19 pandemic, consistent and 
uninterrupted funding is non -negotiable f or building nationwide healthcare resilience. 
The Committee, in this regard, recommends that the Department should strengthen its 
expenditure planning and monitoring mechanisms to demonstrate consistent fund 
absorption capacity, thereby minimizing the like lihood of downward revisions. 
Enhanced transparency in reporting the reasons for underutilization will also improve 
accountability and strengthen the case for higher allocations in ensuing financial years. 
 
(Para 2.1.11) 
 
14. The Committee expresses deep concern over the chronic issue of underutilization 
of funds, highlighted again in FY 2025 –26, where only 75.70% of the reduced RE 
allocation was spent by 31 January 2026. With limited time remaining in the financial 
year, there is a risk of skewed expenditu re in a particular quarter of a financial year, 
which may compromise efficiency and value for money. The Department must 
implement stringent mechanisms for the even distribution of funds throughout the 
financial year. The Committee reiterates its earlier recommendations made in 143rd and 
163rd Reports that the Department cap its expenditure in the final month of the 
financial year in accordance with Ministry of Finance guidelines, and closely monitor 
fund utilization during the last quarter to ensure pruden t and efficient use of public 
funds. 
(Para 2.1.12) 
 

239 
 
GOVERNMENT HEALTH EXPENDITURE IN INDIA  
 
15. The Committee observes that although the Budget Estimates (BE) allocation to 
the Department of Health and Family Welfare has increased in absolute terms over 
recent years, its share relative to the Total Union Budget and GDP has either stagnated 
or declined. Further, reliance on the health cess, constituting over 30% of the 
Department‘s allocation in BE 2026 –27, appears to have substituted for core budgetary 
support rather than supplementing it. While the Department has clarified that PMSSN 
forms part of the established budgetary framework and is subject to audit, concerns 
remain regarding the increasing dependence on cess proceeds and the decline in core 
budgetary support as a share of the total Government budget. The Committee 
recommends that the Department take up the matter with the Ministry of Finance to 
ensure that cess proceeds are genuinely additional to core allocations and do not 
substitute regular budgetary support. 
(Para 2.2.10) 
 
16. The Committee is of the view that the Department should pursue measures to 
ensure that health expenditure aligns with the National Health Policy target of 2.5% of 
GDP, with the Centre contributing at least 0.9% of GDP. The C ommittee, therefore, 
recommends that the Department, in consultation with the Ministry of Finance, prepare 
a clear medium -term roadmap indicating year -wise targets for enhancing the Union 
Government‘s health outlay as a proportion of both GDP and the Total  Union Budget. 
Such a roadmap should align with Finance Commission cycles and national health 
priorities, and be placed before Parliament to ensure transparency and accountability. 
In parallel, the Department may strengthen coordination with States to enco urage them 
to raise health allocations to at least 8% of their budgets, thereby ensuring balanced 
progress toward national health goals. 
(Para 2.2.11) 
CAPITAL VIS-A-VIS REVENUE ALLOCATION 
 
17. The Committee expresses concern on the persistent decline in th e proportion of 
capital allocation within the Department of Health & Family Welfare‘s overall 
budgetary framework. Despite steady increases in total Budget Estimates (BE), the 
capital component has fallen from 6.79% in 2022 –23 to just 2.88% in 2026 –27. At the 
Revised Estimates (RE) stage, the share has similarly contracted, reaching only 2.62% 
in 2025–26. This trend reflects a diminishing emphasis on long -term infrastructure and 
equipment investments, even as the demand for expanded and modernized health 
facilities continues to grow. While the Department has explained that health, being a 
social service sector, has limited scope for capital expenditure, the Committee 
emphasizes that the sector‘s future resilience and accessibility depend on sustained 
capital investment. Revenue allocations, though critical for recurring expenses and 
service delivery, cannot substitute the structural need for robust infrastructure. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends that capital allocations be progressively enhanced to 

240 
 
ensure balanced growth between immediate service requirements and long -term 
capacity building. 
(Para 2.3.5) 
 
18. The Committee also observes recurring underutilization of earmarked capital 
funds due to delays in tendering, procurement bottlenecks, non -submission of utilization 
certificates, and inadequate viable projects from implementing agencies. To address 
these inefficiencies, the Department should strengthen project planning, streamline 
procurement and clearance processes, and enforce stricter timelines for States, UTs, and 
autonomous bodies. Capital investments must be prioritized for constructing tertiary 
centres, upgrading primary and secondary facilities, and equipping them with modern 
machinery alongside adequate human resources to ensure functionality.  The Committee 
therefore recommends reorienting budgetary priorities to arrest the declining trend in 
capital allocation, improve fund utilization efficiency, and commit to sustained 
infrastructure and equipment investments, thereby strengthening healthcar e capacity, 
accessibility, and long-term resilience. 
(Para 2.3.6) 
TARGETS UNDER NATIONAL HEALTH POLICY 
 
19. The Committee observes with deep concern that the allocation to the health 
sector, comprising the Department of Health & Family Welfare, Department of Health 
Research, and Ministry of Ayush, has remained stagnant at 0.28–0.33% of GDP over the 
last five years, which is not in consonance with the National Health Policy (NHP), 2017 
target of raising Government Health Expenditure (GHE) to 2.5% of GDP by 2 025. 
Although GHE has increased from 1.35% of GDP in 2017 –18 to 1.84% in 2021 –22, the 
pace of growth is insufficient to achieve the NHP target within the stipulated timeframe. 
The Committee reiterates its earlier recommendations made in the 126th and 163rd  
Reports, wherein it had urged the Government to increase health expenditure to 5% by 
2025, however, the FY 2025 -26 has now come to an end. The Committee, therefore, 
urges the Department to review the target under NHP to 5% in the next five years. The 
Committee is of considered view that Health Sector must be recognized as a priority 
sector for both economic and social development, requiring sustained investment in 
public health infrastructure, preventive care, and equitable service delivery. 
(Para 2.4.7) 
20. The Committee is not satisfied over delays in updating National Health Accounts 
(NHA) data, with information currently available only up to FY 2021 –22. Despite the 
acknowledged two -year lag, data for FY 2023 –24 should already be available, yet it 
remains pending. In its 163rd Report, the Committee had highlighted that outdated data 
hampers effective monitoring and evaluation of government schemes. The Committee, 
therefore, reiterates its recommendation that the Government leverage Information 
Technology and Artificial Intelligence for real -time data collection and processing, 
ensuring that expenditure data for a given financial year is made available by the last 

241 
 
quarter of the following year. This will enable timely assessment of progress and 
alignment of schemes with intended objectives. 
(Para 2.4.8) 
21. While the decline in Out -of-Pocket Expenditure (OOPE) from 64.2% in 2013 –14 
to 39.4% in 2021 –22 is encouraging, the Committee finds that OOPE still remains a 
significant barrier to healthcare access, part icularly given India‘s large population 
living in poverty. As per the Global Multidimensional Poverty Index 2024, India is home 
to approximately 234 million poor people, the highest in the world, and is the only 
country among the five poorest nations with a medium Human Development Index 
(HDI). As highlighted in the 163rd Report, the Committee is optimistic that current 
figures may be lower, but underscores the urgent need to reduce OOPE further to 
ensure affordability and accessibility of healthcare. The C ommittee therefore 
recommends intensifying efforts to strengthen public health infrastructure, expand 
preventive and primary care services, leverage Public -Private Partnerships, and 
institute robust monitoring mechanisms to reduce OOPE and enhance accessibility. 
 
(Para 2.4.9) 
 
22. The Committee also emphasizes the need to strengthen preventive and primary 
healthcare services as a cornerstone of India‘s health system. A strategic focus on 
preventive care and efficient resource utilization is crucial to achie ving long -term 
improvements in accessibility and quality. Preventive interventions reduce the burden 
on tertiary care facilities, lower Out -of-Pocket Expenditure (OOPE), and improve 
overall health outcomes. Given India‘s score of 67 on SDG -3 (ensuring healthy lives and 
wellbeing) in the Sustainable Development Report 2025, below the world average of 72 
and significantly lower than high -income countries averaging 91, the Committee 
reiterates that stronger investments in preventive health, including immunizat ion, 
maternal and child health, nutrition, and early detection of non -communicable diseases, 
are essential to bridge the gap. 
(Para 2.4.10) 
 
23. The Committee also highlights the importance of leveraging Public -Private 
Partnerships (PPP) and innovative fin ancing mechanisms to bridge the resource gap in 
the health sector. In its earlier reports, including the 163rd Report, the Committee 
emphasized that the current pace of increase in Government Health Expenditure (GHE) 
is inadequate to meet the National Heal th Policy target of 2.5% of GDP by 2025. Given 
the stagnant allocation of 0.28 –0.33% of GDP to the Ministry of Health & Family 
Welfare, Department of Health Research, and Ministry of Ayush, it is imperative to 
explore supplementary avenues of investment. T he Committee expects that PPPs can 
play a crucial role in expanding infrastructure, introducing advanced technologies, and 
improving service delivery, particularly in underserved rural and tribal areas, 
therefore, the Ministry should strive in that direction. 
(Para 2.4.11) 

242 
 
COMPARATIVE ANALYSIS OF HEALTH EXPENDITURE 
 
24. The Committee observes that India‘s Current Health Expenditure (CHE) as a 
percentage of GDP stands at 3.3 percent, which is considerably lower than that of other 
BRICS countries such as Brazil (9.6 percent), South Africa (8.7 percent), and Russia (7.0 
percent), as well as G20 nations where the average often exceeds 10 percent. 
Furthermore, Government Health Expenditure (GHE) in India is only 1.84 percent of 
GDP, far below the 4 –10 percent ran ge observed in most BRICS and G20 countries. 
This limited public investment translates into a high reliance on household spending, 
with Out -of-Pocket Expenditure (OOPE) accounting for 45.1 percent of CHE. 
Comparative analysis shows that countries with high er government spending, such as 
Germany, Japan, and the United Kingdom, not only achieve stronger health outcomes 
but also maintain OOPE levels below 15 percent, thereby ensuring greater financial 
protection for their populations. In light of this, the Com mittee recommends that India 
adopt a phased strategy to raise GHE to at least 5 percent of GDP by 2030, beyond the 
targets set under the National Health Policy (2017). This increase should be directed 
towards strengthening primary and preventive healthcare  with essential infrastructure, 
and reducing inequities in access to healthcare. 
(Para 2.5.5) 
 
25. The Committee notes that despite some progress in reducing OOPE from 67 
percent in 2013–14 to 45.1 percent in 2021 –22, India continues to face one of the hig hest 
household health expenditure burdens among BRICS and G20 countries. This level of 
OOPE is comparable to Mexico (41.4 percent) but remains far above countries such as 
South Africa (6.6 percent) and France (9.2 percent), where robust public financing an d 
insurance systems provide strong financial protection. Evidence from both high -income 
countries like Canada and Japan, and medium -HDI countries such as Namibia (6.5% 
GHE and 7.3% OOPE) and lower -income countries like Rwanda (5% OOPE), 
demonstrates that e ffective risk pooling and community -based insurance models can 
substantially reduce household vulnerability to catastrophic health spending. In this 
context, the Committee recommends that India expand financial protection mechanisms 
by strengthening Ayushm an Bharat and state -level insurance schemes, with particular 
emphasis on outpatient care, diagnostics, and medicines, which are major drivers of 
OOPE. Risk pooling should be incentivized through employer -based and cooperative 
insurance models, especially t argeting informal sector workers who remain outside 
formal coverage.  
(Para 2.5.6) 
 
26. The Committee notes that while the National Health Policy (2017) recommended 
that States/UTs increase their health spending to more than 8 percent of their total 
budget, the actual expenditure remains significantly below this benchmark, averaging 
only 5.3 percent in 2023 –24 and projected at 5.6 percent in 2024 –25. Although some 
states such as Delhi (11.4 percent), Odisha (8.0 percent), and Puducherry (8.7 percent) 
are approaching or surpassing the target, most others continue to allocate between 4 –6 

243 
 
percent, reflecting a persistent gap in prioritization of health within state budgets. Given 
that health is a state subject, the Committee emphasizes that the Department encou rage 
States/UTs to adopt a phased strategy to progressively raise health spending closer to 
the 8 percent benchmark. This should be supported by Union Government initiatives 
such as conditional Result -Based Financing under the National Health Mission, 
strengthening of the Public Health Management Cadre, and adoption of Indian Public 
Health Standards (IPHS) 2022. To ensure accountability and efficient utilization of 
funds, mechanisms such as CAG audits, PFMS tracking, and quarterly financial 
monitoring must be rigorously implemented, thereby ensuring equitable access to 
quality healthcare across the country. 
(Para 2.5.9) 
 
27. In addition to urging States/UTs to progressively raise their health allocations 
closer to the 8 percent benchmark, the Committee recom mends that greater emphasis 
be placed on strengthening state-level innovation and capacity building in health service 
delivery. While Union initiatives such as Result -Based Financing under the National 
Health Mission, the Public Health Management Cadre, an d adoption of IPHS and 
NQAS standards provide a strong framework, their impact will depend on how 
effectively states adapt these measures to local needs. States should be encouraged to 
pilot innovative models in areas such as digital health, telemedicine, and community -
based outreach, particularly in underserved rural and tribal regions, and to integrate 
these with centrally sponsored schemes for maximum efficiency. Moreover, capacity 
building of state health departments through specialized training, improv ed data 
systems, and performance -linked incentives will ensure that increased allocations 
translate into measurable improvements in service delivery. By combining higher 
budgetary prioritization with innovation and institutional strengthening, States/UTs c an 
accelerate progress towards equitable, quality healthcare and contribute meaningfully 
to national health goals. 
(Para 2.5.10) 
15th FINANCE COMMISSION HEALTH GRANTS 
 
28. The Committee is displeased to note that although the 15th Finance Commission 
allocated ₹70,051 crore for health grants for FY 2021 –22 to FY 2025 –26, utilisation 
remains suboptimal. Against ₹44,495.12 crore (63.52% of the allocation) released to 
States, only ₹25,367.75 crore (57.01% of funds released) has been reported as 
expenditure. As observed in its 163rd Report, as of 30 January 2025, only 39.42% of the 
total approved funds had been released, with full disbursement limited to FY 2021 –22, 
and States had utilised just 59.31% of the released funds. The Committee reiterates that 
such low utilisation defeats the very objective of the financial support to orient health 
sector as patient -centric. It therefore strongly recommends that the Government 
undertake a comprehensive study to identify the causes of under -utilisation and 
implement corre ctive measures. The Union Government should also establish a 
dedicated institutional mechanism for regular coordination with States/UTs to address 

244 
 
bottlenecks and ensure timely and optimal utilisation of these critical funds for 
strengthening primary healthcare. 
(Para 2.6.5) 
29. The NHSRC–NIPFP study highlights systemic challenges, including the capital -
intensive nature of the grants, rigid budget provisions, withdrawal of human resource 
support after initial years, and execution difficulties arising from u nfamiliar fund-flow 
mechanisms. States have also faced mismatches between allocations and local needs, 
procurement delays, cost escalations in difficult terrains, and financial misclassifications 
disrupting fund flow. These constraints have slowed infrastr ucture development and 
service readiness despite the availability of funds. In view of these findings, the 
Committee recommends enhancing flexibility in fund re -appropriation to better align 
allocations with State -specific needs, restoring and sustaining h uman resource support 
to ensure service continuity, and empowering District-Level Committees to assign works 
to suitable agencies where local bodies lack capacity. Procurement processes should be 
streamlined, and data reporting systems strengthened to impr ove accuracy and 
transparency, minimising discrepancies between portal data and actual expenditure. 
Addressing these structural and operational bottlenecks will accelerate utilisation of 
Finance Commission health grants and facilitate timely infrastructure  expansion, 
improved diagnostic capacity, and strengthened primary healthcare services across 
States. 
(Para 2.6.6) 
CENTRAL GOVERNMENT HEALTH SCHEME (CGHS):  
 
30. The Committee notes that while the Central Government Health Scheme (CGHS) 
has expanded signif icantly, budgetary allocations remain constrained in relation to 
projected demand. In FY 2024 –25, expenditure under CGHS and PORB was fully 
utilized. In FY 2025 –26 also utilisation stood at 88.43 percent up to January 2026, 
however, with notable reductions under the Capital Head where allocations were cut by 
nearly half at the RE stage. For FY 2026 –27, the Directorate projected a requirement of 
₹9,431.65 crore, but allocations were limited to ₹8,697.86 crore, leaving a gap of over 
₹700 crore, and no additio nal funds secured under the Materials and Supplies head for 
procurement of medicines nationwide.  With the revision of CGHS rates, expansion of 
empanelled hospitals to Tier II and Tier III cities, and operationalisation of 22 new 
wellness centres, expenditu re pressures are expected to rise further. The Committee, 
therefore, recommends that the Department pursue additional allocations of fund at the 
RE stage to meet genuine expenditure, ensure adequate provisioning for medicines and 
consumables, and safeguard  continuity of services for pensioners and beneficiaries. 
Further, capital investments in infrastructure, ICT systems, and medical equipment 
should be protected from mid -year reductions to strengthen service delivery, 
digitisation, and expansion of CGHS facilities, thereby ensuring that the scheme remains 
responsive to the growing needs of its diverse beneficiary base. 
(Para 3.1.7) 

245 
 
31. The Committee notes that the reduction in capital outlay from ₹75.31 crore to 
₹38.86 crore at the RE stage in FY 2025 –26 wa s primarily due to the non -approval of 
the proposed ₹36 crore state -of-the-art multifacility centre at Koliwada and the 
surrender of ₹45 lakh earmarked for vehicle purchases by two CGHS units. While 
allocations under ICT Equipment and Buildings and Structu res Heads were partially 
utilised for procurement of CDAC -compatible computers, implementation of the E -
Sushrut programme, and sanctioned works in Delhi, Chennai, Nagpur, and Kolkata, the 
surrender of major project funds significantly lowered capital utili sation. The 
Committee recommends that the Department strengthen project appraisal and approval 
mechanisms to ensure that large-scale proposals such as the Koliwada centre are cleared 
in advance of budget finalisation, thereby avoiding mid -year surrender of  funds. 
Further, capital allocations should be safeguarded with improved coordination between 
CGHS units and administrative authorities to ensure timely approvals and utilisation. 
This will enhance predictability in capital planning, prevent under -utilisation, and 
ensure that CGHS facilities are adequately modernised to meet the growing needs of 
beneficiaries. 
(Para 3.1.9) 
32. The Committee notes that the Department has proposed several new initiatives 
for the coming financial year, including the implementa tion of the CGHS Drug 
Procurement Policy, 2025, creation of a Project Management Unit (PMU), establishment 
of an on -site call centre, and setting up of 22 new allopathic wellness centres, alongside 
recent reforms such as deployment of the E -Sushrut HMIS, launch of the myCGHS 2.0 
mobile app, and adoption of the DVDMS supply -chain system. While these measures 
represent milestone progress in modernising CGHS, the Committee recommends that 
the Department ensure adequate provisioning of funds and staffing for th e new wellness 
centres, strengthen ICT -enabled monitoring systems, and expedite integration with 
government portals to streamline service delivery. Further, the Committee emphasizes 
the need for regular review and inclusion of the latest and advanced medic al procedures 
and investigations in the CGHS package, so that beneficiaries have access to 
contemporary standards of care without facing denial of treatment or delays. By 
combining expansion, digitisation, supply -chain resilience, and continuous updating o f 
medical services, CGHS can evolve into a more responsive, transparent, and 
beneficiary-centric health system. 
(Para 3.1.14) 
33. The Committee further recommends that the Department place greater emphasis 
on beneficiary-centric monitoring and feedback mec hanisms to ensure that the 
expansion and digitisation efforts under CGHS translate into tangible improvements in 
patient experience. While systems such as E -Sushrut, DVDMS, and the myCGHS 2.0 
app have strengthened transparency and supply -chain efficiency, their effectiveness 
depends on consistent user adoption and responsiveness to beneficiary concerns. 
Beneficiaries should be made aware of these tools through structured outreach, and a 
public dashboard should display feedback and grievances received, timel ines for 
resolution, pendency, and cases resolved, thereby enhancing transparency and 

246 
 
accountability. The proposed on-site call centre must be expanded into a 24×7 facility to 
address emergency grievances, recognizing health as an essential service. Moreover, the 
proposed Anti-Fraud Cell and data analytics framework should not only monitor billing 
irregularities but also track service quality indicators such as waiting times, medicine 
availability, and claim settlement efficiency. Embedding robust feedback loops, 
grievance tracking, and real -time responsiveness into the CGHS framework, periodic 
beneficiary satisfaction surveys, and integration of feedback into policy adjustments  will 
ensure that reforms remain beneficiary-centric, equitable, and aligned with  the evolving 
needs of its diverse beneficiary base. 
(Para 3.1.15) 
34. The Committee takes into account the submission of the Department that the 
consolidated incumbency position of Group A, B, and C posts under CGHS reveals a 
substantial vacancy burden, w ith 2,159 posts lying vacant out of 6,865 sanctioned 
positions, translating into nearly one -third of the workforce.  Critical shortages are 
evident among medical cadres such as General Duty Medical Officers (328 vacant), 
Specialists (96 vacant), Nursing Of ficers (151 vacant), Medical Attendants (332 vacant), 
and Pharmacists (Allopathic- 78 vacant, Ayurvedic- 38 vacant, Homeopathic- 23 vacant) 
which directly affect the delivery of healthcare services at CGHS wellness centres. 
Equally concerning are gaps in t echnical posts such as Medical Lab Technologists (86 
vacant), ECG Technician (8 vacant), and OT Assistants (5 vacant), which compromise 
diagnostic and treatment capacity. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the 
Department urgently prioritize recruitm ent drives, streamline promotion pathways, 
time-bound or assured career progression scheme to keep the morale of the staff high, 
skill upgradation, timely convening of Departmental Promotion Committees (DPCs), 
and rationalisation of recruitment rules to re move bottlenecks, particularly for 
pharmacists whose ineligibility for promotion has created anomalies in staffing. The 
Committee also notes that certain cadres, particularly pharmacists (including Ayurvedic 
and Homeopathic), have limited or no promotion a venues within the existing service 
structure, leading to career stagnation and affecting staff motivation. The Committee, 
therefore, recommends that the Department undertake a cadre restructuring to 
establish a clearer career progression pathway for such c ategories of staff, addressing 
long-standing anomalies which would improve retention and ensure a more motivated 
workforce within the healthcare system. 
(Para 3.1.16) 
35. The Committee further observes that administrative and support functions are 
severely constrained, with 29 out of 31 Accountant posts, 16 out of 25 Administrative 
Officer posts, and all 44 Assistant Depot Manager posts lying vacant, thereby weakening 
financial management, supply -chain oversight, and institutional efficiency. Vacancies in 
posts such as Office Superintendents, Drivers, Translators, and Nursing Midwives also 
hinder day-to-day operations and patient support services. To address these challenges, 
the Committee recommends that the Department institute a robust human resource 
planning framework that aligns sanctioned posts with actual service delivery needs, 
supported by digital HR management systems for real -time monitoring of vacancies and 

247 
 
deployments. A phased but time -bound strategy to fill these posts, coupled with 
improved co ordination between CGHS units and administrative authorities, will be 
essential to ensure uninterrupted healthcare services, strengthen administrative 
efficiency, and enhance the overall responsiveness of CGHS to its growing beneficiary 
base. 
(Para 3.1.17) 
36. The Committee also reiterates its recommendation in 163 rd Report for periodic 
quality control of Generic Medicines at various centers of CGHS through sudden test 
and investigation. A third party assurance mechanism may be evolved in this regard. 
The Committee further reiterates its another recommendation in 155 th and 163rd Report 
to simplify referral procedures further as there is need to significantly reduce the 
burden on patients and their families by eliminating the requirement for repeated visits 
to dispensaries during illness. The Committee notes the provision made allowing CGHS 
pensioner beneficiaries aged 70 and above to directly consult specialists without 
requiring a CGHS referral, however, the Committee reiterates its recommendation to 
further review the guidelines and extend the benefit to all CGHS 
pensioners/beneficiaries aged 60 and above for continuing medical care to CGHS 
beneficiaries. Allowing direct specialist consultations at empanelled private hospitals 
without a CGHS referral would e nsure consistency and continuity of care with the 
existing system, where pensioners/beneficiaries aged 60 and above already receive 
cashless treatment from these hospitals. 
(Para 3.1.18) 
37. The Committee is aware that the Medical Centre at the Parliament House 
Annexe caters to a significant influx of Parliamentarians and dignitaries whose 
professional responsibilities often involve prolonged sittings, frequent travel and 
considerable physical strain. The Committee notes that the absence of a dedicated 
physiotherapist from the CGHS cadre presently limits the availability of timely 
physiotherapy support, often necessitating referrals to external facilities which may 
delay treatment and affect optimal recovery. The Committee, therefore, recommends 
the deployme nt of a full -time physiotherapist from the CGHS cadre at the Medical 
Centre, Parliament House Annexe, so as to ensure prompt physiotherapy care and 
strengthen healthcare support for Parliamentarians and other dignitaries. 
(Para 3.1.19) 
 
38. The Committee o bserves that the contact details of Additional Directors of 
various zones available on the CGHS website and related sources appear to be outdated, 
with no visible updates since September 2025. As a result, beneficiaries, empanelled 
hospitals and other stak eholders often face difficulties in reaching the concerned offices 
due to non -functional or changed telephone numbers. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends that the Department create a dedicated and regularly maintained webpage 
containing the updated contact details of all Additional Directors, including their names, 
telephone numbers and official email IDs. The Committee further recommends that 
these details be reviewed and updated on a quarterly basis to improve accessibility, 

248 
 
strengthen grievance redress al and facilitate better coordination with stakeholders in 
line with ongoing digital health initiatives. 
(Para 3.1.20) 
SAFDURJUNG HOSPITAL (SJH) & VARDHMAN MAHAVIR MEDICAL 
COLLEGE (VMMC): 
 
39. The budget analysis for SJH and VMMC shows that revenue expendi ture has 
been consistently well-utilised, however, capital expenditure continues to face significant 
challenges, with allocations fluctuating sharply due to delays in project clearances, 
phased funding releases, and slow procurement processes. The reductio n of capital 
allocations from ₹238.50 crore at BE 2025 –26 to ₹88.79 crore at RE 2025 –26 highlights 
systemic inefficiencies in planning and execution. Critical projects such as the 
construction of lecture theatres, auditoriums, hostels, and advanced medical  facilities 
have been delayed, undermining institutional growth and service delivery. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department establish a dedicated 
mechanism to expedite inter -departmental clearances, adopt milestone -based funding 
for infr astructure projects, and strengthen procurement planning through framework 
contracts and vendor management. Budgetary projections should be made more 
realistic by aligning estimates with past utilisation trends, and quarterly expenditure 
reviews should be introduced to identify bottlenecks early.  
(Para 3.2.3) 
40. The Committee observes with concern that capital expenditure continues to face 
delays and underutilisation. For instance, in FY 2024 –25, capital spending of ₹148.44 
crore was directed towards proc urement of high -end medical equipment, ICT, 
furniture, and housing projects, whereas in FY 2025 –26, capital utilisation dropped to 
₹67.81 crore due to delays in clearances and slow progress in infrastructure projects. 
The major area of concern is the signi ficant delay and subsequent cost escalation in 
ongoing infrastructure works, most notably the Dwarka Housing Project. The cost for 
this project has increased from the initially approved ₹81.03 Crore to a revised estimate 
of ₹119.20 Crore (an almost 47% inc rease), while the completion date has been 
prolonged from May 2024 to August 2026. The Committee is of the view that the reasons 
cited by the CPWD, such as monsoon waterlogging and the annual Graded Response 
Action Plan (GRAP) construction bans, are forese eable, recurring events in Delhi. The 
Committee recommends that to prevent this recurring issue, the Department should 
strictly make the project management agencies (like CPWD and EPIL) accountable for 
poor execution and ensure it mandatory for them to fac tor predictable local, seasonal, 
and regulatory disruptions into their initial baseline schedules and cost estimates.  
(Para 3.2.7) 
 
41. Earlier also, the Committee in its 134 th, 143rd and 163rd Reports had flagged t he 
issues related to slow pace of constr uction activities and recommended the Department 
to adhere to the timeline for completion of the Dwarka Project. 
(Para 3.2.8) 

249 
 
42. The Committee further observes that the lecture theatre, auditorium, and hostel 
projects remain stalled pending Stage -II forest clearance. The Committee believes that 
the Department needs to establish a dedicated, high -level steering committee to 
aggressively follow up on external dependencies, such as the pending Stage -II MoEFCC 
tree transplantation clearance for the ₹243 Crore VMMC Auditorium and Hostel 
project, ensuring these admin istrative bottlenecks do not translate into further physical 
delays and financial liabilities. This pattern highlights the need for stronger project 
execution mechanisms to ensure that capital allocations translate into timely physical 
achievements. The Co mmittee recommends that the Department adopt a more robust 
monitoring and accountability framework for capital projects. A dedicated inter -agency 
coordination cell should be established to expedite statutory clearances and approvals, 
particularly for projects involving environmental permissions. 
(Para 3.2.9) 
43. In addition to strengthening monitoring and procurement processes, the 
Department should place greater emphasis on project management capacity and risk 
mitigation for large-scale infrastructure works. These delays not only affect utilisation of 
capital allocations but also compromise institutional capacity expansion. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department establish a dedicated project 
management unit within SJH and VMMC to oversee t imelines, coordinate with CPWD 
and other executing agencies, and proactively address risks such as environmental 
clearances, weather disruptions, and regulatory bans. Procurement processes for 
medical equipment and ICT should be streamlined through framewo rk contracts and 
vendor management to avoid delays and ensure timely availability of critical resources. 
Such approach will ensure stabilised capital expenditure, ensure optimal utilisation of 
funds, and support the timely completion of projects such as th e Dwarka housing 
colony, lecture theatre complex, and Maternal & Child Care Centre, thereby 
strengthening institutional capacity and aligning with national health sector priorities. 
(Para 3.2.10) 
 
44. The Committee further finds the clear financial mismatc h between the projected 
demands and the allocated Budget Estimates (BE) for FY 2026 -27. The Department has 
projected a total demand of ₹2424.87 Crore, but the proposed appropriation is only 
₹2170.75 Crore, leaving a substantial shortfall of over ₹254 Crore . Because major 
capital projects like the Maternal & Child Care Centre (MCCC) are merely at the 
conception stage and the Emergency Block ramp is at the tender issuance phase. In view 
thereof, the Committee recommends that the Department must implement a ph ased 
financial prioritization strategy. Available capital funds should be heavily front -loaded 
toward projects with high existing physical progress or critical immediate needs, such as 
the procurement of vital hospital machinery and ICT equipment. Concurre ntly, the 
Department should prepare a rigorous, milestone -based justification detailing the 
progress of the ongoing projects and equipment needs to successfully secure additional 
supplementary grants during the Revised Estimates (RE) stage later in the yea r, 
ensuring patient care and ongoing civil works are not stalled. 
(Para 3.2.11 

250 
 
45. The Committee urges the Department to address the physical and operational 
bottlenecks that are limiting the optimum functioning of SJH and VMMC. Given the 
severe space cons traints and the lack of horizontal expansion scope within the existing 
hospital premises, the Department needs to pivot toward a comprehensive urban spatial 
master plan prioritising brownfield redevelopment with vertical expansion, and 
potentially relocati ng non -clinical administrative functions off -campus to allow 
premium space for patient care. Simultaneously, to resolve the cited manpower 
constraints, the Department should actively leverage "multi -dimensional performance 
monitoring framework." By explici tly linking current staffing deficits to empirical data 
on clinical quality, infrastructure underutilization, and patient satisfaction indicators, 
the Department can build a highly robust, data -driven case to expedite the approval of 
its consolidated proposal for new posts. Such dual approach ensures that existing spaces 
are maximized and that future infrastructure is adequately staffed from the very 
beginning. 
(Para 3.2.13) 
 
46. The analysis of the staffing situation at SJH and VMMC highlights that despite  
the recruitment of 933 posts in 2024 –25 and 509 in 2025 –26, posts such as Clinical 
Research Assistant, Medical Lab Technologists, Senior OT Technician,  Radiographers, 
Clinical Psychologists, Administrative Officers, and Nursing staff show vacancy rates 
ranging from 40% to 100%. This situation directly impacts patient care, teaching 
capacity, and administrative efficiency. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the 
Department strengthen recruitment pipelines by diversifying candidate sourcing, 
introducing bridge training programs to expand the pool of qualified applicants, and 
offering incentives such as retention allowances, career progression pathways, and 
housing facilities to reduce attrition. A more proactive engagement with professional 
councils and universities could also help ensure a steady inflow of qualified candidates 
into specialist and technical roles. 
(Para 3.2.16) 
47. The Committee expresses its concern to combat the high rates of non -joining and 
frequent resignations among medical professi onals, the Department must transition 
from simple recruitment to a holistic retention strategy. This should begin with a data -
driven "Exit and Non -Joiner Survey" to precisely identify why candidates prefer other 
institutions or opportunities abroad. Findin gs should inform a "Total Rewards" 
framework that provides doctors with amenities at par with civil servants, including 
expedited access to the Dwarka housing project, streamlined transportation, and 
dedicated administrative support to minimize non -clinical burdens. For specialized 
technical cadres currently facing up to 100% vacancy, the Department should introduce 
"Targeted Lateral Entry" and clear career progression pathways. By aligning 
emoluments and working conditions with market standards and providi ng a structured 
growth trajectory, the Department can make niche roles at SJH and VMMC more 
competitive against the private sector. 
(Para 3.2.17) 

251 
 
48. The Department should immediately launch a time -bound "Regularization and 
Administrative Reinforcement Pla n" to reduce the systemic reliance on contractual 
staff, which currently includes 232 Nursing Officers and 34 Teaching Staff. Such 
reliance dilutes institutional accountability and "belongingness." Priority must be given 
to fast -tracking the on boarding of  the 1,100 proposed permanent posts and filling 
critical 100% vacancies in leadership and oversight roles, such as the Deputy Director 
Accountant and Office In -charge (Ambulance). A vital component of the strategy is 
stabilizing the "administrative backbon e", specifically addressing the 96% vacancy in 
Assistant Admin Officers and the high shortages in LDC/UDC cadres. By replacing a 
"stop-gap" contractual approach with a robust permanent administrative layer, the 
Department must ensure continuity of service and alleviate the "excessive workload" 
that currently forces clinical staff to manage logistical complexities instead of patient 
care. 
(Para 3.2.18) 
 
DR. RAMMANOHAR LOHIA HOSPITAL (RML), NEW DELHI AND ABVIMS 
 
49. The Committee notes with concern the recurr ing and substantial reductions in 
Capital outlay at the Revised Estimates stage for Dr. RML Hospital and ABVIMS, due 
to delays in completion of the Super Speciality Block (SSB) and non -finalization of 
Medical College tenders, indicating persistent weakness es in project planning, 
tendering, and execution. The volatility in Capital spending undermines infrastructure 
augmentation and long -term capacity expansion. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends that the Department should institute time -bound pre -construction 
clearances, adopt milestone -based monitoring with quarterly physical and financial 
progress reviews, fix accountability for procedural delays, strengthen coordination with 
executing agencies, and create a realistic phasing mechanism for large projects to 
minimise surrender of funds at the RE stage, thereby safeguarding timely infrastructure 
development and service delivery. 
(Para 3.3.2) 
50. In view of the near -completion yet prolonged delay of the Super Speciality Block 
(SSB) at Dr. RML Hospital and ABV IMS, with 96% physical and 89.1% financial 
progress but a time overrun of nearly three years, the Committee recommends that the 
Department should ensure time -bound commissioning roadmap integrating statutory 
clearances, utility connections, equipment insta llation, fire and occupancy approvals, 
and operational readiness, with clearly defined responsibility matrices and weekly 
milestone monitoring at senior level to meet the April 2026 deadline. The Committee, 
further, recommends that the examination and time ly imposition of liquidated damages, 
wherever contractually warranted, should be concluded expeditiously to reinforce 
financial discipline and contractual accountability besides discouraging recurrence of 
time overruns in future infrastructure projects. Th e Committee also advises the 
Department to conduct a post -occupancy audit in late 2026 to identify the specific 

252 
 
bottlenecks that led to the initial 36 -month delay, ensuring these lessons are integrated 
into the hospital‘s broader modernization and redevelopment goals. 
(Para 3.3.5) 
51. With respect to the Medical College building, the Committee notes with concern 
that construction has not commenced due to pending statutory clearances and during 
tendering process, the quoted cost of 44.61% above the estimated  cost necessitating 
revised approval of Project Investment Board. The Committee, therefore, recommends 
that the Department should establish a pre -tender statutory clearance mechanism, and 
undertake realistic cost estimation based on prevailing market condi tions before bid 
invitation to avoid such mistake in future projects. In this regard, the Committee urges 
the Department to expedite appraisal before the Project Investment Board to avoid 
further slippages.  The Department should establish a dedicated inte r-agency liaison cell 
to secure the stalled clearances from the Delhi Fire Service, Forest Department, and 
NDMC, as these are prerequisites for any meaningful progress. 
(Para 3.3.6) 
52. The Committee expresses its concern on the observed pattern of uneven fund 
utilisation and recurring fund surrenders. To move away from the current stop -gap 
approach to creation of infrastructure, the Committee recommends the Department to 
develop a rolling multi -year capital planning framework, may be a five to ten year 
Capital Investment Plan, aligned with projected patient load, academic expansion and 
modernization goals, supported by quarterly milestone -based monitoring and 
strengthened coordination with executing and local authorities, so as to ensure optimal 
utilisation of capital allocations, and systematically address infrastructure gaps. This 
framework should include a Modernization Roadmap that prioritizes the upgrading of 
safety standards and clinical infrastructure in older wards while new blocks are under 
construction, thereby addressing the patient flow constraints and mitigating inadequate 
infrastructure concerns without waiting for the completion of mega-projects. 
(Para 3.3.7) 
53. The Committee acknowledges that while compliance with National Medical 
Commission (NMC) norms necessitates the development of a sports complex for UG/PG 
students. The Committee is of the view to balance student welfare and patient -centric 
infrastructure to achieve a holistic redevelopment plan that strengthens both academic 
standards an d patient care. The Committee, thus, believes that equal priority should 
also be accorded to patient -centric infrastructure such as attendant shelters, clinical 
infrastructure, additional diagnostic block and mobility support. The proposed 
allocation of th e 2020 sqm plot for a sports complex is justified for the mental and 
physical well-being of medical students, but the Committee urges the Department that 
patient attendant facilities should also be given attention and not to be treated as 
auxiliary. Many a ttendants accompany patients from distant areas and require round -
the-clock accommodation near the hospital. The Committee, therefore, recommends 
that the Department should plan to design and construct a spacious, well -equipped 
patient attendant shelter, e nough to cater to number of attendants in proportion to at 
least half of the in -patient beds, with basic amenities such as sanitation, drinking water, 

253 
 
and resting areas, located close to the main hospital building. Alternatively, a free 
electric cart servi ce (shuttle services) should be provided 24×7 to connect the shelter 
with hospital blocks, diagnostic facilities, and parking areas, benefitting elderly 
attendants and patients, improving inclusivity and ease of movement.  
(Para 3.3.9) 
54. The Committee no tes with concern the critical vacancy rate among Senior 
Residents (47.4%) and Group A specialists (25.7%) and is of the view that s uch gaps, 
especially in clinical and frontline categories, are likely to adversely affect service 
delivery, patient safety, a cademic supervision, and overall institutional efficiency. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department adopt a time -bound 
recruitment strategy to fill all critical clinical and technical posts, particularly Senior 
and Junior Residents, nursing  staff, and allied health professionals with an immediate 
transition from emergency contractual hiring to a permanent cadre -strengthening 
model. While contractual engagement may be used as a short -term stop -gap 
arrangement, it should not substitute regular  appointments, as excessive reliance on 
contractual staff may dilute institutional accountability and continuity. The Committee, 
further, recommends that the Department should adopt a Total Support model for 
medical professionals to attract and retain qualified doctors and specialists in the public 
health system by providing improved service conditions in terms of career progression 
avenues, academic incentives, etc. and providing parity with civil servants in terms of 
accommodation, transport facilities  and administrative helpers, thereby allowing 
doctors to focus on clinical duties rather than non-medical tasks. 
(Para 3.3.11) 
55. The Committee further observes that persistent human resource shortages, 
coupled with unregulated and alarmingly prolonged worki ng hours of Junior and 
Senior Residents, sometimes extending up to 24 -36 hours at a stretch, pose serious risks 
to both healthcare providers and patients. The Committee, therefore, recommends that 
the Department should implement a mandatory working -hour re gulatory framework 
for Resident Doctors to minimize fatigue -induced clinical errors and to safeguard their 
mental and physical well -being, drawing an analogy from regulated working -hour 
norms in other critical sectors such as fatigue management protocols u sed for 
commercial pilots in  civil aviation. The Committee further urges the Department to 
devise a transparent mechanism to ensure the presence and accountability of senior 
faculty members and professors during prescribed working hours to provide adequate  
supervision and enhanced clinical training to Resident Doctors, and to prevent 
unsupervised decision -making in critical cases. The Committee observes that majority 
work load is handled by the Resident doctors who form the backbone of the health care 
delivery system but the requisite hand holding by the senior faculty members is absent. 
The Committee recommends for fixing of strong accountability amongst the regular and 
senior faculty members.  
(Para 3.3.12) 

254 
 
56. The Committee believes, to mitigate the syste mic shortage of human resources 
across Groups B and C, and to compliment administrative work, the Department should 
adopt responsible usage of AI and ICT tools to optimize hospital administration, patient 
data management, diagnostic support, and routine mo nitoring. Such technological 
integration, coupled with structured training for staff, can enhance productivity, reduce 
administrative burden on clinical personnel, and partially mitigate human resource 
constraints. 
(Para 3.3.13) 
LADY HARDINGE MEDICAL COLLEGE (LHMC) & SMT. S.K. HOSPITAL 
57. The budgetary trends of Lady Hardinge Medical College (LHMC) and Smt. 
Sucheta Kriplani Hospital (SSKH) indicate a recurring pattern of under -utilisation 
under the Capital head. In FY 2024-25, the Capital allocation was reduced sharply from 
₹101 crore at BE stage to ₹51.50 crore at RE stage, with actual expenditure declining 
further to ₹31.02 crore, largely on account of re -tendering and single responsive bids 
leading to deferment of procurement proposals. Further, in FY 20 26-27, against a 
projected Capital demand of ₹171.60 crore, only ₹92.50 crore has been allocated. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department institute a robust pre-tendering 
and project appraisal mechanism, including advance preparation of de tailed project 
reports (DPRs), realistic cost estimation, wider vendor participation, and strict 
procurement timelines to prevent repeated surrender or deferment of capital funds. A 
quarterly monitoring framework at the level of the Ministry may be introdu ced to track 
progress of major works and procurement proposals, ensuring that capital allocations 
translate into tangible asset creation aligned with institutional expansion plans. 
(Para 3.4.4) 
 
58. Further, the proposed combined project for construction o f UG/PG hostels and 
extension of the IPD Block (96 beds) at an estimated cost of ₹498 crore remains at the 
planning stage, while the allocated capital budget for 2026 -27 appears insufficient to 
meaningfully advance such a major infrastructure initiative. G iven the growing 
academic and clinical load of LHMC and SSKH, and the projected overall requirement 
of ₹1,028.37 crore against an allocation of ₹795.90 crore, the Committee recommends 
that the Department prioritise phased implementation of critical infrastructure projects, 
beginning with those directly augmenting patient care capacity and residential facilities 
for students and resident doctors. Adequate front -loading of capital funds, coupled with 
time-bound administrative approvals, may be ensured to avoi d cost escalation and 
project delays. Aligning capital planning with realistic budgeting and execution capacity 
is essential to strengthen institutional infrastructure and improve service delivery 
outcomes. 
(Para 3.4.5) 
59. The Committee expresses with con cern that out of a sanctioned strength of 2,225 
posts, 652 posts (nearly 29%) remain vacant in the institution, with particularly 
alarming shortages in critical and supervisory categories such as Tutors in the College 

255 
 
of Nursing (91% vacancy), Administrati ve Officer (100%), Radiotherapy Technologist 
(100%), OT Technicians and related cadres (70 –80%), Nursing Officers (31%), and 
Doctors in the Teaching Cadre (21%). Such extensive vacancies, especially in teaching, 
nursing, and technical posts directly linked  to patient care and academic delivery, are 
likely to adversely affect hospital efficiency, quality of clinical services, research 
productivity, and faculty workload. Although the Department has initiated recruitment 
for over 262 posts and is processing do cument verification in a substantial number of 
cases, the Committee recommends the adoption of a time -bound, mission -mode 
approach to clear all pending vacancies, including those on hold due to court cases, non -
finalisation of Recruitment Rules, or absence  of feeder cadres. Recruitment Rules 
should be urgently notified wherever pending, and a periodic review mechanism may be 
instituted to prevent recurrence of structural bottlenecks such as promotional 
stagnation or backlog vacancies. The Committee also rec ommends for strengthening the 
manpower availability by instituting an independent study so that recruitment of 
specialist doctors is done on a larger scale to provide adequate health support to the 
patients and to prevent backlog and overcrowding.  
(Para 3.4.9) 
60. The Committee is of the view that while contractual engagement may serve as a 
temporary stop -gap arrangement, it should not substitute regular appointments, as 
long-term reliance on contractual staff may undermine accountability, institutional 
continuity, and morale. Priority should be accorded to filling up of posts directly linked 
to patient care, such as nursing, OT, laboratory, and radiotherapy staff, to ensure that 
expanding infrastructure is matched with adequate human resources. 
(Para 3.4.10) 
61. The Committee further observes that faculty members are currently burdened 
with excessive academic, clinical, examination, and research responsibilities due to 
vacant teaching posts, even though the student -teacher ratio may formally meet 
regulatory norms. Given the substantial clinical workload and implementation of 
multiple national health programmes, the Department should ensure expeditious filling 
of teaching cadre posts and strengthen supportive administrative structures to enable 
faculty to focus on academic excellence and patient care. In addition, improved working 
conditions, including adequate accommodation, transport facilities, career progression 
avenues, and structured leadership opportunities for doctors with managerial 
qualifications, may be considered to enhance retention and motivation.  
(Para 3.4.11) 
62. The Committee also believes that duty hours of Junior and Senior Residents 
should be rationalised and strictly regulated to prevent fatigue -related clinical errors, 
with a mechanism en suring mandatory supervision by senior faculty during prescribed 
working hours. To partially mitigate human resource constraints and improve 
efficiency, the Department may promote adoption of AI and ICT -based hospital 
management systems, coupled with structured training for staff.  
(Para 3.4.12) 
 

256 
 
63. The Committee recognises the rising burden of mental health concerns, and 
recommends for promotion of institutional wellness initiatives including yoga and 
elements of Indian Knowledge Systems among students an d healthcare workers. The 
committee notes with concern that despite availability of a large number of applicants 
for Senior Residency in LHMC, the intake is low, resulting in long working hours and 
delays for patients.  
(Para 3.4.13) 
KALAWATI SARAN CHILDREN‘S HOSPITAL (KSCH) 
 
64. The Committee expresses with concern the recurring pattern of downward 
revision and under -utilisation of funds in Kalawati Saran Children‘s Hospital (KSCH), 
particularly under the Capital head. In FY 2024 –25, the Budget Estimate (B E) of ₹180 
crore was reduced to ₹155.85 crore at the Revised Estimate (RE) stage, with Capital 
outlay declining sharply by over 50 per cent from ₹7.94 crore to ₹3.91 crore. Although 
the allocation improved in FY 2025 –26, with Capital outlay increasing from  ₹8.50 crore 
at BE to ₹14.40 crore at RE, the actual utilisation remained significantly low at only 
₹2.74 crore as on 12.02.2026. The Committee is anguished to find notes that nearly 30 
tenders on the Government e -Marketplace (GeM) had to be cancelled in t he past three 
financial years due to lack of competition and administrative constraints, delaying 
procurement of essential medical equipment. In view of these trends, the Committee 
recommends that the Department strengthen procurement planning and tenderin g 
processes through wider vendor outreach, standardisation of technical specifications, 
and improved pre -bid consultations to ensure greater participation and timely 
finalisation of tenders. 
(Para 3.5.5) 
 
65. The Committee also emphasises the need for a ro bust monitoring mechanism to 
ensure that capital allocations are translated into timely procurement of essential 
equipment and infrastructure, thereby improving service delivery in the premier 
paediatric institution. 
(Para 3.5.6) 
66. The Committee further observes that KSCH is functioning under considerable 
infrastructural and operational stress, with the existing OPD facilities being inadequate 
to manage the daily patient load and indoor wards frequently operating beyond optimal 
bed capacity. The Committee  also notes with concern the finding of the Central Public 
Works Department (CPWD) that the old KSCH building is in a dilapidated  condition 
and beyond repair, necessitating the phased shifting of wards and departments to the 
renovated premises of the old SSKH building of LHMC, which may pose transitional 
challenges in maintaining uninterrupted patient care. The Committee takes note of the 
Department‘s proposal for upgradation of KSCH from 394 beds to 894 beds at an 
estimated cost of ₹496.94 crore, which is currently at the planning stage. The Committee 
recommends that the project be accorded priority and implemented in a time -bound 

257 
 
manner with adequate budgetary provision in subsequent years to avoid delays and cost 
escalations.  
(Para 3.5.7) 
 
67. The Committee takes note that the expansion of sub -specialty services over the 
past decade has significantly increased the demand for intensive and specialised 
paediatric care, while the existing human resource strength and critical care 
infrastructure remain inade quate. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the 
Department prioritise strengthening of human resources, particularly nursing 
personnel, OT technicians, and other essential support staff, to maintain appropriate 
patient–nurse ratios and ensure safe cli nical practices. Simultaneously, steps must be 
taken to operationalise specialised services such as cardiothoracic and vascular surgery 
(CTVS) and neurosurgery, along with the establishment of dedicated 24×7 emergency 
operation theatres and critical care u nits. The Committee further recommends 
implementation of effective referral protocols and patient -streamlining mechanisms to 
manage overcrowding, alongside continuous monitoring of key performance indicators 
such as bed occupancy, OPD trends, hospital -acquired infection surveillance, and 
regular clinical audits to ensure improved quality of paediatric healthcare services. The 
Committee strongly recommends for additional neonatal facility so that specialised 
neonatal care is extended on a larger scale. The C ommittee also recommends for 
introduction of fellowship programs in collaboration with established bodies like the 
National Neonatology Forum (NNF) which is a over four decade old leading body with 
over ten thousand Neonatologists with global expertise. Th is fellowship program would 
help to impart the requisite skills on a larger scale to the post graduate doctors 
specialising in Paediatrics.  
(Para 3.5.8) 
 
68. The Committee observes with concern that several critical posts in KSCH remain 
vacant, including 100 per cent vacancies in the posts of Paediatrician (Non -teaching 
Specialist), Nursing Superintendent, and Prosthetist & Orthotist, besides significant 
shortages in the Operation Theatre cadre where 10 out of 16 sanctioned posts are 
vacant. Such large num ber of vacancy in key clinical and supervisory positions must 
have adversely affected the quality of patient care, operational efficiency of surgical 
services, and effective nursing administration. The Committee also notes that certain 
nursing posts are be ing filled on a contractual basis, which, though useful as a stop -gap 
arrangement, however such arrangement cannot substitute for a stable and accountable 
regular workforce. While the Department has reported that vacancies are being 
regularly referred to r ecruiting agencies such as UPSC, SSC, and AIIMS, the 
Committee recommends that a time -bound recruitment strategy be adopted to fill up 
vacant critical posts on priority, particularly those directly linked to patient care and 
hospital management. Simultaneously, the Committee urges the Department to consider 
improving working conditions and career progression opportunities for doctors and 
healthcare professionals, including lucrative salary, adequate accommodation, transport 
facilities, and leadership pathwa ys for professionals with managerial expertise, in order 

258 
 
to attract and retain skilled personnel in public health institutions. The Committee 
reiterates that contractual employees should either be regularized or that regular 
recruitment be undertaken on a priority basis to provide specialized healthcare to 
children, considering the critical nature of health services and the level of accountability 
involved in such services. 
(Para 3.5.10) 
69. The Committee further notes that a number of posts remain vacant d ue to 
pending framing or amendment of Recruitment Rules, including posts such as Nursing 
Superintendent, Assistant Nursing Superintendent, Administrative Officer, Clinical 
Psychologist, Physiotherapists, and technical supervisory positions. Such procedural  
delays in finalising Recruitment Rules create structural bottlenecks in the recruitment 
and promotion process, thereby affecting institutional functioning. The Committee 
therefore recommends that the Department must expedite the finalisation of these 
Recruitment Rules in a mission -mode manner and ensure that Departmental Promotion 
Committees are convened promptly thereafter.  
(Para 3.5.11) 
70. In addition, the Committee urges the Department to explore greater use of AI 
and ICT-based hospital management and administrative tools to enhance efficiency and 
reduce the administrative burden on clinical staff. Given the increasing stress and 
mental health challenges within the medical community, the Committee emphasises the 
importance of strengthening psychiatric services and promoting wellness initiatives, 
including yoga and elements of Indian Knowledge Systems, for healthcare workers and 
students, thereby fostering a more supportive and resilient institutional environment. 
(Para 3.5.12) 
PRADHAN MANTRI SWASTHAYA SURAKSHA YOJANA (PMSSY) 
71. The Committee expresses concern that allocations under the Pradhan Mantri 
Swasthya Suraksha Yojana (PMSSY) have witnessed a recurring pattern of reduction at 
the Revised Estimate stage, particularly under the Capital head, with t he allocation 
declining from ₹2140 crore at BE 2024 –25 to ₹1666 crore at RE stage and again from 
₹2140 crore at BE 2025 –26 to ₹1430 crore at RE stage. The Committee further observes 
that such reductions are largely attributable to delays in commencement an d execution 
of major infrastructure projects, including AIIMS at Darbhanga, Madurai and Rewari, 
due to arising from issues such as non -availability of encumbrance -free land, delays in 
statutory and environmental clearances, and site -related constraints. Th e Committee, 
therefore, strongly recommends that the Department must evolve a more robust pre -
project preparedness framework in close coordination with State Governments to 
ensure that key prerequisites such as land availability, statutory approvals and ba sic 
infrastructure facilities are secured prior to budgetary provision. This would help align 
financial allocations with actual implementation capacity. The Committee notes with 
concern that Regional AIIMS receive only a fraction of the allocation provided  to 

259 
 
AIIMS Delhi, yet they are expected to run all programmes on the lines of AIIMS Delhi 
despite significant budgetary constraints. The Committee recommends that a proper 
analysis be undertaken to ensure that the Regional AIIMS do not suffer due to 
inadequate budgetary allocations. 
(Para 3.6.10) 
 
72. The Committee observes that while the PMSSY has significantly expanded 
tertiary healthcare infrastructure in the country, with Six AIIMS approved in Phase I 
fully functional with all key hospital facilities and  services; 12  AIIMS with either 
construction work completed (9) or major part of construction work completed (3) and 
teaching learning & research, IPD/OPD services operationalised; and several 
Government Medical College upgradation projects completed, howe ver, a some projects 
remain pending or are progressing slowly. In particular, AIIMS projects at Madurai, 
Awantipora, Bibinagar and Rewari are still under construction, while AIIMS 
Darbhanga is yet to commence the main civil works. The Committee, therefore,  
recommends that the Department must establish a clearly defined time -bound 
implementation framework with periodic milestone -based monitoring for all ongoing 
projects so that the remaining AIIMS and upgradation works are completed within the 
stipulated timelines. The Committee also emphasises the need for stronger coordination 
with executing agencies and State Governments to ensure that infrastructural and 
administrative bottlenecks do not delay the operationalisation of these institutions 
intended to improve access to tertiary healthcare and medical education. 
(Para 3.6.11) 
 
73. The Committee  takes note with satisfaction that although significant progress 
has been made under the upgradation component of PMSSY, with 71 out of 75 
Government Medical College p rojects completed, a few projects are still pending due to 
factors such as relocation of sites, delays in preparation of Detailed Project Reports and 
administrative approvals. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department 
should expedite the com pletion of the remaining projects, including those at Patna 
Medical College & Hospital, Government Medical College Surat, the proposed Super 
Speciality Block at Government Medical College Keonjhar in Odisha and the 
upgradation project at UCMS -GTB Hospital,  Delhi. The Committee further 
recommends that greater attention be paid to timely preparation and approval of DPRs 
and early finalisation of executing agencies to avoid procedural delays in the future. 
(Para 3.6.12) 
 
74. The Committee is concerned to note the significant shortage of human resources 
across AIIMS institutions, with faculty vacancies of about 37.75 per cent and non -
faculty vacancies of about 31.52 per cent across both established and new AIIMS. The 
Committee emphasises that such large vacancie s may undermine the effective utilisation 
of the extensive infrastructure created under PMSSY and adversely affect the quality of 
healthcare services, medical education and research. The Committee, therefore, 

260 
 
recommends that the Department take time -bound measures to fill up these vacancies 
through accelerated recruitment processes, strengthening of existing mechanisms such 
as the Standing Selection Committees, NORCET and Common Recruitment 
Examinations, and by providing improved working conditions, competi tive  pay scale 
career progression opportunities and adequate residential and institutional facilities to 
attract and retain qualified medical professionals, particularly in relatively remote 
locations. 
(Para 3.6.13) 
 
75. The Committee takes into account t he fact that retention of faculty and 
healthcare personnel in newly established AIIMS, particularly those located in remote 
or less developed regions, remains a persistent challenge. The Committee, in this regard,
