recommends that the Department must formul ate a comprehensive human resource 
retention strategy for such institutions, including incentives for serving in difficult 
locations, improved residential and transport facilities, enhanced academic and research 
opportunities, and appropriate career progre ssion pathways. The Committee also 
emphasises the importance of reducing excessive dependence on contractual staff and 
ensuring that regular appointments are prioritised so as to maintain institutional 
continuity, accountability and a stronger sense of belonging among the workforce. 
(Para 3.6.14) 
 
76. The Committee wishes to highlight that procurement and timely installation of 
advanced medical equipment remains a challenge in several AIIMS due to issues such as 
single tenders, proprietary items and delays in import of specialised equipment. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department should develop a streamlined 
and standardised procurement framework with advance planning and coordinated 
procurement support so that equipment is installed in a t imely manner and the newly 
established AIIMS are able to deliver their full range of clinical, diagnostic and teaching 
services at the earliest. The Committee also recommends that greater use of digital 
technologies, artificial intelligence and ICT -based a dministrative tools must be 
promoted within these institutions to improve efficiency in hospital management, 
monitoring and research activities, while ensuring adequate training of staff for their 
responsible and effective use. The Committee is given to un derstand that centralized 
procurement by HLL/HITES has, in several instances, resulted in the procurement of 
inferior-quality equipment, which had to be returned or replaced. The Committee 
recommends strong oversight of the procurement process and the func tioning of 
HLL/HITES to ensure that substandard critical healthcare equipment is not procured. 
 
(Para 3.6.15) 
77. On being asked whether the Government has studied the model of Community Health 
Centre in Ghanerao, Rajasthan, recognized as the Number One CH C in terms of upkeep and 
cleanliness in the entire state of Rajasthan and as a mark of encouraging Community health 
participation, the Department stated that any healthcare facility meeting the certification 

261 
 
norms as per the laid criteria by Central Qualit y Supervisory Committee (CQSC) can apply 
for quality certification under NQAS. However, as per our SaQsham database, NQAS 
application for CHC Ghanerao has not been received. The Committee notes with concern 
the evasive response of the Department regarding a Community Health Centre (CHC) 
that has established high standards of cleanliness/treatment through a PPP model. 
Instead of encouraging such initiatives as role models, the Department has mentioned 
another procedural step, despite the fact that the CHC is  not seeking any formal 
validation. The Committee recommends for a study of the referred CHC as a best 
practice in Rajasthan and for issuing advisories to other State Governments to 
encourage adoption of similar models in more CHCs so as to engage larger s ociety to 
enable it to contribute towards strengthening of the PHCs/CHCs. 
(Para 3.6.19) 
 
78. The Committee appreciates that several innovative and impactful best practices 
have been developed across different AIIMS institutions in areas such as hospital 
administration, digital governance, patient care, community outreach and academic 
management. These include initiatives such as the Online Emergency Triage System at 
AIIMS Raipur, the SAHAS integrated administrative system and SANTUSHT feedback 
portal at AII MS New Delhi, digital and AI -enabled systems in clinical services, 
community health programmes, and innovative patient support services. The 
Committee recommends that the Department systematically document, evaluate and 
institutionalise these best practice s and develop a formal framework for their 
dissemination and replication across all AIIMS and other major public healthcare 
institutions. Establishing a structured knowledge -sharing mechanism and periodic 
conclaves would enable cross -learning among institu tions and ensure that successful 
innovations are adopted more widely to enhance efficiency, transparency and quality of 
patient care. 
(Para 3.6.22) 
 
79. The Committee observes that many of the best practices implemented by 
individual AIIMS institutions are  leveraging digital technologies, artificial intelligence, 
health information management systems and mobile applications to improve hospital 
administration, patient services, emergency care, academic governance and grievance 
redressal. The Committee recommends that the Department formulate a comprehensive 
digital transformation strategy for all AIIMS and similar tertiary care institutions, with 
a view to integrating hospital management systems, digital feedback platforms, clinical 
decision-support tools and  real-time monitoring systems across the network. Such an 
integrated approach would not only strengthen governance and administrative 
efficiency but also improve patient experience, transparency and accountability in 
service delivery across these premier institutions. 
(Para 3.6.23) 
 
80. The Committee further notes that while several AIIMS have demonstrated 
notable achievements in areas such as hospital cleanliness, patient satisfaction, infection 

262 
 
control and innovative community outreach initiatives, there is currently no uniform 
mechanism to benchmark or evaluate the performance of AIIMS institutions across key 
parameters. While recognising that many new AIIMS are still in the process of attaining 
full functionality, the Committee recommends that the Depart ment gradually evolve a 
transparent and objective performance assessment framework based on measurable 
indicators such as quality of patient care, infrastructure utilisation, academic output, 
research contribution, cleanliness standards, patient satisfacti on and community health 
impact. Such a benchmarking mechanism, when implemented in a phased and 
supportive manner, would encourage healthy competition, promote accountability and 
help maintain uniformly high standards across all AIIMS institutions in the country. 
(Para 3.6.24) 
 
81. The Committee finds that while several healthcare institutions across the country 
demonstrate exemplary performance in areas such as cleanliness, patient care, 
specialised services and community outreach, there is presently no un iform or centrally 
notified mechanism under the Government of India for recognising health institutions as 
Centres of Excellence across all specialties. The Committee further observes that 
recognition of exemplary performance of institutions is presently p rogramme-specific 
and largely confined to particular national health programmes. In this context, the 
Committee is of the view that establishing a structured framework for identifying and 
recognising outstanding healthcare institutions would encourage inno vation, healthy 
competition and the adoption of best practices across the healthcare system. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department should explore the feasibility of 
developing a broad and transparent framework for recognition of Centres of Excellence 
in different areas of healthcare based on clearly defined parameters such as quality of 
infrastructure, affordability and accessibility of quality healthcare services, service 
delivery standards, research output, training capacity and public health impact.  
(Para 3.6.25) 
 
82. The Committee observes that newborn health interventions in the country are 
presently implemented under the broader framework of the National Health Mission 
through facilities such as Sick Newborn Care Units (SNCUs) and N ewborn Stabilization 
Units (NBSUs), and that there is no standalone national programme or dedicated 
Centres of Excellence in the field of Neonatology. Considering the increasing importance 
of specialised neonatal care in reducing infant mortality and addre ssing complications 
related to premature births, congenital disorders and high -risk deliveries, the 
Committee is of the  considered view that focused institutional strengthening in this 
domain is the need of hour. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the 
Department should examine the feasibility of developing a more structured approach 
for strengthening neonatal care services, including the identification and development of 
select tertiary care institutions, developing public -private partnership models , and 
promoting such institutions as specialised referral and training hubs for advanced 
neonatal care, research and capacity building. The Committee recommends for 

263 
 
introduction of advanced fellowship programs in neonatal care besides specialised 
courses like DM etc.  
(Para 3.6.26) 
83. The Committee also notes that neonatal mortality can be significantly reduced by 
strengthening the capacity of local doctors, nurses and frontline health workers through 
targeted training in essential newborn care, neonatal r esuscitation, early identification 
of complications and timely referral. Continuous skill development of healthcare 
providers, particularly at peripheral health facilities, would ensure prompt and effective 
management of neonatal conditions and improve survival and quality of newborn care.  
 
(Para 3.6.27) 
NATIONAL AIDS AND STD CONTROL PROGRAMME (NACP) 
   
84. The Committee expresses the concern that the most pressing risk to achieving the 
Sustainable Development Goal (SDG) 3.3 by 2030 is the low budgetary al location and 
subsequent mid-year compression. The data reveals a drastic reduction in the Revised 
Estimate (RE) for FY 2025 -26, dropping from ₹3442.77 crore to ₹2661.50 crore, and a 
projected shortfall of ₹186.13 crore against NACO's demand for BE 2026-27. To prevent 
these recurring RE cuts, which are often a by-product of slow fund utilization in the first 
two quarters, the Committee recommends that the Department must implement a 
rigorous, month-on-month expenditure tracking mechanism, particularly for utilization 
of ₹2069.62 crore ear marked for Grants -in-Aid and ₹987.59 crore for Supplies and 
Materials. The Committee recommends that the Department should immediately begin 
compiling empirical data on the expanded Sampoorna Suraksha Strategy and the new 
NMC mandate for Anti-retroviral Tr eatment (ART)  centers in Medical Colleges to 
build an unassailable, evidence -based case for securing the required supplementary 
funds at the RE 2026-27 stage. 
(Para 3.7.9) 
85. The Committee believes that to maximize the impact of the preventive and care 
strategies without proportionally increasing the budget, the Department must 
aggressively pursue structural convergence. The Committee, therefore, recommends 
that the proposed interoperability of the SOCH-MIS with the NIKSHAY (TB) and RCH 
(Reproductive and C hild Health) portals should be fast -tracked into a unified, case -
based surveillance dashboard. This will allow for the tracking of individual -level cohort 
data, preventing duplication of efforts and ensuring holistic care for co -infections. 
Additionally, t he Committee is of the view that targeting high -risk populations, 
particularly People Who Inject Drugs (PWID), requires moving beyond isolated health 
initiatives. The Department should formalize a binding, inter -ministerial joint action 
plan with the Minis try of Social Justice and Empowerment (MoSJE) and the Drug De -
Addiction Program (DDAP) to pool resources, co -locate Opioid Substitution Therapy 
(OST) centers, and seamlessly connect vulnerable demographics to lifelong ART 
networks. 
(Para 3.7.10) 

264 
 
86. The Co mmittee is of the firm opinion that to achieve the ambitious SDG 3.3 
target of reducing the HIV incidence rate to 0.025 per 1000 uninfected population by 
2030, the Department must aggressively decentralize its screening and prevention 
framework beyond trad itional clinical settings. The Committee believes that the 
immediate priority should be the rapid universalization of the Sampoorna Suraksha 
Strategy across all 4,571 ICTCs, transitioning them from passive testing centers into 
active hubs that offer a comp rehensive preventive package for all at -risk clients. 
Because the programmatic focus must capture hard -to-reach demographics, such as 
out-of-school youth, and populations in slums, peri-urban, and rural areas, Information, 
Education, and Communication (IEC ) campaigns need to pivot from generic national 
broadcasts to hyper -localized, digital outreach involving regional social media 
influencers and community peer leaders. Furthermore, to complement these behavioral 
interventions, the Committee recommends that  the Department must expedite its 
evidence generation and rollout of "newer bio -medical prevention kits" (such as Pre -
Exposure Prophylaxis). The Committee recommends the Department to ensure that the 
cutting-edge biomedical tools are not restricted to urba n Centers of Excellence but are 
seamlessly integrated into the grassroots Link Worker Scheme to ensure equitable 
access for the most vulnerable populations. 
(Para 3.7.11) 
GLOBAL FUND GRANT UNDER COVID-19 RESPONSE MECHANISM 2021 
87. The Committee notes unde rutilization of allocated funds in FY 2024 –25, with 
only ₹0.51 crore spent against an allocation of ₹125 crore due to minimal level of 
COVID pandemic. The Committee observes that while the re -programming of activities 
was necessary due to overlapping suppo rt from ECRP-2, PMCARES, and PM-ABHIM, 
the delay in consultations, approvals, and procurement processes significantly 
compressed the expenditure window. This pattern highlights the need for a more agile 
procurement and utilisation framework for externally funded grants. The Committee, 
desire that the Department should institutionalise a dedicated fast -track mechanism for 
Global Fund -supported programmes, including pre -approved technical specifications 
and tender templates to reduce lead time, a standing coo rdination cell with State 
Governments and NHM divisions to expedite re -programming consultations, and 
quarterly utilisation reviews with escalation protocols to ensure improved fund 
absorption. Such measures will also enhance credibility with international  partners, 
ensuring India remains a trusted recipient of global health financing. 
(Para 3.8.4) 
88. The Committee notes that while the allocation under the Global Fund grant was 
significantly enhanced in FY 2025 –26 to ₹355.07 crore, with ₹204.66 crore utilised up to 
January 2026, the grant period is scheduled to conclude by June 2026, leaving only 
₹96.86 crore proposed for FY 2026 –27. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the 
Department should consolidate the C19RM -funded activities into the broader 
framework of the National Health Mission and pandemic preparedness initiatives. This 
should include m apping all equipment and infrastructure procured under the grant to 

265 
 
NHM facilities for sustained use, aligning residual funds with priority areas such as 
laboratory strengthening, surveillance, and workforce training, and documenting 
lessons from re -programming delays to inform future negotiations with global health 
financing partners. Such measures will ensure enduring value by transitioning from a 
pandemic-specific intervention to a systemic strengthening initiative aligned with India‘s 
National Health Policy targets. 
(Para 3.8.5) 
FAMILY WELFARE SCHEMES 
  
89. The Committee observes that the overall allocation for Family Welfare 
Schemes has remained broadly stable over the last three financial years, with ₹620.48 
crore at BE 2025 –26, revised upward to ₹645. 48 crore at RE stage, and ₹643.46 crore 
proposed at BE 2026 –27. However, expenditure of ₹462.95 crore up to 17.02.2026 
indicates that a considerable portion of funds remained to be utilised during the 
remaining period of the financial year. At the componen t level, variations in utilisation 
are also observed, particularly under Information, Education and Communication on 
the PNDT Act and the WHO -supported National Polio Surveillance Project. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department strengthen  planning and 
monitoring mechanisms for these components to ensure timely and optimal utilisation of 
allocated resources. Greater emphasis may also be placed on outcome -oriented 
implementation of IEC campaigns, PNDT Act awareness activities, and surveillan ce 
initiatives so that financial allocations translate into measurable improvements in 
gender balance, population stabilisation, and disease surveillance outcomes. 
(Para 3.9.3) 
90. The Committee recognises that Accredited Social Health Activists 
(ASHAs) an d other frontline health workers constitute the backbone of the effective 
implementation of Family Welfare Schemes, particularly in areas such as awareness 
generation, distribution of contraceptives, community mobilisation, and facilitating 
access to repro ductive and maternal health services. Their role is especially critical in 
ensuring last -mile delivery of services under initiatives such as mass IEC campaigns, 
population stabilisation programmes, and family planning interventions. In this context, 
the Co mmittee is of the view that the sustained success of these schemes depends 
significantly on the motivation, retention, and capacity of these grassroots functionaries. 
The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department must take appropriate 
measures, in coordination with States/UTs, to make the remuneration, incentives, and 
career progression avenues of ASHAs and other health workers more attractive and 
predictable. Strengthening their financial security, providing opportunities for skill 
upgradation, and creating clearer pathways for professional advancement would not 
only improve workforce retention but also enhance programme effectiveness and ensure 
better outcomes of Family Welfare initiatives at the grassroots level. 
(Para 3.9.4) 

266 
 
ESTABLISHMENT AND STRENGTHENING OF NCDC BRANCHES AND HEALTH 
INITIATIVES, INTER SECTORAL CO -ORDINATION FOR PREPARATION AND 
CONTROL OF ZOONOTIC DISEASES AND OTHER NEGLECTED TROPICAL 
DISEASES, SURVEILLANCE OF VIRAL HEPATITIS, ANTI MICROBIAL 
RESISTANCE 
91. The Committee finds that the allocation under the scheme for Establishment and 
Strengthening of NCDC Branches and related health initiatives has shown fluctuations 
during the last two financial years, with the Budget Estimate of ₹52.00 crore in 2024 –25 
being reduced to ₹30.37 crore at RE stage and the BE of ₹54.01 crore in 2025 –26 being 
revised downward to ₹35.00 crore. The Committee observes that such reductions were 
primarily attributed to operational constraints including procurement timelines, 
onboarding delays, HR attritio n and transitional challenges in the revised fund flow 
mechanism. While acknowledging the efforts of the Department in implementing 
multiple national programmes under the scheme, the Committee recommends that the 
Department streamline administrative and pr ocurement processes, strengthen financial 
planning, and ensure timely release and utilisation of Grants -in-Aid so that critical 
public health initiatives related to zoonotic diseases, antimicrobial resistance, and viral 
hepatitis surveillance are implemented without interruption. 
(Para 3.10.10) 
92. The Committee appreciates the progress made under the National One Health 
Programme for Prevention and Control of Zoonoses, particularly the formation of 
Zoonosis Committees in 35 States, the establishment of 75 sentinel surveillance sites, and 
the onboarding of the programme onto the Integrated Health Information Platform. 
Given the increasing risk of zoonotic disease outbreaks and the lessons learned from 
recent pandemics, the Committee emphasises the importance  of strengthening the One 
Health framework through deeper integration of human, animal, and environmental 
health surveillance systems. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the 
Department accelerate the development of a unified digital surveillance architecture and 
expand sentinel surveillance sites and regional coordination mechanisms so as to enable 
real-time data sharing, early detection of emerging pathogens, and coordinated response 
across sectors and States/UTs. 
(Para 3.10.11) 
93. The Committee appreciates that the scheme plays a crucial role in strengthening 
disease surveillance infrastructure, including the expansion of laboratories for 
antimicrobial resistance detection, rabies diagnosis, leptospirosis testing, and viral 
hepatitis surveillance a cross several medical colleges and institutions. The Committee 
also observes that under the AMR containment programme, laboratory networks have 
been strengthened in 70 state medical colleges, while surveillance of antimicrobial use 
has been established in 45 hospitals. Considering the growing global threat of 
antimicrobial resistance and the need for robust diagnostic capacity, the Committee 
recommends that the Department further expand laboratory networks and surveillance 
systems, particularly in underserv ed regions, while strengthening national and state 

267 
 
reference laboratories and ensuring regular training, quality assurance mechanisms, 
and antimicrobial stewardship practices across healthcare institutions. 
(Para 3.10.12) 
94. The Committee observes that th e establishment of NCDC State Branches is a 
critical step towards strengthening the country‘s infectious disease surveillance and 
outbreak response architecture. At present, eight NCDC branches are operational, while 
several others are at different stages of construction and are expected to become 
functional by 2027 –28 under PM -ABHIM. While appreciating the progress achieved, 
the Committee stresses the importance of timely completion and operationalisation of 
these branches, which will play a vital role in real-time disease surveillance, outbreak 
investigation, and coordination with State health systems. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends that the Department intensify coordination with States/UTs and 
implementing agencies to expedite construction activitie s, ensure adequate staffing and 
laboratory infrastructure, and make the upcoming NCDC branches fully functional 
within the stipulated timelines so as to strengthen the national public health 
preparedness and response system. 
(Para 3.10.13) 
95. The Committe e notes that the scheme encompasses several emerging priority 
areas such as pandemic preparedness, genomic surveillance, environmental and 
wastewater surveillance, and research on zoonotic spillovers. The Committee further 
observes that the experience gained during the COVID-19 pandemic has highlighted the 
importance of predictive surveillance systems, rapid diagnostics, and integrated 
research platforms to detect and contain emerging infectious diseases at an early stage. 
In this regard, the Committee reco mmends that the Department further strengthen 
research and innovation under the scheme by promoting collaboration with premier 
global research institutions, medical colleges, and international public health bodies. 
Greater emphasis must also be placed on d eveloping advanced diagnostic platforms, 
expanding genomic surveillance networks, and strengthening environmental and 
wildlife surveillance so that the country‘s public health system is better equipped to 
anticipate and respond to future infectious disease threats. 
(Para 3.10.14) 
NATIONAL PHARMACOVIGILANCE PROGRAMME 
 
96. The Committee notes that the National Pharmacovigilance Programme has made 
commendable progress, with India now ranking as the 8th largest global contributor of 
Individual Case Safety Repor ts (ICSRs) and having established 1,150 Adverse Drug 
Reaction Monitoring Centres (AMCs) across the country. The Committee appreciates 
the significant training outreach, with over 9.29 lakh participants sensitised, and the 
recognition accorded by WHO design ating India as a Collaborating Centre for 
Pharmacovigilance in Public Health Programmes. The Committee, however, expresses 
concern that the actual expenditure in FY 2025 –26 stood at only ₹6.39 crore against an 

268 
 
allocation of ₹8.10 crore, reflecting gaps in full fund absorption. Moreover, the uneven 
utilisation of resources risks slowing down the expansion of AMCs and the adoption of 
advanced data-mining technologies for signal detection. 
(Para 3.11.4) 
 
97. The Committee, further, recommends that the Department should strengthen the 
Programme by institutionalis ing regular pharmacovigilance inspections of Marketing 
Authorization Holders (MAHs) to ensure compliance, accelerating the expansion of 
ADR Monitoring Centres to cover underserved regions, and intensifying awareness and 
training programmes for healthcare p rofessionals and consumers to promote timely 
reporting of adverse drug reactions. The Committee urges the Department to leverage 
modern data analytics and artificial intelligence tools for signal detection and risk 
assessment, thereby enhancing the robustn ess of India‘s pharmacovigilance system 
translating into improved patient safety, rational use of medicines, and sustained global 
leadership in pharmacovigilance. 
(Para 3.11.5) 
 
DEVELOPMENT OF NURSING SERVICES 
 
98. The Committee appreciates that the utilis ation of funds under the scheme has 
been broadly satisfactory, with ₹21.93 crore spent against an allocation of ₹22 crore in 
FY 2024–25, and ₹20.50 crore utilised up to January 2026 against an allocation of ₹28.74 
crore in FY 2025 –26. However, the Committe e expresses concern that despite the 
increased allocation, utilisation in FY 2025 –26 has lagged, potentially slowing down the 
pace of training and upgradation activities. The Committee, therefore, recommends that 
the Department should ensure timely release  and absorption of funds by strengthening 
monitoring mechanisms and instituting quarterly utilisation reviews. Further, the 
Committee urges the Department to expand the scale of short -term training courses 
beyond the 52 courses conducted so far, so that a larger proportion of the 42.94 lakh 
registered nursing personnel can benefit from skill enhancement. This will improve the 
quality of nursing services, ensure uniformity in standards across states. The 
Committee, further, recommends that the Department sho uld promote nursing services 
to bridge the gap in the nurse -to-population ratio, which currently stands at 2.43 nurses 
per 1,000 population. 
(Para 3.12.7) 
 
99. The Committee observes that significant reforms have been introduced in 
nursing education, inclu ding the competency -based B.Sc. (N) syllabus, the Nurses 
Registration and Tracking System (NRTS), the establishment of 157 new nursing 
colleges in co -location with medical colleges, and international collaborations for dual 
degree and Doctor of Nursing Pra ctice programmes. While these initiatives are 
commendable, the Committee is concerned that without a consolidated roadmap, the 
reforms risk remaining fragmented and unevenly implemented across institutions. The 

269 
 
Committee, therefore, recommends that the Dep artment should prepare a National 
Strategic Roadmap for Nursing Education and Services, integrating ongoing initiatives 
such as NRTS, Nurse Practitioner Programme (NPCC), Doctor of Nursing Practice 
(DNP), and foreign collaborations into a unified framework . This roadmap should set 
clear targets for expansion of nursing colleges, faculty development, infrastructure 
standards, affordability measures for disadvantaged groups, and to elevate nursing 
education in India to global standards while addressing domest ic shortages and regional 
disparities. 
(Para 3.12.8) 
 
100. The Committee notes that while 5,253 nursing colleges and institutes are 
functional across the country and 176 new institutions were established in FY 2025 –26, 
challenges remain in ensuring equitab le access to nursing education, particularly for 
economically weaker and disadvantaged sections of society. The Committee appreciates 
the measures undertaken by the Government, including the enactment of the National 
Nursing and Midwifery Commission Act, 2 023, rationalisation of infrastructure 
requirements, and expansion of government nursing institutions. However, the 
Committee is concerned that rising costs of education and regional disparities may limit 
opportunities for aspiring nursing students. The Co mmittee, therefore, recommends 
that the Department should adopt a targeted affordability and inclusion strategy, which 
may include provision of scholarships and financial aid for disadvantaged groups, 
incentivising private institutions to reserve seats for  economically weaker sections, and 
prioritising establishment of nursing colleges in underserved rural, tribal, and hilly 
areas. Such measures will not only expand the intake capacity but also ensure that the 
benefits of nursing education reforms reach all  sections of society, thereby improving 
the nurse-to-population ratio in line with global standards. 
(Para 3.12.9) 
 
HEALTH SECTOR DISASTER PREPAREDNESS AND RESPONSE AND HUMAN 
RESOURCE DEVELOPMENT FOR EMERGENCY MEDICAL SERVICES 
 
101. The Committee finds tha t despite allocations of ₹94 crore in FY 2025 –26, actual 
expenditure stood at ₹63.85 crore as of February 2026, with significant reductions in 
capital outlay in both FY 2024–25 and FY 2025–26 due to delays in land availability and 
lower demand from the Pro ject Management Consultant. The Committee expresses 
concern that such delays in execution of secondary and tertiary -level CBRN centres and 
NELS skill centres risk undermining the objectives of disaster preparedness and 
emergency medical response. The Commi ttee, therefore, recommends that the 
Department should establish a time -bound execution framework for infrastructure 
projects, including firm timelines for land acquisition, prototype finalisation, and 
construction milestones. The Committee urges the Depar tment to strengthen 
coordination with State Governments and Project Management Consultants to avoid 
underutilisation of funds and ensure that the proposed ₹95 crore allocation for FY 
2026–27 is absorbed effectively for intended purpose i.e. accelerating th e 

270 
 
operationalisation of CBRN centres and expand NELS skill centres, thereby enhancing 
national preparedness for chemical, biological, radiological, and nuclear emergencies. 
(Para 3.13.6) 
 
102. The Committee observes that while the Health Sector Disaster Pr eparedness & 
Response component has shown reasonable progress, the Human Resource Development 
for Emergency Medical Services (HRDEMS) component reflects stark under -utilisation, 
with only ₹2.33 crore spent against a revised allocation of ₹8.27 crore in FY 2025–26. 
The Committee is concerned that such under -utilisation directly impacts the scale of 
training programmes for doctors, nurses, paramedics, and administrators, which are 
critical for building national capacity in emergency medical response. The Comm ittee, 
therefore, recommends that the Department should adopt a structured capacity -
building plan, with annual targets for the number of personnel to be trained under 
NELS, hospital disaster preparedness, psycho -social care, and radio -nuclear emergency 
management. The Committee further recommends leveraging digital platforms and 
simulation-based training to expand reach and reduce costs, while ensuring that trained 
personnel are equitably distributed across states, including disaster -prone and 
underserved r egions to develop a robust cadre of emergency medical professionals 
capable of responding to all-hazard public health emergencies. 
(Para 3.13.7) 
 
NATIONAL ORGAN TRANSPLANT PROGRAM (NOTP) 
 
103. The Committee notes that despite allocations of ₹15 crore in FY 2024 –25 and FY 
2025–26, actual expenditure stood at only ₹7.05 crore and ₹9.03 crore (till January 2026) 
respectively, reflecting persistent under -utilisation of funds under NOTP. The 
Committee expresses concern that delays in submission of proposals by States and 
institutions, coupled with limited uptake of grants for infrastructure development, 
training, and awareness activities, are constraining the full potential of the Programme. 
The Com mittee, therefore, recommends that the Department should establish a 
structured fund utilisation framework, including mandatory annual action plans from 
States, time -bound release of grants, and quarterly monitoring of expenditure against 
physical targets. Further, the Committee urges the Department to incentiv ise States to 
strengthen transplant infrastructure in public hospitals, hire transplant coordinators, 
and expand awareness campaigns. Such measures will ensure that the earmarked ₹15 
crore allocation for FY 2026 –27 is absorbed effectively, thereby enhancin g 
transparency, efficiency, and inter-region equity in organ transplantation services. 
(Para 3.14.8) 
 
104. The Committee observes that while India has made significant progress in organ 
transplantation, ranking third globally in total transplants and first  in living donor 
transplants, the deceased donor rate remains relatively low, with challenges such as non-
declaration of brain death, lack of sensitisation among doctors, and reluctance of 

271 
 
families even after pledges by the deceased. The Committee is conce rned that these 
barriers undermine the objective of creating a sustainable and equitable transplantation 
ecosystem. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department should intensify 
efforts to promote deceased organ donation by mandating training f or doctors on brain 
death certification, strengthening the role of transplant coordinators in family 
counselling, and expanding nationwide IEC campaigns such as Angdaan – Jeevan 
Sanjeevani Abhiyaan. These steps will help increase the deceased donor rate pe r million 
population and ensure equitable access to life -saving transplants for patients on the 
national waiting list. 
(Para 3.14.9) 
 
105. The Committee further recommends closer inter -sectoral coordination among 
different agencies to ensure timely retriev al and transplantation of organs. Preventive 
measures such as early diagnosis and management of non -communicable diseases like 
diabetes and hypertension should also be integrated into awareness campaigns to reduce 
organ damage. The Committee is also concer ned about the high cost of organ 
transplantation and the financial burden it places on patients and their families. The 
Committee  therefore, recommends that the Department should take steps to make 
transplantation more affordable by ensuring inclusion of all transplant procedures 
under Government social insurance schemes like PMJAY, reducing the cost of 
immunosuppressant drugs through measures like reduction in taxes and supporting 
manufacturing of quality generic drugs in India, and considering their cove rage under 
insurance schemes at least for the first month post -surgery. These measures, alongside 
improved coordination and preventive strategies, will help create a more equitable and 
accessible transplantation ecosystem in the country. 
(Para 3.14.10) 
 
106. The Committee strongly recommends that organ transplantation facility should 
be encouraged / developed in rural areas of the country. Centers/ Medical Colleges 
providing affordable transplant services be designated/ encouraged as Centre for 
Excellence and be provided with additional autonomy. 
(Para 3.14.11) 
 
NATIONAL TELE MENTAL HEALTH PROGRAMME  
 
107. The Committee recommends for introduction of the Indian Knowledge System 
programme on lines of the UGC mandated programmes in all Medical/Dental/ 
Pharmacy / Nursing / Allied Healthcare councils/institutes of the country to address the 
burden of Mental Health. This should be integrated with the National Tele Mental 
Health Programme. 
 
(Para 3.15.2) 
 

272 
 
108. The Committee notes that despite allocations of ₹90 crore in FY 2024 –25 and 
₹79.60 crore in FY 2025 –26, actual expenditure stood at only ₹41.73 crore and ₹28.82 
crore respectively, reflecting persistent under -utilisation of funds. The Committee 
expresses concern that delays in recruitment of human resources, slow mapping of 
States/UTs as Central Nodal Agencies, and initial low call volumes have constrained 
the pace of implementation. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the 
Department should institut e a time -bound recruitment and deployment framework 
for counsellors and mental health professionals, ensuring adequate staffing across all 
Tele MANAS cells. The Committee further urges the Department to strengthen 
monitoring mechanisms through a real -time digital dashboard, linking fund release to 
measurable outputs such as call volumes, counselling sessions, referrals, and follow -
ups. Special emphasis should be placed on expanding accessibility of Tele MANAS 
services to rural and remote populations by iden tifying underserved districts and 
leveraging community health workers, thereby expanding the reach of Tele MANAS 
and bridging the treatment gap in mental health services.  
(Para 3.15.8) 
 
109. The Committee observes that Tele MANAS has handled nearly 32 lakh  calls 
since its launch, with multilingual counselling support and integration of video 
consultations through e -Sanjeevani. While these achievements are commendable, the 
Committee is concerned that affordability, inclusivity, and emerging mental health 
challenges require further attention. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the 
Department should expand the scope of Tele MANAS by integrating primary mental 
healthcare services into Ayushman Arogya Mandirs, introducing peer education 
programmes for ment al health in schools and colleges, and addressing digital 
addiction through dedicated counselling modules. The Committee further 
recommends strengthening affordability by ensuring coverage of mental health 
consultations under Government insurance schemes l ike PMJAY, and by promoting 
workplace-based occupational mental health initiatives. Interdisciplinary research on 
the linkages between lifestyle factors, such as ultra -processed food consumption 
highlighted in the Economic Survey 2025 –26, and mental health  disorders should also 
be supported to inform preventive strategies. These measures will ensure that Tele 
MANAS evolves into a comprehensive, inclusive, and sustainable national mental 
health support system.  
(Para 3.15.9) 
 
NATIONAL DIGITAL HEALTH MISSION ( NDHM)/ AYUSHMAN BHARAT 
DIGITAL MISSION (ABDM): 
110. The Committee notes with serious concern the glaring discrepancies between 
the consolidated budgetary allocations and the actual fund releases to States/UTs 
under the Ayushman Bharat Digital Mission (ABDM ). The data reveals a pattern of 
intermittent or complete stagnation in fund flow, most notably in States like Odisha, 
which recorded absolute zero releases from FY 2021 -22 through December 2025, and 

273 
 
Delhi, which received its first marginal tranche only in  FY 2025 -26. Even in 2025 -26, 
fund release to Telangana and Odisha is nil as on 31.12.2025. The total fund released 
to all States/UTs in FY 2021 -22 was 25% of the total allocation, which has been 
improved to 53% in FY 2025 -26 (as on 31.12.2025). S uch dispa rities undermine 
uniform adoption of ABDM across the country. The Committee is of the view that 
such persistent bottlenecks indicate deep -rooted administrative challenges at the State 
level, likely stemming from delays in submitting Utilization Certificate s (UCs) or a 
lack of technical capacity to implement NHA guidelines. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends that the Department should constitute a dedicated 'State -Level 
Facilitation and Capacity Building Taskforce.' This taskforce should proactively 
engage with lagging States to resolve procedural deadlocks, streamline the submission 
of UCs, provide targeted technical assistance and capacity building for lagging States  
and ensure that the allocated human resources and Information, Education & 
Communication (IEC) funds are efficiently utilized to drive the mission at the 
grassroots level.  
(Para 3.16.11) 
 
111. The Committee believes that while the creation of nearly 85.83 crore 
Ayushman Bharat Health Accounts (ABHAs) and the linking of 87.66 crore health 
records are highly commendable achievements, there remains a critical lag in the 
corresponding adoption of the ecosystem by healthcare providers. With only 4.61 lakh 
facilities registered on the Health Facility Registry (HFR) and a mere 2.53 lakh 
facilities act ively utilizing ABDM -enabled software, the foundational goal of seamless 
interoperability is at risk of underutilization. The Committee recommends that th e 
₹350.00 crore Revenue allocation for FY 2026 -27 be strategically front -loaded toward 
incentivizing provider onboarding. The Department should accelerate the deployment 
of the Unified Health Interface (UHI) and the National Health Claims Exchange 
(NHCX), demonstrating tangible administrative and financial benefits, such as 
expedited insurance claim settlements to private and public health facilities. By 
shifting the focus from mere citizen enrollment to driving active software adoption at 
the clinic and ho spital level, the Department can truly operationalize the longitudinal 
health records ecosystem.  
(Para 3.16.12) 
 
112. The Committee observes that while ABDM has achieved remarkable progress 
with over 85 crore ABHA IDs and 87 crore linked health records, ch allenges remain 
in extending services to rural and tribal populations due to poor digital connectivity 
and limited digital literacy, alongside rising concerns about privacy and 
cybersecurity. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department should 
prioritise offline and assisted modes of ABHA creation, strengthen telemedicine 
services, and leverage Ayushman Arogya Mandirs and community health workers to 
expand accessibility in underserved areas. At the same time, the Committee urges the 
Department t o establish a robust national cybersecurity framework for digital health, 
including regular audits, compliance standards, and intensified IEC campaigns to 

274 
 
build public trust by clearly communicating privacy safeguards and citizens‘ rights 
under the Health Data Management Policy.  
(Para 3.16.13) 
SCHEME FOR ALLIED HEALTH CARE PROFESSIONALS (AHPs)  
 
113. The Committee believes that the introduction of the new Scheme for Allied 
Health Care Professionals (AHPs) with a ₹1000 Crore allocation for FY 2026 -27 is a 
landmark step toward addressing the shifting disease burden, where 65% of cases now 
arise from Non-Communicable Diseases requiring specialized diagnostics and long -term 
care. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department prioritize the 
establishment and upgradation of government -led Allied Healthcare Professionals 
Institutions (AHPIs) to balance the current disparity where ~88% of seats are currently 
concentrated in private institutes. To ensure the target of 100,000 AHPs within five 
years is met with clinical excellence, the Department must move beyond mere 
infrastructure development and focu s on the strict implementation of the 15 mandatory 
National Commission for Allied and Healthcare Professions (NCAHP) curriculums 
across all selected disciplines, including high -demand areas like Molecular Diagnostics, 
Genomics, OT Technology and Digital Pa thology. The Committee is further of the view 
that the proposed "Gap Analysis" of existing institutions in terms of norms specified by 
NCAHP must be concluded in a time -bound manner to ensure that the 100% funding 
for State and Central institutes is utiliz ed for high -fidelity laboratories and advanced 
imaging equipment that mirror the requirements of modern private -sector labs and 
hospitals.  
(Para 3.17.6) 
 
114. The Committee recommends that the Department ensure timely and effective 
implementation of the p roposed Central Sector Scheme for strengthening allied and 
healthcare professions. While the scheme rightly focuses on establishing new institutes 
and upgrading existing ones with 100% funding over five years, the Committee urges 
the Department to adopt a phased rollout plan with clear annual targets to expand seat 
capacity and introduce new courses in line with national health workforce requirements. 
Further, the Committee recommends prioritizing geographic equity by ensuring that 
Tier-2 and Tier-3 cities, as well as underserved regions, benefit from new institutes and 
upgraded facilities. Strong monitoring mechanisms, integration with State Programme 
Implementation Plans, and alignment with Indian Public Health Standards (IPHS) 
norms will ensure that the scheme not only expands educational capacity but also builds 
a sustainable pipeline of skilled allied and healthcare professionals to strengthen India‘s 
health system. 
(Para 3.17.7) 
STATUTORY AND REGULATORY BODIES  
 
115. The Committee observes that several s tatutory regulatory bodies under the 
Department, such as the National Medical Commission (NMC), Dental Council of India 

275 
 
(DCI), Pharmacy Council of India (PCI), Indian Nursing Council (INC), and the 
National Board of Examinations (NBE), receive only nominal  budgetary allocations 
from the Union Government, whereas emerging bodies such as the National Commission 
for Allied and Healthcare Professions (NCAHP) have been provided comparatively 
higher allocations for establishing regulatory frameworks and instituti onal systems. 
Considering the increasing demand for quality healthcare professionals and the need to 
maintain uniform standards in medical, dental, pharmacy, nursing, and allied health 
education, the Committee recommends that the Department strengthen coor dination 
among these regulatory bodies and promote greater convergence in regulatory 
practices, accreditation mechanisms, and digital registries of professionals. The 
Committee emphasises the need to support the operationalisation of the National 
Commission for Allied and Healthcare Professions so that the large and diverse allied 
health workforce in the country can be effectively regulated, trained, and integrated into 
the healthcare delivery system. 
(Para 3.18.4) 
116. The Committee observes a concerning, systemic trend of downward budgetary 
revisions at the Revised Estimate (RE) stage across regulatory bodies, culminating in a 
reduction from ₹547.45 crore (BE) to ₹493.15 crore (RE) in FY 2025 -26. The Committee 
notes with specific concern the chronic underu tilization of funds by the National 
Commission for Allied and Healthcare Professions (NCAHP), which recorded an actual 
expenditure of merely ₹0.75 crore against a BE of ₹20.00 crore in 2024 -25. The 
Committee believes that such financial inertia severely cr ipples the standard -setting 
mandates of these nascent statutory bodies. The Committee, therefore, recommends that 
the Department institute a mandatory, quarterly expenditure review mechanism for all 
statutory councils. This oversight is critical to ensure that the ₹494.92 crore allocated for 
FY 2026-27 is fully absorbed and translated into actionable regulatory outcomes rather 
than being surrendered. 
(Para 3.18.5) 
117. The Committee is concerned to note that the bodies like NMC/PCI/ DCI/ Central 
Drugs Standard Control Organisation (CDSCO) have been collecting hundred of cores 
of Rupees in the name of licensing/ regulation. The Committee recommends for proper 
audit, rationalisation of their expenses. The Committee further recommends that the 
licensing fees by  CDSCO for Medical Devices need to be reduced by at least 75 % to 
make India Competitive as a low cost manufacturing supplier in comparison to 
countries like China. 
(Para 3.18.6) 
Food Safety and Standards Authority of India (FSSAI) 
 
118. The Committee note s that the bulk of the allocation under Statutory and 
Regulatory Bodies is accounted for by the Food Safety and Standards Authority of India 
(FSSAI), which plays a critical role in regulating food safety standards across the 
country. The Committee observes  that out of the 62 lakh Food Business Operators 

276 
 
operating in the country, nearly 61 lakh operate squarely under the jurisdiction of State 
Authorities, making state-level enforcement the absolute linchpin of the Food Safety and 
Standards Act. The Committee  observes that while ₹306.31 crore was approved under 
MoUs for 21 States/UTs in FY 2025 -26, only ₹110 crore has been disbursed as the first 
tranche. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department immediately 
expedite the release of the remaining funds.  
(Para 3.18.12) 
119. The Committee observes the effective implementation of the Food Safety and 
Standards Act, 2006 requires strong coordination between the Centre and the States, 
along with adequate laboratory infrastructure and trained manpower. W hile the 
Department has taken several steps such as expansion of food testing laboratories, 
strengthening the Food Import Clearance System, and training of food handlers, the 
Committee recommends that the Department further augment laboratory capacity, 
ensure uniform enforcement standards across States/UTs, and expand training 
programmes for food safety officials and food handlers. The Committee is of the view 
that FSSAI must strategically direct state -level grants toward expanding its current 
network of 25 2 primary and 24 referral laboratories. Funding should be specifically 
earmarked to establish high -tech microbiological laboratories at the district level to 
ensure robust, grassroots safety screening. 
(Para 3.18.13) 
120. The Committee believes that the pr oposed regulatory amendments by FSSAI, 
specifically increasing the registration turnover threshold from ₹12 lakh to ₹1.5 crore 
(to align with GST norms) and transitioning to perpetual licenses, are highly progressive 
steps necessary to reduce the complianc e burden on small Food Business Operators 
(FBOs). The Committee, therefore, recommends the swift gazette notification and 
implementation of these amendments. Furthermore, the Committee is of the view that 
the proposed complete digital integration of FSSAI' s licensing system with the National 
Single Window System (DPIIT) must be executed in a strictly time -bound manner. This 
integration will eliminate the redundant submission of documents across various 
municipal and central departments, thereby truly actual izing a seamless operational 
environment for the country's 67 lakh registered food businesses. 
(Para 3.18.14) 
121. The Committee notes with serious concern the stark regional disparity in the 
availability of FSSAI -notified testing infrastructure. While sta tes like Maharashtra, 
Tamil Nadu, and Gujarat have a high concentration of facilities, some States and Union 
Territories, namely, Arunachal Pradesh, Mizoram, Sikkim, Andaman & Nicobar 
Islands, Chandigarh, Ladakh, Puducherry and Lakshadweep lack even a sing le notified 
laboratory. Furthermore, Referral Food Laboratories are restricted to just 11 states 
namely, Andhra Pradesh, Gujarat, Haryana, Karnataka, Kerala, Maharashtra, Punjab, 
Tamil Nadu, Telangana, Uttar Pradesh and West Bengal. The Committee believes that 
such skewed distribution severely undermines the equitable enforcement of food safety 
standards nationwide. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department 

277 
 
immediately prioritize the unrepresented States and UTs for the deployment of the 
pending 236 "next -generation" Mobile Food Testing Labs (MFTLs) once their 
specifications are finalized by May 2026. Concurrently, the Department must allocate 
targeted financial assistance to establish permanent, state -owned primary and referral 
laboratories i n the North -Eastern states and UTs to eliminate their dependency on 
distant facilities. 
(Para 3.18.21) 
122. The Committee is of the view that robust regulatory frameworks are rendered 
ineffective without adequate manpower and institutional grievance mechan isms. The 
Committee observes that out of FSSAI's 822 sanctioned posts, 279 (nearly 34%) remain 
vacant, perpetuating an undesirable reliance on contractual staff. Additionally, the 
complete absence of Food Safety Appellate Tribunals in Andhra Pradesh and Dadra and 
Nagar Haveli & Daman & Diu, along with the delayed constitution in Bihar, Karnataka, 
and Mizoram, deprives citizens and Food Business Operators (FBOs) of essential, timely 
legal recourse. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department ini tiate a 
fast-track, time -bound recruitment drive in coordination with the relevant recruiting 
agencies to fill the 279 vacancies at FSSAI. Furthermore, the Department must 
proactively engage with the defaulting State/UT governments to ensure the immediate 
constitution and operationalization of their respective Food Safety Appellate Tribunals. 
(Para 3.18.22) 
 
123. The Committee believes that the proposed legislative amendments to the Food 
Safety and Standards Act, 2006, are critical for modernizing the regul atory landscape. 
The current statutory ambiguities, such as penalizing retailers for manufacturing -level 
non-compliances beyond their control, restricting the compounding of minor offences 
only to registered (and not licensed) FBOs, and limiting botanical extractions to 
outdated solvent technologies, create unnecessary compliance burdens and stifle 
industry innovation. The Committee is of the view that redefining "unsafe food" to 
remove contradictory interpretations and expanding permissible food -grade solv ents 
will significantly enhance operational predictability. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends that the Department expedite the drafting and parliamentary introduction 
of these legislative amendments to ensure that the statutory framework aligns with 
contemporary technological realities and fair regulatory practices. 
(Para 3.18.23) 
National Medical commission 
 
124. The Committee observes that while the National Medical Commission (NMC) has 
successfully overseen a massive expansion of medical education cap acity, reaching 818 
colleges, its own regulatory architecture is suffering from acute personnel deficits. The 
Committee notes with serious concern the absolute vacancy in the crucial category of 
Whole-Time Members in Autonomous Boards, with 0 out of 8 sanc tioned posts filled, 
alongside significant vacancies in encadred administrative posts. The Committee 
believes that the consequent heavy reliance on 236 outsourced and contractual staff 

278 
 
members compromises institutional accountability, dilutes a sense of be longingness, and 
raises the risk of unethical practices to secure continued engagement. The Committee, 
therefore, recommends that the Department urgently initiate a time -bound recruitment 
process to fill all statutory, ex-officio, and encadred vacancies.  
(Para 3.18.27) 
 
125. The Committee also recommends that in order to further strengthen the quality 
and consistency of postgraduate medical education in the country, it may be considered 
to ensure uniformity in faculty –student and patient –student ratios acr oss all medical 
institutions, irrespective of ownership. Such uniformity would help promote equitable 
clinical exposure, adequate academic mentorship, and consistent training opportunities 
for postgraduate students nationwide. As graduates from all recogni zed institutions 
contribute to the same national healthcare system, maintaining uniform standards 
would further reinforce confidence in the competence and training of medical specialists 
produced across the country. 
(Para 3.18.28) 
Dental Council of India 
 
126. The Committee notes with concern the persistent violation of Section 46(5) of the 
Dentists Act, 1948, which explicitly allows registered dental professionals to practice in 
any State. Despite established legal provisions and subsequent circulars issue d by the 
Dental Council of India (DCI), several States, including Bihar, Madhya Pradesh, 
Telangana, Uttar Pradesh, and Delhi, continue to mandate local state registration as a 
prerequisite for applying to permanent vacancies. The Committee is of the view t hat 
such restrictive practices act as a significant barrier to the mobility of skilled healthcare 
professionals and create an unnecessary financial and administrative burden on 
candidates. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department, in 
coordination with the upcoming National Dental Commission (NDC), issue a mandatory 
directive to all State Health Secretaries and Directorates of Medical Education ensuring 
that inter-state registration is recognized during the application and examination stages,  
with the provision that successful candidates may transfer their registration under 
Section 46A after final selection. 
(Para 3.18.33) 
127. The Committee observes a critical depletion in the human resource capacity of 
the Dental Council of India, with 47 o ut of 95 sanctioned posts currently vacant, 
representing a nearly 50% vacancy rate. The Committee believes that the instruction in 
the year 2023 to halt fresh regular recruitment, while intended to facilitate the transition 
to the National Dental Commissio n (NDC), has resulted in an administrative vacuum 
that threatens the quality of regulatory oversight and the processing of Letters of 
Permission (LOPs) for BDS and MDS courses. The Committee, therefore, recommends 
that the Department prioritize the early c onstitution and operationalization of the 
National Dental Commission (NDC) as mandated by the NDC Act, 2023. The Committee 
is of the view that the swift establishment of the Commission‘s permanent organisational 

279 
 
and administrative structure is the only sus tainable way to fill this regulatory void and 
resolve the current staffing crisis, ensuring that the quality and affordability of dental 
education are not compromised during this prolonged transitional phase. Till the 
constitution of NDC, the Department ma y consider a special "Transition Recruitment 
Plan" or the deputation of personnel from other health departments.  
(Para 3.18.34) 
ALL INDIA INSTITUTE OF MEDICAL SCIENCES (AIIMS), New Delhi 
 
128. The Committee notes with appreciation the satisfactory utiliza tion of budgetary 
funds by AIIMS, New Delhi, and acknowledges the gradual increase in the Budget 
Estimate (BE) to ₹5500.92 crore for FY 2026 -27. However, the Committee observes that 
this allocation falls short of the Institute's projected requirement of ₹5 758 crore. The 
Committee believes that fully funding the Institute is non -negotiable, especially as 
AIIMS is executing a complex, 190 -acre integrated Master Plan while simultaneously 
maintaining unhindered patient care services. The Committee, therefore, r ecommends 
that the Department proactively review and enhance the allocation at the Revised 
Estimate (RE) stage to bridge this ₹257 crore deficit. Ensuring adequate liquidity is 
critical to support not only the ongoing phased redevelopment but also the smoo th 
initiation of upcoming mega -projects, such as establishment of a CBRNE facility and 
expansion of JPNATC and Centre of Excellence in Child Health at Jhajjar. 
(Para 3.19.5) 
 
129. The Committee views with serious concern the severe procedural bottlenecks 
stalling vital patient -care infrastructure. Specifically, the Committee notes that the 
Super Specialty OPD Block at Masjid Moth is caught in a prolonged DoE reappraisal 
loop due to tender costs exceeding the ₹500 crore threshold, while the 150 -bedded 
Critical Care Block is severely delayed by pending tree transplantation approvals and 
shifting Environmental Clearance (EC) goalposts by the MoEF&CC. The Committee is 
of the view that such bureaucratic delays defeats the urgent mandate of these projects. 
The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department immediately constitute an 
empowered Inter-Ministerial Steering Group to aggressively liaise with the Department 
of Expenditure (DoE), MoEF&CC, and the Delhi Government. This group must secure 
a specialized,  fast-track clearance pathway for all AIIMS infrastructural projects to 
prevent further cost escalations and time overruns. 
(Para 3.19.6) 
 
130. The Committee observes a significant failure in the initial planning and inter -
agency coordination regarding the  ₹4441 crore Redevelopment of Residential Colonies 
project. The Committee believes that the Delhi Development Authority's (DDA) 
rejection of the commercial monetization of the West Ansari Nagar (WAN) campus, 
which formed the very basis of the project's sel f-financing model, has severely derailed 
the initiative. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department urgently 
finalize and submit the revised Cabinet Note with a realistic, updated financial structure 

280 
 
that does not rely on contested land -use conversions. Furthermore, the Committee is of 
the view that while the revised financial model is being approved, the Department must 
ensure uninterrupted funding so that the ongoing construction of the Type IV and Type 
V quarters (costing ₹118.92 crore and ₹143.94 crore , respectively) meets their stipulated 
completion deadlines of 2026 and 2027. 
(Para 3.19.7) 
 
131. The Committee notes with concern the profound lack of progress in critical 
projects, specifically the Expansion of the National Drug Dependence Treatment Cent re 
(NDDTC) and the establishment of the ₹358.19 crore IT and Network Security 
Infrastructure. Despite the project‘s official commencement dated of 18 th March 2024 
with 18 -month completion timeline and an advance payment of ₹8.60 crore to the 
CPWD for the N DDTC, physical progress remains at 'Nil', while the ambitious IT 
modernization project faces an exceptionally tight completion deadline of September 
2026, given the complexity of digitizing a massive healthcare ecosystem. The Committee 
believes that such d elays in both civil works for vulnerable populations and the digital 
resilience of the Institute‘s data systems are unacceptable in a premier national 
institution. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department institute a 
unified "High-Level Project Monitoring Cell" to hold both civil executing agencies (like 
CPWD) and technical consultants (like BEL) strictly accountable through monthly 
milestone tracking. The Committee is of the view that the Department must formulate 
and enforce penalty clause s in MoUs to held executing agencies financially accountable 
for construction inertia. The Committee further urges the Department to establish a 
dedicated Cyber -Security Cell within AIIMS to manage and protect the new digital 
infrastructure from the increasing global threats to healthcare data. 
(Para 3.19.8) 
 
132. The Committee observes that while AIIMS, New Delhi is undergoing a massive 
internal redevelopment, its role in decompressing tertiary care through satellite facilities 
remains behind schedule. Spe cifically, the construction of the 30 -bedded Community 
Health Centre at Fatehpur Billoch and the New Ward Block at CRHSP Ballabhgarh are 
both trailing their original stipulated completion dates, with the latter showing a dismal 
physical progress of only 15 % nearly a year after its start. The Committee believes that 
the chronic overcrowding at the main Ansari Nagar campus can only be permanently 
mitigated if these peripheral and community -based facilities are operationalized to 
handle primary and secondary w orkloads. The Committee, therefore, recommends that 
the Department institute a "Satellite Project Acceleration Taskforce" to oversee these 
specific expansions and ensure their timely completion, thereby guaranteeing that rural 
and semi-urban populations ga in access to AIIMS -standard care without necessitating 
travel to the high-burden New Delhi facility. 
(Para 3.19.9) 
133. The Committee observes with concern a staggering 35% vacancy rate (452 
vacancies) in faculty positions, along with J unior/Senior residents (700+ vacancies), and 

281 
 
non-faculty posts (2,540 vacancies) which constrain service delivery and strain existing 
staff. Most alarmingly, the data reveals that while 32 faculty members resigned or 
superannuated in the years 2024 -25 and 2025 -26 (till Januar y 2026), zero new faculty 
members joined the institute during this period. The Committee believes that this 
recruitment paralysis severely jeopardizes the academic and clinical standards of India's 
premier medical institution. The Committee, therefore, rec ommends that the 
Department launch a focused, time -bound recruitment drive with clear milestones, for 
example, target filling (at least 85%) of sanctioned posts by fast -tracking advertised 
vacancies, conducting rolling interview panels, and decentralising selection timelines to 
avoid protracted offer -to-joining delays. T he 328 currently advertised faculty positions 
may be finalised within the first two quarters of FY 2026 -27. Furthermore, the 
Committee is of the view that the persistent reliance on contract ual faculty (currently 73 
Assistant Professors) must be phased out in favor of permanent appointments to ensure 
institutional belongingness and long -term accountability, as contractual arrangements 
should serve only as temporary stop -gap measures. L ong-standing contractual 
incumbents meeting eligibility criteria should be offered a regularisation/absorption 
pathway. The Department should introduce retention incentives tied to hard -to-fill 
specialties and underserved postings to improve the offer-to-joining ratio. 
(Para 3.19.15) 
134. The Committee is concerned about excessive continuous duty hours for Junior 
and Senior Residents and the risk of clinical errors and burnout, thereby compromising 
patient safety. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Depa rtment formulate 
and strictly enforce a "Clinical Duty Hours Regulation" policy with mandatory rest 
periods, and monitored rosters , drawing a direct analogy from other safety -critical 
professions like civil aviation to prevent fatigue -induced accidents.  T he presence of 
senior faculty should be ensured in wards and in operating theatres during peak hours 
by aligning faculty schedules with clinical demand and by appointing designated 
supervisory rosters. Structured mentorship and protected teaching time shou ld be 
introduced so that trainees gain supervised clinical experience rather than learning by 
trial. Additionally, to ensure the retention of high -quality talent, the Committee 
recommends that medical professionals be provided with improved working conditi ons, 
including accommodation, transportation, and administrative support, on par with the 
facilities afforded to senior civil servants, and transparent career progression with 
time-bound promotions . The Committee also suggests the integration of Yoga and t he 
Indian Knowledge System (IKS) within the workforce to address the rising incidence of 
depression and psychiatric issues among the medical community. 
(Para 3.19.16) 
135. The Committee notes that AIIMS, New Delhi, continues to struggle with high 
patient l oads in OPD and Emergency services, leading to long waiting times (may 
extended to several months) for investigations and surgeries; and optimum and 
uninterrupted supply of drugs and consumables. The Committee believes that physical 

282 
 
infrastructure alone ca nnot solve the congestion at a tertiary care center of this scale. 
The Committee, therefore, recommends the immediate implementation of the proposed 
"Digital Command and Control System" and ―Performance -Based Accountability‖, 
utilizing AI -driven analytics to forecast patient demand and optimize surgical 
scheduling, and identifying bottlenecks so that human resources match infrastructure 
growth. Furthermore, the Committee is of the view that a robust digital triage tool must 
be deployed to strengthen the ref erral system, effectively redirecting non -tertiary cases 
to appropriate secondary centers. This will ensure that the specialized resources of 
AIIMS are reserved for the most complex clinical cases, thereby improving overall 
operational efficiency and patient satisfaction. 
(Para 3.19.17) 
136. The Committee believes that while AIIMS, New Delhi, currently captures 
extensive hospital utilization and service -delivery statistics, the existing internal 
monitoring mechanism lacks the necessary external validation t o drive systemic 
improvements. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department facilitate 
an independent audit to evaluate hospital -level allocations, utilisation and service 
delivery, and key performance indicators like bed occupancy rates, bed t urnover rates, 
and average length of stay, ICU admissions, surgical volumes, laboratory and 
radiological investigations, hospital infection rate including healthcare associated 
infections, on quarterly basis. The Committee is of the view that the outcomes of these 
audits must be subjected to public scrutiny to ensure absolute institutional transparency 
with audit results directly informing the strategic allocation of resources and the 
planning. 
(Para 3.19.18) 
137. The Committee believes that the governance of a massive, multi -specialty 
ecosystem like AIIMS requires a blend of clinical expertise and formal administrative 
training. The Committee, therefore, recommends that doctors with specialized 
managerial qualifications be prioritized for top -tier policy -making and administrative 
positions within the Institute. Additionally, the Committee is of the view that the medical 
and nursing curriculum must be reformed to include an introductory syllabus on Ayush 
and the Indian Traditional Medicinal Systems. This sens itisation will not only 
complement allopathic treatments through palliative and preventive care but will also 
enhance the affordability and accessibility of the healthcare system. The Committee 
concludes that this multidisciplinary approach is essential fo r producing a more holistic 
and resilient healthcare workforce. 
(Para 3.19.19) 
138.  The Committee notes that AIIMS, New Delhi, along with its extended 
establishments including JPNATC, NCI Jhajjar and CRHSP Ballabgarh, handled an 
exceptionally large patien t load of 5.42 crore OPD cases (including casualty) and 3.92 
lakh IPD admissions during the period 01.01.2025 to 31.12.2025. While the Committee 

283 
 
appreciates the measures adopted by the Institute to streamline patient access through 
the Online Registration System (ORS), token -based walk -in system, patient feedback 
mechanisms such as the SANTUSHT portal, and the provision of Vishram 
Sadans/shelter homes, it observes that the extremely high patient inflow continues to 
place considerable pressure on hospital in frastructure and services. The Committee also 
notes that several blocks and centres do not have full operationalisation of available 
Operation Theatres (OTs) and that patients continue to face long waiting periods for 
diagnostic tests, surgeries and proced ures. The Committee, therefore, recommends that 
the Department take urgent steps to ensure that all sanctioned OTs across various 
blocks are made fully functional at the earliest through adequate staffing, equipment 
provisioning and maintenance support, an d also augment diagnostic and surgical 
capacities to reduce waiting periods for critical procedures. The Committee further 
recommends that the feedback received through various mechanisms should be made 
public and the relevant feedbacks should be considered for identifying the genuine 
problems and their redressal. 
(Para 3.19.22) 
 
139.  The Committee further observes that the heavy patient load at AIIMS, New 
Delhi is partly due to the absence of adequate ter tiary care facilities in several 
surrounding regions. While satellite institutions such as AIIMS Jhajjar and the facilities 
at Ballabgarh, Faridabad and Dakshinpuri have helped serve populations in the 
southern outskirts of Delhi, the northern periphery of  the National Capital Region —
including North Delhi, parts of northern Haryana and adjoining areas of western Uttar 
Pradesh, continues to remain comparatively underserved in terms of access to advanced 
tertiary healthcare. In view of the persistent demand f or specialised medical services 
and the continuing pressure on the main AIIMS campus, the Committee recommends 
that the Department examine the feasibility of establishing an additional satellite centre 
of AIIMS in the northern outskirts of Delhi to expand access to tertiary healthcare, 
reduce patient congestion at the main institute and provide timely treatment to 
populations in the surrounding regions. 
(Para 3.19.23) 
 
140. The Committee notes that AIIMS, New Delhi is actively engaged in extensive 
research on antimicrobial resistance (AMR) at both hospital and community levels and 
functions as a designated Infectious Disease Research & Diagnostic Laboratory (IRDL) 
as well as a nodal coordinating centre for the Antimicrobial Resistance Surveillance 
Network (A MRSN). While the Institute‘s work in areas such as rapid diagnostics, 
genomic profiling, biomarker identification, bacteriophage therapy, CRISPR -based 
approaches and point -of-care diagnostics is commendable, the Committee emphasises 
that the outcomes of such research should translate into scalable public health solutions. 
The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department promote stronger 
collaboration between AIIMS, ICMR laboratories, State medical colleges and public 
health institutions, and establi sh a national knowledge -sharing platform to disseminate 

284 
 
research findings and innovations to strengthen AMR surveillance, diagnostics and 
antimicrobial stewardship across the country. 
(Para 3.19.28) 
141. The Committee observes that AIIMS, New Delhi does no t require students 
graduating from its Degree, Postgraduate or Super -specialty programmes to undertake 
any service bond, nor does the Institute maintain data on the current professional 
engagement of its alumni within the country. Considering that the Inst itute represents 
one of the most prestigious publicly funded medical institutions and produces a large 
pool of highly trained medical professionals each year, the Committee is of the view that 
systematic tracking of alumni and their professional contributi ons would provide 
valuable insights into the national healthcare workforce. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends that the Department consider developing a structured database to assess 
the extent to which trained specialists contribute to the domestic heal thcare system, 
identify gaps in specialist availability across regions, and inform future policy decisions 
relating to medical education, workforce planning and retention of highly skilled 
professionals within the country. 
(Para 3.19.29) 
142. The Committee  notes that the Institute incurred substantial expenditure on 
electricity (₹108.86 crore) and water (₹3.97 crore) during FY 2024 –25, reflecting the 
scale of its operations and infrastructure. The Committee appreciates the steps taken by 
the Institute to en hance solar power generation capacity from 703 KWp to 1108 KWp, 
along with ongoing rooftop solar projects across various blocks and centres, as well as 
measures such as rainwater harvesting and reuse of treated water through sewage 
treatment plants (STPs) for horticulture purposes. While these initiatives are 
encouraging, the Committee is of the view that there remains considerable scope to 
further strengthen sustainable energy and water management practices within the 
Institute. The Committee, therefore, r ecommends that the Department encourage 
expansion of renewable energy capacity, adoption of energy -efficient technologies, and 
systematic water conservation measures, with clearly defined targets for reducing 
dependence on conventional electricity and fres h water consumption. Such measures 
would not only reduce operational expenditure in the long term but also promote 
environmentally sustainable practices in large public healthcare institutions. 
(Para 3.19.30) 
 
POST GRADUATE INSTITUTE OF MEDICAL SCIENCES AND RESEARCH 
(PGIMER), CHANDIGARH  
143. The Committee notes with concern the protracted delays in operationalizing 
critical facilities, specifically the 300 -bedded Advanced Neurosciences Centre and the 
Advanced Mother & Child Centre, which have been under co nstruction since 2019 and 
2021 respectively, yet remain stalled at 99% and 97% completion. The Committee 
believes that the cited administrative bottlenecks, such as delayed statutory clearances, 

285 
 
late site handovers, and utility shifting disputes, must be a ggressively resolved through a 
dedicated inter -agency task force to prevent further cost escalations. The Committee, 
therefore, recommends that the Department immediately facilitate the final 
commissioning of these near -complete projects to directly benefi t patient care. 
Furthermore, the Committee is of the view that the budgetary allocation of ₹2504.65 
crore for FY 2026 -27, which falls significantly short of the Institute's projected demand 
of ₹2865.00 crore, must be augmented at the Revised Estimate (RE) stage. This financial 
reinforcement is vital to ensure the smooth initiation of the proposed ₹472.00 crore 
Sarangpur campus (Phase-I) without financially starving the ongoing satellite projects. 
(Para 3.20.6) 
 
144. The Committee observes that the main camp us of PGIMER is severely 
overburdened, compromising the quality of specialized tertiary care. The Committee is 
of the view that merely constructing physical satellite centres at Una, Ferozepur, and 
Sangrur is insufficient unless coupled with a formalized, policy-driven network linking 
these facilities. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department mandate 
the immediate development and implementation of a digitized "scientific referral 
system" across the neighboring states. This system must active ly upgrade peripheral 
health facilities to act as primary gatekeepers, utilizing strict digital triage protocols to 
filter and redirect non -tertiary cases. By doing so, the highly specialized resources and 
personnel of PGIMER will be exclusively reserved f or genuine, complex clinical 
interventions, thereby effectively decongesting the institute. 
(Para 3.20.7) 
 
145. The Committee believes that the successful integration of the five new major 
clinical and satellite centres relies entirely on concurrent advanc ements in digital 
infrastructure and human resources. The current reliance on manual processes and an 
outdated Hospital Information System (HIS) hampers both clinical efficiency and data -
driven decision-making. The Committee, therefore, recommends the imme diate funding 
and expedited rollout of a modernized, comprehensive HIS to fully digitize record -
keeping, streamline patient flow, and enhance real -time monitoring through existing 
mechanisms like the 24x7 Control Room. The Committee is further of the view that the 
Department must authorize a dedicated, time -bound recruitment drive to be executed 
prior to the final handover of the new Neurosciences and Mother & Child centres. It is 
imperative that no newly constructed, state -of-the-art facility remains non -operational 
or underutilized due to a preventable shortage of specialized medical and paramedical 
staff. 
(Para 3.20.8)
