146. The Committee notes with serious concern the glaring human resource deficit at 
PGIMER, Chandigarh, particularly the 1,848 vacant non -faculty positions against a 
sanctioned strength of 7,004. Coupled with 139 faculty vacancies and nearly 300 vacant 
resident posts, the Committee believes that managing an overwhelming daily average of 

286 
 
10,918 OPD patients with a depleted workforce severely  degrades the quality of tertiary 
care and overwhelms the existing staff. Furthermore, the Committee is of the view that 
the existence of 7 non -functional operating tables (in NEOT, NHE OT, and Day Care 
OT) out of a total of 75 is unacceptable in a premier  national institute where surgical 
waitlists are chronically long. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the 
Department immediately sanction a targeted, time -bound recruitment drive prioritizing 
the massive non -faculty and resident vacancies. Concurren tly, the Institute must be 
directed to utilize emergency maintenance funds to repair or replace the non -functional 
OT tables within the current quarter, ensuring that the hospital's surgical capacity 
operates at 100% efficiency. 
(Para 3.20.12) 
147. The Committee observes that while PGIMER has initiated commendable patient-
centric practices, such as the SARATHI initiative, online lab reports, and integration 
with the "Mera Aspataal" portal, many of these interventions remain highly 
fragmented. For instance, the token system is currently restricted only to the Cardiac 
Centre, and the advanced Queue Management System remains merely "planned" under 
HIS-II. The Committee believes that managing a staggering daily footfall of nearly 
11,000 patients requires univers al, standardized digital crowd control across all 
departments. The Committee is of the view that the feedback mechanisms (physical 
performas, written complaints, Sarathi app, and online portals) must be synthesized to 
yield actionable administrative insights. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the 
Department mandate the expedited, institute -wide rollout of the token and Scan -and-
Share systems across all OPDs without waiting for the full HIS -II deployment. 
Furthermore, the Committee recommends the est ablishment of a unified 'Patient 
Experience Cell' to consolidate data from all feedback streams into a single monthly 
analytical report for the Director, which may be available for public view also, ensuring 
that patient grievances systematically drive operational improvements. 
(Para 3.20.13) 
JAWAHARLAL INSTITUTE OF POST GRADUATION MEDICAL EDUCATION 
AND RESEARCH (JIPMER), PUDUCHERRY 
 
148. The Committee notes that the Budget Estimate (BE) for 2024 –25 was ₹1440.00 
crore, which was reduced to ₹1370.00 crore at the RE stage, with an actual expenditure 
of ₹1351.96 crore, while for 2025 –26 the BE of ₹1450.00 crore was revised to ₹1460.79 
crore, against which the expenditure as on 31.01.2026 stands at ₹1087.52 c rore, 
indicating comparatively lower utilisation during the year. Although the BE for 2026 –27 
has been increased to ₹1496.46 crore, against projected requirement of ₹1546.46 crore, 
reflecting growing operational and infrastructure demands. The Committee, t herefore, 
recommends that the Department ensure timely release and optimal utilisation of funds, 
while also strengthening internal monitoring mechanisms for infrastructure projects and 
institutional expenditure so that sanctioned allocations translate into  timely completion 
of projects and improved healthcare delivery capacity. 
(Para 3.21.7) 

287 
 
149. The Committee notes the progress of several infrastructure projects at 
Jawaharlal Institute of Postgraduate Medical Education and Research, Puducherry, 
including t he Regional Cancer Centre annexe building, critical care hospital block, 
residential quarters and the proposed 476 -bedded teaching hospital at the Karaikal 
campus. While some projects are nearing completion, others are still at relatively early 
stages of p hysical progress. Given the rising burden of non -communicable diseases 
(NCDs), particularly cancer and cardiovascular diseases, and the existing shortage of 
inpatient beds, operation theatre capacity and cardiac intervention facilities, the 
Committee stresses the need for expedited completion of ongoing infrastructure projects 
and timely approval of new residential and clinical facilities. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends that the Department prioritize the immediate operationalization of the 
Regional Ca ncer Centre Annexe, which currently stands at 99% physical progress. 
Furthermore, the Committee believes that the Institute must aggressively fast -track the 
development of the Sedrapet Campus. Shifting trauma and transplant cases to that 
dedicated 50 -acre satellite facility is paramount to effectively decongesting the main 
campus and freeing up critical operating theatre (OT) space for elective and specialized 
NCD surgeries. 
(Para 3.21.8) 
 
150. The Committee notes that while JIPMER has outlined comprehensiv e structural 
expansions to manage its disproportionate NCD burden and trauma influx, the mere 
addition of physical infrastructure at the main and Sedrapet campuses will not 
autonomously resolve the systemic waitlists and bed deficits. The Committee believe s 
that these expansions must be inextricably linked to rigorous, real -time performance 
auditing to prevent the new specialized centres from succumbing to the same 
operational bottlenecks currently congesting the existing facilities. 
(Para 3.21.9) 
 
151. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department mandate the 
immediate deployment of a centralized, digital monitoring system explicitly tied to the 
newly defined Key Performance Indicators (KPIs). The Committee is of the view that 
metrics evaluating resource efficiency, such as the downtime versus uptime of high-value 
life-saving assets like Linear Accelerators and Cath Labs, must be strictly audited on a 
monthly basis. Furthermore, the Committee recommends that the Department enforce 
strict accountab ility regarding capacity management and service delivery targets. 
Indicators such as the Outpatient -to-Inpatient Conversion Rate, the 80 -90% Bed 
Occupancy Rate target, and the Trauma Survival Rate must be actively utilized to 
adjust administrative strategies dynamically, ensuring that the heavy investments in the 
Regional Cancer Centre and the new Advanced Trauma Centre translate directly into a 
measurable reduction in patient waitlists and improved clinical outcomes. 
(Para 3.21.10) 
 

288 
 
152. The Committee is concerned to note the substantial vacancies in faculty positions, 
with 85 posts lying vacant out of a sanctioned strength of 412, and the continued reliance 
on contractual staff in several service areas. While acknowledging that recruitment is 
underway thro ugh centralised mechanisms such as AIIMS CRE and NORCET, the 
Committee emphasises that timely filling of vacancies for doctors, nurses, technicians 
and other healthcare personnel is essential to match the expanding infrastructure and 
rising patient load. T he Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department take 
expedited measures to fill all vacant positions on a regular basis. Concurrently, the 
Committee is of the view that the Department should take measures to improve 
retention like providing better working conditions and career progression opportunities 
for medical professionals.  
(Para 3.21.12) 
 
NATIONAL INSTITUTE OF MENTAL HEALTH AND NEURO SCIENCES 
(NIMHANS), BENGALURU 
 
153. The Committee notes that for 2025 –26, the BE of ₹860.00 crore was revised to 
₹866.40 crore, against which ₹693.44 crore has been utilised as on 12.02.2026, indicating 
relatively slow pace of utilisation. While the BE for 2026 –27 has been enhanced to 
₹917.21 crore, the Committee is of the view that effective utilisation of availa ble 
resources is equally important for strengthening institutional capacity. The Committee, 
therefore, recommends strengthening financial planning and monitoring mechanisms so 
that funds allocated to premier institutions like National Institute of Mental H ealth and 
Neuro Sciences are utilised efficiently. 
(Para 3.22.4) 
 
154. The Committee notes the progress of infrastructure projects at NIMHANS, 
Bengaluru, including the completion of the Common Laboratory Complex and 
Psychiatry Specialty Block, while major projects such as the OPD Complex and the 300-
bedded Poly Trauma Centre are at preliminary stages of design and project 
management consultancy. Considering the growing burden of mental health and 
neurological disorders and the need for specialised trauma an d rehabilitation services, 
the Committee emphasises the importance of timely execution of these critical 
infrastructure projects. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department 
closely monitor the progress of these projects, expedite the approval  and appointment of 
project management consultants, and ensure time -bound completion of the OPD 
complex and poly trauma facility, so that the Institute can significantly expand its 
clinical, research and patient care capacities. 
(Para 3.22.5) 
 
155. The Committee observes that despite a sanctioned strength of 2,468 posts across 
various categories, the Institute continues to face significant vacancies in key faculty 
positions such as Professors (7), Associate Professors (5), and Assistant Professors (41), 
along with 36 vacancies in other Group ―A‖ officers and a very large gap in support 

289 
 
staff, especially Hospital/Office Assistants where 303 posts remain unfilled. Such 
shortages inevitably affect clinical services, academic activities, research output, patient 
flow management, and the quality of care. The Committee, therefore, recommends that 
the Department take time -bound measures to fill the large number of vacancies, 
especially in clinical faculty, nursing, technical and support staff categories, in order to  
align human resources with the expanding infrastructure. 
 (Para 3.22.8) 
 
156. The Committee observes that demanding work conditions and comparatively 
limited support infrastructure often contribute to attrition among doctors and other 
critical healthcare staff. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department 
take focused measures to improve working conditions and introduce structured 
retention policies aimed at sustaining a stable and motivated workforce. Such measures 
may include provision of ade quate residential accommodation near the campus, 
reliable transportation facilities, availability of support staff and helpers, safe and well -
equipped working environments, and clearly defined career progression pathways.  
(Para 3.22.9) 
 
157. The Committee  further notes that the Institute has identified several systemic 
challenges including overcrowded facilities, fragmented patient flow, limited 
technological integration, and increasing demand for specialized services. The 
Committee recommends accelerated adoption of digital health technologies including 
Hospital Information Systems, Electronic Medical Records, telemedicine platforms, and 
AI-enabled monitoring tools to streamline patient management, reduce waiting times for 
diagnostics and consultations, an d improve operational efficiency of operation theatres 
and hospital beds.  
(Para 3.22.10) 
 
158. The Committee also underscores the importance of institutionalising a robust 
patient feedback mechanism through both digital and offline channels so that patien ts 
from all backgrounds can share their experiences; such feedback should be analysed 
periodically and transparently displayed on institutional platforms to drive continuous 
quality improvement. Further, in view of the growing recognition of integrative 
healthcare, the Committee suggests that sensitisation regarding traditional Indian 
systems of medicine may be incorporated within medical, nursing, and allied health 
training to complement preventive and palliative care approaches and improve 
affordability and accessibility of healthcare services. 
(Para 3.22.11) 
 
159. The Committee takes into consideration the significant contribution of the 
NIMHANS, Bengaluru in strengthening the country‘s mental health ecosystem through 
innovative digital platforms, decentr alised service delivery, and large -scale capacity 
building initiatives. Programmes such as Tele -MANAS, the Digital Academy, and tele -

290 
 
mentoring models for primary healthcare providers have demonstrated the potential of 
technology-enabled systems to bridge t he long -standing treatment gap, particularly in 
rural and underserved areas. However, considering the rising burden of mental health 
disorders and the limited availability of specialised professionals across the country, the 
Committee recommends that the D epartment further institutionalise and scale up such 
digital mental health platforms across all States and Union Territories. Strengthening 
Tele-MANAS with enhanced multilingual support, integration with district hospitals 
and primary health centres, and l inkage with the District Mental Health Programme 
(DMHP) would ensure timely crisis response, improved referral pathways, and 
continuity of care, thereby significantly expanding access to mental health services in 
remote and underserved regions. 
(Para 3.22.19) 
 
160. The Committee notes that community -based mental health models implemented 
by NIMHANS, including the Sakalawara Rural Psychiatry model, NAMAN 
programme, and tele -mentoring initiatives such as the ECHO model and Diploma in 
Primary Care Psychiatry ( DPCP), have shown encouraging results in reducing 
treatment gaps, improving early detection, and empowering non -specialist healthcare 
providers. As such initiatives demonstrate that task -sharing approaches, combined with 
structured training and community e ngagement, can effectively extend mental health 
services beyond tertiary care institutions. The Committee therefore recommends that 
the Department adopt these models more widely through integration with the National 
Health Mission and the District Mental H ealth Programme, ensuring that trained 
primary care doctors, community health officers, ASHAs, and other frontline workers 
are equipped to identify and manage common mental health conditions at the 
community level. Such decentralised service delivery model s would significantly reduce 
the burden on tertiary institutions while making mental healthcare more accessible and 
affordable for the population. 
(Para 3.22.20) 
 
161. The Committee further notes that several targeted interventions undertaken by 
NIMHANS, s uch as Yuva Spandana for youth mental health, SAMVAD for child 
protection and psychosocial care, USHAS –YASHAS for self -harm prevention, and 
specialised programmes in geriatric and perinatal mental health that have addressed 
critical and often under -recognised segments of the population. Given the growing 
prevalence of stress, anxiety, and self -harm among youth and vulnerable groups, the 
Committee recommends that the Department prioritise expansion of these life -cycle-
based mental health programmes across th e country through collaboration with 
educational institutions, community organisations, and state governments. Particular 
emphasis may be placed on strengthening school and college -based mental health 
support systems, expanding counselling services, and in tegrating psychosocial support 
within maternal and geriatric healthcare services.  
(Para 3.22.21) 

291 
 
162. The Committee takes note of the policy announcements made in the Union 
Budget 2026 -27 regarding the establishment of a second national -level mental healt h 
institute in North India, the up -gradation of regional mental health institutes viz. 
Ranchi, Tezpur, other for decentralized specialist care, and the strengthening of 
emergency and trauma care centres with integrated psychiatric services. While these 
initiatives are expected to significantly enhance the country‘s mental healthcare 
infrastructure, the Committee emphasises the need for a coordinated implementation 
framework under the technical leadership of NIMHANS. The Committee recommends 
that the Departm ent ensure timely operationalisation of the proposed institute and 
regional upgrades while establishing strong academic, training, and digital linkages with 
NIMHANS to maintain uniform standards of care, research, and training across the 
network. In additi on, NIMHANS may be entrusted with developing national protocols, 
quality benchmarks, and monitoring systems to guide the expansion of mental health 
services.  
(Para 3.22.22) 
 
163. The Committee recommends for integration of the Indian Knowledge System 
with modern medicine in a scientific and evidence -based manner to address the growing 
challenges of mental health in the country. Such integration, including the incorporation 
of traditional wellness practices, may complement existing treatment approaches, 
strengthen preventive mental healthcare, and contribute to reducing the rising burden 
of mental health disorders. 
(Para 3.22.23) 
 
NORTH EASTERN INDIRA GANDHI REGIONAL INSTITUTE OF HEALTH AND 
MEDICAL SCIENCES (NEIGRIHMS), SHILLONG

Health and Medical Sciences (NEIGRIHMS), Shillong has demonstrated efficient 
utilisation of its allocated funds in 2024 –25, with 99.71% utilisation of the Revised 
Estimate, however, in 2025 -26, the 80 .91% utilisation of the Revised Estimate as of 
10.02.2026 indi cates moderate utilisation. Further, the Committee observes a 
considerable gap between the projected demand of ₹704.30 crore for 2026 –27 and the 
allocated amount of ₹565.26 crore, particularly under certain revenue components and 
capital expenditure. Given  the expanding scope of services, the proposed establishment 
of the Regional Cancer Centre, and the increasing operational requirements of the 
Institute, the Committee recommends that the Department undertake periodic 
assessment of the Institute‘s financia l needs and ensure adequate and timely budgetary 
support so that critical activities relating to patient care, procurement of medicines and 
consumables, recruitment of manpower, and maintenance of infrastructure are not 
adversely affected. Adequate capital  investment should also be prioritised to support 
modern medical equipment and infrastructure expansion in the region. 
(Para 3.23.4) 

292 
 
 
165. The Committee has been given to understand that  the ongoing construction of 
the 150 -bedded Critical Care Block in NE IGRIHMS under PM -ABHIM, which has 
achieved over 90% physical progress and 85.50% financial progress, although the 
project has experienced delays due to initial site -related constraints, forest clearance

completion of critical healthcare infrastructure projects. The Committee further notes 
the proposed new projects such as the Drug De -addiction Centre, NELS facility, and

services in the North Eastern region. 
(Para 3.23.5) 
 
166. The Committee notes with concern that 399 posts remain vacant out of the 
sanctioned strength of 1,977  in the Institute, including 56 faculty posts, 205 Group ‗B‘ 
posts, 44 Group ‗C‘ posts and 49 Senior Resident Doctor posts , which may significantly 
affect the Institute‘s capacity to deliver timely and quality healthcare services in the 
North Eastern region. Persistent shortages of faculty, resident doctors and paramedical 
staff can adversely impact key service delivery indicators such as patient waiting time, 
bed turnover rate, utilisation of medical equipment, emergency service response time, 
etc., besides affecting critical quality indicators including infection rates, mortality rates, 
patient satisfaction, research output, etc . The Committee recommends that the 
Department should adopt proactive measures to widen the pool of applicants through

North Eastern region.

in FY 2025 -26. The Committee observes a notable shortfall between the Institute's

crore for FY 2026-27. The Committee is of the view that the allocated Capital budget of 
₹112.00 crore may prove insufficient to comfortably procure and install high -end, life-
saving medical assets, such as the planned LINAC machine for radiation oncol ogy 
(costing approximately ₹30 crore), Biplane systems, and 4K 3D Laparoscopy sets, 
alongside the necessary civil works. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the 

293 
 
Institute urgently overhaul its internal procurement and administrative timelines to 
accelerate fund utilization in the final quarter. The Department must proactively re -
evaluate the Capital allocation at the Revised Estimate stage and provide the additional 
funds required so that the acquisition of this critical diagnostic and therapeutic 
equipment is not hindered by foreseeable financial constraints. 
(Para 3.24.3) 
168. The Committee acknowledges the severe geo -political and climatic challenges, 
including ongoing communal violence, curfews, internet bans, and landslides, that have 
plagued Manipur since May 2023, severely disrupting the supply chains for manpower 
and construction materials. Despite these formidable hurdles, the Committee believes 
that the timely completion of the 150 -bedded Critical Care Block (currently at 50% 
progress) and the  1350 KLD -STP/250 KLD -ETP (at 40% progress) is absolutely non -
negotiable for the region's health security. The Committee, therefore, recommends that 
the Department establish a dedicated coordination mechanism with the State 
Government and central security agencies to facilitate a protected logistics corridor for 
essential medical and construction supplies. The Committee is of the view that such 
strategic, inter -agency interventions are imperative to ensure the Executing Agency 
(HSCC Ltd.) meets the June 202 6 deadline for the Critical Care Block, and to expedite 
the immediate functional handover of the completed Maternity Block. 
(Para 3.24.4) 
169. The Committee notes that while RIMS, Imphal was designated as a Regional 
Cancer Centre in 2006, its transition in to a full -fledged Tertiary Cancer Care Centre 
remains constrained by significant gaps in specialized personnel and infrastructure. The 
Committee believes it is concerning that the Radiation Oncology Department is 
currently forced to oversee Medical Oncolog y and Palliative care services due to a lack

fast-track the creation and recruitment of specialized faculty posts for Surgical 
Oncology, Medical Oncology, and Nuclear Medici ne. Furthermore, the Committee is of 
the view that the proposal for the 150 -bedded Cancer Hospital under the Institute's 
Redevelopment Plan must be prioritized for financial clearance to eliminate existing 
infrastructure bottlenecks and reduce the reliance  on interim referral arrangements for 
the North Eastern population. 
(Para 3.24.10) 
 
170. The Committee observes with grave concern that out of 550 total vacancies at 
RIMS, Imphal, a staggering 325 posts, including the crucial Nursing Officer cadres, are 
currently stalled due to pending litigation before the Hon‘ble High Court of Manipur. 
The Committee believes that a 45% vacancy rate in Group C positions severely 
undermines the Institute‘s ability to operationalize new facilities, such as the newly 
constructed Maternity Block and the upcoming 150 -bedded Critical Care Block. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department, in coordination with the 
Institute‘s legal counsel, move for an expedited hearing or interim resolution of these 
court cases to r elease these vital posts for recruitment. The Committee is, additionally, 

294 
 
of the view that the 63 vacancies currently available for immediate recruitment should 
be filled without further delay to support the Institute‘s recent expansion into renal

(Para 3.24.11) 
 
171. The Committee notes the ambitious Master Plan for the Redevelopment of 
RIMS, Imphal, estimated at ₹3,642.91 crore over an eight -year period. The Committee 
believes that such a large -scale modernization is essential for RIMS to function 
effectively as the  13th Center of Excellence for Rare Diseases and to sustain its newly 
initiated open-heart surgery and pediatric cardiac programs. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends that the Department provide a clear, multi -year funding commitment for 
Phase-I of this r edevelopment to ensure that diagnostic modernization, ICU expansion,

The Committee is further of the view that the Department should seriously explore the

(Para 3.24.12) 
REGIONAL INSTITUTE OF PARAMEDICAL AND NURSING SCIENCE, AIZAWL 
 
172. The Committee observes that 19 posts remain vacant out of the sanctioned 
strength of 64 Group ‗A‘ posts, including key academic positions such as Professors, 
Associate Professors and Assistant Professors. Given t hat RIPANS plays an important 
role in strengthening the paramedical and nursing workforce in the North Eastern 
region, the Committee recommends that the Department take proactive steps to expedite 
recruitment and address structural issues affecting faculty  availability. Measures to be 
undertaken include timely completion of promotional processes, review of recruitment 
rules where necessary, wider national outreach for faculty recruitment, and provision of 
incentives to attract qualified professionals to ser ve in remote and geographically 
challenging regions. Ensuring adequate faculty strength is essential for maintaining 
academic standards, expanding training capacity, and supporting the functioning of the 
newly operational hospital facilities. 
(Para 3.25.5) 
 
173. The Committee also takes note of several operational challenges faced by 
RIPANS owing to its location in a remote and hilly region, including difficulties in 
logistics and supply chains, frequent disruptions due to heavy rainfall and landslides, 
lack of local servicing centres for medical equipment, limited physical space for future 
expansion, and recurring issues relating to water supply and power connectivity. These 
factors can adversely affect the timely availability of essential medical supplies,  
maintenance of equipment, and the overall efficiency of healthcare delivery. The 
Committee therefore recommends that the Department, in coordination with the State 
Government and other concerned agencies, explore measures to strengthen logistical 

295 
 
support systems, establish regional maintenance and servicing facilities for medical 
equipment, and develop contingency mechanisms for uninterrupted supply of essential 
utilities such as water, electricity and internet connectivity. 
(Para 3.25.6) 
 
LOKPRIYA GOPINAT H BORDOLOI REGIONAL INSTITUTE OF MENTAL 
HEALTH (LGBRIMH), TEZPUR 
 
174. The Committee finds that the Lokpriya Gopinath Bordoloi Regional Institute of 
Mental Health (LGBRIMH), Tezpur has demonstrated satisfactory utilisation of the 
funds allocated to it, wit h 99.40% utilisation of the Revised Estimate in FY 2024 –25 and 
over 82% utilisation of the Revised Estimate in FY 2025 –26 up to January 2026. 
However, the Committee observes that the Budget Estimate of ₹69.32 crore for FY 
2026–27 is significantly lower than the Institute‘s projected requirement of ₹93.48 crore, 
resulting in a shortfall of about ₹24.16 crore. The Committee further notes with concern 
that after meeting the committed expenditure under GIA (Salary) and GIA (General), 
only a nominal amount of ₹0 .50 crore remains under the Capital head, which is 
insufficient for procurement of essential hospital equipment and infrastructure 
development, despite the recommendation of ₹11.70 crore by the SFC for capital 
expenditure. Considering that LGBRIMH is a key tertiary care mental health institution 
serving the entire North Eastern region, the Committee recommends that the 
Department review the Institute‘s financial requirements and provide adequate capital 
support in subsequent budget allocations so that criti cal equipment procurement, 
infrastructure development, and service expansion are not adversely affected. 
(Para 3.26.7) 
 
175. The Committee also notes that 57 posts remain vacant out of a sanctioned 
strength of 323, including 20 Group ‗A‘ posts, along with substantial shortages of Senior 
Resident Doctors and Junior Residents. The Committee further observes that 
recruitment could not be undertaken during FY 2025 –26 due to insufficient budgetary 
provision under the salary head, which has affected the Institute ‘s ability to expand 
services and maintain adequate clinical, academic, and research capacity. Given that 
LGBRIMH serves as the only tertiary -level standalone mental health institute in the 
North Eastern region, such manpower shortages can significantly co nstrain its ability to 
address the growing burden of mental health disorders in the region. The Committee 
therefore recommends that the Department ensure adequate financial provision for 
manpower requirements and expedite recruitment processes to fill vaca nt posts in a 
time-bound manner. Simultaneously, efforts should be made to create a more attractive 
professional ecosystem through improved facilities, research opportunities, and 
academic incentives in order to reduce attrition and attract qualified menta l health 
professionals to serve in the Institute. 
(Para 3.26.8) 
 

296 
 
176. The Committee further notes that LGBRIMH continues to face several structural 
and institutional challenges, including limited faculty strength, the need for specialised 
multidisciplinary centres, constraints in managing co -morbid medical conditions among 
psychiatric patients, and the absence of adequate autonomy to design and expand 
training programmes comparable to other premier national institutes. The Committee, 
therefore, recommends t hat the Department consider strengthening the institutional 
capacity of LGBRIMH through enhanced academic and administrative autonomy, 
enabling it to develop specialised training programmes, expand multidisciplinary 
services, and confer advanced qualificat ions in mental health disciplines. Additionally, 
the Committee suggests that the Department expedite the preparation and approval of 
estimates for pending capital and maintenance works through CPWD and other 
executing agencies so that essential infrastructure development and campus facilities can 
be completed in a timely manner. Strengthening the institutional and infrastructural 
capacity of LGBRIMH will be critical for improving access to specialised mental 
healthcare and training of professionals across the North Eastern region. 
(Para 3.26.9) 
National Health Mission 
 
177. The Committee notes that the National Health Mission (NHM) has played a 
pivotal role in strengthening India‘s public health system and advancing progress 
towards the targets set under the  National Health Policy, 2017 and Sustainable 
Development Goal -3. Significant improvements have been recorded in several health 
indicators, including an increase in life expectancy to 70.3 years, reduction in Infant 
Mortality Rate to 25 per thousand, Mater nal Mortality Ratio to 88 per lakh live births, 
and high levels of immunisation coverage and antenatal care. The Committee 
appreciates the progress made in elimination of diseases such as Kala -Azar and 
sustained control of leprosy, along with improved TB t reatment coverage. However, the 
Committee observes that certain targets, such as further reduction in under -five 
mortality, neonatal mortality, and improvement in family planning coverage, still 
require sustained attention. The Committee therefore recommen ds that the Department 
strengthen targeted interventions under NHM, particularly in States and districts that 
continue to lag behind national averages, through enhanced monitoring, data -driven 
planning, and focused support under Programme Implementation Pl ans (PIPs). Greater 
emphasis may also be placed on preventive healthcare, early detection of diseases, and 
behavioural change communication to consolidate the gains already achieved and 
accelerate progress towards remaining health targets. 
(Para 4.1.7) 
 
178. The Committee observes the steady expansion of services under the National 
Urban Health Mission (NUHM), with a significant increase in the number of Urban 
Primary Health Centres, polyclinics, Ayushman Arogya Mandirs, and healthcare 
personnel over the last three years, which has contributed to rising OPD attendance and 
improved service delivery in urban areas. While these developments indicate progress in 
strengthening urban health infrastructure, the Committee emphasises that rapid 

297 
 
urbanisation, migratio n, and the growth of informal settlements continue to place 
considerable pressure on urban public health systems. The Committee therefore 
recommends that the Department should strengthen the NUHM framework by ensuring 
adequate human resources, improving ur ban health infrastructure, and enhancing 
coordination with Urban Local Bodies to effectively address the healthcare needs of 
vulnerable populations, including urban poor and migrant workers. The Committee also 
suggests expanding community participation thr ough Mahila Arogya Samitis and 
ASHAs, and strengthening initiatives such as free diagnostics, free drug services, and 
mobile medical units to ensure equitable and accessible healthcare services in urban 
areas. 
(Para 4.1.8) 
Budgetary Allocation 
 
179. The Co mmittee notes that the expenditure under the National Health Mission 
(NHM) for FY 2024 –25 exceeded the Revised Estimates, indicating strong demand for 
financial resources and the critical role of the Mission in supporting public health 
systems across the c ountry. However, the Committee also observes that in FY 2025 –26, 
the utilisation of funds up to 31.01.2026 stands at about 72.41 per cent of the Revised 
Estimates, which appears relatively low considering that only two months remain in the 
financial year. The Committee is of the view that such variations between allocation, 
utilisation, and actual expenditure point towards issues relating to financial planning, 
timely release of funds, and absorption capacity at the State/UT level. The Committee, 
therefore, recommends that the Department must strengthen monitoring and 
coordination mechanisms with States/UTs to ensure timely submission of Programme 
Implementation Plans (PIPs), faster approval of Record of Proceedings (RoPs), and 
more efficient utilisation of allocated funds. The Committee also desires that the 
Department put in place a robust system for periodic review of expenditure trends so 
that mid -year corrections can be undertaken to avoid both underutilisation and 
overshooting of allocations. 
(Para 4.1.15) 
180. The Committee observes that a substantial portion of the allocation under NHM 
for FY 2026–27 is directed towards the Flexible Pool for Reproductive and Child Health 
(RCH), Health System Strengthening, National Health Programmes, and the National 
Urban Health Mission, which together account for the major share of the total 
allocation. Given the expanding scope of services under the Ayushman Arogya Mandirs 
and the ongoing infrastructure development under the Pradhan Mantri Ayushman 
Bharat Health Infrastructure Mission, the Committee emphasises the need for sustained 
investments in strengthening primary healthcare infrastructure, human resources, and 
diagnostic capacities at the grassroots level. The Committee recommends that the 
Department prioritise effective implementation of these components by ensuring 
adequate staffing of health facilities, timely operationalisation of newly created 
infrastructure, and improved integration of digital health systems such as HMIS for 
real-time monitoring of service delivery. The Committee also urges the Department to 
work closely with States/UTs to address regional disparities in health infrastructure and 

298 
 
service delivery so that the benefits of NHM interventions reach vulnerable and 
underserved populations in both rural and urban areas. 
(Para 4.1.16) 
181. The Committee notes with satisfaction that no Utilisation Certificate (UC) is 
pending under the National Health Mission (NHM) and that the release of funds to 
States/UTs is being carried out in accordance with the p rovisions of the General 
Financial Rules, whereby up to 75 per cent of the Central allocation is released on the 
basis of provisional UCs and the remaining 25 per cent upon submission of audited UCs. 
While this reflects an overall sound financial complianc e framework, the Committee is 
of the view that the effective utilisation of funds must be complemented by tangible 
improvements in health infrastructure, quality of services and accessibility of healthcare 
facilities, particularly in underserved regions. I n this regard, the Committee 
recommends that the Department further strengthen its monitoring and evaluation 
mechanisms to ensure that the financial resources released under NHM translate into 
measurable improvements in service delivery, especially through  initiatives such as the 
National Quality Assurance Standards (NQAS), the Free Drugs and Free Diagnostics 
Service Initiatives, and teleconsultation platforms. The Committee also desires that the 
Department develop a comprehensive outcome -based monitoring f ramework, linking 
financial releases with performance indicators such as quality certification of health 
facilities, availability of essential drugs and diagnostics, and reduction in Out -of-Pocket 
Expenditure (OOPE) for patients. 
(Para 4.1.21) 
 
182. The Committee further notes that despite the various initiatives undertaken by 
the Government under NHM, including incentives for medical professionals, multi -
skilling of doctors, expansion of medical education capacity and digital health initiatives 
such as teleconsultation and the Ayushman Bharat Digital Mission (ABDM), challenges 
related to shortage and uneven distribution of human resources, particularly in rural 
and remote areas, continue to persist. The Committee is of the considered view that 
strengthening the health workforce is critical for ensuring the effective functioning of 
primary, secondary and tertiary healthcare systems. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends that the Department, in close coordination with States/UTs, intensify 
efforts to address the gaps in availability of doctors, specialists, nurses and paramedical 
staff by ensuring timely recruitment, rational deployment and adequate capacity 
building of healthcare personnel. The Committee also urges the Department to further 
leverage digital hea lth platforms, telemedicine services and the expanding network of 
Ayushman Arogya Mandirs to enhance access to specialist care and improve the quality 
of healthcare delivery, particularly in aspirational districts, remote rural areas and 
urban slums, there by ensuring equitable access to affordable healthcare across the 
country. 
(Para 4.1.22) 
183. The Committee finds the Ayushman Arogya Mandirs (AAMs) are envisaged as 
the cornerstone of comprehensive primary healthcare under the National Health 

299 
 
Mission, delivering twelve packages of services including reproductive and child health, 
management of communicable and non -communicable diseases, palliative and elderly 
care, mental health services, and basic emergency care. The Committee also takes note 
of the expans ion of essential drugs and diagnostics at PHC -AAMs and SHC -AAMs, as 
well as the extensive use of teleconsultation services which have facilitated more than 41 
crore consultations as on 30.11.2025. While appreciating these efforts, the Committee is 
of the view that the effectiveness of AAMs depends largely on the consistent availability 
of trained human resources, uninterrupted supply of medicines and diagnostics, and 
robust digital connectivity, particularly in remote, tribal and aspirational districts. The  
Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department strengthen the operational 
capacity of AAMs by ensuring regular monitoring of service delivery standards, timely 
replenishment of drugs and diagnostics, and adequate training and retention of 
Community Health Officers and frontline health workers. The Committee also desires 
that the Department expand the integration of telemedicine and digital health platforms 
so that specialist consultations and referral services are seamlessly available at the 
primary healthcare level, thereby improving continuity of care and reducing the burden 
on secondary and tertiary healthcare facilities. 
(Para 4.1.28) 
 
184. The Committee takes note of the progress made in improving maternal and child 
health indicators in the count ry, particularly the steady decline in the Maternal 
Mortality Ratio and the targeted interventions aimed at reducing Infant Mortality Rate 
through initiatives such as Facility Based Newborn Care, Mother Newborn Care Units, 
Special Newborn Care Units and co mmunity-based follow-up through ASHA workers. 
While acknowledging that several States have already achieved the Sustainable 
Development Goal target for Maternal Mortality Ratio, the Committee observes that 
disparities persist across regions and that high -burden districts continue to require 
focused attention. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department intensify 
targeted interventions in States and districts with relatively higher maternal and child 
mortality indicators by strengthening institu tional delivery systems, improving referral 
transport and emergency obstetric care services, and enhancing community -based 
monitoring through frontline health workers. The Committee also urges the Department 
to leverage digital initiatives such as the prop osed longitudinal health tracking system 
under Rashtriya Bal Swasthya Karyakram to ensure early identification of high -risk 
mothers and children, better follow -up care, and improved data -driven planning for 
maternal and child healthcare programmes across the country. 
(Para 4.1.29) 
 
STRENGTHENING OF STATE DRUG REGULATORY SYSTEM 
185. The Committee expresses serious concern that the budgetary allocation for the 
Strengthening of State Drug Regulatory System (SSDRS) was reduced from ₹56.00 
crore in BE 2025 -26  t o nil at the Revised Estimates stage for FY 2025 –26, and that no 
provision exists in the Budget Estimates for 2026 –27, thereby meaning that no 

300 
 
budgetary allocation was made for  strengthening of States Drug Regulatory System. 
The Committee is concerned to find such abrupt financial hiatus, resulting from 
procedural delays in approving the scheme's extension and the subsequent SSDRS 2.0 
proposal, threatens to derail the hard -won infrastructural gains achieved since the 
scheme's inception. Allowing newly esta blished state drug testing laboratories and 
upgraded drug control offices to face a funding vacuum undermines the core objective 
of ensuring affordable, high -quality generic medicines. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends that the Department pursue the imm ediate approval of the ₹496.16 crore 
SSDRS 2.0 scheme with the Department of Expenditure. Furthermore, the Committee is 
of the view that the Department must explore interim financial mechanisms or 
supplementary grants to ensure that the 19 new and 28 upgra ded State drug testing 
laboratories do not face operational paralysis while awaiting the formal rollout of the 
revamped scheme. 
(Para 4.2.6) 
186. The Committee observes that while SSDRS 1.0 successfully expanded the 
national functional testing capacity to 1,66,830 samples per annum, the actual 
realization of this capacity remains critically constrained by persistent State -level 
manpower gaps. Conversely, the Committee commends the remarkable efficacy of the 
Risk-Based Inspection (RBI) initiative, noting tha t out of 1,230 inspected units, a 
staggering 1,140 required stringent regulatory actions such as Stop Production Orders 
(SPOs) and license suspensions. The Committee believes that state -of-the-art testing 
infrastructure is rendered futile without an adequa te cadre of trained analysts and 
uncompromising enforcement mechanisms. The Committee, therefore, recommends that 
the Department, under the ambit of the upcoming SSDRS 2.0, mandate specific, time -
bound targets for States to recruit and upskill regulatory a nd technical personnel as a 
precondition for future fund disbursements. The Committee is of the view that the Risk - 
Based Inspection framework must be permanently institutionalized, standardized, and 
fully digitized across all State Drug Control offices to  ensure that crackdowns on non -
compliant manufacturing practices remain consistent, transparent, and data -driven 
nationwide to avoid public health crisis occurred in Madhya Pradesh due to 
consumption of contaminated cough syrup. 
(Para 4.2.7) 
187. The Commi ttee notes with concern the significant administrative bottlenecks 
hindering the swift clearance of medical devices, particularly the Class C and Class D 
categories regulated by the Central Drugs Standard Control Organisation (CDSCO). 
The Committee observe s that out of 2,999 manufacturing and import applications 
processed, approximately 62% were subjected to queries more than twice, and 253 
import applications, even those possessing USFDA/CE certifications, have been pending 
for over 90 days. The Committee believes that such repetitive query cycles indicate a 
systemic lack of clarity in the initial submission guidelines and point toward an 
inefficient, iterative assessment process. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the 
Department immediately overhaul  the 'MD Online' portal to include a mandatory, AI -
assisted pre-submission validation checklist to ensure applications are complete before 

301 
 
formal submission. The Committee is of the view that CDSCO must adopt a "single 
comprehensive query" policy, wherein all deficiencies in an application are identified 
and communicated to the applicant in a single instance, rather than in piece -meal 
fashion. The Committee, furthermore, recommends the establishment of a dedicated 
facilitation cell within CDSCO to assist do mestic manufacturers in navigating the 
Medical Device Rules (MDR) 2017, thereby reducing the delays currently attributed to 
pending applicant responses. 
(Para 4.2.14) 
 
188. The Committee acknowledges the constitutionally mandated decentralized 
framework wh ere State Authorities regulate drugs and Class A/B devices, while the 
Centre oversees imports and Class C/D devices. The Committee recommends for a 
decentralized framework for imports and Class C/D devices. The Committee strongly 
recommends that CDSCO approval for imports be completely decentralized to the State 
Authorities. Centralization with the DGCI at the Central level has created major 
bottlenecks and is the biggest hurdle in India being at the forefront of Medical devices 
manufacturing. The Committee  recommends for decentralization of approval for all 
Medical Devices with the State Drug Authorities to reduce pendency. In this current 
phase when we are competing with China, Vietnam, Malaysia giving CDSCO approvals 
for even already approved devices/ tec hnologies beyond a period of 45 days in resulting 
in India losing its competitive edge.  
(Para 4.2.15) 
 
189. The Committee further observes that CDSCO has generated substantial revenue 
from medical device licensing, collecting between ₹87.18 crore and ₹118.69 crore 
annually over the last three financial years. The Committee, therefore, recommends that 
the reven ue earned should be used for self -financing of the Institution. A designated 
percentage of the revenue collected by CDSCO be ring -fenced and directly reinvested 
into upgrading the digital and technical infrastructure of both the Central and State 
regulatory bodies. The Committee is of the view that the Department must utilize these 
funds to develop a unified, real -time National Regulatory Dashboard that seamlessly 
integrates CDSCO‘s 'MD Online' portal with State -level licensing databases. This will 
ensure t hat intelligence regarding non -compliant manufacturers, suspended licenses, 
and spurious drug trails is instantly shared across jurisdictions, leveraging both central 
and state expertise to guarantee uniform enforcement of the Drugs and Cosmetics Act 
nationwide. 
(Para 4.2.16) 
 
190. The Committee finds significant vacancy position in the regulatory workforce 
under the Drugs and Medical Devices verticals, particularly at the level of Drugs 
Inspectors and Assistant Drugs Inspectors. While the sanctioned streng th under the 
Drugs Vertical stands at 613 posts, as many as 257 posts remain vacant, including 186 
vacancies of Drugs Inspectors. In the Medical Devices Vertical, only 24 personnel are in 

302 
 
position against a sanctioned strength of 249 posts. The Committee n otes that delays in 
recruitment were partly due to litigation and pending revision of Recruitment Rules, 
and that proposals for filling a number of posts have now been forwarded to the Union 
Public Service Commission. The Committee is of the view that such  a large number of 
vacancies could adversely affect regulatory efficiency, quality surveillance and 
enforcement activities. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department 
take urgent and time -bound measures, in coordination with the Union Public Service 
Commission, to expedite the recruitment process and fill the vacant posts. The 
Committee also urges the Department to review the overall human resource framework 
of the regulatory system and consider strengthening technical capacity through 
specialised recruitment, structured training programmes and career progression 
mechanisms so that the regulatory apparatus keeps pace with the rapid growth and 
technological advancement of the pharmaceutical and medical devices sectors in the 
country. 
(Para 4.2.18) 
TERTIARY CARE PROGRAMS (TCPs)  
191. The Committee notes that the Tertiary Care Programmes under the National 
Health framework support a number of critical public health initiatives such as the 
National Mental Health Programme, National Programme for Pre vention and Control 
of Cancer, Diabetes, Cardiovascular Diseases and Stroke, Trauma Care, Health Care 
for the Elderly, Telemedicine and Tobacco Control. The Committee, however, observes 
a recurring pattern of reduction in allocations at the Revised Estimat es stage however 
partial utilisation of funds across several components. In 2024 –25, the actual 
expenditure of ₹287.72 crore fell short of the Revised Estimates of ₹322.90 crore, and in 
2025–26, only ₹167.10 crore (about 57 per cent of the reduced RE) had been utilised up 
to 17.02.2026. The Committee observes with serious concern that the shortfall is largely 
attributed to delays at the State level in fulfilling procedural requirements such as 
utilisation of earlier instalments, creation of Single Nodal Acc ounts, submission of 
utilisation certificates, and compliance with financial reporting norms. The Committee 
is of the view that these procedural and administrative constraints are adversely 
affecting the timely implementation of important tertiary healthca re programmes. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department strengthen coordination and 
capacity-building mechanisms with States/UTs to ensure timely compliance with 
financial procedures, faster on boarding of institutions under the SNA -SPARSH system, 
and prompt submission of utilisation certificates and procurement proposals so that the 
allocated funds are effectively utilised for strengthening tertiary healthcare services. 
(Para 4.3.8) 
 
192. The Committee expresses dissatisfaction to find that  several critical components 
such as the National Mental Health Programme and the National Programme for 
Prevention and Control of Cancer, Diabetes, Cardiovascular Diseases and Stroke have 
witnessed substantial reductions in allocations at the Revised Esti mates stage due to 
delays in project proposals, procurement processes and institutional readiness. 

303 
 
Considering the rising burden of non -communicable diseases, mental health issues and 
trauma-related cases in the country, the Committee is of the view that s ustained and 
predictable funding for these programmes is essential for strengthening specialised 
healthcare services. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department should 
adopt a more proactive planning and monitoring framework to ensure timely 
submission of proposals by States/UTs, expeditious approval of projects, and improved 
utilisation of funds under these programmes. The Committee also desires that special 
attention be given to expanding telemedicine infrastructure, strengthening trauma and  
emergency care facilities, and enhancing institutional capacities for cancer and mental 
health care so that tertiary healthcare services become more accessible and responsive to 
the growing healthcare needs of the population. 
(Para 4.3.9) 
National Mental Health Programme 
 
193. The Committee notes that the Manpower Development Scheme under the 
National Mental Health Programme has contributed significantly to strengthening the 
availability of trained mental health professionals in the country through the 
establishment of Centres of Excellence and the strengthening of postgraduate 
departments in medical institutions. The Committee notes that the Scheme has 
facilitated the creation of 594 training seats across key mental health disciplines and has 
produced 2345 trained professionals over the years.  
(Para 4.3.14) 
194. While acknowledging these achievements, the Committee, however, is of the view 
that the growing burden of mental health disorders and the shortage of specialised 
professionals across many States re quire further expansion and modernisation of 
tertiary mental health infrastructure. The Committee, therefore, recommends the 
Department to strengthen the Tertiary Care Services for effective implementation of 
National Mental Health Program (Tertiary Care),  Program may be restructured and 
continued through the fresh identification and selection of Centres of Excellence, 
aligning with current sectoral priorities and emerging needs. To sustain the increase in 
trained mental health manpower, to develop state -of-the-art mental healthcare facilities, 
and to promote the generation of knowledge and evidence through research on mental 
health issues, it is imperative to restructure the National Mental Health Program 
(Tertiary Care). In the current healthcare scenario,  there is a pressing need for 
integrated neuropsychiatric care encompassing Psychiatry, Neurology, and 
Neurosurgery under a single institutional framework. The convergence of these 
disciplines is essential for addressing complex mental and neurological dis orders 
comprehensively and efficiently. The National Mental Health Program needs to be 
restructured to create state -of-the art infrastructure, advanced diagnostic and 
therapeutic facilities, interdisciplinary collaboration, academic excellence, digital hea lth 
integration and strong research and policy development capabilities by providing 
financial aid to Mental Health Institutes and Hospitals in the country. The Manpower 
Development Scheme A & B may be modified to incorporate Mental Health Institutes, 

304 
 
Medical Colleges, or Hospitals by new identification and selection including the 
opportunity for the Institutes that were previously approved under Manpower 
Development Scheme A & B, during earlier plan periods. 
(Para 4.3.15) 
195. The Committee expresses deep concern over the increasing incidence of 
depression and other psychiatric disorders in recent years, including within the medical 
and healthcare community. The Committee is of the view that the rising burden of 
mental health conditions calls for strengthen ing the availability of trained psychiatrists 
and promoting greater focus on mental health within the healthcare system. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department take steps to expand 
psychiatric education and training by strengthening menta l health departments in 
medical institutions, increasing postgraduate opportunities in psychiatry and 
encouraging medical graduates to pursue the specialization. At the same time, the 
Committee emphasises the importance of preventive and holistic approache s to mental 
wellbeing and suggests that practices such as Yoga and other elements of the Indian 
Knowledge System be promoted.  
(Para 4.3.16) 
National Programme for Prevention & Control of Non -Communicable Diseases (NP -
NCD)- Tertiary care 
196. The Committee  also notes that under the National Programme for Prevention 
and Control of Non -Communicable Diseases (NP -NCD), support is being provided to 
State Cancer Institutes and Tertiary Care Cancer Centres to expand specialised 
oncology services across the country . The establishment of Hospital -Based Cancer 
Registries under the National Cancer Registry Programme has enabled systematic data 
collection and evidence-based planning for cancer control. The Committee observes that 
several institutions have been supported  under the scheme, however, the Committee 
desires that remaining all approved institutes become fully functional given the rising 
burden of cancer and other non -communicable diseases in the country. The Committee, 
in this regard, recommends that the Depart ment intensify efforts to ensure early 
operationalisation of all sanctioned State Cancer Institutes and Tertiary Cancer Care 
Centres by addressing delays in infrastructure development, procurement of equipment 
and staffing. The Committee desires the Depart ment to strengthen the cancer registry 
network and ensure effective utilisation of the generated data for policy formulation, 
resource allocation and targeted interventions aimed at improving early detection, 
treatment outcomes and equitable access to tert iary cancer care services across the 
country. 
(Para 4.3.18) 
 
 
 
 

305 
 
HUMAN RESOURCE FOR HEALTH AND MEDICAL EDUCATION 
 
197. The Committee understands that the Human Resource for Health and Medical 
Education (HRH & ME) scheme plays a crucial role in addressing th e shortage of 
medical professionals and reducing regional imbalances in access to medical education 
and healthcare services. The Committee points out that although substantial allocations 
have been made under the Head, there has been a significant reductio n at the Revised 
Estimates stage. A  Budget Estimate of ₹1,274.79 crore was provided in 2024 –25, which 
was reduced to ₹579.12 crore at the Revised Estimates stage (indicating substantial 
reduction at RE stage). Against this, the actual expenditure during the year amounted to 
₹549.33 crore. For the year 2025 –26, an amount of ₹1,675.00 crore was provided at the 
Budget Estimates stage and revised to ₹1,630.00 crore at the Revised Estimates stage, 
against which an expenditure of ₹779.48 crore had been incurred up to 31.01.2026 
(which is about 48% of reduced RE allocation). 
(Para 4.4.6) 
 
198. The Committee finds that under the scheme, a total of 157 new medical colleges 
have been approved but out of that 137 Medical Colleges are functional, with expansion 
of MBBS seats in existing institutions, and  the creation of postgraduate medical seats 
across the country. While appreciating the initiatives, the Committee is of the view that 
timely completion and operationalisation of approved institutions are essential for 
translating financial allocations into  tangible improvements in healthcare manpower. 
The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department strengthen monitoring and 
coordination with States/UTs to ensure timely submission of DPRs, completion of 
infrastructure projects, recruitment of facult y and adherence to funding conditions 
under the SNA -SPARSH framework so that the approved medical colleges and 
expanded seats become fully functional within the stipulated timelines. There is need to 
strengthen infrastructure in medical college and maintai n quality of Medical education 
to produce professionals of global quality instead of below -average mass production of 
bunch of incompetent medical professionals who will definitely prove threat to human 
life and safety due to lack of clinical exposure and quality education.  
(Para 4.4.7) 
 
199. The Committee further notes that under the scheme for augmenting nursing 
education, the construction of six Colleges of Nursing in Rajasthan has been completed 
but none of them had commenced operations during the acad emic year 2025 –26. 
Considering the critical shortage of trained nursing professionals in the healthcare 
system, the Committee is of the view that such delays undermine the objective of 
strengthening the health workforce. The Committee, therefore, recommend s that the 
Department work closely with the concerned State Government to ensure early 
operationalisation of the completed Colleges of Nursing and to expedite similar projects 
across other States. The Committee also urges the Department to adopt a 
comprehensive approach towards strengthening the entire healthcare workforce by 
simultaneously expanding medical, nursing and allied health education capacities, 

306 
 
particularly in underserved regions, so that the growing demand for skilled health 
professionals in the country can be effectively met. 
(Para 4.4.8) 
200. The Committee strongly recommends for adoption of nearby Primary Health 
Care (PHC), CHC and sub centers hospitals by medical colleges both government and 
private can significantly strengthen healthcare de livery at the grassroots level. It can 
improve the availability of doctors and ensure a steady presence of interns, postgraduate 
trainees, and other healthcare personnel. This additional manpower can enhance service 
delivery, support preventive and promoti ve health activities, and improve continuity of 
care. Such collaboration would particularly benefit maternal and child health services 
by ensuring better antenatal care, safe deliveries, immunization coverage, and early 
identification and management of hig h-risk cases, while also providing valuable 
community-based training opportunities for medical students. 
 
(Para 4.4.9) 
PRADHAN MANTRI AYUSHMAN BHARAT HEALTH INFRASTRUCTURE 
MISSION (PM-ABHIM) 
 
201. The Committee notes that the Pradhan Mantri Ayushman Bharat  Health 
Infrastructure Mission (PM-ABHIM) was launched with a substantial outlay of ₹64,180 
crore for the period 2021 –22 to 2025 –26 with the objective of strengthening public 
health infrastructure across primary, secondary and tertiary levels of care. The Mission 
envisages a wide range of infrastructure interventions including construction of 
building-less Sub -Health Centres as Ayushman Arogya Mandirs, establishment of 
Urban Health and Wellness Centres, Block Public Health Units, Integrated Public 
Health La boratories and Critical Care Hospital Blocks across districts. While 
appreciating the comprehensive design of the Mission aimed at strengthening health 
systems, surveillance networks and emergency preparedness, the Committee is of the 
view that the success  of such a large -scale initiative depends on timely completion of 
infrastructure, operationalisation of facilities and adequate human resources and 
equipment support. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department 
strengthen coordination and moni toring mechanisms with States/UTs to ensure timely 
completion of sanctioned infrastructure, adherence to project timelines and optimal 
utilisation of funds so that the envisaged public health facilities become fully functional 
at the earliest to facilitate healthcare of the global standard. 
(Para 4.5.3) 
202. The Committee is displeased to observe the persistent and drastic under -
utilization of funds under the PM -ABHIM scheme, evidenced by the severe reduction of 
the 2025–26 Budget Estimate from ₹4,200.00 crore to a Revised Estimate of ₹2,442.50 
crore, of which  only 59% had been utilized by January 2026. The Committee believes 
that while the onboarding of all 31 States and UTs onto the SNA -SPARSH platform is a 
necessary financial reform for transparency, but such transition should not become a 

307 
 
perennial bottlene ck that stalls the rollout of critical health infrastructure. The 
Committee, therefore, desires that the Department should immediately deploy dedicated 
Task Forces. These task forces must move beyond basic capacity building and actively 
assist state treasu ries in mapping implementing agencies, reconciling unspent balances, 
and expediting the fulfillment of conditions required for subsequent lump -sum releases. 
The Committee is of the view that until the SNA -SPARSH system is fully stabilized, the 
Department should conditionally ease procedural rigidities to ensure that fund flows for 
life-saving infrastructure are not artificially constricted. 
(Para 4.5.7) 
203. The Committee observes that the fundamental delays in executing PM -ABHIM 
are consistently attributed to the gestation periods inherent in capital -intensive projects, 
including the preparation of Detailed Project Reports (DPRs), land acquisition, and 
state-specific tendering processes across various components like Integrated Public 
Health Laboratories (I PHLs) and Critical Care Hospital Blocks (CCHBs). The 
Committee believes that leaving States entirely to their own administrative capacities to 
navigate these complex pre -construction phases inevitably leads to the highly uneven 
physical and financial progr ess currently observed. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends that the Department establish a centralized 'Infrastructure Project 
Management Unit' (IPMU) strictly dedicated to PM -ABHIM. This unit should provide 
States with pre -approved, standardized DPR tem plates, centralized architectural 
blueprints, and model tendering documents to bypass local bureaucratic inertia. 
Furthermore, the Committee is of the view that future fund releases must be linked to 
centrally monitored timelines for land allotment and hum an resource recruitment, 
ensuring that once physical infrastructure is erected, it does not lie dormant due to a 
lack of specialized medical personnel. 
(Para 4.5.8) 
204. The Committee is displeased to find the stark disparity between the initiation of 
infrastructure projects and their actual operationalization under PM -ABHIM. While 
85% of the 621 approved Critical Care Blocks (CCBs) have commenced construction, a 
critically low 4% are currently functional. Similarly, functional rates for Integrated 
Public Health Laboratories (IPHLs) and Block Public Health Units (BPHUs) languish at 
35% and 39%, respectively. This is a matter of concern to the Committee. The 
Committee believes that the ultimate metric of a healthcare scheme's success is not 
merely the progression of civil works, but the actual delivery of accessible services to the 
public. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department immediately pivot 
its primary monitoring focus from 'work started' milestones to 'functional status' 
outcomes. This must be achieved by strictly synchronizing the procurement of essential 
medical equipment and the recruitment of specialized human resources with the 
ongoing civil construction timelines. The Committee is of the view that the national 
Program Management Un it must mandate that states initiate hiring and procurement 
processes well before the physical completion of the buildings, ensuring that newly 
constructed facilities do not sit idle due to administrative delays. 
(Para 4.5.11) 

308 
 
205. In view of recurring and  concerning trend of substantial budgetary reduction at 
RE stage and further under -utilization, of allocated fund, the Committee wishes to 
highlighted that despite such grave inability to absorb funds, the allocation of ₹4,200.00 
crore has been proposed fo r FY 2026 -27. The Committee believes that while capital -
intensive schemes involve inherent gestation periods, however projecting estimates 
should calculate the ground -level execution capacity, otherwise vagabond budgeting 
with distorts broader financial pl anning. The Committee, therefore, recommends that 
the Department formulate realistic, milestone -based budgetary demand intricately tied 
to the actual absorptive capacity of implementing agencies of States and UTs.  
(Para 4.5.12) 
AYUSHMAN BHARAT-PRADHAN MANTRI JAN AROGYA YOJANA (AB-PMJAY) 
 
206. The Committee notes that the budgetary allocation for AB -PMJAY has seen a 
progressive increase, with ₹9,500.00 crore allocated for FY 2026 -27, however, the 
Committee observes that fund utilization in FY 2025 -26 appear ed sluggish, with only 
₹6,868.22 crore (approximately 73% of the BE) utilized as of January 2026. The 
Committee believes that such slow pace of expenditure is inconsistent with the recent, 
massive expansion of the scheme‘s mandate, keeping in view inclusio n of 37 lakh 
families of ASHAs and Anganwadi workers, as well as the universal coverage of all 
senior citizens aged 70 and above. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the 
Department conduct a mid -year actuarial review of the claims ratio to ensure tha t the 
BE for 2026-27 is sufficient to cover the anticipated surge in secondary and tertiary care 
demand from these high -utilization groups. The Committee is of the view that the 
Department must streamline the settlement process between the National Health 
Authority (NHA) and State Health Agencies to accelerate fund absorption, ensuring that 
the world's largest health assurance scheme is backed by a financial pipeline that is as 
responsive as its expanded mandate. 
(Para 4.6.5) 
207. The Committee observes tha t as of January 2026, 33,271 hospitals have been 
empanelled, with a split between public (17,508) and private (15,763) institutions. While 
the creation of 43.17 crore Ayushman Cards is commendable, the Committee believes 
that the true efficacy of the schem e lies in the "portability" and "cashless" nature of the 
services delivered. The Committee is concerned that with 11.46 crore authorized 
hospital admissions, the pressure on the existing network of 33,271 hospitals will 
intensify considering the inclusion of senior citizens. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends that the Department launch a targeted drive to empanel more private 
tertiary care specialty hospitals, particularly in underserved and remote areas, to reduce 
the travel burden for vulnerable populations. Furthermore, the Committee is of the view 
that the Department must institutionalize a robust, real -time "Patient Satisfaction and 
Outcome Audit" to ensure that the "completely paperless and cashless" promise is 
strictly adhered to on the ground, prev enting any instances of unauthorized out -of-
pocket charges at the point of care. 
(Para 4.6.6) 

309 
 
208. The Committee notes the enhanced allocation of ₹9,500 crore for FY 2026 –27 
under Ayushman Bharat–Pradhan Mantri Jan Arogya Yojana (AB PM -JAY) in view of 
the expansion of beneficiary categories, including senior citizens aged 70 years and 
above and frontlin e workers such as ASHA, AWW and AWH. The Committee is of the 
view that while the scheme has contributed to reducing out -of-pocket expenditure and 
has enabled a large number of cashless hospitalisations across the country, the 
Committee observes that challe nges remain in terms of outreach, timely claim 
settlement, and adequacy of coverage for critical and high-cost treatments.  
(Para 4.6.21) 
 
209. The Committee reiterates its recommendation that the Department undertake a 
comprehensive review of the scheme with a view to further widening coverage, including 
rationalisation of the eligibility criteria for senior citizens by considering lowering the 
age threshold from 70 years to 60 years irrespective of socio -economic status. The 
Committee also recommends that  the Department evaluate the feasibility of enhancing 
the existing insurance cover from ₹5 lakh to ₹10 lakh limit in view of rising medical costs 
and expensive tertiary care interventions. Further, the Department may review and 
expand the list of procedure s and diagnostics covered under the scheme, particularly 
high-end investigations such as CT scans, MRI and nuclear imaging, as well as advanced 
treatments for critical illnesses, so as to ensure that beneficiaries receive comprehensive 
and financially prot ected healthcare services without being compelled to incur 
additional out-of-pocket expenditure. 
(Para 4.6.22) 
 
210. The Committee notes that the Government had announced the inclusion of gig 
and platform workers under the scheme with the objective of exte nding health 
protection to this emerging segment of the workforce, however, the Committee is 
constrained to observe that the proposal has not yet been operationalised as the matter is 
stated to be under consideration with the Ministry of Labour and Employm ent. The 
Committee emphasises that gig and platform workers constitute a rapidly growing 
section of the labour force that often lack access to formal social security and health 
coverage. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department take up the 
matter with the Ministry of Labour and Employment on priority so that the modalities 
for identification, enrolment and coverage of gig and platform workers are finalised 
expeditiously and the scheme is implemented without further delay. Early 
operationalisation of this initiative would help extend the benefits of financial protection 
in healthcare to a vulnerable and expanding workforce segment and strengthen the 
overall objective of inclusive health coverage. 
(Para 4.6.23) 
 
211. The Committee further notes  that despite the growing utilisation of the scheme, 
several implementation-related challenges persist, including delays in raising demand by 
some States/UTs, limited awareness among eligible beneficiaries, and occasional delays 

310 
 
in settlement of claims of empanelled hospitals. The Committee, therefore, recommends 
the Department to intensify awareness and outreach campaigns across rural, tribal and 
remote areas so that eligible families are fully informed about the benefits of the scheme 
and are not left out from enrolment.  
(Para 4.6.24) 
212. The Committee further recommends that the Department should strengthen 
coordination with States/UTs to ensure timely raising of claims and efficient utilisation 
of allocated funds, particularly in regions where utilisat ion remains low. The Committee 
further recommends that the Department to streamline and strictly monitor the claim 
settlement mechanism to ensure that payments to empanelled hospitals are processed 
within the stipulated timelines. In addition, the package rates under the scheme may be 
periodically reviewed in consultation with States/UTs and healthcare providers so as to 
maintain financial viability for hospitals and prevent denial of treatment to 
beneficiaries. Such measures would enhance the effectiveness , transparency and 
sustainability of the scheme while ensuring equitable access to quality healthcare for all 
eligible citizens. 
(Para 4.6.25) 
213. The Committee observes that the effectiveness of the scheme is closely linked to 
the availability and equitable distribution of empanelled hospitals and quality healthcare 
infrastructure across States/UTs. The Committee wishes to highlight that although a 
large number of hospitals have been empanelled under the scheme, but there continue to 
be regional disparities, particularly in rural, aspirational and remote areas where access 
to empanelled facilities remains limited. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the 
Department, in coordination with States/UTs, take proactive measures to expand the 
network of empa nelled public and private hospitals in underserved regions so that 
beneficiaries do not have to travel long distances to avail treatment. The Committee also 
emphasises the need to strengthen public health institutions and encourage their greater 
participation under the scheme, along with periodic quality audits of empanelled 
facilities to ensure adherence to prescribed standards of care. Such steps would enhance 
accessibility, improve service delivery and ensure that the benefits of the scheme reach 
beneficiaries across all regions in an equitable manner. 
(Para 4.6.26) 
 
214. The Committee notes that the success of the scheme also depends on robust 
monitoring, transparency and effective use of digital platforms for beneficiary 
identification, hospital empanel ment and claim processing. While the Department has 
developed IT-based systems for implementation and monitoring, the Committee is of the 
view that there is scope for further strengthening data analytics and real -time 
monitoring mechanisms to detect irregu larities, prevent fraudulent claims and improve 
overall efficiency of the scheme. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the 
Department to  strengthen the digital ecosystem of the scheme by integrating advanced 
analytics, periodic audits and grievance r edressal mechanisms so as to ensure 
transparency and accountability in its implementation. The Committee desires that 

311 
 
feedback received from beneficiaries and empanelled hospitals be systematically 
collected and analysed to identify operational issues and improve service delivery under 
the scheme. Such measures would help in ensuring that the scheme functions in a 
transparent, efficient and beneficiary -centric manner while safeguarding public 
resources. 
(Para 4.6.27) 
PROMOTION OF INDIGENOUS MEDICAL TECHNOLO GY AND EASING 
REGULATIONS 
 
215. The Committee considers that the Department should adopt a coordinated 
strategy in 2026 –27 to promote indigenous medical technology while simultaneously 
improving affordability and accessibility of healthcare. This should in clude prioritising 
procurement under ―Make in India‖ guidelines, rationalising duties and taxes on 
essential medical devices and drugs, and streamlining regulatory approvals through a 
single-window mechanism to encourage innovation and faster adoption.  
(Para 5.1.4) 
AYUSHMAN BHARAT SCHEME 
216. The Committee recommends  that the Department should further build on the 
demonstrated expansion of Ayushman Bharat –PMJAY by instituting a comprehensive 
monitoring and sustainability framework  to manage the significant  rise in beneficiaries 
and expenditure. While the increase reflects improved access, awareness, and hospital 
empanelment, the Committee urges the Department to regularly review Health Benefit 
Packages to align with clinical and cost trends, strengthen frau d detection through 
AI/ML analytics, and ensure equitable distribution of empanelled hospitals across 
underserved regions.  
(Para 5.2.2) 
217. The Committee recommends that the Department must expedite the planned 
expansion of Ayushman Arogya Mandirs (AAMs)  in Delhi from the current 370 to the 
proposed 1,139 centres through a phased but time‑bound roadmap. While approvals 
have been secured and implementation activities are underway, the Committee urges the 
Department to ensure that infrastructure strengtheni ng, staffing, and service readiness 
requirements are completed within defined milestones.  
(Para 5.2.4) 
218. The Committee recommends that the Department  must expedite the extension of 
Ayushman Bharat –PMJAY benefits to AYUSH hospitals and ensure full 
implementation across all major cancer institutions, including Tata Memorial Hospital 
and those under the National Cancer Grid. While 255 cancer hospitals including
