Chittaranjan National Cancer Institute are already affiliated and providing services 
under the scheme, the Committee urges the Department to establish a time-bound 

312 
 
roadmap for universal empanelment of recognised AYUSH and cancer care centres , 
backed by streamlined regulatory approvals and adequate reimbursement packages.  
(Para 5.2.6) 
219. The Commi ttee recommends that the Department take proactive measures to 
make high -cost immunotherapy cancer treatments, now exempted from customs duty, 
more accessible under public schemes like Ayushman Bharat –PMJAY. While PMJAY 
currently provides coverage of ₹5 lakh per family and supports over 500 oncology 
procedures, the Committee urges the Department to develop special high -cost treatment 
packages for immunotherapy and advanced cancer care, supported by pooled financing 
and negotiated pricing with manufacturers.  
(Para 5.2.8) 
220. The Committee is of the considered view that the Department must strengthen 
affordability and accessibility of high-cost treatments under Ayushman Bharat–PMJAY 
by ensuring that revisions to Health Benefit Pack age (HBP) rates are undertaken in a 
systematic and time -bound manner , with transparent alignment to market costs and 
utilisation trends. The Committee further urges the Department to incentivise private 
hospital empanelment by addressing reimbursement conc erns, simplifying regulatory 
processes, and enhancing grievance redressal mechanisms.  
(Para 5.2.10) 
221. The Committee recommends that the Department consider revising the 
Ayushman Bharat–PMJAY coverage framework by introducing a differential treatment 
cost model for high -cost and complex procedures. The current ₹5 lakh coverage limit 
does not adequately meets the needs of most beneficiaries, as certain advanced 
interventions such as organ transplants like liver transplant, cardiac surgeries, and 
immunotherapy treatments far exceed the threshold. The Committee, therefore 
reiterates its recommendation to enhance the ₹5 lakh coverage limit to ₹10 lakh under 
Ayushman Bharat, and create special high -cost packages within PMJAY, supported by 
pooled financing, neg otiated pricing with hospitals and manufacturers, and co -funding 
mechanisms with States. Such targeted enhancement will ensure that beneficiaries 
requiring expensive, life -saving procedures are not financially excluded, while 
maintaining fiscal sustainability for the scheme overall. 
(Para 5.2.12) 
222. The Committee desires that the Department should further should strengthen 
mechanisms to ensure time-bound approval of cashless treatment cases under 
Ayushman Bharat–PMJAY, given persistent delays despite defi ned turnaround norms. 
While measures such as Aadhaar -based authentication, retrospective pre -authorization, 
auto-approval for high -utilisation packages, and real -time dashboards have been 
introduced, the Committee urges the Department to institute strict m onitoring of 
adherence to the six -hour Turn-Around Time (TAT) , backed by penalty provisions for 
hospitals or agencies causing undue delays. Additionally, periodic audits of 

313 
 
pre-authorization workflows, expansion of auto -approval to more low -risk procedures, 
and deployment of AI-enabled case tracking should be prioritised.  
(Para 5.2.14) 
223. The Committee is of the firm view that the Department must issue guidelines for 
clear and standardised documentation of diagnosis and prescriptions by doctors under 
Ayushman Bharat–PMJAY to strengthen transparency in treatment and billing. While 
bundled package payments and Standard Treatment Guidelines already promote 
uniformity, the Committee urges the Department to integrate mandatory digital 
prescription and diagnosi s records into the PMJAY IT platform, linked with 
Aadhaar-based beneficiary authentication to ensure traceability of clinical decisions, 
reduce unwarranted variations in practice, and enhance accountability of empanelled 
hospitals.  
(Para 5.2.16) 
224. The Committee recommends the Department to intensify efforts to achieve 100% 
Ayushman Card saturation within a defined timeline, building on the success of 
initiatives such as Aapke Dwar Ayushman  and BIS -2.0 self -verification. While over 43 
crore cards have al ready been issued, the Committee urges the Department to ensure 
last-mile enrolment through expanded offline modules in connectivity -poor regions and 
continuous mobilisation of frontline workers. To strengthen acceptance in private 
hospitals, the Departmen t should address reimbursement concerns, streamline 
empanelment processes, and enhance transparency through real -time dashboards and 
grievance redressal mechanisms.  
(Para 5.2.18) 
225. The Committee recommends that the Department publish a clear status rep ort 
on the functionality of Ayushman Arogya Mandirs (AAMs), specifically highlighting the 
availability of essential medicines, diagnostic services, and expanded primary care 
including mental health, oral, ENT, and eye health services. While over 1.83 lakh AAMs 
are operational, the Committee urges the Department to institute regular audits and 
public disclosure of service readiness, ensuring that each centre meets the prescribed 
standards of comprehensive care. To improve accountability and awareness, the 
Committee desires that besides NHA portal, the updated lists of empanelled hospitals 
should also be prominently displayed at local AAMs.  
(Para 5.2.20) 
AIIMS INSTITUTION 
226. The Committee recommends that the Department adopt a dual strategy of digital 
optimisation and infrastructure expansion  to address the daily footfall of 18,000 –20,000 
patients at AIIMS Delhi. While initiatives such as ORS, TRED, inter -AIIMS referral 
systems, two -shift OPDs, and ABDM integration have been acknowledged by the 

314 
 
Committee, th e Department should prioritise capacity augmentation and early 
completion of ongoing projects at AIIMS Delhi and its extended Jhajjar campus.  
(Para 5.3.4) 
227. The Committee recommends that to ensure optimal utilization of tertiary care 
institutions like AIIMS and prevent excessive patient load, it may be considered to 
strengthen a structured referral -based healthcare delivery system. Under such a 
framework, tertiary care hospitals like AIIMS would primarily manage patients 
requiring advanced diagnostics, specialized interventions, and complex 
multidisciplinary care. Common and uncomplicated conditions should ideally be 
managed at primary and secondary healthcare facilities, supported by clear referral 
guidelines and effective triage mechanisms. Strengtheni ng the infrastructure, specialist 
availability, and diagnostic capabilities at district and sub -district hospitals, along with 
implementing robust referral and reverse -referral pathways, would help ensure 
appropriate patient distribution across levels of c are. Such measures would enable 
tertiary institutions to focus on complex clinical care, research, and high -quality 
postgraduate medical training while improving efficiency and accessibility across the 
healthcare system. 
(Para 5.3.5) 
228. In parallel, the Department should undertake a review study of adjoining regions 
to assess patient load and healthcare gaps , and consider establishing satellite centres of 
AIIMS in Delhi‘s periphery and nearby regions to decentralise  routine and moderately 
complex care, re serving the Delhi main AIIMS campus for high -end tertiary 
procedures. This dual approach will reduce overcrowding, improve access, and 
strengthen equitable healthcare delivery across the National Capital Region. In this 
context, the feasibility of utilisin g the 400 acres of centrally acquired land at Badarpur 
may also be examined for setting up of another AIIMS with separate administrative 
structure, particularly to serve patients from Delhi, Haryana, and Uttar Pradesh. 
(Para 5.3.6) 
229. Given the availability of unutilised land parcels and ongoing projects such as the 
RAK OPD Block, Mother and Child Block, Super -Specialty OPD at Masjid Moth, and 
the 150 -bedded Critical Care Block under PM -ABHIM, the Committee urges the 
Department to adopt a phased expansion  plan with dedicated faculty recruitment and 
infrastructure strengthening . Additionally, the upcoming AIIMS at Rewari, Haryana, 
scheduled for completion in December 2026, should be leveraged to reduce referral 
pressure on AIIMS Delhi. These measures will e nhance patient access, reduce 
overcrowding, and ensure equitable distribution of advanced healthcare services in the 
National Capital Region. 
(Para 5.3.7) 

315 
 
230. The Committee recommends that the Department urgently address the 
infrastructure gaps and staff shortages at AIIMS Guwahati, given its critical role in 
serving patients from all eight North‑Eastern states. While the Institute has made 
commendable progress with 37 functional departments, advanced diagnostic facilities, 
and steadily rising OPD/IPD numb ers, the current staffing levels, 117 faculty against 
183 sanctioned posts and 879 non‑faculty against 1,630 sanctioned, remain inadequate to 
meet the growing patient demand. The Committee, therefore, strongly recommends the 
Department to adopt a phased expansion plan under PMSSY, ensuring early completion 
of approved capital works such as additional hostels and clinical facilities. 
(Para 5.3.11) 
231. The Committee recommends that the Department chalk out time -bound Action 
Plan to strengthen tertiary health care capacity in Tripura by building upon the 
complete upgradation of Agartala Medical College under PMSSY. The Committee urges 
the Department to establish an AIIMS in Tripura. The Committee  also urges the 
Department to undertake a feasibility review of r egional healthcare needs in the 
North‑East, particularly in underserved states, to explore feasibility of AIIMS‑like 
institutions or satellite centres, thereby reducing dependence on AIIMS Guwahati and 
improving equitable access to advanced healthcare for patients across Tripura and 
neighbouring states. 
(Para 5.3.13) 
232. The Committee expects the Department to intensify measures to improve patient 
experience and staff behaviour at AIIMS Delhi, particularly during night -time 
emergency services when patient vulnerability is highest. The Committee urges the 
Department to institutionalise regular, mandatory training modules for all categories of 
staff, backed by periodic performance audits and patient feedback mechanisms. 
(Para 5.3.15) 
 
233. The Committee, keep ing in view the high attrition rate in AIIMS, recommends  
the Department to introduce a structured inter -AIIMS transfer policy to allow doctors 
to relocate closer to their hometowns, thereby reducing attrition and improving 
retention. While existing measure s such as academic allowances, research funding, and 
housing facilities are valuable, enabling faculty mobility across AIIMS institutions will 
directly address relocation concerns and provide flexibility without loss of seniority or 
service benefits. This approach, combined with accelerated recruitment and 
strengthened career progression pathways, will help retain talent within the AIIMS 
system while ensuring equitable distribution of qualified doctors across regions. 
(Para 5.3.17) 
 
234. The Committee notes the submission of the Department that all newly established 
All India Institute of Medical Sciences institutions, including AIIMS Rishikesh, are 
planned with standardized infrastructure under the approved Master Plan and Detailed 
Project Report, including  departments of forensic medicine, mortuary facilities and 

316 
 
simulation laboratories for practical training. While acknowledging the existence of 
such provisions on paper, the Committee recommends that the Department put in place 
a periodic monitoring and ev aluation mechanism to ensure that these facilities are fully 
functional, adequately equipped and effectively utilized for practical training, 
particularly in critical areas such as forensic medicine and postmortem services, so as to 
maintain uniform standa rds of medical education and training across all AIIMS 
institutions. 
(Para 5.3.19) 
FOOD SAFETY AND STANDARDS AUTHORITY OF INDIA 
235. The Committee recommends that the Department, through FSSAI, intensify 
efforts to curb the misuse of preservatives in packaged food, given the rising incidence of 
chronic kidney diseases. While regulatory limits on additives are already harmonised 
with Codex standards and enforcement mechanisms such as surveillance, random 
sampling, and third -party audits are in place, the Com mittee urges the Department to 
strengthen risk -based monitoring and targeted enforcement drives for high -risk food 
categories. Additionally, the Committee recommends enhancing public awareness 
campaigns on the health risks of excessive preservative consump tion, coupled with 
stricter penalties for non-compliance by Food Business Operators. 
(Para 5.4.2) 
REGULATION OF QUACKS PRACTICING MEDICINE 
236. The Committee is of considered view that the Department must strengthen 
enforcement against quack practitioners by ensuring strict implementation of the 
Clinical Establishments Act, 2010 in States and Union Territories, particularly in North 
India where the problem is acute. The Committee recommends the Department to 
institute regular inspections, coordinated drives , and public reporting mechanisms to 
identify and penalize unauthorized practitioners. Further, the Committee recommends 
enhancing coordination between State Medical Councils, District Authorities, and law 
enforcement agencies to ensure that only duly qual ified and registered medical 
professionals are permitted to practice.  
(Para 5.5.2) 
STRENGTHENING PREVENTIVE CARE 
237. The Committee is of the firm view that the Department must strengthen 
preventive public health systems to proactively manage seasonal out breaks of diseases 
like malaria, particularly in high -risk urban areas and undertake immediate measures 
to contain and eliminate the outbreak then and there. The Committee, therefore, urges 
the Department to institutionalise year -round surveillance, rapid diagnostic capacity, 
and integrated vector management with special focus on urban slums and densely 
populated areas. Further, the Committee recommends scaling up community awareness 
drives beyond seasonal observances, ensuring sustained behavioural change in 
sanitation and mosquito-control practices.  
(Para 5.6.2) 

317 
 
238. The Committee recommends that the Department intensify efforts to ensure 
equitable immunization coverage for migrant populations, who often face 
mobility-related barriers in accessing routine  services. While the Universal 
Immunization Programme already provides free vaccines nationwide, the Committee 
urges the Department to strengthen micro -planning, targeted outreach at high -density 
migrant habitations, and real -time tracking through the U -WIN digital platform. The 
Committee also recommends enhancing inter -state coordination and portability of 
immunization records, so that migrant families can seamlessly continue vaccination 
schedules after relocation.  
(Para 5.6.5) 
239. The Committee emphasiz es that the Department must strengthen nationwide 
implementation of the STEMI -based Heart Attack Management Programme under 
NHM while ensuring uniform standards of care across States through the 
hub-and-spoke model. The Committee further urges the Departme nt to develop a 
structured, nationwide stroke management programme on similar lines, with dedicated 
stroke units, 24×7 imaging facilities, and referral linkages for advanced interventions, 
and the setting up of Mobile Stroke Units at District Hospitals to ensure timely diagnosis 
and treatment in underserved areas. This dual approach, consolidating STEMI care and 
institutionalising structured stroke management, will enhance early diagnosis, timely 
stabilization, and equitable access to advanced emergency car e, thereby reducing 
mortality and long-term disability from acute cardiac and cerebrovascular events. 
(Para 5.6.8) 
240. The Committee recommends  that the Department evaluate the feasibility of 
introducing a structured Adult Immunization Programme targeting elderly populations, 
particularly citizens aged 60+ with comorbidities, in view of the rising pneumonia 
burden and declining respiratory health highlighted by Longitudinal Ageing Study in 
India (LASI) findings. While elderly care is currently addressed th rough NPHCE and 
Ayushman Bharat CPHC, the Committee urges the Department to initiate expert 
consultations with National Technical Advisory Group on Immunization (NTAGI)  and 
CDSCO to assess epidemiological evidence, cost-effectiveness, and operational feasibility 
of pneumococcal vaccination for high-risk groups. 
(Para 5.6.11) 
 
241. The Committee recommends that the Department  to expedite the rollout and 
public availability of indigenous cervical cancer vaccines, given their potential 
affordability and impac t on reducing disease burden. While CDSCO has already 
granted approval for manufacturing and marketing of the quadrivalent HPV vaccine 
under the New Drugs and Clinical Trials Rules, 2019, the Committee urges the 
Department to ensure timely procurement, inc lusion in the national immunization 
schedule, and awareness campaigns to maximize uptake. 
(Para 5.6.13) 

318 
 
MEDICAL EDUCATION 
 
242. The Committee recommends that the Department to continue expanding medical 
education capacity while simultaneously addressing th e demand-supply gap created by 
the large number of NEET aspirants each year. The Committee urges the Department to 
adopt a multi-pronged strategy . This should include further expansion of government 
medical colleges in underserved regions, including the No rth-East region like the States 
of Arunachal Pradesh and Nagaland, strengthening faculty availability, and enhancing 
infrastructure for super -specialty training. These measures will ensure that the 
adequate increase in medical seats translates into equitable access and quality training.  
(Para 5.7.3) 
243. The Committee has been apprised of that under the present regulations of the 
National Medical Commission, MBBS students are currently permitted a maximum of 
four attempts to clear the First Professional Ex amination. Considering the academic 
intensity of foundational subjects such as anatomy, physiology, and biochemistry, this 
restriction can be overly stringent for many students adjusting to the demanding nature 
of medical education. The Committee, therefor e, suggested that the permissible limit be 
increased to six attempts, while ensuring that students complete the entire MBBS course 
within a maximum period of ten years from the date of admission. This balanced 
approach will maintain academic standards whil e providing a more humane and 
student-friendly framework, preventing capable students from being forced to 
discontinue medical education due to early academic setbacks. 
(Para 5.7.4) 
STRENGTHENING OF HEALTH INFRASTRUCTURE AND RESOURCES 
244. The Committee desires that the Department should intensify efforts to strengthen 
healthcare infrastructure in Tier -2 and Tier -3 cities, where higher mortality rates 
highlight critical gaps in service delivery. While NHM and PM -ABHIM are already 
supporting the establishmen t of sub -centres, PHCs, CHCs, sub -district hospitals, and 
critical care blocks, the Committee recommends the Department to ensure timely 
completion and operationalisation of these facilities with adequate staffing and 
equipment. The Committee also recommen ds that States be encouraged to 
institutionalise Public Health Management Cadres to professionalize hospital 
administration. Segregating clinical, public health, and management roles will improve 
efficiency, accountability, and patient outcomes. 
(Para 5.8.2) 
245. The expansion of premier institutions such as the All India Institute of Medical 
Sciences (AIIMS) has significantly strengthened tertiary healthcare in India. However, 
establishing AIIMS in every region requires substantial financial investment and  long 
gestation periods. A pragmatic approach would be to establish Regional Medical 
Institutes (RMIs) in underserved regions as advanced tertiary hospitals with strong 

319 
 
teaching and research facilities. These institutes can address region -specific healthcare 
needs and significantly improve access to specialized treatment. States such as Tamil 
Nadu demonstrate how institutions like Madras Medical College evolved in to large 
tertiary referral centres serving multiple districts. By first developing RMIs and la ter 
upgrading the most successful institutions into AIIMS -level centres, the government can 
ensure faster healthcare expansion, better regional equity, and sustainable institutional 
growth. 
(Para 5.8.3) 
246. The Committee recommends that the Department accelerate the establishment of 
trauma centres in every district to reduce the burden on major city hospitals. While 196 
Trauma Care Facilities and 14 Centres of Excellence have already been sanctioned, and 
expansion is planned under Budget 2026 -27 to increas e district capacities by 50%, the 
Committee urges the Department to lay down a clear phased implementation timeline 
with annual targets to ensure timely coverage across all districts. The Committee 
recommends leveraging Centres of Excellence for capacity b uilding, standardized 
treatment protocols, and referral linkages so that district -level trauma centres are fully 
equipped to handle emergencies. The structured expansion, backed by monitoring and 
accountability mechanisms, will strengthen emergency care na tionwide and reduce 
preventable mortality from trauma and burn injuries. 
(Para 5.8.5) 
247. The Committee views that the Department must address service gaps, especially 
strengthening women and child healthcare, at Community Health Centres by ensuring 
rational deployment of specialists  such as gynaecologists and paediatricians with 
establishing facilities like ambulance services and blood storage units, even in facilities 
with lower delivery loads, particularly in rural areas, so that broader care availabili ty is 
not compromised. The Committee urges the Department to guide States to create 
regular posts under IPHS norms  and strengthen retention policies to stabilize specialist 
availability at CHCs. Further, the Committee recommends expanding reservation and 
incentives for in -service candidates serving in rural areas , alongside scaling up DNB 
seats and targeted training, to build a sustainable pipeline of specialists. The measures 
so undertaken will ensure that CHCs remain adequately staffed, provide comprehens ive 
maternal and child health services, and reduce dependence on district hospitals for 
routine care.  
(Para 5.8.8) 
248. The Committee recommends  that the Department ensure the timely 
operationalisation of approved Day Care Cancer Centres (DCCCs)  across al l 
States/UTs, with particular attention to regions where centres remain non -functional 
despite approvals and fund allocation, such as Delhi. While 479 centres are reported 
functional against the target of 200, uneven distribution and delays in staff deploy ment 
highlight the need for stronger State-level accountability and monitoring mechanisms . 
The Committee recommends integrating DCCCs more closely with the broader 
NP-NCD framework through population-based screening, referral linkages, and 

320 
 
partnerships with medical colleges, accredited private facilities, and NGOs . Establishing 
a clear phased timeline for expansion, coupled with transparent reporting on manpower 
and equipment availability, will ensure equitable access to cancer care and reduce the 
burden on tertiary hospitals. 
(Para 5.8.11) 
DISEASE CONTROL 
249. The Committee believes that while the Department has achieved significant 
epidemiological milestones, evidenced by the steep decline in malaria cases in Tripura 
and the sustained dengue case fatality rate of 0.09% nationally, the existing framework's 
reliance on state -initiated Programme Implementation Plans (PIPs) for critical 
infrastructure risks creating regional disparities in healthcare access. The Committee is 
of the view that passive central sup port is insufficient for addressing acute rural gaps, 
even with the commendable expansions under the Integrated Health Information 
Platform (IHIP). The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department 
immediately transition from a responsive to a proac tive national mandate, directly 
standardizing the deployment of advanced diagnostic laboratories, Rapid Response 
Teams (RRTs), critical care units, ambulance services and blood storage units across all 
high-burden and rural districts, ensuring that inter -sectoral coordination under the One 
Health approach is uniformly operationalized rather than contingent upon individual 
state proposals. 
(Para 5.9.4) 
250. The Committee appreciates the targeted strategies under the TB Mukt Bharat 
Abhiyan to address drug -resistant TB, particularly at the rural hospital level. While 
significant progress has been made through universal drug susceptibility testing, 
availability of newer drugs, and decentralized DR -TB centres, the Committee urges the 
Department to ensure full dep loyment and optimal utilization of portable AI -enabled 
diagnostic technologies, such as handheld X -ray machines and NAAT facilities, across all 
districts. The Committee also recommends greater involvement of private healthcare 
partners in TB detection, dia gnosis, notification, and treatment under a strengthened 
public–private partnership framework. Further, integration of portable diagnostics with 
Nikshay tracking, nutrition support, curbing illicit liquor consumption and treatment 
adherence mechanisms shou ld be strengthened, alongside capacity building and training 
of frontline health workers for early detection, diagnosis, and timely referral. 
(Para 5.9.6) 
251. The Committee notes that portable X -ray systems are being used for rapid and 
accessible chest imaging for early detection of Tuberculosis. The Committee is, however, 
concerned about the influx of cheap devices, reportedly sourced from countries 
bordering India and marketed as ―Make in India‖, emitting high radiation exposure. 
Considering the high pat ient load in Government healthcare facilities, the Committee 
recommends that all deployed portable X -Ray systems adhere to the highest available  
global safety and precision standards of 0.4mm or smaller focal spot of X- Ray tube with 

321 
 
adjustable tube curre nt of minimum 5 mA to a maximum of 8 mA so as to ensure 
responsible use of diagnostic radiation for improved patient safety. 
(Para 5.9.7) 
CANCER PREVENTION 
 
252. The Committee is of the strong view that the Department must intensify efforts to 
strengthen national cancer screening programmes, particularly for breast, cervical, and 
oral cancers, in high -burden states such as Madhya Pradesh. The Committee, in this 
regard, recommends prioritizing capacity building of frontline health workers, 
expansion of screening infrastructure at Ayushman Arogya Mandirs, and integration of 
tertiary care facilities with district -level programmes. A clear monitoring framework 
with state-wise reporting on screened, diagnosed, and treated cases will abridge service 
gaps, uniform coverage, reduce late -stage detection, and strengthen cancer control in 
high-burden regions.  
(Para 5.10.3) 
253. The Committee appreciates that the Department build upon the nationwide 
rollout of the HPV vaccine for adolescent girls by considering to the s uggestion for the 
launch of a structured ―Mother and Daughter Cancer Prevention Programme 
(MDCPP).‖  The Committee recommends that MDCPP should combine mammography 
screening for women above 40 years with the ongoing cervical cancer vaccination drive 
for ad olescent girls, thereby creating a comprehensive, life‑course approach to cancer 
prevention. Such an integrated initiative would strengthen early detection and 
prevention strategies, reduce the burden of breast and cervical cancers in high‑risk 
populations, and promote awareness through family‑based outreach. By aligning 
vaccination and screening under a unified framework, the Department can ensure 
greater coverage, continuity of care, and long‑term impact on women‘s health outcomes 
nationwide. 
(Para 5.10.5) 
 
254. The Committee desires that the Department must ensure effective 
operationalisation of cancer care facilities approved under the Tertiary Care Cancer 
Scheme, particularly the State Cancer Institute at Guwahati Medical College in Assam 
and the three institutions in Maharashtra. The Committee urges the Department to 
establish a robust monitoring framework to track progress on infrastructure, manpower 
deployment, and equipment installation. The Committee recommends that reasons for 
shifting the approved  facility from Dhule to Jalgaon be clearly documented and 
communicated to ensure transparency and accountability in planning. Strengthening 
coordination with State Governments and ensuring timely completion of sanctioned 
projects will also help expand equitable access to advanced cancer care across regions. 
(Para 5.10.7) 
 

322 
 
NATIONAL MEDICAL COMMISSION AND OTHER REGULATORY 
INSTITUTIONS 
 
255. The Committee strongly recommends  that the Department must ensure that the 
rapid expansion of MBBS and PG seats under th e oversight of the National Medical 
Commission is matched with robust quality assurance mechanisms to safeguard training 
standards and prevent future unemployment or under -skilled specialists. In parallel to 
the establishment of new medical colleges, seat expansion, and recognition of DNB 
qualifications, the Committee urges the Department to strengthen faculty development, 
clinical exposure, and skill -based training modules  across institutions. Further, the 
Committee recommends instituting periodic accredit ation, competency -based 
assessments, and structured career planning  to align medical education output with 
national health system needs. By balancing expansion with quality and workforce 
planning, the Department can ensure that the growing pool of medical graduates and 
specialists are well -trained, employable, and capable of delivering high -quality 
healthcare services nationwide. 
(Para 5.11.2) 
256. The Committee is of the view that the Department should offer competitive pay 
other incentives under NHM to en courage MBBS doctors to serve in rural and remote 
areas. While monetary measures such as hard area allowances, honoraria, and 
negotiable salaries have been introduced, however, the Committee urges the Department 
to expand non -monetary incentives including preferential admission in postgraduate 
courses, additional marks or reservation in NEET -PG for rural service, and improved 
living and accommodation facilities. Further, the Committee recommends scaling up 
multi-skilling and continuous professional developm ent programmes to enhance career 
prospects for doctors serving in difficult areas. A balanced package of financial, 
academic, and professional incentives will ensure sustained availability of qualified 
medical professionals in rural hospitals. 
(Para 5.11.4) 
257. The Committee notes a concerning inconsistency in the financial reporting and 
legislative oversight mechanisms among the apex regulatory bodies under the 
Department of Health and Family Welfare. The Committee takes into account the 
substantial incre ase in revenue generated by regulatory bodies such as the National 
Medical Commission, which rose significantly from ₹125.36 crore in 2022 -23 to ₹406.17 
crore in 2024-25, largely through application, inspection and licensing fees following the 
revision of norms. While the Department has stated that the accounts are audited by the 
Comptroller and Auditor General of India and laid before Parliament, the Committee 
recommends that the Department ensure the preparation and publication of more 
detailed, consolida ted annual financial reports indicating the source -wise revenue 
generation and head-wise utilization of funds by all regulatory bodies under its purview, 
including the Dental Council of India and Pharmacy Council of India, so as to 
strengthen transparency, accountability and parliamentary oversight. 
(Para 5.11.13 

323 
 
258. The Committee also observes that significant amounts are collected through 
regulatory and licensing fees, including those processed through the Bharatkosh portal 
by the Central Drugs Standard Control Organization. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends that the Department evolve a uniform reporting and disclosure framework 
for all health sector regulatory bodies to periodically report revenue receipts, security 
deposits and their utilisation, particularly in the context of increased application fees for 
medical colleges, so that the financial resources generated through regulatory functions 
are transparently monitored and effectively aligned with the broader objectives of 
strengthening medical education and healthcare regulation in the country. 
(Para 5.11.14) 
 
259. The Committee believes that it is financially imprudent and contrary to the 
principles of parliamentary accountability for statutory bodies generating hundreds of 
crores in regulatory ch arges to evade legislative scrutiny. Specifically, the Pharmacy 
Council of India (PCI) generates upwards of ₹300 crore annually through Pharmacy 
Education Regulatory Charges (PERC) but bypasses parliamentary presentation by 
exploiting General Financial Rul e (GFR) 238 (5) and (6), citing that its direct 
government grant for salaries is only ₹20 lakh. Similarly, the Dental Council of India 
(DCI) retains its annual revenue of approximately ₹36 crore entirely as internal receipts 
under the provisions of the Dentists Act, 1948. 
(Para 5.11.15) 
 
260. The Committee is of the view that revenue generated by statutory regulatory 
bodies through mandatory fees, inspections, and licensing is inherently public money. 
Permitting these funds to be retained autonomously as in ternal receipts or parked 
indefinitely in Fixed Deposit Receipts (FDRs) without mandatory parliamentary 
oversight creates a severe accountability vacuum and contravenes Government‘s 
standard financial propriety. 
(Para 5.11.16) 
 
261. The Committee firmly be lieves that the Department should complement the 
expansion of PG and super -specialty seats with targeted strategies to encourage medical 
students to opt for critical disciplines such as neurosurgery and cardiovascular surgery. 
While seat growth has been aligned with disease burden and tertiary care requirements, 
the Committee urges the Department to strengthen mentorship programmes, structured 
career pathways, and financial/academic incentives to attract talent into these high -need 
specializations. 
(Para 5.11.18) 
CENTRAL GOVERNMENT HEALTH SCHEME 
 
262. The Committee desires that the Department may consider to extend cashless 
treatment facilities for retired Air India employees by ensuring seamless linkage 

324 
 
between CGHS, Air India Assets Holding Limited (AIAHL) , and UTI Infrastructure 
Technology and Services Limited (UTIITSL), thereby reducing delays in service 
delivery. Further, the Committee recommends that expansion of CGHS wellness clinics 
across districts be examined with integration into the broader contin uum of care. A 
phased approach to extending CGHS coverage to other autonomous bodies such as KVS 
and NVS will promote equity and optimize resources besides extending. 
(Para 5.12.2) 
 
NATIONAL HEALTH MISSION 
263. The Committee recommends that the Department strengthen measures to ensure 
timely and adequate payment of honorarium to ASHA workers  under NHM, given their 
expanding workload and responsibilities. In parallel, the Committee urges the 
Department to establish a transparent and streamlined payment mecha nism under the 
SNA SPARSH fund flow system to avoid delays and ensure predictability of income. 
Further, the Committee recommends examining the scope for enhanced central 
allocation under NHM Flexipool funds  to support increased remuneration and 
performance-linked incentives for ASHAs and facilitators. A balanced package of 
financial recognition, social security coverage, and career progression opportunities will 
sustain motivation, strengthen community health outreach, and improve service delivery 
at the grassroots level. 
(Para 5.13.4) 
264. The Committee is of the view that the Department  should chalk out Accelerated  
Action Plan to expand the Pradhan Mantri National Dialysis Programme (PMNDP) in a 
structured manner to meet the growing demand for dialysis services. While financial 
and technical support is being provided to States/UTs under NHM, the Committee urges 
the Department to ensure universal availability of dialysis units at district hospitals, 
with clear annual expansion targets and monitoring of se rvice quality. Further, the 
Committee recommends strengthening public –private partnership models for rapid 
infrastructure scaling, alongside establishing a transparent mechanism for donor 
management and coordination with organ transplant registries. The Co mmittee also 
desires that Department ensure ‗Mobile Dialysis Facility‘ for remote, rural and hilly 
areas. 
(Para 5.13.6) 
265. The Committee susggests the Department to extend  incentives for private parties 
to set up hospitals in Tier -II and Tier -III cities  under NHM, particularly through 
well-structured Public –Private Partnership (PPP) models. While support is currently 
provided based on State/UT proposals in their Programme Implementation Plans, the 
Committee urges the Department to develop a uniform incen tive framework that 
includes financial viability gap funding, concessional land allotment, tax benefits, and 
streamlined regulatory clearances to attract private investment in underserved regions. 
The Committee further recommends prioritizing district -level hospital expansion in 

325 
 
underserved regions at Block level and to ensure equitable access to secondary and 
tertiary care.  
(Para 5.13.8) 
266. The Committee is of the view that the Department may consider revising the 
eligibility criteria for cochlear impla nts under RBSK 2.0 to include children up to 3 
years of age, in view of clinical evidence that early intervention significantly improves 
language and cognitive outcomes. While the current limit is 2 years, expanding eligibility 
will ensure that children di agnosed slightly later are not excluded from timely 
treatment. 
(Para 5.13.10) 
MEDICINE 
 
267. The Committee is of the considered opinion  that the Department, in coordination 
with the Department of Pharmaceuticals and NPPA, must undertake measures to 
minimize the gap between MRP and actual sale prices of medicines  by ensuring stricter 
enforcement of ceiling prices under DPCO, 2013 and enhancing transparency in retail 
pricing across States. Uniform monitoring mechanisms should be instituted to prevent 
regional disparities and ensure affordability for patients nationwide. Further, the 
Committee recommends that the Department examine the proposal to extend duty 
exemptions to essential post -transplant immunosuppressant drugs , given their critical 
role in patient survival and long-term care.  
(Para 5.14.2) 
Mental Health 
 
268. The Committee recommends that the Department to seek additional budgetary 
support for suicide prevention initiatives and infertility services in tertiary care 
hospitals and government medical c olleges. In parallel, the Committee urges the 
Department to ensure sustained funding and integration of suicide prevention services 
into district and medical college hospitals, with emphasis on counselling, crisis 
intervention, and follow-up care. 
(Para 5.15.3) 
269. The Committee, further, recommends that the Department examine the 
establishment of infertility clinics within tertiary care institutions, in compliance with 
guidelines and financial provisions, to address rising demand and reduce dependence on 
private facilities. A combined approach of targeted funding, integration into existing 
infrastructure, and structured service delivery will strengthen both mental health and 
reproductive health outcomes nationwide.  
(Para 5.15.4) 
270. The Committee notes w ith concern that Mental health has emerged as a major 
public health concern requiring urgent policy attention. Data from the World Health 
Organization indicates that one in seven individuals in India experiences some form of 
mental disorder, yet the availa bility of trained psychiatrists and mental health 
professionals remains severely limited. According to the National Crime Records 

326 
 
Bureau, India reports over 1.7 lakh suicides every year, reflecting the urgent need for 
expanded psychiatric services and early intervention systems. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends strengthening psychology and psychiatry departments in medical colleges, 
increasing postgraduate seats in psychiatry, and availability of trained counsellors and 
psychologists, particularly at the community and primary healthcare levels.  
(Para 5.15.5) 
271. The Committee recommends that suicide prevention efforts be strengthened 
through a community-based approach that emphasizes training of teachers, community 
healthcare workers, and other key sta keholders to identify early warning signs of 
emotional distress and suicidal behavior and provide timely support or referral. Such 
capacity building initiatives, along with accessible counselling services and sustained 
public awareness campaigns to reduce stigma around mental health, can help ensure 
early intervention and create a supportive environment for individuals at risk.  
 
(Para 5.15.6) 
ORAL HEALTH 
 
272. The committee understands that Oral diseases represent a significant but often 
neglected componen t of the national disease burden. According to the World Health 
Organization, oral diseases affect billions globally, and in India dental caries affect 60 –
90% of school children, while periodontal diseases impact nearly 50% of adults. India 
produces more t han 35,000 dental graduates annually, yet the potential of this 
workforce remains underutilized due to limited national oral health programmes. 
Establishing a Ministry of Oral Health, on the lines of the Ministry of AYUSH, could 
institutionalize preventive  dental care, strengthen research, and integrate oral health 
services in to primary healthcare. International public health models, particularly in 
Brazil and the United Kingdom, shows that structured national oral health programmes 
significantly reduce long-term disease burden and healthcare costs. 
(Para 5.16.1) 
CENTRAL ARMED POLICE FORCES INSTITUTE OF MEDICAL SCIENCES 
 
273. The Committee notes that the 970 -bedded Central Armed Police Forces Institute 
of Medical Sciences (CAPFIMS) hospital at Maidangarhi h as been completed and that 
patient care services have commenced since May 2025 with OPD and diagnostic services 
operational in multiple departments. The Committee also takes note that the institute is 
being run as a campus of All India Institute of Medical  Sciences, New Delhi, and that 
recruitment of regular faculty and staff for different departments is presently 
underway. The Committee recommends that the Department take expeditious steps to 
complete the recruitment of regular faculty and operationalize t he full range of hospital 
and academic services, including the medical, nursing and paramedical colleges, so that 
the institution can function at its intended capacity and effectively serve both CAPF 
personnel and the general public. 
(Para 5.17.2)
